## Life
Through Rebirth Has i98os Post-Modernism died, or has Postmodernism, as it is streamlined today, been born again?
Both arguments are made, but whatever one feels about PM, the life and death of social constructs is a necessary fiction.
We cannot think without them.
The fortunes of civilization are contemplated using Edward Gibbon's famous model, The History of the Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire (even though it survived its fall).
And the crises of modern cities are seen through the lens of Jane Jacobs's The Death and Life of Great American Cities (even though New York has revived its economy twice since she wrote her prophetic words in 1961).
In the i92os art world so many movements came and went that the maxim became: "all the isms are wasms," while—in the i98os—so many wasms revived so quickly that I inverted the proposition with equal validity (and dubiety).
The temptation of critics, and producers such as Henry Ford, is to consign unlikeable trends to the junk-heap of history.
Such death sentences follow on from the historian's metaphor of the birth, maturation, and decline of cultures.
Having had a good run of ten years, Post-Modernism itself was declared moribund by critics by 1985.
But the organic metaphor has a much longer and funnier history than the spasms of “isms" and "wasms," one that goes back further even than Giorgio Vasari's defining usage in Lives of the Artists (1550).
And, following Renaissance architects such as Filarete, Vasari also used the phrase “modern" in a polemically vitalistic way.
Vasari's notion was that the "good Modernism of the Classical Revival" ("buona maniera moderna") had thankfully rendered obsolete the bad Gothic manner of the Germans that was contemporary.
His Italian readers cheered.
But they did have trouble remembering that buona moderna meant ancient, and every so often Vasari had to remind them of the counterintuitive truth, that old dead means new life.
Strange.
One clear explanation for the life and death oscillation, or its necessary fiction, is revealed by the fashion industry, with its iron proposition: nothing is so old as last year's trend.
Thus the global economy thrives on putting the recently fashionable on sale, in order to usher in the next incipient "dead stock." Turnover demands obsolescence.
Furthermore, aesthetic fatigue, as observed by nineteenth-century theorists of art, also demands refreshment.
The Renaissance was based on this psychological insight-spiritual reinvigoration—recounted in the Gospel of John: "unless one is born again he cannot see the kingdom of God." And as historians have recounted, there were many renascences since AD 8oo, using the same vitalistic metaphor.
But how accurate is the art cycle of birth, growth, maturation decay; or of Early, High, Late, Decadent?
Skepticism arrived hard in the twentieth century when historians began emphasizing the survivals of dead Gothic architecture.
What if every movement, even those defined negatively—Gothic, Mannerism, Baroque, Rococo, Impressionism, Fauvism, and Post-Modernism—carried on?
Chaos might rule, and PhDs on Modernism cease.
Indeed confusion would exist, as it did for Vasari.
To put some order into this conceptual tangle, the art and architectural historian Anthony Blunt wrote an analytical piece with an eighteenth-century title, Some Uscs and Misuses of the Terms Baroque and Rococo as Applied to Architecture (i973).
He showed that the terms correctly rely on a common overlap of some ten definers.
To put it bluntly, any stylistic or cultural category is only statistically cogent, only more or less right-with the implication that no architect is completely Baroque or Rococo.
Such a fate for Poštmodernism, of waxing and waning under many aliases, of dying and being reborn, is a statistical affair.
It is also the reason I have continued to map these fortunes in several evolutionary trees, showing that architects and designers jump from one school to another, "more or less” pulled by the strange attractors that make up its coherence.

## Complexity
l: 1970s and 1980s As Judith Gura rightly points out in her "Introduction," Postmodernism of all persuasions is fundamentally concerned with “inclusiveness," is tied to pluralism as a philosophy, and is “not complete." It is open-ended as a historical movement, just as Modernism, its parent, was termed “an unfinished project" by Jürgen Habermas in 198o (reacting to Post-Modernism).
As Gura shows regarding the first period of Post-Modernism, roughly 197o to i985, there are many differences under the capacious umbrella: late Modernists, radical designers, and certain mavericks who do not fit under any parasol.
And still there is an identifiable cluster of architects who adopt the hybrid style of double coding, the characteristic mode of pluralism.
Rightly, to my mind, she emphasizes the Italian design and architectural culture of the i96os-198os as seminal, and is one of the first writers to bring out its importance.
What also emerges from her study is the pluralism of the individuals, something I have always stressed in my evolutionary diagrams.
As the philosopher Isaiah Berlin might have said, with his emphasis on incommensurability: all Post-Modernists are pluralists, but not all pluralists are postmodern.
A lot of traditionalists also support the institutions of society and the tolerance that guarantee variety.
And to make sense of this global bouillabaisse, one has to observe and respect such differences: to distinguo, as the Gothic philosophers demanded.
If there is a limit to Gura's view, it is the reduction to architecture and design, understandable with a book on these subjects.
But it should be said that Post-Modernism, like Modernism, was unlike all the other short-lived ism-wasms.
It sparked mini-movements in all the arts, in all practices and fields, including politics and philosophy.
There is postmodern dance and postmodern cheese and a postmodern carnivalesque.
Umberto Eco, who has written most eloquently on many aspects of the condition, considers it a transcultural phenomenon.
And so it is, like Mannerism or minimalism.
ROBERT VENTURI, who called himself a Mannerist-Modernist while decrying the PM label, was one of the many writers in the i96os who defined an early version of complexity theory.
His “gentle manifesto," Complexity and Contradiction in Architecture (i966), was the first and most cogent polemic on the subject, although it was published five years after Jane Jacobs used complexity theory in her polemic about the city: and Herbert Simon had already applied the same ideas to other fields.
This i96os complexity paradigm pushed systems design, among other rubrics.
Complexity thinking became in the i97os the assumption for Post-Modern architecture and its emphases on communicating with a multicultural society by adopting multiple-coding and a host of other varying strategies.
Hence the designers Gura treats here worked with a philosophy of difference to support the pluralism.

## Complexity
Il and the Rebirth of Postmodernism In the i98os, as the computer emerged to simulate growing and changing complexity, the Santa Fe Institute was set up by three Nobel Prize-winning scientists.
Its defining goal was to cast light on the new nonlinear sciences of complexity, which they predicted would be “the approach of the twenty-first century." Chaos theory, which went viral in ı987, and countless fields investigating self-organizing systems, from biology to galactic growth to the swarming of birds—all could be simulated by this new instrument.
The computer did for Post-Modernism what the telescope and microscope did for Modernism: illuminate a whole new worldview.
In brief, it shifted focus from the simple linear equations of Newton, Smith, Darwin, Marx, and Freud to the nonlinear feedback loops of self-organizing systems.
In the i99os, nonlinear, emergent architecture grew in the brains of these machines.
FRANK GEHRY's Guggenheim Museum Bilbao was the exemplar of this new paradigm, ushering in Complexity II, the second stage of Postmodernism and its amazing rebirth.
Greg Lynn became its creative theorist, Peter Eisenman\* shifted to fractal architecture (among many approaches), and Rem Koolhaas\* and Herzog & de Meuron designed digitized PM ornament and built fragments of the Time City.
Schools of architecture churned out thousands of Zahas, the Bilbao Effect turbocharged late capitalism with the iconic building, and TERRY FARRELL, somewhat exaggerating the situation, proclaimed, "today everybody is a Postmodernist."

## Canonic
Buildings of Complexity Il By the mid-i99os, Post-Modernism roared back in every way but in name, no doubt aided in its rebirth by the conspiracy of silence.
A partial list of recent canonic buildings makes the point.
Herzog & de Meuron are at the top, since their work had been so minimalist before that their shift was most striking.
Moreover, in 1995 they were at the forefront of the new ornament, having built a few libraries.
This shift was followed soon by some contextual counterpoint, and then by big icons designed around multiple metaphors.
Their CaixaForum in Madrid (2oo3-8) was the first supreme example of this cryptic Postmodernism, exhibiting all three qualities: contextualism, computer ornament, and the iconic Time City.
Its dramatic contrasts of past and present were carried through with consummate double coding.
It was as if Venturi were whispering in their ear.
They designed further PM gems, which were also icons meant to communicate with both a mass and elite public, taking the linguistic metaphors of the PM movement to a new height.
Their Beijing National Stadium (2oo8), aka "the Bird's Nest,' designed with the artist Ai Weiwei, is clearly the iconic building of the decade, and consciously designed as such around the metaphors of a bowl (signifying a communal gathering), the local Chinese cracked-ice patterns (of the structure and ornament), and the eponymous dish of bird's nest soup (the most expensive in the land).
What might constitute a shortlist of other Postmodern returns to its earlier self, the period of the 197os?
High on the list would be the complex ornament, which could be designed and built relatively cheaply, now by the computer.
Whereas Modern production depended on the repetition of identical units, it could be varied endlessly, as were the curves of Gehry's museum at Bilbao.
Such digitized ornament reached a convincing constructional complexity in two works by Foreign Office Architects: their John Lewis department store (2o02-4) in Leicester, England, and Ravensbourne College, London (2005-1o).
A structural version of such ornament was built by Toyo Ito and Cecil Balmond, the Serpentine Pavilion in London (2oo2).
Here the Modernist white square was rotated by an algorithm to produce a delightful complexity of form; one would hardly guess it had so simple an origin.
Norman Foster's “Gherkin," aka 3o St.
Mary Axe and Swiss Re Tower (2ooo-04), was the supreme iconic skyscraper in the City of London.
Other Modernists, leaving the high church of abstraction, also produced stimulating metaphors nearby: the “Cheesegrater" (Richard Rogers) and “The Shard" (Renzo Piano, and so-named by him).
Finally, a goal of early Post-Modernism had become mainstream and conventional: communication with a large and varied public.
No surprise that the most successful icons were coded with multiple meanings that referred to the context of the building, or the associative metaphors related to its function.
Gehry's Disney Hall, Los Angeles (ı989- 2oo4), was derived from acoustic forms and his Fondation Louis Vuitton, Paris (2oio-14), also from a collision of sail-forms; clashing curves in musical antiphony and a Cubist sailboat in the city where Cubism flourished one hundred years earlier.
The suitability of such metaphors to the building task might be questioned at first, but for cultural centers that have taken on the role of cathedrals today, appreciated at second glance.
Inevitably there was a complex reaction within this new tradition.
Such were Peter Eisenman's anti-iconic icons, like those of Peter Zumthor.
They ask us to be skeptical about the dominant genre.
A grounded form of critical grammar is Eisenman's spartan, and powerful, Memorial to the Murdered Jews of Europe, Berlin (i998–2005).
The kind of multiple meaning I find most convincing is that related to the city context, its past and its transformation—the Time City.
This Postmodern genre reached a high stylistic point with David Chipperfield's Neues Museum in Berlin (1994–2oo9), a delicate collage of fragments from the past.
EMBT, the Barcelona team now led by Benedetta Tagliabue, has overseen the Santa Caterina Market, Barcelona (1997–2005), a sensuous reconstruction of an old marketplace that pulls together into a multivalent whole the street life and context, the fruit and vegetables.
Here the Time City is explicitly enjoyed, ornamented, and symbolized.
No shortlist of recent PM buildings is complete without some of provocative creations of Rem Koolhas, which reinvent moves in architectural organization.
The Seattle Central Library, the Dutch Embassy in Berlin, and the Casa da Música in Porto all innovate with new versions of the route-building, a transformation of the traditional street.
But I find his versions of the Time City more sątisfyingly complex, particularly the Fondazione Prada in Milan (2oo8-15).
My personal list of canonic PM, post-2ooo, ends back with Herzog & de Meuron, whose masterwork opened in 2oı7, the Elbphilharmonie in Hamburg.
Another piggyback building, like their Madrid Forum, it celebrates the Time City with fragments and transformations of the past, now viewable from an elevated city square i2ıft (37m) above ground.
The old urban morphology of brick warehouses is celebrated, as is the Elbe river, the shipping industry, and many other local landmarks.
Clearly this is a contextual icon that both transforms and repeats the signs and realities of Hamburg, none more so than the vital place of music in the city's life.
Both inside and out it is a masterpiece of musical design, with an iconology that reflects the plural reality of this independent metropolis.
Although it arrived on the scene many years after the Post-Modern movement started, it demonstrates the continued relevance of pluralism as the most important, and challenging, art of our time.
Such Postmodern returns, carried out by architects of different persuasions, show that the i97os movement survives, and is reborn anew.
In that sense, Post-Modernism is what it has always been: the loyal opposition to Modernism, its wayward child and sometime critic.
Postmodernists are sometimes Modernists on a holiday, or those rediscovering the pleasures of expression or of relating to the past.
The culture of architecture is so mixed that it recalls the duality existing within physics today; as scientists say, on Monday, Wednesday, and Friday we use the classical theories of Newton and company; while on Tuesday, Thursday, and Saturday it is weird quantum physics.
The fact these two theories are incommensurable and not yet resolvable into a single theory is our truth and condition.

## INTRODUCTION
Postmodernism is the most provocative, most controversial, most varied and least understood term in the panoply of twentieth-century design.
From its pre-emptively self-designated name (most movements are named only after they have ended, and by historians rather than the practitioners) to its in-your-face designs, it invites confusion.
Even in its heyday, a reporter referred to Postmodernism as a term that “everyone seems to use although no one knows exactly what it means."'In part, the confusion lies in the use of the lower-case 'postmodern," describing a period of time that can incorporate the present, and “Postmodern," designating a particular movement or style.
Postmodernism lies precipitously balanced between what has been called late Modernism, and Radical Design.
Adding to the confusion, many practitioners moved easily from one to the other.
Every style has a theory, but Postmodernism is tied to a philosophy as well: a notion of pluralism rooted in the nineteenth century and subsequently applied to areas of culture, including poetry and literature.
That history makes its translation into tangible objects both intellectually challenging and somewhat unclear.
Postmodernism actually constituted both a movement and a style.
As a movement, it rebelled against the sterility of rational Modernism as expressed in the International Style; as a style, it took issue with formalism and functionalism, championing the use of quotidian materials, historical references, and exuberant color and pattern.
Furthermore, there were several strains of it, making definition still more elusive.
Modernist critics disparaged this mixture as a “fruit salad" of design, while its creators saw it as an inventive amalgam of genres.
Postmodernism was defined by attitude as much as aesthetic, as its arresting but deliberately nonconforming objects challenged accepted standards of appropriateness and good design.
The use of the term in architecture was initiated by the architect and theorist CHARLES JENCKS, who became its chief apologist and spokesman through many books on the subject, beginning in 1977 with The Language of Post-Modern Architecture.
Jencks defined its principles and interpreted its meaning not only for the architecture and design community but for the general population as well, viewing it as a positive force that faced the problems Modernism had failed to solve.
Jencks identifies two strains of Postmodernism: adhocism, which focuses on improvisation (and justifies the inclusion of FRANK GEHRY and other maverick designers as postmodernists), and a focus on historicist references and ornament.
From its earliest incarnations, postmodern design was a subject of contentious debate.
Everyone had an opinion about it; it was simultaneously applauded and derided, embraced and rejected.² In combining sophisticated concepts with pop culture references, it was the first recent style to be both elitist and egalitarian, mixing high and low in the characteristic duality that its progenitors called “double coding," a term suggesting that there is more than one way to look at a building or an object.
In allowing unfettered free expression, however, Postmodernism encouraged self-indulgent designs that sometimes descended into revivalism and kitsch; as one publication noted, “Bad taste and uselessness hav[e] the advantage of being within everybody's reach."3 The proliferation and overexposure of such objects—Postmodernism's lesser examples—bear primary responsibility for the style's early decline in the late i98os.
ABOVE Stanley Tigerman, The 'Titanic', 1978, photocollage depicting the demise of Modernism: Mies van der Rohe's Crown Hall (Chicago, 1956) sinking into Lake Michigan.
Although the strands of postmodern design coa lesced in the mid-ı97os, its seeds were planted much earlier, and its fruits were harvested beyond its decline.
But, given its equivocal nature, defining its precise start and finish dates is like trying to hit the proverbial moving target.
Its nineteenth-century origins notwithstand ing, Postmodernism's movement into architecture and design came amid the cataclysmic social and cultural changes of the decades after the Second World War.
Most narratives depict the architectural aspect of Postmodernism as primarily a response to Modernism.4 But its intellectual foundations were more complex than mere rebellion against the International Style.
Postmodernists believed that architecture must be multivalent, communicating with its users on many levels, and embraced not only historicism and ornament but color and humor too.
They sought escape from the limitations placed on architecture by its focus on functionalism, believing Modernism's rational approach was an obstacle to meaningful design.5 The style was set in motion by ROBERT VENTURI, with the house he designed for his mother in 1964, and the 1966 publication of Complexity and Contradiction in Architecture.
Favoring forms of decoration that had been sidelined by Modernism, he proclaimed that “less is a bore," distressing committed Miesians and disrupting the modernist canon.
Ornament was no longer a crime; hybridity was preferable to homogeneity.
Intimations of postmodern style could be seen even earlier—particularly in Italy, where Postmodernism's classicist leanings were as much a return to the country's ancient Roman roots as a rejection of midcentury modernist ideals.
Even the Novecento movement of the early i92os used neoclassical elements, as did the Fascist architects who followed.
In Italy the challenge of postwar recovery stimulated a burst of design innovation that helped polish an image that had been tarnished by the country's recent Fascist regime.
Aided by America's Marshall Plan funds, the country repaired its infrastructure and retooled factories, making what would prove to be foresighted investments in research and technology.
Italian producers began collaborating with major designers to create innovative, export-worthy products, and American museums showcased this renaissance, helping to reestablish Italy as a center of enterprise and creativity.6 Italian designers had been exploring the applications of materials developed during wartime, particularly plastics, using injection molding and other processes to reposition synthetics as acceptable design media, rather than as stand-ins for natural materials.
In i963 Richard Sapper\* and Marco Zanuso\* designed the first piece of polyethylene furniture, a child's chair for Kartell\*, and designers like Joe Colombo, Vico Magistretti, and Zanini\* used both rigid and flexible foam plastics in ingenious furniture designs, including the first modular seating.
Design became a market stimulus and a public relations tool, catapulting Italy to the forefront of postwar consumer culture.
Meanwhile, the austere Modernism that had emanated from the Bauhaus and crystallized in the glass-walled International Style skyscrapers was falling from favor, and a design rebellion was emerging.
Disillusionment with Modernism's failed promises—it had not made a better world, much less the utopian ideal sought in the aftermath of two world wars—led to splinter groups that challenged the status quo.7 In parallel to the pessimistic protests, a wave of utopianism generated visionary pro posals for social reforms that would transform cities and the lives of their inhabitants.
These ideas were woven into the conceptual underpin nings of postmodern thought, and would later be implemented in the New Urbanism movement.
In i96os Europe, a number of groups rebelled against the state of design and the consumer-driven culture, hoping to recapture the social relevance abandoned by Modernism.
These included Archigram\*, Archizoom\*, Superstudio\*, Studio 65\*, and the best-known and most productive Studio Alchimia\*, whose participants included ETTORE SOTTSASS,ALESSANDRO MENDINI, and ANDREA BRANZI, and whose designs often prefigured those of the MEMPHIS collective later founded by Sottsass.
This Radical Design movement was also known as # Postmodernism is the most provocative, most controversial, most varied and least understood term in the panoply of twentieth-century design.
Anti-Design or Controdesign.
The approaches of the cohorts varied, but most of them produced conceptual projects—theoretical displays or parodies of existing objects that highlighted the banality of modern design and the failure of the built environment to meet the needs of modern society.
Exploiting their links to popular culture, they drew public attention witn exhibitions of visionary architecture and provocative furniture (often only in prototype), mandating that designed objects should not be merely stylish, but socially and culturally relevant.
By encouraging the exploration of new ideas, these practitioners set the stage for the postmodern movement, and, beginning with the fourteenth Milan Triennale in i968, their ideas began to bear fruit in the marketplace.
Most of the radical activities had ended by i98o, however, with the final manifesto by Coop Himmelb(l)au\* founder Wolf Prix, who set fire to installations at London's TOP Robert A.
M.
Stern, facade for the Strada Novissima exhibit at the Venice Biennale, 198o.
ABOVE Michael Graves, design for the Strada Novissima, Venice Biennale, 1980.
Architectural Association, declaring, "Architecture must blaze," in protest against the “uptight architecture" of the 197os.
Despite having become static, Modernism provided fertile soil for the flowering of anti-modern thinking; though they produced little more than ideas, the activities of the radicals provided the spark that would ignite Postmodernism.
The Museum of Modern Art in New York celebrated Italy's resurgence in 1972 with the exhibition “Italy: The New Domestic Landscape," curated by Emilio Ambasz and showcasing 18o household objects by more than ioo designers.
The most influential part of the exhibition, however, was a display of twelve specially commissioned conceptual environments by Archizoom and Superstudio as well as individual designers including Gae Aulenti\*, Joe Colombo, GAETANO PESCE, ALDO ROSSI, and Ettore Sottsass.
By highlighting Italian creativity in designs for the home—even though some of the concepts were unrealistic or impractical—the exhibition brought international attention to the country's accomplishments.
Italy became the undisputed star of the design firmament.
In self-defense, producers in other countries were motivated to invest in avant-garde design, and there were key contributors in the United Kingdom, Japan, Germany, Spain, Portugal, and elsewhere.
Following the social and political instability and cultural change that had characterized the i96os and ı97os—with protest and countercultural movements building across the United States and in Europe, the Vietnam War, economic recession and rising inflation—the i98os brought change of a different kind.
There was good—the fall of the Berlin Wall--and bad: the AIDS epidemic and the first signs of global warming.
The United States perhaps exemplified the era, with the rise of the conservative New Right, opposing big government and high taxes and supporting a free market.
Perhaps in response to the economic uncertainty, affluent “yuppies" ushered in an era of conspicuous consumption.8 the archetype of Thorstein Veblen's society fixed on the need to acquire objects as symbols of status.
Technology changed everyday life, with personal computers, Swatch watches, the Walkman, and cell phones becoming commonplace.
The world of popular music responded with Michael Jackson, Prince, and Madonna; and the newly launched MTV gave an outlet to hard rock, heavy metal, rap and hip-hop artists.
Art went anti-establishment, using found materials or idealizing everyday objects, introducing new forms of expressionism in painting, assemblage or photography by rebels like the Arte Povera groups in Italy, or Jean-Michel Basquiat, David Salle, and Robert Mapplethorpe in America.
Anyone could make art, on any subject, of any material, challenging the commercial attitudes of elitist galleries.9 The radicals rebelled against the rampant consumerism of the time, and the postmodernists mocked it.
A defining event in the birth of the postmodern movement was an exhibition in the Venice Biennale in ı98o—the first year that architecture had been included in what is still one of the most important international art fairs.
Under the direction of PAOLO PORTOGHESI, the Strada Novissima was a papier mâché street of 23 × 3ıft (7 × 9.5m) building facades following the theme "The Presence of the Past." The twenty invited architects from nine countries included Robert Venturi, ROBERT A.
M.
STERN, MICHAEL GRAVES, LÉON KRIER, and HANS HOLLEIN.
Acknowledging that Modernism was a failure, the designs suggested a return to academic order and historical tradition.
In the book published in conjunction with the exhibition, however, Portoghesi, Vincent Scully, Charles Jencks, and Christian Norberg-Schulz put forth divergent views of Postmodernism, an indication that the movement was still struggling to define itself.
Despite the controversy generated by the Strada Novissima, it was adapted for showings in Paris and then San Francisco, drawing broader attention to anti-modernist ideas.
Modernism had essentially denied the role of history in shaping design, but for many postmodern architects and designers, looking back instead of forward could provide a solution to Modernism's shortcomings by linking with the established past instead of the undefined future.
The most overt expressions of historicist-based postmodern design were in architecture.
Now iconic buildings by architects such as Graves in America, Hollein in Austria, JAMES STIRLING in England, Rossi in Italy, and MARIO BOTTA in Switzerland were antithetical to the strict geometry of the International Style, introducing non-rectilinear forms, lively color, and ornament adapted from classical structures.
Among the first and most provocative works were Graves's much-criticized Portland Building (i982) TOP Michael Graves, Portland Building, Portland, Oregon, 1982.
ABOVE Philip Johnson, AT&T Building, New York City, 1985. and Philip Johnson's\* AT&T Building (i984) in Manhattan, which was a reversion to everything Johnson and the International Style had rejected.
Not every architect of the i98os was a postmodernist, but all those practicing in that era were influenced by its concepts—even if it was merely to denounce them.
Many avowed followers of the established modernist aesthetic broadened their vocabulary to include elements reprising designs of the relatively recent past, if not the ancient world.
Prominent architects like Richard Meier\*, despite making incursions into historicist-influenced design, held fast to Bauhaus and International Style tenets.
Others, like IAMES WINES, Peter Eisenman\*, and Daniel Libeskind\*, have been classified as Deconstructivists, which many historians consider an offshoot (or after-effect) of Postmodernism.'º But they and many of their contemporaries are variously designated as modern, late modern, and postmodern, underlining the fact that design involves personal values and commercial interests as much as a philosophy, and that labels are not always as accurate as they might appear.
As a radical new style, Postmodernism was more easily acceptable for public buildings and commercial structures than private residences.
A commitment to living space is more personal, and more long-term: faint-hearted clients need not apply.
And a little bit of historicism goes a long way, as became evident in the planned communities that emerged in the 198os with the New Urbanism movement, whose primary theorist was Krier.
In its efforts to standardize the design of cities in an industrial society, the International Style version of Modernism had created soulless, homogeneous environments.
"It can be said that there is not a single urban model that has not failed or is not on the road to failure," Branzi commented in ı984." The New Urbanism, seeking the diversity prescribed by critics like Jane Jacobs, sought to remedy that, recreating the ambience of European towns or nineteenth-century villages with walkable configurations of historicist-influenced homes clustered around town centers.
DiSNEY'S Celebration in Florida, with buildings by Stern, Venturi and DENISE SCOTT BROWN, Johnson, and others, was designed with sometimes ostentatious renditions of postmodern style.
The result, many critics felt, was a cartoon-like interpretation of an impossible utopian vision.
A less exaggerated application of the concept was devised by Andrés Duany\* and Elizabeth Plater-Zyberk\* for Seaside\*, Florida, with public buildings by major-name architects including Rossi and Krier, and private ones individually commissioned by their owners according to a set of design standards intended to encourage diversity, recalling the vernacular design of a traditional Southern town.
Architecture was the public face of the new aesthetic. but it was the i98r launch of the Memphis group in Milan that opened the contentious dialogue within the design community and captured the attention of the consumer public.
With Sottsass as its organizer and prime spokesperson, the collaborative showed his designs and those of compatriots including MICHELE DE LUCCHI, Alessandro Mendini, and Branzi, and non-Italians including PETER SHIRE, Hollein, and SHIRO KURAMATA.
The pieces were illogical configurations of furniture forms in jazzy colors and with laminate patterns that played with viewers' expectations, upending accepted ideas of appropriateness and mocking established criteria of good taste.
The audacious exteriors belied the serious ideas underlying the designs, and though many critics called them superficial, Memphis intrigued the international press, which found photogenid fodder for unprecedented media coverage.
Although embraced by a cult of enthusiasts, most Memphis designs failed to reach a wide consumer market; the high prices and limited quantities were as much a cause as the exhibitionistic designs.¹² Memphis made more headlines than actual sales, and the collaborative disbanded in i988.'3 In the consumer marketplace, avant-garde designs normally inspire adaptations or blatant copies by lower-priced producers, but the trickle-down effect of the Memphis look was undermined by careless manufacturers and poorly designed, pseudo-postmodern objects.'4 It did, however, stimulate a move to more adventurous and original design—perhaps its most important legacy.
Roughly concurrent with Memphis was the emergence of a new category in furniture: limited edition, not-necessarily-functional objects later given the designation design-art.
A coterie of London-based designers played with industrial materials and detritus, adding historical references to furniture that prioritized concept over comfort and were light years away from mass-produced goods.
In New York, the innovative Greenwich Village gallery Art et Industrie commissioned conceptdriven designs, and European galleries like Néotù\* in Paris, Aram in London and Design Gallery Milano in Milan engaged in similar efforts.
In their opposition to machine-made objects, artisans in the crafts community BELOW Memphis showroom, Dallas, Texas, 1984. shared a sensibility, if not necessarily an aesthetic, with the postmodernists, leading some of its wittiest practitioners to make clever, hand-carved pieces that played with the provocative new style.
Without the need to produce more than one of each object, there was little risk in exploring a different aesthetic.
Though postmodern furniture required audacious buyers, smaller objects proved less problematic.
Producers including ALESSI, CLETO MUNARI, SWID POWELL, and ACME commissioned leading architects to design products that generated considerable publicity, though not necessarily considerable sales.'5 The most prominent Italians of the Memphis group, as well as others including Hollein, Botta, Venturi, and Graves, designed silver coffee services and tableware, porcelain dinnerware, and jewelry that enabled them to be creative in different media on a smaller scale.
Although the producers recruited an international roster of talent, Postmodernism had definite geographic limits: Italy and the United States were its touchstones, with major architects in the United Kingdom and Japan, and a few elsewhere in Europe.
Creating postmodern interiors proved more challenging than designing objects: elements like classical columns and pediments could be used to sculpt backgrounds or reconfigure space, but postmodern furniture tended to dominate an interior and was generally incompatible with other styles.
Relatively few interiors apart from those of committed collectors adopted postmodern design, and the few that did were more likely to be designed by architects than by interior designers, who generally gave a wide berth to the style.
Notable exceptions were design showrooms, which, being frequently redecorated, were committed to keeping au courant, and spaces by ARATA ISOZAKI and Graves for Sunar-Hauserman\*, Stern for Shaw-Walker, THOMAS BEEBY and STANLEY TIGERMAN for FORMICA, and MICHAEL VANDERBYL for Bernhardt were among the most striking interiors.
None, of course, survive.
Commercial interiors in postmodern style—office buildings, restaurants, theaters, and such—required to be more enduring, were understandably more conservative.
A noteworthy exception was Disney, whose Swan and Dolphin hotels were designed by Graves to make the flamboyant most of Postmodernism's double coding, projecting symbols to which the general public could immediately respond.
In textiles and wall coverings, Postmodernism produced spirited patterns in sparkling hues by designers like Sottsass and NATHALIE DU PASQUIER, providing welcome relief from the blandness of most contemporary offerings.
Some patterns recalled New Wave graphics and Russian Constructivism, but the most original of them combined Memphis-like clashing shapes and colors.
Like most postmodern furniture, however, they were too extreme to make major inroads in the marketplace.
More # Postmodernism's movement into architecture and design came amid the cataclusmic social and cultural changes of the decades after the Second World War. subdued iterations appeared in floor coverings, including area rugs by Sottsass, Graves, and other architects who had ventured into product design.
Postmodernism proliferated most successfully in objects like clocks, mugs, table ornaments, and desk accessories, which provided maximum eye-appeal at minimal cost; impulse purchases that did not require a long-term commitment.6 Postmodernism in graphic design was not so much a specific set of style markers as an explosion of experimentation.
It produced kaleidoscopic designs in posters, album covers, and magazines with pop, cartoony or abstract-geometric motifs.
It also encouraged exciting new approaches to typography.
American designers, including David Carson\*, APRIL GRIEMAN, ED FELLA, and KATHERINE MCCOY, contributed some of the most successful of these.
Publications like The Face in the United Kingdom and Ray Gun magazine in America introduced a radical new look, with irregular columns, asymmetrical layouts, and electrifying graphics that almost vibrated on the page.
No matter how widely accepted in the marketplace, any style will begin to fade, yielding over time to reassessment or reinterpretation.
Postmodernism was no exception, as inferior iterations at mass-market levels eviscerated its appeal to the design cognoscenti.
In December 1986, Interior Design reported that Postmodernism had been “diagnosed as seriously ill"'7 when students in London walked out of a Graves lecture, and three months later the New York Times speculated about a change in attitude that was either a “backlash" against Postmodernism's kitschy interpretations or a sign that the style had simply run its course.8 By the final years of the decade, Postmodernism was being referred to as a movement in decline, if not altogether moribund.
It was, most probably, to be expected.
A highintensity style marked by rejecting convention without defining new principles was difficult to maintain.
The political message of the radicals, the refnement of the architecture, and the exuberance of Memphis had been diluted by low-budget copies and kitschy trinkets in formulaic motifs and colors.
Its cutting edge dulled, Postmodernism became as repetitive and predictable as the Modernism it sought to replace.
As a specific LEFT Archizoom Associati, Superonda seating, fauxleather fabric over foam, 1967. have seen hundreds of articles in magazines and online publications, on blogs and imagesharing platforms, proclaiming the return of Postmodernism.
Many of the discussions cite the appropriateness of postmodern thought for today's multicultural, multigenerational, hyperactive, ultra-consumerist society.
Although an understanding of Postmodernism will probably not solve the conundrum of contemporary culture, its ideas are very much in the spirit of the modern world.
In redefining boundaries and expanding its vocabulary, Postmodernism permanently changed the way we think about design.
It left in its wake a heritage of diversity, an embrace of complexity and free expression, an appreciation of historic and vernacular traditions, and—perhaps most important—a receptiveness to # ..rther than dying and being reborn, it had just receded temporarily into the background.
Postmodernism hasn't been revived; it never left. style, it had become a piece of the historical continuum it referenced—a piece that was buried in the landslide of the digital age.
This would begin to change with the millennium, and a new era of globalism.
The broadening of design horizons to embrace national differences and varying aesthetics also encouraged receptiveness to ideas previously dismissed for departing from familiar and comfortable norms.
With the passage of time, what was once disdained was accepted, and one-time iconoclasts became respectable.
The resurgence of Postmodernism began with its discovery by a generation too young to have experienced its original incarnation, and its rediscovery by those who had failed to understand or appreciate it the first time around.
The new century brought with it “Memphis Remembered," an exhibition at London's Design Museum in 2oo1, and in 2oo6 the Los Angeles County Museum of Art held a Sottsass retrospective.
But certainly the most influential stimulus to the revival of interest was the 2oll exhibition “Postmodernism: Style and Subversion, 1970-199o" at London's Victoria and Albert Museum, which, though it covered cultural areas beyond design, generated considerable conversation and the assertion by its curators that "we are all postmodernists now."19 The clarion call was eagerly picked up by novelty-seeking media, and the past several years new and experimental design.
As Aaron Betsky has noted, "Postmodernism did not reinvent the past.
Nor did it jettison the future."2o The proclaimed demise of the movement was simply a misunderstanding; rather than dying and being reborn, it had just receded temporarily into the background.
Postmodernism hasn't been revived; it never left.

## Author's
Note Notwithstanding its title, this book does not presume to tell the complete story of postmodern design, which continues to evolve, but to document its most dominant years.
I have painted Postmodernism with a broad brush, including designs that were deliberately postmodern, and others simply influenced by it; designers in the movement's mainstream, and others who occasionally explored the territory.
This inclusiveness, and the accompanying images, makes the point that Postmodernism consistently overflowed its defined boundaries.
No architect, artist, or designer remained untouched.
Some took a historicist approach while others focused on new forms, some worked with found materials and others with those from nature, some embraced machines and others artisanry, and still others merely took license to express their own ideas.
Yet all of these, in one way or another, can be called postmodern, and for the purposes of this compilation, will be.

## MASTERS
OF POSTMODERN DESIGN # Supernovas Sottsass and Venturi: Creating and Defining Postmodernism in the 1980s R.
Craig Miller OPPOSITE TOP Ettore Sottsass, Casablanca sideboard, serigraphed laminate, 198ı, produced by Memphis.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM Sottsass, Tartar console, black lacquer, reconstituted wood veneers, serigraphed laminate, 1985, produced by Memphis.
ETTORE SOTTSASS and ROBERT VENTURI (see both also under “Living with Postmodern Design") were central figures in creating and defining Postmodernism in the ı98os but in fundamentally different ways, for in their work they showed that Postmodernism was not a rigid, monolithic movement but one capable of great diversity.
In charting these new pathways they would profoundly change the course of twentieth-century design by redefining the concept of what constituted modern design.
Sottsass embraced an approach that can perhaps best be called radical Postmodernism, whereby designers “investigat[ed] the spiritual and sensual qualities of an object," consciously blurring the line between art and design; “the outer skin of a piece of furniture...[was also] interpreted completely as image carrier."' Sottsass, moreover, believed strongly that design should have a social and cultural mission, and “the emotional nature of the relationship between people and the things that they use in daily life" was a central concern of his.² While Sottsass did create important designs for mass production, for companies like Olivetti and ALESSI (see under “Edited Design”), it was the work done primarily as limited editions or one-of-a-kind pieces, sold through groups such as MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") or high-end design galleries, that would perhaps prove to be the most seminal.
This design approach came to be viewed as an “Italian version” of Postmodernism and was quite pervasive not only in Italy but in Europe.
Venturi, conversely, advocated an approach that is now called historical Postmodernism.
These designers overtly used and reinterpreted historical motifs and placed a decided emphasis on the aesthetic experience.
There was a strongly philosophical and theoretical basis to Venturi's work, and his designs were surprisingly, unlike Sottsass's oeuvre, largely mass-produced by American companies such as Knoll\* and SWID POWELL (see essay under “Edited Design").3 Venturi thus became a symbol of a distinctive “American version" of Postmodernism.
To place them in a larger context, Sottsass and Venturi were part of a generation of artists born between the world wars, though Venturi was the younger by eight years.
By the i98os, they were in their late fifties and sixties and at the peak of their careers.
Both were striving to break the hegemony of Modernism and consequently rewrite the history of design.4 Both designers had also trained as architects, but their careers evolved quite differently.
Sottsass worked primarily as a designer, and his major architectural work came late in his career after the completion of a residence (i986-9o) for the American collector Daniel Wolf in Ridgway, Colorado.
Conversely, Venturi worked primarily as an architect, and his major work as a designer was done in a relatively brief period in about 1978–93.5 Thus Sottsass's output as a designer was enormous, while Venturi's was limited in comparison; both nonetheless became international leaders of the star architect-designer movement that arose in the i98os.
What is of the greatest importance are the conceptual and aesthetic models that they established for the design field.
A critical analysis of their work reveals remarkable similarities as well as contrasts.
Their approaches to design were quite different in the ways they handled form, materials, color, pattern, technology, and even the terminology used for naming their pieces.
Like other postmodernists, Sottsass and Venturi rebelled against Modernism, a driving force throughout much of twentiethcentury design.
They wanted to create a new conceptual approach that was not focused on function, mass production, new materials and technologies, or abstract forms.
Sottsass developed a more intuitive but strongly sensory approach to design.
He was an extremely articulate man but not one to write treatises.6 He worked from the micro to the macro, from details to the larger wholé, in a design process he called percorsi (paths), which was used primarily for his interiors and architecture.7 Sottsass was also a great draftsman, working in a spontaneous design process whereby objects were developed relatively quickly, producing as many as ı5o new designs in a year.8 There were also clear aesthetic influences on Sottsass.
It is important to note that Sottsass was not born in Italy: he was Austrian by birth and thus came out of a decorative tradition going back to designers such as Josef Hoffmann, as exemplified by the latter's neoclassicizing work after 191o.
Sottsass knew the history of neoclassical architecture and design; but he knew it so well and was so adept that his work never featured the overt historical references that were so consciously an element in Venturi's work.
Then again, there was an almost neoclassical sense of form in Sottsass's work of the early i98os; his designs have a base, a shaft (or center section), and a capital.
He was very conscious of the importance of the base in his objects, and its relationship to the ground.9 But, like Venturi, Sottsass was also fascinated with the flip side of the coin: everyday pop and vernacular culture, as well as kitsch.
Sottsass had traveled widely, and absorbed and adapted aspects of the art of non-Western countriesparticularly India, which Sottsass visited with his first wife in i96ı: “He later described the journey as a search for origins and an exploration of the sensory (rather than the rational) terms of existence; India expanded his understanding of the connections between objects and the rituals of daily life.
The colours, forms, language and mystical references of Indian culture reverberated in his work for decades to come."1o Venturi had a far more rational and philosophical approach to design." He was one of the most brilliant theoreticians of the second half of the twentieth century.
His two most famous books reflect the strongest influences in his work: Complexity and Contradiction in Architecture (i966) reveals Venturi's vast knowledge of Western history and the influence of great European cities like Rome, while FROM TOP Sottsass, preliminary furniture sketches for Memphis, 198ı; side and front elevations by Robert Venturi for the Chippendale chair, 198o; Venturi, Rauch & Scott Brown, Chippendale chair, bent laminated wood and bird's-eye maple veneer, cotton Tapestry pad, 1978–84.
Learning from Las Vegas (1972; co-authored with DENISE SCOTT BROWN, also see under “Living with Postmodern Design", and Steve Izenour) shows his fascination with the pop and vernacular of the everyday and the appeal of bustling American urban centers such as Las Vegas.
Both books became important manifestos for Postmodernism and among the most influential publications of the twentieth century, Venturi looked primarily to Western design for inspiration for his forms, which he would then abstract and simplify.
His work was cerebral and high-style, with layered references to historical styles, displaying little of the sensuality or spontaneity of Sottsass.
Scott Brown herself has called it a "Quaker Puritanism," a term that could perhaps only be considered as an attribute in a city like Philadelphia.'² Venturi, like his Italian counterpart, liked to mix opulent and vernacular forms and materials for sensory impact; he even delighted in brashly juxtaposing different historical styles.
The influence of pop was also evident in Venturi's designs, in which "cartoon graphics, flatness, large scale, references to commercial art, and the 'contradictions of scale and context' he championed in Complexity and Contradiction" can be seen.'3 An analysis of Venturi's work reveals a pronounced two-dimensionality: in his forms, patterns (stenciling, silk-screening, enameling), and materials (plywood and plastic laminate).
In contrast to Sottsass, Venturi worked from the macro to the micro, starting out with a larger concept and then refining every detail as the design progressed.
He was also a great and tireless draftsman, but his design process was more deliberative, taking time to develop and refine objects.
It is worth noting that the three designs discussed here were Venturi's first serious experiments in furniture;'4 he thus succeeded in the enormous challenge of producing major works on his first try—no small feat.
Sottsass's and Venturi's approaches are best exemplified by looking at examples of their furniture designs done in the early i98os, when Postmodernism was in its formative years.
Looking back more than three decades later, it is easy to forget how radical these designs were when they first appeared to challenge Modernism.
Sottsass preferred case furniture as the form with which to develop his ideas, as opposed to the chair; in his complex compositions he would increasingly blur the lines between cupboards and tables, combining elements freely.
Case furniture allowed Sottsass to play with oversized, bold, and exploding forms—highly original designs without historical precedent.
He was not interested in new technology or innovative materials; rather, his pieces were handcrafted by skilled artisans employed by Memphis, the company he created to produce his designs and those of his likeminded cohorts.
Sottsass's designs for Memphis could be production or limited edition.
He was prodigious and produced new collections for Memphis annually.
His initial pieces from the early i98os were quite planar or two-dimensional in form and pattern; these designs were not about function but “the forms, colours, materials and volumes used."'5 Sottsass employed vernacular plastic laminate for the finished surface of his pieces but embellished them with bold patterns of his own design or vivid colors; he would on occasion use multiple patterns on objects.
There is also a consciously kitsch aspect to many of these designs.
Sottsass's work is meant to be over-the-top, bordering on the vulgar;6 there is a Promethean explosion of exuber ance, in contrast to Venturi's rarefied, intellectual energy.
It is also important to note the exotic names that Sottsass chose for early designs: Casablanca, Ashoka, Murmansk, and Euphrates, for example.'7 The influence of Postmodernism on the contemporary design field has been so pervasive over the last three decades that it is now hard to imagine the sheer shock and consternation that ensued when Memphis debuted in i98r.
Pieces such as Casablanca (or Carlton) were the perfect proclamations of this new radical approach to design, for, at 7ft (2.im) high and 5ft (ı.5m) wide, their forms were like giant, exploding exclamation points.
In the early years of Memphis, Sottsass's forms were characteristically planar and were covered in two-dimensional patterns (in this case Spugnato) or sheets of brightly colored plastic laminate.
In contrast, his compositions were often symmetrical and traditional tripartite forms with a base, shaft, and capital, which is especially evident in his sketches, which bristle with energy.
Perhaps most importantly, in designs such as Casablanca, with its flaring arms and monumental scale, Sottsass was “arranging objects or volumes...giving a...dynamic sense" to his work.'8 Four years later, a dramatic change could be seen in designs like the Tartar console (i985), one of Sottsass's first attempts at creating more plastic or three-dimensional forms.'9 He was also beginning to play with asymmetrical shapes, making his compositions even more dynamic.
Tartar, in fact, marks a profound shift in Sottsass's work: here he uses a collage of richer materials “with different linguistic backgrounds"²o (multicolored wood veneers and TOP Venturi, Rauch & Scott Brown, Queen Anne side chair, bent laminated wood and Grandmother laminate, 1978-84.
CENTER Venturi, Art Deco side chair, bent laminated wood and plastic laminate with applied decoration, 1978-84.
BOTTOM Venturi, preliminary, sketch of Art Deco chair, C.
1975. lacquer instead of just plastic laminates); his composition is more complex (three columnar forms and a pylon are used to support multiple askew planar tops); and the design is decidedly more massive (the table is almost 6ft/ı.8m long and 3ft/9ocm wide).
Tartar would prove to be an important harbinger of later furniture designs by Sottsass.² In contrast to Sottsass's use of case furniture, Venturi preferred to develop his ideas using the chair form.
He began by drawing antique furniture to learn how to assimilate and reinterpret historical styles.22 His designs are cerebral and highly original adaptations and reinterpretations of historical forms.
Indeed, in approaching the Knoll project, Venturi set as his task—with his customary intellectual bravura—to reinvent and reinterpret the history of the modern chair from the eighteenth century to the ascendancy of Modernism in the i92os: an astounding feat.23 Venturi, like Sottsass, was also using objects as "symbols," but in different ways; Venturi treated the elevations of chairs like the facades of his buildings: "the design and construction of the Knoll chairs was equivalent to the decoration of a modernist shed."²4 Regarding this historical imagery, Venturi himself said: “This kind of symbolism involves exaggerated scale, color, and pattern to promote the sense of representation, to promote tension among the elements with the design and to accommodate the exaggerated stimuli our modern sensibilities are attuned to."25 He was not using new technology or innovative materials, though his designs were mass-produced by Knoll.
His basic form was a two-dimensional molded plywood chair, recalling the 193os work of Alvar Aaito, a designer he much admired.
The construction was simple: a back panel joined to a bent seat panel.²6 For his finished surfaces, he most often chose plastic larinate, preferring mundane and everyday materials; but the profile and detailing of the chairs was complex, Venturi playing with the two-dimensional undulations of the molded plywood and the curving profiles of the chairs.
Using a simple "blank," Venturi was able to achieve enormous variety by employing a multitude of forms, colors, patterns, andtextiles.
There were four models used for the Knoll series: chairs covered in the Grandmother pattern plastic laminate; chairs finished with solid-color plastic laminates (black, surf, yellow, burgundy); chairs veneered with solid-color plastic laminate highlighted with elaborate painted decoration to simulate carved or applied ornament; and chairs laminated with veneers of light, naturalgrain maple or dark gray-stained bird's-eye maple, an exception to his general avoidance of rich materials.
The chairs could also be embellished with an upholstered seating pad, but both his forms and textile patterns were highly two-dimensional.
In the end, it took Venturi some six years (1978-84) to refine the design and achieve his exacting details.27 The form of Venturi's Queen Anne side chair is a visibly literal adaptation of an eighteenthcentury chair, with its curved stiles, central splat, and subtle undulations in the front legs to evoke cabriole legs.
TheGrandmother pattern—here in a plastic laminate adapted from an identical textile—is one of Venturi's most astute designs.
It was inspired by “a flowered tablecloth that had belonged to an associate's grandmother, over which a pattern of black dashes inspired by the conventional lining of business envelopes was superimposed."28 In this design, Venturi combines historicism and the vernacular with great élan.
The Art Deco chair is slightly smaller in scale but a more original design.
Here Venturi focused his attention on the design of the back panel, an extraordinarily intricate composition employing solid and void, geometric and floral patterns, and subtle and bold colors—again, complexity and contradiction.
Preliminary drawings show his arduous design process, in which he used black and colored markers to create patterns on full-size profiles of the chairs.
The Chippendale chair is one of Venturi's most luxurious designs.
Here Venturi's attention is on the form of the back, with its sculptural crest rail and perforated splat.
It is its materials, however, that give the design such elegance: gray-stained bird's-eye maple veneer (sumptuous color with an intricate surface) is contrasted against his Tapestry pad, a colorful and dramatic oversized floral pattern “overlaid with an abstract geometric pattern of rotated white squares in purple ones."29 Again, drawings by Venturi show his painstaking search for the perfect interplay of subtle profiles in multiple dimensions.
This brief overview reveals the extraordinary breakthroughs made by Sottsass and Venturi in the early [98os.
In creating and defining two distinct aspects of Postmodernism, they caused a seismic shift in twentiethcentury design, for they redefined modern design so that it was no longer just Modernism but a much more inclusive and multifaceted movement.
Their designs, moreover, still resonate today in the work of later generations of designers who have built on and extended their philosophical, conceptual, and aesthetic legacies. # MICHAEL GRAVES 1934-2015 PRODUCTS LIKE THE WITTY WHISTLING BIRD TEA KETTLE MADE THIS INDIANA-BORN ARCHITECT THE BEST-KNOWN AMERICAN POSTMODERNIST.
RIGHT Big Dripper, porcelain tea service, for Swid Powell, 1985.
No single object of postmodern design became as internationally recognized as the pyramidshaped tea kettle, capped with a whistling bird, designed by Michael Graves (also see under "Living with Postmodern Design") for ALESSI (see under “Edited Design") in i985.
More than a million were sold in the first three years following its introduction,3º and the kettle, still in production, remains an iconic image of the style.
In a career lasting just over five decades, the prolific Graves designed some four hundred buildings and over two thousand products ranging from a costly silver coffee service to inexpensive kitchenware.
Although his buildings are his most important works, Graves's distinctive products, often both functional and witty, made him the best-known postmodernist in America.
Born in Indianapolis, Indiana, Graves attended the University of Cincinnati and the Harvard Graduate School of Design, where he received a MArch degree in ı959.
He worked briefly in the office of George Nelson, but left when he was awarded a coveted Prix de Rome fellowship, spending two years at the American Academy in Rome.
He returned to the United States and began to teach at Princeton University in i962.
He opened his namesake firm in Princeton in i964, and continued to teach at the university even after becoming one of the country's most prominent architects.
Along with Peter Eisenman\*, Charles Gwathmey.
John Heiduk\*, and Richard Meier\*, Graves was known in the i97os as one of the New York Five (also known as the “Whites"), a group of architects allied with the Le Corbusier tradition of mostly white buildings.
Graves was the only one of the group to move away from that allegiance, adopting a more accessible aesthetic that employed both color and metaphorical references to classicism.
In i982 he designed one of the earliest, and certainly the most controversial iterations of postmodern architecture, the Portland Building in Portland, Oregon.
A massive slab on a pedestal-like base, the building has stucco facades adorned with pilasters and accented with color, and a 6.5-ton hammeredcopper classical statue over the entrance.
It was an instant sensation, provoking both broad media coverage and vociferous criticism for its unconventional form and its impractical fittings in terms of accessibility and function.
Despite the criticism, the building propelled Graves into the ranks of prominent American architects.
Graves's later works achieved a happier marriage of aesthetics and functionality; among these were the Humana Building (i985) in Louisville, Kentucky; the winningly whimsical Walt Disney World Swan and Dolphin resorts (i99o) in Orlando, Florida; the Denver Public Library (ı995); and a post office in DISNEY'S (see “Edited Design") planned community of Celebration, Florida (i996).
He also designed residential projects, in which he eschewed modernist open-plan interiors, reverting to the traditional practice of having separately defined rooms.
In his own Princeton home, he integrated classical with contemporary elements to create a villa from what had been an old warehouse.
Its interiors combine Graves's postmodern furniture designs, which clearly draw on Biedermeier precedents, with an eclectic mix of new and old furnishings.
His showroom designs for Sunar-Hauserman\*, a producer of office furnishings, beginning in i98o, combined classical iconography and flowing displays of draped fabrics with a sophisticated palette of apricot, gray, French blue, taupe, and ochre that was widely copied.
He divided the walls into three horizontal sections, crested with moldings.
In i98ı he added fluted and marbleized columns with pediment-topped capitals and vaulted ceilings, and the following year introduced more monumental treatments and fabrics patterned with architectural motifs.
The showrooms became archetypes of postmodern design for public interiors.
Graves's first project with the Italian producer Alessi was a silver coffee and tea service for their 1983 Tea & Coffee Piazza project; he eventually produced more than 15o different objects for the firm, including clocks, salt and pepper dispensers, cooking utensils, trays, a kitchen timer, and the bestselling tea kettle.3' Though designer-name products at popular prices are ubiquitous today, Graves was the first prominent architect to enter the mass market.
In i987 he began working with Target on a houseware collection that included a \$24.99 version of his bird-crested tea kettle, which became the company's most successful product.
He enthusiastically took on the challenge of making good design affordable and by the time the association ended in 2oi2, he had designed more than two thousand Target products.3² Though many of his fellow postmodernists moved on to other styles, Graves continued to work with classically inspired forms, though in somewhat less extravagant iterations.
Even his late work, including hotels in the Middle East and East Asia, remained identifiably postmodern.
Paralyzed by a spinal infection in 2003, Graves was confined to a wheelchair for the remainder of his life, but continued to design, teach, and lecture, redirecting much of his attention to design for people with disabilities, in both architecture and products.
Even his most functional objects reflect his commitment to humanize architecture with designs that would not only enhance life, but make people smile.

OPPOSITE The living room of the Schulman House. # CHARLES JENCKS b.1939 BALTIMORE-BORN ARCHITECT, WRITER, AND LANDSCAPE DESIGNER AND PREEMINENT THEORIST OF POSTMODERN ARCHITECTURE.
RIGHT Cover illustration from The Story of Postmodernism: Five Decades of the Ironic.
Iconic and Critical in Architecture (2oı); Jencks's concept of iconic building forms, rendered by Madelon Vriesendorp.
OPPOSITE The Moonwell in the Thematic House, London, 1984.
If ETTORE SOTTSASS (also see under "Living with Postmodern Design") was the figurehead of Postmodernism, Charles Jencks (also see under "Living with Postmodern Design") was its chief historiographer.
In a titanic output of books, articles, and lectures, Jencks—a writer and theorist of considerable skill as well as an architect and landscape designer—codified and clarified the philosophy of Postmodernism to an audience that found its aims as difficult to understand as its pluralist aesthetics.
His writings became sourcebooks for the architecture and design communities, as well as ongoing references for historians and students Beginning with The Language of Post-Modern Architecture (ı977),33 which has been printed in ten languages and multiple editions, Jencks transposed the term from literature to architecture, providing a theoretical foundation for what began as simply a revolt against the International Style.
He has published more than thirty books and edited several more.
His best-known works include Adhocism: The Case for Improvisation (with Nathan Silver, 1972), What is Postmodernism?
(i986), and The Story of Post-Modernism: Five Decades of the Ironic, Iconic and Critical in Architecture (2oı), whose cover illustrated some of architecture's most recognizable buildings, based on what Jencks defined as the five traditions of postmodern architecture.34 It was Jencks who posited a specific date for the death of Modernism: the 1972 destruction of the Pruitt-Igoe housing project in St.
Louis, Missouri, an ill-fated example of the failure of the International Style to improve the lives of the thousands of residents who had moved into the complex when it opened in 1955, and saw it deteriorate into a slum.
Jencks was born in Baltimore, Maryland, and studied English literature at Harvard, receiving his BA in 196ı before changing course to attend Harvard's Graduate School of Design in 1965, where he studied under Sigfried Giedion and Reyner Banham, both of whom had published theoretical works that dealt with the history of Modernism and would certainly have influenced Jencks's inclination toward intellectual study over conventional practice.
He moved to the United Kingdom in the mid-ı96os and attended University College London, where he received a PhD in architectural history in 1970.
Despite his connection to Postmodernism, Jencks classified his own work as “symbolic,"35 in its allusion to cultural ideas and historical references.
The Garagia Rotunda (i977) in Cape Cod, Massachusetts, illustrates his theory about adhocism, the assemblage of unrelated elements in an almost improvised fashion.
His own Thematic House (i984) in London was a far more complex project, which he designed to represent symbolic architecture, an approach he identified as a “next stage" of postmodern architecture.
Over five years, he remodeled (with the architect TERRY FARRELL, see under “Architecture") an 184os townhouse to incorporate symbols expressing the ideas of cosmic time (the passage of the Moon, Sun, and seasons) and cultural time (the progress of civilization).
He sought to return meaning to ornament, feeling that in Postmodernism it had declined into kitschy and meaningless signs.
Human forms can be seen in doors and windows, and the entrance hall has stenciled images of the dominant themes.
Four rooms, one representing each season, are clustered around the central staircase, and Jencks also designed furniture relating to the themes, the best known of which are his Sun table and Sun chair (1984).
In addition to his significance as writer and lecturer on the postmodern movement, Jencks has also been an influential landscape designer, beginning with the Garden of Cosmic Speculation (i989), an extraordinary 30-acre (ı2-ha) expanse in Scotland which celebrates the many aspects of nature by engaging both the mind and the senses (including, according to its designer, the sense of humor).
In recent years, he has designed unique and evocative gardens and landscapes in the United Kingdom, Switzerland, and East Asia, as well as outdoor sculpture.
ABOVE The Cosmic Oval entry hall, Thematic House, with the house's themes stenciled over its mirrored doors.
RIGHT The central Solar Staircase in the Thematic House, representing the solar year, with 52 steps divided into 365 grooves.
In 1995, with his late wife Maggie Keswick, Jencks co-founded the Maggie's Centres, a group of drop-in centers that provide a caring environment for cancer patients and their families.
The centers operate from buildings designed by some of the world's leading architects and are successful examples of architecture's potential contribution to improve quality of life.
RIGHT Jencks's bedroom, Thematic House: a white-onwhite evocation of Charles Rennie Mackintosh.
OPPOSITE The Thematic House's Sundial Arcade, a stepped-down window seat overlooking the garden.
RIGHT The third-floor library, with Jencks-designed Skyscraper chest.
BELOW Sun chairs, designed for the Thematic House, MDF and ColorCore laminate, 1984. # ALESSANDRO MENDINI b.1931 ITALIAN AVANT-GARDE SPOKESMAN.
CELEBRATED AS AN INFLUENTIAL EDITOR AND CRITIC AS WELL AS DESIGNER.
A contemporary incarnation of the Renaissance man, Alessandro Mendini (also see under “Living with Postmodern Design") has exercised his creative talents in virtually every area of design, despite the fact that he originally aspired to become a cartoonist, His accomplishments in architecture, urban planning, furniture, and product design parallel a career as a critic and editor of three of Italy's most influential publications.
In that position he was perhaps the most influential of all postmodernists in spreading the new gospel of design and inspiring a generation of practitioners across Italy and internationally.
A native of Milan, Mendini trained at the prestigious Politecnico di Milano, but sees himself as a generator of ideas rather than exclusively a designer or architect.
From the i96os on, as a member of two important Radical Design groups, he was an articulate and highly visible spokesperson for the Italian avant-garde: a founding member of Global Tools\* (i973), he was later a partner in Studio Alchimia\* (i979) with ETTORE SOTTSASS (also see under "Living with Postmodern Design") and others, where he explored “banal design" in objects like the black-and-white-patterned Ollo collection (i979).
When Sottsass left to form MEMPHIS (see under "Edited Design”), Mendini declined to join the group, though he contributed to Memphis's first collection.
While Memphis intended to sell its products, even if to a limited market, Mendini preferred to focus on design that bypassed commercial considerations.
From his earliest work, Mendini showed a fondness for flamboyant gestures and borderlinekitsch.
His designs are always visually arresting but are often ironic or allegorical commentaries that combine concept with function.
His “redesigns," which include the Kandissi sofa and a reworking of Marcel Breuer's Wassily chair, are improvisations on existing archetypes, whose witty embellishments are implicitly critical of the originals.
The Poltrona di Proust (ı978), a piece that Mendini has referred to as a “hermaphrodite design" and a "fake antique,"36 is a replica eighteenth-century armchair painted all over with a multicolored, Pointillist-style motif.
It has become an iconic image of transgressive Italian design, and is still in production.
Mendini's editorial career began in 1970, when he left an architectural office to work at Casabella, where he remained until ı976.
In 1977, he founded the magazine Modo.
As publisher of Domus from 1979 to 1985, he was responsible for the magazine's transformation into a wide-ranging LEFT Table and chairs, silkscreened laminated wood, from the Ollo collection for Studio Alchimia, 1988.
OPPOSITE Macaone table, black lacquer, 1985, produced by Zanotta. publication that mixed architecture and design with literature, fashion, and pop culture, with distinctive graphics that made it a must-read for the design community.
Mendini documented the emergence of Postmodernism in those critical years, promoting its designers and their designs, including his own.
In so doing, he was chief proselytizer for the style to a broad international audience.
During his years as an editor, Mendini continued his other activities.
In i983, he co-founded with ANDREA BRANZI (see under "Design") the Domus Academy in Milan, the first school ever to offer graduate degrees in architecture and design.
He also served as creative director of ALESSI (see under “Edited Design"), and later played the same role for Swatch, while for Cartier, Hermès, and other brands he designed a variety of objects, from furniture and jewelry to carpets, wristwatches, even a motorcycle.
LEFT Star and Fish enameled brooches, for Acme's Architects Collection, c.
1987.
ABOVE Wassily chair, from the series Redesigns of Chairs of the Modern Movement, steel and leather, for Studio Alchimia, 1983.
In ı989 he founded Atelier Mendini with his brother Francesco.
The firm's architectural projects included the Casa della Felicità (ı988) in Lago d'Orta, for the Alessi family, to which he asked fellow postmodernists ALDO ROSSI (see under "Architecture'), Ettore Sottsass, and ROBERT VENTURI, among others, to contribute.
Mendini also coordinated the design of the Groninger Museum in Groningen, Netherlands (1988–94).
He designed one wing of the building himself and invited PHILIPPE STARCK (see under "Design"), Coop Himmelb(l)au\*, and MICHELE DE LUCCHI (see under “Design")—architects of entirely different attitudes—to design others.
The whole is a tour de force of brilliantly provocative architecture.
Mendini continues to negotiate the boundaries between art, architecture, and design, moving effortlessly between them and contributing to each.
And while others of his compatriots and collaborators now reject the term Postmodernism, Mendini embraces it.
