ABOVE Cetonia cabinet lacquered wood and painted polychrome, limited edition for Zanotta, 1984. # ETTORE SOTTSASS 1917-2007 AUSTRIAN-BORN, ITALIAN-BRED MEMPHIS FOUNDER, PROLIFIC DESIGNER AND PUBLIC FACE OF POSTMODERNISM.
If one designer can be said to have served as the public face of postmodern design, it was Ettore Sottsass (also see under “Living with Postmodern Design").
In his role as the quotable spokesperson of the MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") collaborative, he was a passionate promoter of the style and his like-minded colleagues.
During an almost seven-decade career that began along more conventional lines, he deftly negotiated the barriers between modern industrial design, radical rebellion, and a new generation of architects and designers.
Sottsass was best known for his furniture and glass designs, all of which exhibited a consistent love of color, pattern, and provocative deviation from conventional form.
He was also known for compulsively recording visual experience; carrying a camera whenever he left the house, he photographed virtually everything that caught his eye and used it as inspiration for his work.
Sottsass was born in Innsbruck, Austria, and grew up in Italy, where his family moved when he was twelve years old, in part so that he could follow in his father's footsteps by studying architecture.37 He received his architectural degree from the Politecnico di Torino (i939), served in the army during the Second World War, and worked in his father's practice before moving to Milan in i946, where he opened an architecture and industrial design practice and began writing for Domus.
Hired as a design consultant for Olivetti in 1957, Sottsass applied his admiration of pop culture to normally staid consumer goods: he co-designed Italy's first mainframe computer, a sleek black form of which only about forty were made, and drew public notice with the lipstickred Valentine typewriter (1969).38 Frustrated by the constraints of corporate work, Sottsass broadened his range, in 1964 designing a group of monolithic cupboards covered in plastic laminate—a tacky facade that mocked the stodgy form and prefigured his more extreme pieces of the i98os.
He began to work in ceramics, his large-scale Totems (ı965) being one result, and was retained as art director for Poltronova\*, designing furniture for them from i966.
Sottsass also began to participate in experimental projects with the Radical Design groups: he was part of both Studio Alchimia\* and the Global Tools\* collective.
His founding of Memphis in 198o grew out of these early activities, but with Memphis he moved from theoretical concepts to designing actual products for sale.
Taking the spotlight on an international stage, Memphis established Postmodernism as the style of the moment.
Sottsass drew inspiration wherever he traveled, including from America, where he worked briefly in George Nelson's office and later spent time after traveling to California in 1962 for medical treatment.
There, Sottsass was intrigued by Pop art and radicals like the Beat poet Allen Ginsberg.
His frequent trips to India were another important influence on his work, reflected in his fondness for brilliant colors and in objects that reference mysticism or have Indian-inspired names, such as the Offerta a Shiva (i964), the Ashoka lamp (1981), and the Tantra collection (i986).39 In 1972, Sottsass participated in "Italy: The New Domestic Landscape" at the Museum of Modern Art in New York, contributing a series of fiberglass microenvironments on casters that could be rearranged as needed by the occupants.
Like much of his work, it received considerable attention and critical praise.
In Sottsass's furniture, rectilinear or angular silhouettes are accentuated by vivid color and pattern; his glassware uses similarly bright hues to embellish curvilinear forms.
Many pieces are implicitly erotic or ceremonial, including the Tantra group and a set of glass objects (produced after his death) inspired by kachina dolls, which he had seen when visiting Nelson years earlier.
Working in a greater range of media than his Memphis colleagues, Sottsass was a prolific talent, designing a variety of objects for some one hundred clients,4º including jewelry for ACME and CLETO MUNARI and objects for ALESSI (see all under "Edited Design").
For his design consultancy, Sottsass Associati, he assembled a group of enthusiastic and talented young designers, four of whom— ALDO CIBIC, GEORGE SOWDEN, MATTEO THUN, and MARCO ZANINI (see all under “Design") —would become part of Memphis.
The firm took on large projects like the design of stores and showrooms for the clothing company Esprit\*, as well as interiors, exhibitions, and furniture.
After leaving Memphis in 1985, Sottsass continued to design with his characteristic enthusiasm.
Having created relatively few pieces for Memphis, he introduced a collection of twenty-five assertive objects called Furniture of the Rituals of Life for New York's Blum Helman Warehouse that same year, using luxury materials he had previously avoided: marble, exotic woods, glass, and mosaic.
The limited-edition designs included tall cabinets and long tables with names like In Praise of Epicurus and What's the Name of Your Swiss Bank?
These were priced like artworks, ranging up to \$30,000.4' BELOW Carlton bookshelf/ room divider from the first Memphis collection, wood and plastic laminate, 1981.
Despite concentrating on products through most of his career, Sottsass designed more than twenty homes, including three in the United States: his first, for the collector and dealer Daniel Wolf (ı982), in a spectacular mountain site in Colorado; a vivid retreat in Maui (i997) for Adrian and Lesley Olabuenaga of Acme Studios; and a California home for the Silicon Valley entrepreneur David Kelley (i997).
Though all very different, the buildings have a familial resemblance, suggesting clusters of brightly colored building blocks.
Sottsass's life was a tireless search for new ways to move beyond International Style Modernism, dismantling the past to reinterpret the contemporary world—“It must be possible to design instability,"42 he said—and to improve it through good design He inspired all those in his immediate circle, and his influence carried forward to the generations of designers who followed.

1985.

ROBERT VENTURI AND DENISE SCOTT BROWN b.
1925 and 1931 PARTNERS IN THEORY AND PRACTICE, THEYBROUGHT NEW RESPECT FOR VERNACULARARCHITECTURE AND HISTORICISM.
As serious about the theories behind his architecture as he was committed to their execution, Robert Venturi was the first of his contemporaries to make waves in the calm sea of Modernism.
With the publication of Complexity and Contradiction in Architecture in i966, he called attention to the idea that architecture was not a simple matter of following rules, but should offer a depth of meaning and the “messy vitality" of the built environment that had been erased in the sterile glass boxes of the International Style.
Dismissing Mies van der Rohe's celebrated phrase "less is more," he proclaimed, "less is a bore," and followed his dictum in his designs.
The i972 book Learning From Las Vegas, written with his wife and collaborator Denise Scott Brown, and Steven Izenour, explored urban sprawl, celebrating vernacular architecture and the roadside "decorated shed." Though in later years Venturi and Scott Brown (also see under "Living with Postmodern Design") took exception with the course taken by postmodern architecture, decrying what they labeled PoMo, the concepts outlined in these influential works provided much of the foundation for Postmodernism's embrace of historicism and contextualism.
Robert Venturi was born in Philadelphia and attended Princeton University, where he received an undergraduate degree in 1947 and his MArch in i95o.
He worked in the offices of Oscar Stonorov and Eero Saarinen and, in i954, was awarded a Prix de Rome.
Spending two years at the American Academy in Rome, Venturi met Louis Kahn, and returned home to Philadelphia to work in Kahn's office.
He began his own architectural practice in ı958, and joined with John Rauch in 1964 to establish Venturi & Rauch (now Venturi, Scott Brown & Associates).
From 1951, Venturi taught architectural theory at the University of Pennsylvania for several years, where he formulated the concepts outlined in his writings.
His first architectural commission was the home completed in 1964 for his mother Vanna, in the Philadelphia suburb of Chestnut Hill.
His best-known building, it was perhaps the earliest work of postmodern architecture, with a facade that commingles several architectural styles.
A huge influence on architectural pedagogy, particularly in the United States, it led schools to re-examine their neglect of historic precedents.
It was followed by major projects in what became known as postmodern style that include Guild House (i964), a residence for senior citizens; the classically pedimented Sainsbury Wing of London's National Gallery (ı991); and private residences combining historical and vernacular vocabularies.
Venturi's first venture into product design was a collection of nine chairs designed between 1978 and i984 for Knoll\*.
The laminated-plywood chairs were formed like silhouettes, with backs shaped in cartoon-like depictions of classic period styles ranging from Queen Ańne to Art Deco.
As the silhouettes translate historiç shapes into a modern material, the decoration recalls polychrome ornament.
Screenprinted with colorful motifs appropriate to each style, the chairs—particularly the Queen Anne, covered in a “grandmother's tablecloth" pattern—were instant successes with architecture and design aficionados and are among the most recognizable postmodern objects.
For ALESSI (see under "Edited Design"), Venturi designed a tea and coffee service that used historical styles as reference, including a Rococo coffee pot, a neoclassical sugar bowl and an Art Nouveau creamer.
He also designed a cartoon-like riff on a traditional cuckoo clock, evidence of the tongue-in-cheek attitude that underscores all his work: he is often quoted as calling himself "the Dennis the Menace of architecture," after the mischievous comic character.
Denise Scott Brown was born in Zambia, attended the University of the Witwatersrand in South Africa, and graduated from the Architectural Association, London in 1955.
She received Master's degrees in architecture and city planning in 196o and i965 respectively from the University of Pennsylvania, where she and Venturi met while they were both teaching.
They married in 1967, when Scott Brown was teaching in California, and she relocated to Philadelphia to join his firm, becoming a principal in i969; it was renamed Venturi, Rauch & Scott Brown in i98o.
In addition to her architectural work, Scott Brown has taught and lectured at the University of Pennsylvania School of Fine Arts, and at Harvard, Yale, and Princeton universities.
Working primarily as a team, the Venturis exemplify both innovative Postmodernism and a productive design partnership.
More than that, their work underscores the importance of commitment to an idea, regardless of convention.

## FORMS
OF POSTMODERN DESIGN

## ARCHITECTURE
Modernism had changed the landscapes of cities; as early as i96ı, critics such as Jane Jacobs were decrying its destruction of the eclecticism that had previously made cities livable.
Having given life to the International Style in i93ı, the Museum of Modern Art gave grounds for its rejection in i975, claiming, “Although the modern movement succeeded in creating individual masterpieces, it has failed disastrously in urban planning."' The international exhibition “The Presence of the Past" at the i98o Venice Biennale was a manifesto for postmodern architecture, drawing on history to paint a picture of warmth, color, and approachability, and reminding architects of what had been lost in Modernism's rejection of historicism.² But postmodernist architecture at its most flamboyant was more an American phenomenon than a European one, encouraged by theorists and journalists looking for new directions.
Author Tom Wolfe's From Bauhaus to Our House (i98ı) was a bestselling diatribe against a sterile architectural style foisted on the public by elitist tastemakers.3 There were two approaches to postmodern architecture's mode of looking back: one more overtly (and sometimes cartoonishly) historicist and symbolic, and the other more conservatively replicating classical styles.
Postmodern buildings often retained the International Style's rectilinear forms and flat roofs, selectively adding elements from other periods, including geometric shapes, peaked or arched rooflines, and classical ornamentation.
Each architect, of course, had his or her own interpretation, in a strategic borrowing that often combined different styles in assemblages ranging from subtly witty to sternly serious.4 Building designs became busier; nostalgia was accepted, even encouraged; and historicism embraced all periods, from classical to neo-Gothic.⁵ The bellwether of postmodern style had been ROBERT VENTURI's (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") house for his mother, built in 1964, though the first to attract broad public notice were MICHAEL GRAVES's (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") Portland Building (i982) and Philip Johnson's\* publicity-generating and critically trashed AT&T Building (i984).
Other noteworthy early examples included TERRY FARRELL's TV-am (i982), JAMES STIRLING's Neue Staatsgalerie (1983), and CHARLES MOORE's Piazza d'Italia (ı978).
In America, the style flourished during a real estate boom as an antidote to the tedious corporate structures that had proliferated in the i96os and ı97os.
Postmodernism offered the ideal means of creating instant brand identity, as witnessed in Michael Graves's Team Disney building (1991), with a frieze of the Seven Dwarfs on the facade.
Understandable even to a lay audience, such buildings made the most of Postmodernism's ability to convey multivalent messages and act as corporate signposts.
Fillips like Farrell's giant eggcups on the TV-am roof and the faux-classical capitals on JOHN OUTRAM's Pumping Station added a note of humor, as architecture celebrated ornament for its own sake.
Even establishment firms adopted postmodern imagery: Kohn Pedersen Fox for the Procter & Gamble headquarters in Cincinnati; César Pelli for the World Trade Center in New York.
In a decade of "starchitects," virtually # Postmodern buildings often retained the International Style's rectilinear forms and flat roofs, selectively adding elements from other periods geometric shapes, peaked or arched rooflines, and classical ornamentation.
ABOVE Apartments by Oswald Mathias Ungers for the International Building Exhibition, Berlin, 1987. all prominent practitioners were pulled along with the tide.
Almost every new corporate structure erected in America in the i98os had the requisite columns, pediments and arches—as formulaic an approach as had been taken with Postmodernism's much-criticized predecessor style.
There are limits of permissibility, however, for architecture: it cannot abandon the requirements of function, security, and comfort.
It is, then, not surpris ing that Postmodernism was expressed most boldly in facades, and often did not migrate to interiors.
Spaces like showrooms, retail stores, and restaurants were, to some degree, exceptions, being redecorated every few months or years with changing fashions.
Hence Postmodernism ran rampant in malls, giving birth to elaborate projects including Jon Jerde's Horton Plaza (i985) in San Diego and FRANK GEHRY's Edgemar center (ı988) in Santa Monica.
In other countries, architects were more likely to use classical elements as integral parts of a structure, rather than as surface appliqués, as in RICARDO BOFILL's monumental Espaces d'Abraxas or Kengo Kuma's spectacular M2 Building.
Some, like MARIO BOTTA and ALDO ROSSI, preferred muscular, geometric forms, with little ornament.
In Japan, architects like ARATA ISOZAKI and Kisho Kurokawa drew on both historical tradition and the ideas of the Metabolists, a i96os movement that developed utopian ideas for urban planning.
Similarly, in southwestern EurOpe, TOMÁS TAVEIRA and OSCAR TUSQUETS BLANCA melded local tradition with the modern.
And LÉON KRIER and followers of the New Urbanism focused on context, reviving the livable, walkable city with an eclectic mix of historic and vernacular styles.
Its archetypes were Celebration, Florida, the DISNEY (see under "Edited Design") development of the i99os, and the perhaps more successful Seaside\*, Florida, which includes one of Krier's few built projects among a diversity of individual architectural styles.
Although they rejected modernist theory, postmodernists never established a consistent theory of their own, focusing on what they opposed more than what they favored—other than historicism.
In opposition to this approach, Deconstructivism—evolved from the theories of French philosopher Jacques Derrida—produced disconcertingly chaotic forms that were as poorly understood as Postmodernism itself.
Bernard Tschumi\* and AMES WINES were the among the leading proponents of a style also embraced by Frank Gehry, Daniel Libeskind\*, and Zaha Hadid.6 What theorists and practitioners both understood, however, were the drawbacks of a purely rational approach: “The common denominator of the post-modern experiments is the quest for a communicative, meaningful architecture."7 Postmodernism in the private sector was largely limited to an affluent market of designsavvy clients.
In America this produced scaled-up versions of shingle-style cottages, and in Europe, adaptations of classical or vernacular designs.
Though the homes were elegant, their interiors were rarely postmodern.
Notable exceptions were projects by radical European architects including ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), ALESSANDRO MENDINI (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), and BOŘEK ŠiPEK (see under “Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") that eschewed historicism for bold forms and brilliant colors— ideal surrounds for extravagant furniture designs.
The recession of the late i98os and early 1990s played a key part in the decline of postmodern architecture; bold silhouettes gave way to forgettable, conservative buildings.
But the recent revival of interest in postmodern design, with support from preservationists and historians, shows that Postmodernism, despite its faults, was more than a fad.
In rekindling the search for meaning in architecture, for buildings that evoked emotion rather than indifference, for imagery that reflected both respect for the past and OPPOSITE, FROM TOP Tomás Taveira, apartments in a housing development coordinated by Arata Isozaki, Fukuoka, Japan, 1990; Michael Graves, Snyderman House, Fort Wayne, Indiana, 1972; Jon Jerde, Horton Plaza shopping center, San Diego, California, 1985.
CLOCKWISE FROM TOP RIGHT Philip Johnson, Town Hall, Celebration, Florida, 1996; Kengo Kuma, M2 Building, Tokyo, Japan, 1991; Robert Venturi, Eclectic Houses sketches, 1970s; Ettore Sottsass, David Kelley House, Silicon Valley, California, 2000. concern for the present, for a love of color and an affinity for social interaction, Postmodernism brought a human element back to architecture.
It was probably the most democratic style in contemporary history.
For this, if nothing else, it should be celebrated. # The Politics of Postmodern Architecture Sean Griffiths The aesthetic of MICHAEL GRAVES (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design")—a pop-inflected, taste-challenging form of appliqué classicism—was the dominant form of architectural expression in the West during the i98os.
FRANK GEHRY called it "the architecture of Ronald Reagan." Graves was a New York liberal, a member of the New York Five, a group of architects dedicated to the reinvigoration of the Corbusian tradition of purist Modernism.
They became Tom Wolfe's villainous “Whites" in From Bauhaus to Our House, his i98ı polemic against modern architecture.
Wolfe's goodies were the “Grays," the proselytizers of Postmodernism.
By the i97os, however, Graves had transformed into a full-blown postmodernist.
The architectural theorist CHARLES JENCKS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") located the death of Modernism as July 15, 1972, the date of the demolition of Minoru Yamasaki's Pruitt-Igoe public housing scheme in St.
Louis.
But the critical attack on Modernism had begun a decade earlier, with the i96ı publication of Jane Jacobs's concerted attack on modernist city planning, The Death and Life of Great American Cities.
Ebenezer Howard and Le Corbusier, whose urban ideas were relentlessly speared in the book, had been highly influential in advocating the rationalization of cities by compartmentalizing their functions into separate zones.
But the outcomes of these experiments had been controversial, as superblocks of housing, often lacking amenities such as shops, community facilities, and employment, replaced the close grain of traditional cities.
Jacobs decried top-down solutions to urban issues as the work of dangerous bureaucrats who failed to recognize people's ability to make “plans of their own." For acobs, acts of comprehensive redevelopment had only made problems of poverty, crime, and desolation worse, Instead, she championed the residents of Boston's North End who, using bartered skills and small loans, transformed slum neighborhoods into rejuvenated, vibrant places.
For Jacobs, examples like this exemplified a community's innate understanding of the city as a form of organized complexity, which compared favorably to the simplistic rational ism of the city planners.
In his book Complexity and Contradiction in Architecture (i966) ROBERT VENTURI (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") brought this kind of thinking to the appreciation of individual buildings.
Attacking the reductive aesthetics of modern architecture and anticipating Tom Wolfe, Venturi made a claim for the ambiguous over the simplistic in architecture, stating his preference for “both-and" over “either-or” and for "gray" over “black or white." Venturi's argument was structured around concepts of complexity versus simplicity, embracing convention as well as invention and promoting adaptability against rigorous function alism, all of which was to be skillfully resolved as a "difficult whole." Among works by famous architects from history, Venturi also celebrated ordinary build ings that had gained new meanings through adaption.
Both these texts were of great importance in the development of postmodernist architecture.
Both rejected the rationalist, expert-led imposition of architectural and urban solutions in favor of a people-centered approach to architecture and city planning.
At the same time, neither was arguing from an explicitly ideological position.
So how did it come to be that an idea with such humanist and grass-roots-oriented allegiances resulted, by the ig8os, in architecture that could be associated with Ronald Reagan?
ABOVE Venturi, Scott Brown & Associates, Sainsbury Wing, National Gallery, London, 1991.
Jacobs's and Venturi's books coincided with the appearance of the first cracks in the social democratic ideology that had prevailed after the Second World War.
Planning projects were the ultimate physical embodiment of “big government," whereby benevolent experts implemented what was good for people.
At the time of the New Deal in the United States, big government had been popular and had provided employment, renewal, and optimism at a time of economic hardship.
By the early 196os, however, the negative effects of powerful government, not least in the area of city planning, were beginning to be felt and resisted.
The revolt against “expertise" manifested itself in the development of grass-roots political actions, such as the BELOW Robert Venturi, drawings of homes with classical elements, 1970s. anti-Vietnam War and Civil Rights movements in the United States.
The security and prosperity that social democracy enabled had generated a new consumerist individualism, leading to diverse effects like the explosion of youth culture and the rise of the American homeowner associations.
New forms of libertarianism were emerging on the Left and the Right, but the ideas exemplified by postmodern architecture, originating in resistance to the technocratic overreach of government were, as it turned out, especially ripe for appropriation by the New Right, and the result was the i98o election of Ronald Reagan.
Postmodernist pluralism was a form of resistance to the universalism of Modernism.
But it was also a matter of cultural assertion, and the emergent culture wars of the i96os were given further impetus in the world of architecture by the publication in i972 of Robert Venturi's second book, Learning from Las Vegas.
Written with his wife, the socially oriented planner DENISE SCOTT BROWN (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), and colleague Steven Izenour, the book tapped into a new pop consciousness and suggested that architects might learn something from the tastes of the ordinary people upon whom they imposed their expertise.
The book caused outrage among modernists.
"Ideology in its purest form," shouted the critic Kenneth Frampton.
For architects and other liberals, Las Vegas was the ultimate expression of vulgar capitalism.
But Learning from Las Vegas was not strictly “about" the values of Las Vegas at all.
Indeed, the authors implored their readers to suspend moral judgment, and to some extent the book was actually a product of the counterculture.
As a study of the new automobile-driven urbanism of the Strip and as the promotion of a methodology for revealing the order that under lay the apparent visual chaos of this new kind of city, its thinking (and the graphic styling) paral leled that of more acceptably avant-garde figures such as Buckminster Fuller and Marshall McLuhan.
However, whereas Venturi's earlier Complexity and Contradiction in Architecture offered a subtle and challenging route toward an architecture of "the difficult whole," in Learning from Las Vegas the authors introduced a concept that significantly influenced the architecture of the i98os.
They called it "the decorated shed." The idea denoted an architecture of simple structures, decorated with signs, electronics, and ornamentation.
Though perhaps a valid typology in the context of Las Vegas, it nevertheless gave architects license to splash skin-deep decorations over otherwise simple building types regardless of function or context.
This approach was exemplified by Michael Graves's Portland Building of 1982 in Oregon.
This building, which graced the cover of the seminal The Language of Post-Modern Architecture by Charles Jencks, was essentially a modernist steelframe building clad in a marble skin of flattened, pop-styled classicism.
It became the model for many commercial developments over the next ten years, its decorative approach bringing some relief and gaiety after years of austere Miesian minimalism.
But perhaps more than that, it constituted a relatively cost-effective way for developers to give otherwise mundane buildings a bit of razzamatazz.
Ironically, RIGHT Destruction of the 1956 Pruitt-lgoe housing project in St.
Louis, Missouri, 1972-76—often cited as the death knell of Modernism. given Venturi and Scott Brown's previous celebration of convention in construction, it was a new marketoriented innovation in development and construction that promoted the “decorated shed" as the new way of doing architecture.
Deregulation of financial services by the Thatcher and Reagan administrations in the i98os led to an explosion in demand for new office space in global financial centers.
This in turn encouraged the development of speedy and cost-efficient design and construction methodologies.
One such system, imported from the United States into the United Kingdom, was called fast-track construction.
Architects were now hired to do concept designs, while the in-house design teams of developers/construction companies carried out the detailing.
The decorated shed concept lent itself only too well to these procedures, and the architect's input was increasingly reduced to the design of appliqué cladding.
This was the method used in i988 to design the controversial Broadgate project in the City of London, a huge development of office space at a scale never before seen in the city.
Later schemes in London, such as the first phases of the Canary Wharf development in the abandoned Docklands of the east, utilized the same techniques, much to the dissatisfaction of architects, whose control and level of input into the design of buildings diminished as the power of project and cost managers grew.
While a sophisticated form of postmodernist architecture flourished in cultural buildings, such as HANS HOLLEIN's and JAMES STIRLING's respective museums in Mönchengladbach and Stuttgart, Germany, mainstream architecture became associated with the superficiality of a brash, vulgar, and consumerist culture.
This architecture not only represented the culture of the new economics but was also a manifestation of the techniques that were deployed to finance, design, construct, and market buildings.
It was in this sense that Postmodernism came to be thought of as the architecture of Ronald Reagan.
Despite the successes in the idiom by architects like Hollein and Stirling that it claimed as its own, the profession was never entirely comfortable with the new populist, skin-deep architecture.
The modernists suffered bitter defeats, exemplified by the row over the design of the extension to the National Gallery in London.
An infamous interven tion by Charles, Prince of Wales—who described the modernist design by Ahrends, Burton & Koralek as a "monstrous carbuncle on the face of a much-loved and elegant friend"—caused the scheme to be aban doned.
That the commission for the replacement design was awarded following a competition to the parents of Postmodernism, Robert Venturi and Denise Scott Brown, whose effort was later described by the editor of the influential British publication Architectural ABOVE Michael Graves, renderings of facade treatments for the Portland Building, c.
1978.
OPPOSITE TOP Broadgate, a 32-acre (ı3-ha) mixed-use complex in London, begun around 1990.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM Hans Hollein, Abteiberg Museum, built into a hillside in Mönchengladbach, Germany, 1988.
Review as “picturesque, mediocre slime," added insult to injury.
It was a moment that marked the apotheosis of postmodern architecture in London but also signaled its decline.
Thanks to Prince Charles, Postmodernism, despite its beginnings as a liberating movement against the excesses of top-down expertise, became aligned in the minds of the profession and the public with reactionary politics.
In its embrace of activism, participation, and popular culture, architects had seen in Postmodernism a threat to their ability to exert some control in the built environment.
The failures of Modernism had been especially painful, since the brave new world it had heralded had been inspired by the best of intentions.
Never before had architects thought of themselves as so socially engaged.
At the same time, they had also been reluctant to acknowledge the capture of Modernism by powerful corporatism.
It was as if, in an echo of their own critiques of Postmodernism as a merely skin-deep form of architecture, they had thought that the application of the modernist “look" could clothe capitalism in an acceptable signification of social worth.
Nonetheless, Postmodernism's days as the dominant architectural philosophy were over, and an architecture drawing on a retroactive embrace of neo-Constructivist aesthetics emerged in an equal and opposite reaction to the rise of Postmodernism.
Perhaps Postmodernism's greatest legacy is its implicit acknowledgment in contrast to the modernist quest for timelessness, of the idea of architectural fashion.
Its embrace of the skin-deep created a template for new strands of architectural Postmodernism to develop, facilitated by ever-faster new technologies.
And indeed, in recent years a new kind of “cutand-paste" Postmodernism has emerged among a younger generation of architects capitalizing on the ability to manipulate images digitally and to utilize the Internet for the dissemination of ideas.
The political character of this new Postmodernism is still emerging, but the grounds on which its battles are being fought are those of taste and politics.
In challenging the current mainstream of “iconic" and "tasteful" Modernism, younger architects are tapping into the issues of class, inclusion, and culture that have become the dominant political issues of the present.
It is also clear that the new Postmodernism, in its embrace of the image, in its engagement with social activism, and in its attempts to develop new, entrepreneurial approaches to practice, is a reflection of its origins in an internet culture—which itself is keeping the ideas of seminal figures like Jane Jacobs, Denise Scott Brown, and Robert Venturi very much alive and relevant.

## ARQUITECTONICA
Bernardo Fort-Brescia, b.
1951 Laurinda Spear, b.
1950 MIAMI'S TROPICAL PALETTE AND ADVENTUROUS FORMS MARK THE DISTINCTIVE STYLE OF THIS PARTNERSHIP.
Some practitioners, in their mature years, prefer to downplay their youthful exploits.
For Miami-based Arquitectonica, that may be understandable, since the firm's highly telegenic buildings were used as backdrops for a popular i98os television show depicting the city as young and hip, and perhaps more frivolous than serious.
Though the show has long ended, Arquitectonica has grown in stature (along with their home city), and one of those early works has become an icon of Postmodernism.
Established in i977 by Bernardo Fort-Brescia and Laurinda Spear, along with Andrés Duany\*, Elizabeth Plater-Zyberk\* and Hervin Romney (the only partner who then had an architect's license8), Arquitectonica developed a highly individual style inspired by the tropical colors of their Miami environment.
After three of the original partners left,9 Spear and Fort-Brescia, by then married, became partners in a firm that would gain international recognition.
Spear is a Minnesotan who grew up in Miami, studied art at Brown University, and received her MArch from Columbia University's Graduate School of Architecture in 1975.
Before Arquitectonica, she worked briefly in urban planning, and has since studied landscape architecture.
Fort-Brescia is a native of Lima, Peru, and was educated there and in Europe, coming to America to attend Princeton and the Harvard Graduate School of Design (i975).
He then moved to Miami to teach in the University of Miami's architecture program.
Arquitectonica's first project, in ı977, was a house in Miami Shores for Spear's parents and, like the house that ROBERT VENTURI (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") designed for his mother, it drew instant attention to the new firm.
Called the Pink House, with facades in multiple hues of that color, it was a shocking departure from conventionally bland modern buildings and prefigured the palette of bright pastels that would distinguish much of Arquitectonica's work.'o Looking to make their mark, the young firm accepted commissions that many architects avoided, including a trio of high-rise residential buildings in downtown Miami (apartment houses were not considered prestigious projects), and turned them into tour de force design statements.
The Atlantis (i982) was a waterfront condominium whose monolithic form was punctuated by a “sky court," complete with palm tree, spiral staircase, and blue whirlpool, and topped with a bright red triangular roof ornament.
It was photographed and published around the country and across the world, appearing in the opening credits for the television show Miami Vice, with other color-saturated Arquitectonica projects often used as background scenery.
The result was an elevated profile for the newly stylish city—and the architects.
Arquitectonica also fearlessly took on shopping centers, making pedestrian locales like the Sawgrass Mills (i987) discount mall appealing for their design as well as for bargains.
Arquitectonica's contributions to postmodern product design include a table for MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") and jewelry for ACME (see under “Edited Design"), as well as textiles and wallcoverings.
Spear has also designed laminates for FORMICA (see under "Edited Design") and ceramics for SWID POWELL (see essay under “Edited Design").
Though they have worked around the world, Arquitectonica's roots, and much of their most important work, is in South Florida and, in the tradition of Miami-based midcentury architect Morris Lapidus, tends to be extroverted, almost exhibitionistic.
They often deploy gravity-defying forms with unexpected curves and angles, and in the 199os designed three Disney All-Star resorts that dressed up motel-type buildings in Pop art and DISNEY (see under “Edited Design") iconography.
For Arquitectonica, every building must express an idea, one that is both keyed to its purpose and appropriate to its surroundings." LEFT Pink House, facing Biscayne Bay, Miami, Florida.
1976 ABOVE Pink House, the landward facade.
RIGHT Pool between two wings of the Pink House.
BELOW Madonna table, lacquered wood, for Memphis, 1984.

BOTTOM RIGHT Atlantis building facade.

1985.

THOMASBEEBY b.1941 CHICAGO-BASED PROPONENT OF THE NEWURBANISM, WHOSE ARCHITECTURE IS BASEDON A RESPECT FOR HISTORICISM.
RIGHT Hole in the Wall Gang Camp, Ashford, Connecticut, 1988.
Beeby was one of the Chicago Seven\* architects, a group that came to national attention in i976 when they protested the emphasis on Miesian Modernism in an exhibition at the city's Museum of Contemporary Art by organizing a counterexhibition suggesting a new approach to the city's architecture.
These early supporters of what would soon be labeled Postmodernism staged what became the notorious “Exquisite Corpse" show, proposing alternative styles that referenced history.
By reviving interest in traditional architecture and urban design, the group helped to shift the direction of Chicago's development Following the same principles, Beeby has been an influential voice in the New Urbanism movement, which seeks to improve the urban experience by reintroducing diversity and human-scale buildings.
After graduating from Cornell University (i964) and the Yale School of Architecture (1965), Beeby worked for C.
F.
Murphy Associates before forming Hammond Beeby & Associates with Jim Hammond, where he was chief designer for more than four decades.
He is currently Chairman Emeritus of the firm (now HBRA Architects) Beeby's career has combined architectural practice with teaching, first at Illinois Institute of Technology (ı973-79), where he was one of the first instructors who had not attended that school, then as director of the School of Architecture at the University of Illinois, Chicago (i98o-85), and finally as Dean of Yale School of Architecture (i985–92).
He has continued to teach and lecture there as well as at a variety of prestigious museums and institutions.
Beeby's buildings have been strongly influenced by his respect for classicism, and his best-known works include the Rice Building addition to the Art Institute of Chicago (i988), an understated, historicist structure that was praised for avoiding the “self-conscious prettiness" of many traditional buildings.'² For the Harold Washington Library Center (i99ı), on the other hand, Beeby designed a trafficstopping downtown landmark for what was then the country's largest circulating library (it is still one of the largest).
Beeby's building was criticized for its classical-inspired postmodern design,'3 which won the commission in i987 over proposals favoring a more modern structure.
The architect defended it as a hybrid of modern and nineteenthcentury ideals, inverting Miesian architecture by putting the building's core on the exterior rather than the interior.'4 Like many postmodern buildings, it references historical precedents, in this case those by Chicago masters; Beeby has noted the allusions in the window system to H.
H.
Richardson's Marshall Field Warehouse, and in the classical ornament, brickwork, and rusticated base to Daniel Burnham's Rookery.
For Paul Newman's Hole in the Wall Gang Camp (ı988), Beeby designed a whimsical row of facades that suggest an old-time Western film set, again following his characteristic blending of modern and traditional—in this case the vernacular elements revisited by the postmodernists.
And his riotously colorful showroom for FORMICA (see under “Edited Design") is a showcase for both the company's products and the postmodern style.
Among his other important projects are the Meadows Museum in Dallas (i965) and Baker Institute at Rice University, Houston (i993).
The American Institute of Architects, of which Beeby is a fellow, has given seven of his projects their highest design distinction.
In 2o13 Beeby received the prestigious Driehaus Architecture Prize, which was established to honor contributions to classical architecture and urbanism in the modern world.
ABOVE Harold Washington Library Center in downtown Chicago, 1991.
RIGHT Harold Washington Library entrance area.
OPPOSITE Bimah, North Shore Congregation Israel, Chicago, 1983, part of a 116,000 sq. ft (1,0777 sq. m) addition to a building by Minoru Yamasaki.
ABOVE LEFT Formica showroom entrance, Chicago, 1984.
ABOVE RIGHT Children's furniture for the Chicago Public Library, Sulzer Branch, 1985.
LEFT Frieze made of Corian in the Formica showroom, 1984. # RICARDO BOFILL b.1939 BARCELONA ARCHITECT WHO USES CLASSICAL FORMS IN TIMELESS STRUCTURES THAT RECALL ANCIENT WORLDS.
RIGHT The Palacio, Espaces d'Abraxas, Marne-la-Vallée, Paris, 1978–83.
Some of Postmodernism's most dramatic structures were designed by this Spanish architect, who practiced independently of the movement's mainstream.
His work ensured the acceptance of classical iconography as major elements in buildings that also considered contemporary context and incorporated modern materials.
Born in Barcelona, Bofill grew up surrounded by the diversions of maverick architect Antoni Gaudí, yet his work is altogether different, drawing instead from a classical vocabulary.
Indeed, some of his postmodern era works have the appearance of timeless structures that, save for their modern surroundings, might have been recovered from ancient worlds.
He attended architecture school in Barcelona and graduated from the Geneva School of Architecture (i96o).
In i963, he established his practice in Barcelona, gathering architects, engineers, planners, a sociologist, writers, film-makers, and philosophers into what became the Taller de Arquitectura.
This unusual combination of talents has since been dedicated to a specific challenge: finding new ways to deal with the problems of modern urban space.
Over several decades, Bofill's work has developed an increasingly sophisticated personal language, first adopting elements of traditional Catalan architecture, then gradually marrying classical forms and ornamentation with elements of twentieth-century Spain into distinctive forms that are postmodern in their classical references but very spare in ornamentation.
In addition to large residential projects, Bofill's office completed urban design and master-planning commissions around the world.
His City in the Space, a conceptual housing project, explored his ideas for a livable city, with half the space reserved for community use, circulation, and gardens, and the buildings themselves designed according to a modular system of cubic forms.
Walden 7 (i975), outside Barcelona, further implemented his ideas in a fortress-like apartment complex with eighteen buildings clustered around seven courtyards, linked by a system of connecting bridges and balconies that creates an appealing variety of spatial experiences, as well as interior and exterior vistas.
Though the exterior is a uniform deep red, the courtyards are relieved with a variety of bright hues.
Walden 7 brought Bofill international attention, and further commissions.
Beginning in the late 197os, Bofill carried out most of his architectural work in France, designing building projects to meet the country's need to house its growing migrant population in new towns being developed in the aftermath of the Second World War.
He became celebrated for large-scale projects like Les Espaces d'Abraxas (i983), outside Paris, a monumental environment with classical details like arches, columns, and pilasters for an effect that was at once historicist and futuristic.
It was used as background for two Hollywood films.'5 This and other Bofill designs for industrialized social housing incorporated elements from historic French architecture. responding to the perceived starkness of modernist buildings like Le Corbusier's Unité BELOWMain plaza, Espaces d'Abraxas, with Palacio, and center Arc containing 20 apartments, 1983.
BOTTOM Freestanding classical arch on grounds of the Palacio. d'Habitation.
Rich in classical detailing, they have the massive appearance of ancient stone structures but were executed with modern technology, in precast concrete—considerably less expensive than the stone they replicated.
His approach has been referred to as “radical classicism."i6 Bofll has since translated his distinctive vocabulary with great success to other countries, adapting his classical aesthetic to harmonize with different local cultures.
Some designers never have the opportunity to implement their architectural concepts for their own use, but Bofill did just that in perhaps his most widely published project, the transformation of a nineteenth-century cement factory into an extraordinary residence and workplace.
La Fábrica proves that even purpose-built architecture can be renovated and transformed for another use rather than destroyed.
Purchased in 1973, the eight-building complex (all that remained of thirty original silos) was sensitively restored and revived into an undeniably modern space that retains vestiges of its past in its use of simple industrial materials.
The result is a project that reveals its inspiration, but subtly.
La Fábrica is the Taller de Arquitectura's headquarters as well as Bofill's family home.
ABOVE LEFT Walden 7, main courtyard and entrance, Sant Just Desvern, near Barcelona.
ABOVE RIGHT Ananda, private villa in Miami, Florida, 2000.
RIGHT Ananda villa, open to the sky and sea.
OPPOSITE TOP Walden 7, Sant Just Desvern, near Barcelona, 1975.
MARIO BOTTA b.1943 A CORE VOCABULARY OF MUSCULAR GEOMETRIC FORMS MARK THE BUILDINGS AND FURNITURE OF THIS SWISS MASTER.
RIGHT Sketch of residence in Origlio, Switzerland, c.
1982.
Mario Botta became an architect by accident.
He never liked attending classes, and left secondary school at the age of fifteen to work in an architect's office, where, happily, he discovered the drawing skills that ultimately led him to the profession that has made him internationally famous.
Though he is considered a postmodernist, Botta's work in no way resembles that of others given that designation.
Said to combine native Swiss reticence with the exuberance of Italy, it avoids explicit historical references, conspicuously declines to play with color, and has none of the provocative elements associated with postmodern design.
His buildings are notable for their use of traditional masonry, reflecting the influence of his early mentors Le Corbusier and Louis Kahn.
They incorporate muscular, geometric forms, bold black-and-white pattern, and the ingenious manipulation of light and shadow.
Yet his designs are clearly rooted in history; his buildings have the presence of classical structures, interpreted with a lightness that is altogether contemporary.
Botta was born in the town of Mendrisio in the canton of Ticino, an Italian-speaking region of Switzerland, and most of his architectural projects are in and around nearby Lugano, where he has his office.
It is also the location of the architectural studio he joined as a teenager; he trained there for three years, first as draftsman and then apprentice, and completed his first design project, a local parish house.
He left in ı96ı to attend the Liceo Artistico art college in Milan, Italy, where he reportedly skipped many of the classes, though he did take the examinations required to graduate.
After deciding to commit to a career in architecture, he enrolled in the Istituto Universitario di Architettura (IUA) in Venice.
There he had the opportunity to learn from three of the century's most important modernists: he seized the chance to work in Le Corbusier's studio during his first year of study, had Carlo Scarpa as a teacher and thesis adviser, and in his final vear, assisted Louis Kahn on an exhibition and plans for the new Palazzo dei Congressi—although Botta spoke no English and Kahn spoke no Italian.
Returning to Switzerland after graduating from IUA, he worked briefly in a Lugano architecture studio and opened his own practice there in 197o.
His designs included many single-family houses that explored the relation between man and nature, as well as several important churches that brought him international attention.
All Botta's designs begin with rough sketches, which he refines and reworks into their final form.
Most of his buildings follow an axial plan—that is, with the elements organized along a given axis, as opposed to being arranged around a central point—and many are enclosed by a circular frame.
Yet the spare vocabulary allows for many different variations; his work has a consistency, but no two buildings look alike.
Critic Paul Goldberger has called them “self-assured essays in geometry."17 In 1982 Botta began to design furniture and lighting, primarily for Italian firms Alias\* and Artemide\*.
His product design is clearly kin to his architecture, using a similar vocabulary.
Named in sequence, his Prima to Quinto chairs use tubular steel and spare geometric forms that reflect his links to Corbusier and Bauhaus concepts, and his lighting designs are similarly RIGHT Residence in Origlio, Switzerland, 1982.
RIGHT Residence in Viganello, Switzerland, 1981.
FAR RIGHT Casa Rotonda, Stabio, Switzerland, 1981.
ABOVE Quinta chairs, steel tubing and perforated sheet steel, for Alias, 1985.
BELOW Preliminary sketches for Prince chair, from the Sesta series, 1984.
OPPOSITE Prince chair, enamel on steel mesh, leather, 1985. bold and minimal.
For CLETO MUNARI (see under "Edited Design”), he designed a pair of pitchers and a vase, all of which lean more toward a Bauhaus aesthetic than a postmodern one.
Botta's best-known buildings include the San Francisco Museum of Modern Art (i995) and the Church of San Giovanni Battista in Ticino (ı996), and in recent years he has designed theaters, libraries, and museums, including the Bechtler Museum of Modern Art (2oio) in Charlotte, North Carolina, his second commission in the United States.
In addition to his practice, Mario Botta has also written and edited books, and has served as a visiting professor at the École Polytechnique Fédérale de Lausanne in Switzerland.

TERRY FARRELL b.1938 THE UNITED KINGDOM'S FIRST POSTMODERNIST DESIGNER, WHOSE ATTENTION-GRABBING BUILDINGS HAVE DOUBLE-CODED MEANING.
Breaking ranks with the top tier of late twentiethcentury English architects, Terry Farrell became something of a pioneer when he designed the first postmodern building in the United Kingdom.
While contemporaries like Richard Rogers, Norman Foster, and Nicholas Grimshaw continued their allegiance to modernist principles, Farrell changed direction to move along the less confining paths charted by early proselytizers of the new style.
In his role as a champion of urban planning, he has also been influential in helping to shape government policy on development and planning in London.
After graduating from Newcastle University in England in 96o.
Farrell received a Master's in urban planning from the University of Pennsylvania, where he met ROBERT VENTURI and DENISE SCOTT BROWN (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") and was taken by their belief in an approach to architecture that embraced both historicism and popular culture.
He returned home to partner with Grimshaw in an architectural practice, but left in i98o when he became disenchanted with the established modernist style.
Opening his own firm he sought to move from designing housing projects to working with clients willing to pursue a more adventurous path.
His earliest success, and one of his most attention-getting commissions, was the TV-am building in London's Camden Town (i982).
In addition to its then radical postmodern design, the building marked another milestone—the inauguration of morning television broadcasting in the United Kingdom.
The impossible-to-miss structure, converted from an industrial garage, became an ideal example of the “double coding" prescribed by Venturi and CHARLES JENCKS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design” and “Living with Postmodern Design").
The two-faced exterior had a distinctive striped, serpentine front and contrasting rear facade topped with a row of eleven huge eggcups as architectural ornament— a Pop art fillip suggesting its link to breakfast-time.
The interior announced its links to historicism in colorful areas that evoked exotic locales such as Japan, Mesopotamia, Egypt, and Africa.
(The building, popularly known as Eggcup House, was recently controversially renovated into far more conventional form.) The furnishings, which Farrell also designed, were restrained iterations of the MEMPHIS (see under "Edited Design") aesthetic.
Two other milestones in his career are icons of modern London and the postmodern style: Embankment Place at Charing Cross station (i990) and the MI6 headquarters (ı994), the latter a familiar image to viewers of espionage films and television shows.
With the opening of a Hong Kong office in ı991, the firm expanded internationally and has completed major projects in several Chinese cities, India, and Australia Farrell has also designed interiors, which are not so much examples of a particular style as improvisations exploring a diversity of moods narratives, and expressions.
They reflect the openness to personal expression that Farrell considers a key legacy of Postmodernism.
He has pointed out that the movement encouraged "the tolerance of different tastes, and the idea that homes, interiors, cities are the product not only of a historical variance over time, but also of personality types and taste types."18 LEFT Rendering of Comyn Ching Triangle, 1985.
BELOW MI6 Building, headquarters of the British Secret Intelligence Service, London, 1994.
TOP Eggcup finials atop the TV-am building, London, 1982 LEFT The atrium of the TV-am studios.
ABOVE Alban Gate (125 London Wall), City of London, 1992. # FRANK GEHRY b.1929 CALIFORNIA ICONOCLAST WHOSE WORK MOVED FROM VERNACULAR TO DECONSTRUCTIVIST.
AND BEYOND.
Though he would not now be thought of as a postmodernist, Frank Gehry's approach to architecture, particularly his early work, justifies his inçlusion in a compendium of that era.
Gehry was also critical of the sameness of International Style Modernism, and his early work suggested vernacular influences similar to those seen in the buildings by ROBERT VENTURI (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design”).'9 His designs have always challenged convention, particularly through the use of quotidian materials corrugated alumnum and chain-link fencing for his Santa Monica home (i978), ordinary cardboard for his Easy Edges furniture, or ColorCore lamnate for his award-winning fish sculptures and lamps.
Even his later architecture, with undulating computer generated forms like those of the Guggenheim Museum Bilbao (ı997) and the D)ISNEY (see under "Edited Design") Concert Hall (2oo3), is sometimes designated postmodern, despite the absence of visible historicist influences.
Ephraim Owen Goldberg was born in Toronto, Canada; the family changed their surname when they emigrated to Los Angeles in i947.
When he became interested in architecture, Frank (the name he adopted in his school years) drove a truck to pay for art classes, and attended the University of Southern California on a scholarship, receiving his degree in 1954.
He worked for the architectural firm Victor Gruen Associates before spending a year in the US Army Special Services, making furniture for soldiers.
Enrolling at Harvard Graduate School of Design in 1956, Gehry studied city planning for a year, but left to return to California.
He worked for Pereira & Luckman, then Gruen Associates, and spent a year in Paris in the office of architect André Remondet.
In 1962 he opened Frank Gehry & Associates (now Gehry Partners) in Los Angeles.
Though his early work was influenced by International-Style precepts, Gehry shared the rebelliousness of the active Los Angeles arts community and was one of the new wave of California architects and designers who challenged establishment ideas in their inventive use of materials and unconventional forms.
His first success was in 1971, not in architecture but with Easy Edges, a collection of furniture assembled from glued layers of corrugated paper.
It upended notions of the materials from which furniture could be made, and was successful enough to merit a reprise collection, Experimental Edges, which debuted in ı979.
Gehry's use of ordinary packing material accorded with CHARLES JENCKS's (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") designation of the ad-hoc strain of Postmodernism.
Gehry's first prominent architectural project was his own home in Santa Monica, a 192os bungalow that he remodeled in i978 (and expanded in i99ı) by encasing it in an exploded structure of rough plywood and corrugated metal, exorcizing what the architect described as the "ghosts of cubism" with a convention-defying design that placed him in the front ranks of the newly designated Deconstructivist movement.
Deconstructivism was considered by many to be an offshoot of Postmodernism's espousal of quotidian materials and rule-breaking forms.
Though eschewing the postmodern use of color and historicist motifs, the house was a clear break with the form-follows-function tenets of orthodox Modernism.
It was followed by other radical projects, including the Winton Guest House (i987) in Owatonna, Minnesota, an assemblage of geometric forms in varied materials that cleariy suggests a postmodern sensibility.
The originality of his work brought Gehry international renown and, in i989, the coveted Pritzker Prize.
His product designs have drawn equal attention, most notably the quirky fish lamps of Formica ColorCore, originally conceived as part of a commission for which FORMICA (see under "Edited Design") invited designers to use the new material.
In the often-reprinted story, Gehry was frustrated in his efforts to come up with something and threw a large piece of ColorCore on the floor, where it shattered into fragments of translucent color, inspiring him to use it as layers of fish scales.²0 Moving from drafting-table to computergenerated imagery, Gehry's work changed completely with the organic forms of the Weisman Art Museum (i99o) in Minneapolis, Minnesota, prefiguring his iconic Guggenheim Museum Bilbao (1997).
The witty Chiat/Day building (1991) in Venice, California, with its famous binocularsculpture entrance by Claes Oldenburg and Coosje van Bruggen, and the so-called Dancing House in Prague (i996) are postmodern in their doublecoded visual messages, but it is the undulating forms and polished titanium panels of his later work that have plačed him at the forefront of the "starchitect” generation.

LEFT Lamp in the form of a coiled rattlesnake, papier mâché and gouache, 1989. # HANS HOLLEIN 1934-2014 VIENNESE ARCHITECT WHOSE BUILDINGS AND FURNITURE USED BOLD FORMS AND UNDECORATED SURFACES.
RIGHT Schullin II jewelry boutique, Vienna, Austria, 1982.
OPPOSITE Retti candle shop, Vienna, 1966.
Austrian architect Hans Hollein was not a member of any postmodernist group, but shared their interest in classicism, though his work also reflects the influence of early Austrian modernists like losef Hoffmann and the Secessionists.
His designs in architecture and furniture are elegant and sophisticated, eschewing the air of casual improvisation that marks much postmodern design.
In that sense, he can be considered as bridging the divide between strict Modernism and its postmodern successor.
Hollein received his early training at the Akademie der bildenden Künste in his home city of Vienna, traveling to the United States to complete his studies at the Illinois Institute of Technology, where he studied with Mies van der Rohe, and the University of California at Berkeley, where he received his MArch in i96o.
He made the most of his time in America, driving around the country in a second-hand car, managing to meet Frank Lloyd Wright and Richard Neutra, and visiting the seven American cities and towns named Vienna.
With the same adventurous spirit, he worked in architecture practices in Australia, Sweden, South America, and Germany before returning home to open his own office in 1964.
His first commission was certainly his smallest: the i2ft- (3.5m-)wide Retti candle shop, on an elegant Vienna shopping street, won him broad recognition for its sophisticated aluminumfronted design.
It won a \$25,ooo award, more than the cost of the entire project.
More commissions followed, and though much of Hollein's work was in Austria, the following two decades also brought commissions for public spaces in Germany and Paris, museums in Egypt and Germany, and theaters and concert facilities in Japan and the United States.
Rejecting the doctrines of functionalism, Hollein was a leader in the rebellion against the existing modernist aesthetic.
In 1965 he led a group of Viennese artists and architects in transforming an established architectural publication into Bau: Magazine for Architecture and Urban Planning.
Taking an interdisciplinary approach to architectural criticism, they incorporated art and politics in a fashion magazine format, with bold graphics and striking images.
Its most influential piece was Hollein's 1968 polemic “Everything Is Architecture," which challenged the restrictions of Modernism, arguing that all human production could be considered architecture, and suggesting the corollary that "all are architects." Though published for only six years, Bau's arresting design and provocative contents, including articles by innovative firms like Coop Himmelb(l)au\* and Haus-Rucker\*, drew attention not generally given to intellectual publications.
Throughout this period, Hollein criticized functionalism in speeches, writings drawings, and projects.
As he told an interviewer in 2oo9, “I always tried to look at architecture through an artistic lens," citing the influence of modern art on postmodern architecture.2' In i976 he was invited by the Cooper-Hewitt, National Design Museum to create the concept for its opening exhibition in New York's Carnegie Mansion.
His idea for “Man transFORMS: Aspects of Design” was to illustrate the process of design rather than its products, in imaginative settings that encouraged viewers to make their own connections with the materials and appreciate the importance of design itself, rather than any particular style.
Among the ten participating architects and designers were ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design” and “Living with Postmodern Design"), ARATA ISOZAKI, and Richard Meier\*.
Hollein's Austrian Travel Bureau (1976) in Vienna is widely cited as an example of postmodern design, but its translucent glass ceiling also references Otto Wagner's ı912 Postal Savings Bank.
In his contribution to the Strada Novissima exhibit at the i98o Venice Architecture Biennale, he adopted a column Adolf Loos had designed in 1922.
These and other projects reflected both Hollein's interest in historicism and his allegiance to his celebrated Viennese predecessors.
His product designs—consistently elegant— combined historical references with postmodern egalitarianism.
Invited to design furniture for MEMPHIS's (see under “Edited Design") first collection, Hollein contributed the Schwarzenberg sideboard, a graceful piece in birds-eye maple veneer, more Biedermeier than bohemian.
The Mitzi (ı98ı) and Marilyn (ı981) sofas, in a sleek mash-up of styles, emit echoes of Deco, Biedermejer, and Pop.
When ALESSI (see under "Edited Design") invited Hollein to participate in the Tea & Coffee Piazza project in i983, he designed a service on a tray shaped like an aircraft carrier.
He also designed gold jewelry and silver tabletop accessories for CLETO MUNARI (see under 'Edited Design’), and a piano for Bösendorfer.
For most of his career, Hollein combined architecture and design with teaching.
He taught at Washington University in St.
Louis during his time in America, and was a professor at the Kunstakademie Düsseldorf from 1967 to i976.
He continued teaching at the Universität für angewandte Kunst, Vienna, while completing several major architectural projects, including OPPOSITE Schullin I jewelry boutique, Vienna, 1974.
BELOW Museum für Moderne Kunst, Frankfurt, Germany, 1991.
RIGHT Haas Haus, Vienna, 1990. the much-published Haas Haus in central Vienna (i99o), the Museum für Moderne Kunst (ı99ı) in Frankfurt, and many more that reflect his elegant interpretation of Postmodernism.
Hans Hollein's work has not gone unrecognized.
In addition to several international honors, he was awarded the prestigious Pritzker Prize in 1985.

LEFT Schwartzenberg table, painted and gilded wood, burled veneer, for Memphis, 1981. # ARATAISOZAKI b.1931 JAPANESE ARCHITECT WHOSEIDIOSYNCRATIC STYLE WAS ROOTEDIN THE CONCEPTS OF METABOLISM, RIGHT Demonstration Robot, Expo '70, Osaka, Japan, 1970.
OPPOSITE Art Tower, Mito, Japan, 1990.
When an architect first gains notice on an international stage with a giant robot, it's a hard act to follow.
Arata Isozaki's Demonstration Robot at lapan's Expo ′7o world's fair was a traffic-stopping gigantic mechanical figure that emitted smoke, smells. light, and sound, and whose base became a stage for evening music performances.
Undaunted by the challenge, Isozaki went on to design dozens of buildings and objects that were equally avant-garde (though not necessarily as entertaining), becoming one of the best-known and most respected of his generation of Japanese architects.
Born in Oita Prefecture, Isozaki graduated in i954 from the University of Tokyo and worked for nine years in the office of Kenzo Tange, the most important architect to emerge from that country in the years after the Second World War.
He became interested in the Metabolists, a group of young architects who were developing grand utopian schemes for ideal cities that would grow and change like human organisms, in futuristic concepts akin to those of Italy's Radical Design groups.
Isozaki, however, moved beyond the utilitarian concerns of the Metabolists to conceive buildings of striking form but no identifable style.
They are a personal interpretation of Modernism that avoids easy characterization, though he is generally considered a postmodernist.
Though his earliest work was rectilinear, Isozaki began to explore the vault form, using semicircular precast concrete for barrel-vaulted structures that recall both Japanese and Grecian precedents.
He adopted historicist forms to suit each specific project, for example combining the pyramid with the barrel vault in the Museum of Contemporary Art, Los Angeles (i986), using proportions based on the Golden Section in a striking but serene structure that reflects both Eastern and Western aesthetics.
The Art Tower Mito (i99o) is a merger of architecture and sculpture, a snake-like twist of triangles inspired by Brancusi.
But Isozaki's most unabashedly postmodern project was the Team Disney building in Orlando, Florida (i99ı), a giant cylinder surrounded by a cluster of vari-colored cubic forms.
Isozaki's ventures into product design reveal a subtle wit, as in the Marilyn chairs (i973), which add Monroe-like curves to a Mackintosh form that he considered too severe,²² and his robot-like Fuji bookcase-cabinets (i98ı) for MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design").
He also designed jewelry for ACME (see under “Edited Design") and CLETO MUNARI (see under “Edited Design") and was invited to participate in ALESSI's (see under "Edited Design") celebrated Tea & Coffee Piazza project, but failed to complete his design on time.
In his architecture, Isozaki seeks to overcome the dichotomy between two cultures, melding the structure and technology of the Western world with the form and sensibility of the East, but without ever repeating himself: "Each time, I try to invent something else."23 His inventiveness has extended to other disciplines, including scenic design and music, and as well as having taught in several universities he is a prolific writer, though most of his books have been published only in Japanese.
