LÉON KRIER b.1946 LUXEMBOURG-BORN ARCHITECT.URBAN PLANNER, AND MOST INFLUENTIAL THEORIST OF NEW URBANISM.
BELOW Poundbury, Dorset, England, 1993.
An architect who has generally preferred writing about and teaching the discipline to practicing it, Léon Krier has spent his career attempting to define and correct the problems raised by contemporary architecture.²4 He is considered the most important theorist of the New Urbanism, an urban planning movement that was officially founded in i993 but had actually begun as early as the i97os, when architects and planners began to focus on the failings of International Style Modernism.
New Urbanism advocates an approach to the development of modern cities that would reject cookie-cutter functionalism, instead reinventing the diverse, walkable coinmunities of the years before the advent of the automobile, Modernism, and suburban sprawl.
In proposing inclusive and accessible architecture, Krier believed that there need be no barrier between high art and popular taste.
As consultant to Charles, Prince of Wales on architectural issues, Krier was responsible for developing the master plan for Poundbury, the traditional-style (and somewhat controversial) English town extension built in i993 with the Prince as its major supporter.
Born in Luxembourg, a modestly sized city of traditional architecture, Krier was nonetheless an early enthusiast of Modernism, though a visit to Le Corbusier's concrete-block Cité Radieuse in Marseilles reportedly alienated him from the style.
By the time he followed his older brother Rob to the University of Stuttgart to study architecture, he had decided that the prevailing style was meaningless, seeking change only for its own sake, and that discarding traditional architectural elements was uncongenial to humans.
Krier left his studies in Stuttgart for London in i968, and worked briefly as an assistant in the office of JAMES STIRLING (at precisely the time when architects were beginning to look back to historicist styles) before returning to academia to teach architecture and town planning while running his private practice.
He contributed a design to the important Strada Novissima project at the 198o Venice Biennale, and his relatively few built architectural works include a tower in the planned community of Seaside\*, Florida, and a town hall in Florida, both historicist forms.
Nevertheless, Krier believes there need be no struggle between traditional design and Modernism, asserting that, "The real struggle ought solely to be between good and bad design."25 Despite preferring theory to practice, Krier has completed buildings in Luxembourg, France, Italy, Spain, Germany, the United States, and the United Kingdom.
He has also designed furniture for Giorgetti that is clearly influenced by traditional styles.
Krier's extensive and influential teaching career has taken place on both sides of the Atlantic, first at the Architectural Association and the Royal College of Art in London and then in America at Princeton, Yale, University of Virginia, and University of Notre Dame.
Through his academic work he has been a considerable influence on urbanists and town planners like Andrés Duany\* and Elizabeth Plater-Zyberk\*.
He has organized many exhibitions and has RIGHT Tower in Seaside, Florida, 1984.
BELOW Interior of Windsor Town Hall, Windsor, Florida, 1999.
BELOW Windsor Town Hall. # CHARLES MOORE 1925-1993 AMERICAN ARCHITECT AND ACADEMIC WHOSE PERSONALITY WAS REFLECTED IN HIS EXTROVERTED DESIGNS.
It is not often that architects are remembered as much for their winning personalities as for their artistic achievements, but Charles Moore (also see under "Living with Postmodern Design") was an exception, lauded as frequently for his good-natured conviviality as for his design skills, which were considerable.
He was both prolific and peripatetic, relocating around the country and establishing new, collaborative practices as he went along.
He left a legacy of successor firms as well as hundreds of buildings, ten books, many awards, and a generation of architects whose careers he nurtured Moore's buildings and the several residences he designed for himself are examples of a stellar creative.talent and a penchant for unpredictability, mingling innovative ideas with history and the vernacular.
He never lost sight of the importance of humor, or as he put it, "the sense of the superficial."26 He concerned himself with the essential questions of architecture: privacy and public space, building with respect for the environment, and the significance of both past and present.
Moore's academic credentials were impeccable.
A Michigan native and preternaturally bright, he entered the University of Michigan at the age of sixteen. receiving his BArch there in 1947.
Moving to San Francisco, he worked in several architectural offices and became a registered architect by the time he was twentyone.
A fellowship from Cranbrook Academy in Michigan gave him the opportunity to travel through Europe to see, sketch, and film historic architecture before taking his first teaching job at the University of Utah.
After serving in Korea in the US Army Corps of Engineers, he earned an MFA and PhD in architecture from Princeton University (1957).
He moved to California to teach at the University of California at Berkeley, then back east to become dean of Yale School of Architecture (where he hired ROBERT VENTURI and DENISE SCOTT BROWN—see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"—to teach), to California again to teach at UCLA, and finally to Texas to become a professor at the University of Texas at Austin.
In each location, he formed new partnerships and continued to practice architecture as a principal of each firm.27 Moore's architecture, even at its most understated, always had an edge; he took familiar elements and put them together in unfamiliar ways, spiced with bold color, lively graphics, and the fillip of materials like aluminum film and neon.
He was also seriously committed to a sense of place.
His design for the Sea Ranch community (i963) in Sonoma County blénded spare geometric forms seamlessly into an ocean-view landscape and became a paradigm for contextual design in resort communities.
His idiosyncratic—and somewhat disorienting— New Haven residence (i969) was published in Playboy magazine, but the Piazza d'Italia (ı978) in New Orleans, Louisiana, is his best-known work, and his favorite.
A fantasy recreation of a Roman ruin, with neon-trimmed arches, parodied classical columns, comic-book colors, and a tiled map of Italy underfoot, it treads lightly on the border between populist and kitschy.
It was intended to be the centerpiece of a downtown urban redevelopment project; the development never happened, but the Piazza became a muchpublished postmodern icon.
Of his many writings, the most influential was “You Have to Pay for the Public Life" (i965), a piece for the Yale journal Perspecta (then edited by ROBERT A.
M.
STERN (also see under “Living with Postmodern Design"), which anticipated the theories of Postmodernism in celebrating the vernacular and proposed Disneyland as an admirable example of monumental architecture and public space.
In thirty-five years of working, teaching, traveling, and collecting admirers (he was often described as the “pied piper of postmodernism"²8), Moore accumulated a personal library of thousands of architectural monographs and exhibition catalogs, travel slides and i6mm films, which are administered by the University of Texas at Austin along with his architectural archive.
The Charles W,.
Moore Foundation maintains his Texas home and promotes his teachings about the importance of environment, landscape, and a sense of place.
Even more important, perhaps, was his concern for the people who would experience his architecture; the architect Billy Tsien, a former student, summed it up in saying that, "making a wonderful place for people drove his work."29 And as Moore said of his architecture, “We try to make it happy."3o RIGHT Moore Ruble Yudell, late entry for Chicago Tribune Tower competition, 198o.
OPPOSITE Piazza d'Italia, downtown New Orleans, 1978–89.
ABOVE Home office area of Moore's residence, New Haven, Connecticut, published in Playboy, 1969.
TOP RIGHT Preview Bank tower, Celebration, Florida, 1983.
RIGHT Slatback chair by Moore/Andersson Architects, wood, 1990. # JOHN OUTRAM b.1934 BRITISH ARCHITECT OF EXUBERANT BUILDINGS THAT BOTH REFLECT AND MOCK HISTORICISM.
The man who, according to the London Sunday Times, became "Britain's most eye-catching architect" didn't originally intend to become one.
The third generation of a military family, John Outram lived in British India until he was twelve years old, and when he was sixteen wrote to his father, "I want to be an engineer and design things that work."3' He changed his mind, however, and did his national service as a pilot, intending to pursue a career in aeronautics.
Though the service gave him the opportunity to visit America and Canada, he found flying insufficiently stimulating, and changed course to study architecture.
He trained in London, at Regent Street Polytechnic (now Westminster University), qualified as an architect in i96o, and worked for architectural offices first in Cyprus where he became attracted by classical forms, and then for public sector and private firms, including classical architect Louis de Soissons Outram opened his London practice in 1973, when his plan to emigrate to Cyprus was cut short by political tensions that ended in Turkey's invasion of the island.
As John Outram Architects became a going concern, he progressed from designing kitchens and bathrooms for relatives to modest commercial buildings and then to more ambitious projects.
The Pumping Station (i988), an exuberant public structure on the Isle of Dogs, London, brought considerable publicity—and awards—for its originality.
The design is a tongue-in-cheek mix of styles that pokes fun at both historicism and the machine aesthetic, with a classical pediment of corrugated iron, columns with polychrome capitals, and a non-functioning turbine as ornament.
Outram went on to win competitions for major projects, including overseas commissions in the United States and the Netherlands.
Outram's work is as much a reflection of his outsize personality and outspoken style as of his allegiance to postmodern principles.
His virtuoso use of color and deft application of classical motifs and ornament make his buildings altogether different from those of his contemporaries; immediately recognizable, they are guaranteed to lift spirits.
In Outram's hands, classical forms are ingeniously manipulated to serve as spacedefiners as well as ornamental motifs.
In one case, he conceived an altogether new capital that he (tongue firmly in cheek) designated the Sixth Order.
In another, for the Judge Institute of Management Studies (i995, renamed Judge Business School) at Cambridge University, he used clusters of columns to construct the Basilican Core and a series of convivial social spaces in what would otherwise have been a cavernous and unwelcoming interior.
Believing that anything he might want to design for his buildings had already been invented, he came up with variations on existing forms that look altogether new.
Handcrafted details are part of any Outram project—he considers the British RIGHT Four-story central space in Duncan Hall, 1997. to be natural craftspeople whose pleasure in making things has been hamstrung by contemporary culture.
His buildings give free expression to such elements, and have a charm that reflects the amiable nature of the architect himself.
Though engaging in practice, Outram was always interested in concept, committing himself to devising a “universal theory of architecture." Over a period of seventeen years he documented his ideas in “44 Lectures' that dissected every aspect of the craft.
His writings, discursive and opinionated, are filled with personality.
Rejecting a label for his architecture, he asserts that Postmodernism was a “spineless title"3² whose undoing was its political unacceptability in seeking discipline and pursuing the past, particularly in the context of the Cold War.
Believing that its principles were too simplistic, Outram has dismissed Postmodernism as “merely incompetent."33 Notwithstanding his criticism of the architectural profession, Outram ranks among its leading practitioners, and was part of the select group chosen to represent the United Kingdom in the 199ı Venice Architecture Biennale, along with Norman Foster, Richard Rogers, and Nicholas Grimshaw.
ABOVE Decorated column in the main hall of Judge Business School, University of Cambridge, England, 1995.
ABOVE RIGHT Central staircase in the main hall, Judge Business School.
RIGHT Capital on a column at Duncan Hall.
RIGHT Pumping Station, Isle of Dogs, London, 1988.
BELOW Judge Business School exterior. # PAOLO PORTOGHESI b.1931 ITALIAN ARCHITECT WHOSE HISTORICIST DESIGNS ARE DISTINCTIVE FOR THEIR INCLINATION TO THE BAROQUE.
BELOW Mosque of Rome, completed in 1994.
OPPOSITE Conference Hall in Termi di Montecatini thermal baths, Italy, 1987.
From the earliest years of his long career as architect, theorist, and educator, Paolo Portoghesi rejected the idea that Modernism could deny the influence of history, a position that put him at odds with the leading figures of Italian architecture, including the influential critics Leonardo Benevolo and Manfredo Tafuri.
According to Portoghesi, Postmodernism was “a refusal, a rupture, a renouncement, much more than a simple change of direction."34 He believed that a focus on tradition could stimulate innovation rather than stifle it, and that an examination of even the most modern works would reveal their foundations in historical architecture.
Portoghesi's interest in organic forms of Modernism, shared with his Rome colleague Bruno Zevi, can be seen in his architecture, which uses sweeping, curved forms for dramatic effect.
As president of the architectural section of the Venice Biennale from 1979 to 1992, Portoghesi was responsible for Postmodernism's public debut: an exhibition at the i98o Biennale called Strada Novissima, for which twenty architects were invited to create building facades along the theme "The Presence of the Past." The participants included luminaries such as MICHAEL GRAVES (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), HANS HOLLEIN, ROBERT A.
M.
STERN, and ROBERT VENTURI and DENISE SCOTT BROWN (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), all of whom designed spaces that were unambiguously classical.
The exhibition was a showcase of virtuoso styles with an underlying message: it stressed the importance of history to modern architecture.
Generating controversy as well as accolades, it brought Portoghesi's ideas, and the new historicist strain of Postmodernism, before an international audience.
Portoghesi had another influential role, as editor of the journal Controspazio from 1969 to 1983, and was dean of architecture at the Politecnico di Milano in the significant years from 1968 to 1978, when modernist dogma came under fire.
His own architectural studies were at the University of Rome, from which he graduated in 1957.
He began teaching there in 196ı and established his Rome-based architectural practice with Vittorio Gigliotti in 1964.
Portoghesi became known as a specialist in classical architecture, particularly inclining toward the Baroque, and his approach to Modernism often reflected the muscular, organic forms of that style.
RIGHT Palazzo buffet plate, porcelain, for Swid Powell, 1988.
BELOW Liuto armchair and sofa, for Poltronova, 1981.
Portoghesi designed a number of objects that may be considered Radical rather than MEMPHIS-influenced (see under “Edited Design") postmodernist, including jewelry for CLETO MUNARI (see under “Edited Design”) and a coffee service for the ALESSI (see under “Edited Design") Tea & Coffee Piazza project.
His most celebrated architectural project is probably the Mosque of Rome (i994), the largest mosque outside the Islamic worid.
The structure incorporates a traditional silhouette in a clearly modern structure that nevertheless evokes a grand classical temple framing a traditional mosque form.
The dramatic interior combines a contemporary framework with traditional elements such as Islamic tilework, and accommodates up to two thousand worshippers.
The interior conference hall he designed in a section of the famed Terme di Montecatini baths in Pistoia (i987) is a soaring. skylight-topped space framed with wooden pillars.
He has also designed private residences, churches, theaters, and business complexes.
BELOW Sterling silver flatware for Cleto Munari, C.
1982.
BOTTOM Sterling silver coffee service, for Alessi Tea & Coffee Piazza project, 1983.

## h ALDO
ROSSI 1931-1997 ITALIAN ARCHITECT WITH A CONSISTENT DESIGN VOCABULARY OF SPARE GEOMETRIC ELEMENTS.
RIGHT Tea and coffee service for Alessi, 1983.
OPPOSITE Ossuary in San Cataldo cemetery, Modena, Italy, 1971.
The best-known Italian architect of his generation, Aldo Rossi was also influential as a designer, artist, writer, teacher, and theoretician, and the leader of one of Italy's most important architectural movements-one that fed into the phenomenon that became Postmodernism, but is barely remembered today.
His distinctive architecture used a spare vocabulary of primal forms drawn from the vernacular structures of his native country.
Designed with little extraneous ornament, his buildings are visually striking and often uncannily evocative Born in Milan, Rossi studied architecture at the Politecnico di Milano, and on graduation in 1959 began to work at the magazine Casabella, continuing to do so after opening his architectural practice the same year.
Italy was then engaged in the challenges of rebuilding a country after the destruction of the Second World War, and a group of young architects, including Rossi, Carlo Aymonino, and Manfredo Tafuri, were concerned that the functionalist, modern buildings then in fashion were not only incompatible with existing older structures but insufficiently connected to either history or modern society.
Neo-rationalism, or La Tendenza, as their movement was called, sought to solve the problem using the existing vocabulary of architectural forms instead of seeking avantgarde, utopian solutions.
These basic forms, developed over history and retained in the collective memory, would respect the existing fabric of the city and relate to those living in it.
Rossi and his colleagues in La Tendenza produced exhibitions, proposals, and theoretical discussions, and were influential as teachers, though they built relatively little during the subsequent two decades.
Rossi's most important book, L’architettura della città, was published in ı966, the same year as Complexity and Contradiction in Architecture by ROBERT VENTURI (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design").
Indeed, he expressed similar ideas to Venturi, focusing on the need to reject functionalism and recapture meaning in architecture, and viewing the city as a dynamic entity that evolved over time and was capable of absorbing change without losing meaning.
To Rossi, architecture was more than something to look at: it could elicit an emotional response.
Rossi's first built project was the Teatro del Mondo, for the 1979 Venice Biennale.
A yellow octagonal tower on a barge that floated off St.
Mark's Square, it was a fanciful and ephemeral reminder of the eighteenth-century Venetian tradition of floating theaters.
After the Biennale, it was rowed across the Adriatic to Dubrovnik, where it remained until 1981.
Probably his best-known project is the austere San Cataldo cemetery (i971) in Modena, which used the limited vocabulary of basic geometric forms that he repeated throughout his career.
Hotel Il Palazzo (ı987) in Fukuoka, Japan, used a similar architectural language, as did his later Bonnefantenmuseum (i995) in Maastricht, Netherlands.
Rossi designed only four private homes, including one in Florida's planned community Seaside\*, and his final project, an elegant residence in suburban Michigan that was completed after his death by members of his Milan office.
The three-story home, of limestone, brick, cedar, and glass, is a sophisticated and mature work that is both classically referential and supremely modern.
Rossi's teaching began early in his career, at the Politecnico di Milano (ı965), continuing later at the University of Venice as well as in appointments in Switzerland, Spain, and the United States.
Rossi's superb draftsmanship led to museum exhibitions that featúred his drawings and color renderings.
His successful product designs included carpets, furniture, and a series of celebrated espresso pots for ALESSI (see under “Edited Design”) that reiterated the forms used in his architecture.
TOP LEFT Complex, enamel brooch for Acme, c.
1985.
CENTER LEFT Teatro del Mondo enamel brooch, C.

1978.
ABOVE Interior of the Bonnefantenmuseum, Maastricht, Netherlands, 1995.
OPPOSITE TOP Bonnefantenmuseum.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM Residence in Seaside, Florida, C.
1985.
His design for that company's Tea & Coffee Piazza project was the bestselling service of the group.
Its pedimented case and conical shapes suggest inspiration from the Wiener Werkstätte; indeed, he had edited a book on Viennese decorative art in the i97os.
In 199o Rossi became the first Italian to receive the Pritzker Prize.
In his embrace of classical elements, and his insistence on viewing the built environment in its historical context, Rossi is considered one of the most important postmodernists.
Inspired by the environment, he once said, "observation is the best architecture school." LEFT Parigi chair, for Unifor, 1989, now produced by Molteni.

M.
STERN b.1939 NEW YORK ARCHITECT WHOSE ELEGANTLY REFINED DESIGNS EVOKE THE CLASSICAL REVIVAL.
RIGHT Rendering for a doorway in the Strada Novissima exhibition, Venice Biennale, 1980.
In Robert Stern's (also see under “Living with Postmodern Design") interpretations, Postmodernism is urbanized and sophisticated, bearing the classical hallmarks of the style but eschewing the extravagances that made it either irresistible or anathema to other architects.
Save for the occasional diversion, his restrained approach makes postmodern works by this architect more closely allied to classical revival, prefiguring his recent New York apartment buildings, which meld past and present with suave translations evoking the grandeur of prewar Park Avenue homes.
Though defnitely a postmodernist, and respectful of the style, Stern viewed it as a logical continuation of Modernism rather than a complete break from it, recognizing that admiring historic styles need not call for slavishı copying or precise revivals, but judicious adaptation of appropriate elements.
In his view, Postmodernism “looks back to history to see how things were done and to remind itself that many good ways of doing things which were cast aside for ideological reasons can be usefully rediscovered."35 Stern was born in New York, attending Columbia University as a fine arts major, and received his architecture degree from Yale University in 1965.
He worked briefly in the office of Richard Meier\* and in city planning before opening an architectural office with a partner in ı969, and his own practice in ı977.
His firm was prominent enough by i98o to be invited to contribute to the Strada Novissima project organized by PAOLO PORTOGHESI for the Venice Biennale, for which he designed a striking doorway that recalled the majesty of a grand classical temple.
Stern's residential work often combines classical elements with suggestions of traditional shingle-style Victorian homes.
In a New Jersey residence from i982, a spectacular indoor swimming pool area is glamorized with Deco-Grecian stairs, columns evoking the palm trees in the Brighton Pavilion, and a caryatid-decorated freplace.
In other projects, he combined elements drawn from Stanford White and Josef Hoffmann with a postmodern vocabulary.
In a showroom for Shaw-Walker (1982), he tamped down Postmodernism's flamboyance for the traditional office furniture producer, creating a dignified but stylish environment with a temple-like entrance, marble floors, and copper-colored torchères.
In chameleon fashion Stern could also be suitably exhibitionistic: for DISNEY (see under "Edited Design"), he designed a hotel at Walt Disney World, Florida, that recalled a Victorian beach resort, and a colorful Casting Center said to be inspired by the Palazzo Ducale in Venice, but with witty, Disney-derived iconography.
ABOVE Walt Disney World Casting Center, Lake Buena Vista, Florida, 1989.
ABOVE RIGHT Marblefloored lobby in a Boston office building, 1991.
Stern's ventures into product design have included silverware fór ALESSI (see under "Edited Design"), tableware for SWID POWELL (see essay under “Edited Design"), jewelry for ACME (see under “Edited Design"), and a rug for BD Ediciones\*.
He has also designed furniture, bedding, lighting, and showroom interiors.
His firm, known as RAMSA, now employs several hundred people and works on projects all over the world.
Stern has combined architecture with teaching, heading the Historic Preservation program at the Columbia Graduate School of Architecture, Planning and Preservation (1984–88), and from 1998 to 2016 was dean of Yale School of Architecture.
In i986 he hosted the television series Pride of Place, and has written or edited more than two dozen books, including (with others) the monumental series New York 1900, 1930, 1960, and 2000.

TOP LEFT Mixed-use building in the Back Bay area of Boston, Massachusetts, 1991. # JAMES STIRLING 1926-1992 GLASGOW-BORN, LONDON-BASED ARCHITECT WHOSE ICONIC WORKS MADE DECORATIVE USE OF BRICK AND CONCRETE.
RIGHT Interior of Clore Gallery wing, Tate Britain, London, completed 1987.
OPPOSITE Detail of Clore Gallery facade.
Among those structures cited as the “first" example of postmodern architecture is the Engineering Building at the University of Leicester (i963), a cluster of brick-faced geometric solids that pre-dates even the iconic Vanna Venturi House.
Though visually more akin to Brutalist structures than postmodernist ones, it reveals a commitment to move beyond the limits of International Style on the part of James Stirling, who designed it with his then partner James Gowan.
One of the most forward-thinking British architects of the postwar years, Stirling resisted being labeled a postmodernist, rejecting the style's undue focus on surface.
In an impressive career marked by major public and institutional commissions, he was responsible for some of the United Kingdom's most significant late twentieth-century works.
Though many of them incorporated elements of the postmodern vocabulary, he remained uncommitted to any specific style.
Despite his significance, Stirling's name is far less known than those of many of his contemporaries Stirling was born in Glasgow, Scotland, and interrupted his schooling to serve in the armed forces during the Second World War, returning to study architecture at the University of Liverpool (95o). where he was schooled in the traditional Beaux Arts aesthetic.
Moving to London in 1956, he began a successful partnership,with Gowan that lasted seven years and was influential in reawakening interest in concrete and brick as modern building materials.
During the next eight years Stirling worked independently, producing muscular, Brutalist-influenced structures in concrete and terracotta tile, including buildings at Cambridge and Oxford that were critically praised despite, in the latter case, delays and problems in construction.
After that, Stirling did little work in the United Kingdom until 1971, when he formed a partnership with Michael Wilford\*, who had worked with him since 1956, and began to win commissions for major projects both there and abroad.
The pair's most celebrated work, generally credited to Stirling since it was commissioned and begun before the partnership was formed, is undoubtedly the Neue Staatsgalerie (i984) in Stuttgart, Germany, which was lauded by Emilio Ambasz as a beautifully crafted work at “the exalted level of memorable architecture," as well as “a work full of parodies,"36 noting its references to Postmodernism, high-tech, and neoclassicism.
In two decades, the firm James Stirling Michael Wilford produced an impressive body of work that included major museums, galleries, libraries, and theaters.
The most admired among them include the Arthur M.
Sackler Museum at Harvard University (i985) and the Clore Gallery (i987) addition to London's Tate Britain, which adopted symbolic elements drawn from the classical vocabulary.
Stirling's last completed work, the office building No i Poultry (designed 1985, completed i997, and in 2o16 became the youngest Grade Il\* listed building in the country), is most distinctive for its trapezoidal shape, encased in pink-and-yellow striped limestone.
Revealing a postmodern penchant for color, it reprises the multihued exterior of the Neue Staatsgalerie.
Stirling taught for many years at Yale University and at several schools in Europe.
In ı99ı he was awarded the Pritzker Prize, and after his death the United Kingdom's most prestigious annual architecture award was renamed for him, In 2oi, two exhibitions at Yale—one in the School of Architecture and one at the Yale Center for British Art—featured lecture notes, models, and drawings from his archive, and traveled to London, Stuttgart, and Montreal, opening a window onto his working methodology and igniting interest in an exceptional talent.
BELOW The curving facade of Stirling and Wilford's Neue Staatsgalerie, Stuttgart, Germany, 1984 BELOW Arthur M.
Sackler Museum at Harvard University, Cambridge, Massachusetts, 1985.
RIGHT No i Poultry, London, designed 1988 but built in 1997.
ABOVE Southgate Estate, Runcorn, Cheshire, England, built 1977, demolished 1989.
ABOVE Engineering Building, University of Leicester, England, 1963. # TOMÁS TAVEIRA b.1938 VERSATILE PORTUGUESE ARCHITECT WHO MOVES EASILY FROM MONUMENTAL BUILDINGS TO WHIMSICAL OBJECTS.
RIGHT Interior of Torres das Amoreiras, Lisbon, Portugal, 1986.
Sometimes described as the enfant terrible of Portuguese architecture, Tomás Taveira has enjoyed a career combining local notoriety with international renown.
As a major star in a country not generally known for innovative design, he has designed buildings that changed the landscape of a city, and witty collections of objects that reveal his whimsical side.
Taveira was born in Lisbon to a poor family, and at the age of seventeen began working as a junior assistant in the architecture office of Nuno Teotónio Pereira, where his drawing talent was noticed and nurtured, and he enrolled in the Universidade Técnica de Lisboa to study architecture.
He worked with the firm of Francisco da Conceição Silva before establishing his own office, and later traveled to America, receiving a graduate degree in urban planning and design from Massachusetts Institute of Technology in 1978.
His sometimes flamboyant architectural style was not readily accepted in Portugal, a country once restrained by dictatorship and coming late to modernization.
Taveira's designs were a challenge not only to the bland Modernism of the time, but to a population unaccustomed to color and ornament.
Impossible to overlook his bold postmodern buildings brought him both celebrity and criticism.
At a time when Portugal had just joined the EEC and Lisbon was barely beginning to modernize, Taveira's Torres das Amoreiras (i986) was unlike anything the city had ever seen, a multi-tower complex of offices, housing, and shopping center in a city unaccustomed to grandscale architecture, not to mention anything so overtly decorative.
Opened to great fanfare, the lavish project of aluminum, glass, marble, and concrete was a catalyst that transformed an area formerly used for parking buses into a thriving cosmopolitan center in downtown Lisbon.
The shopping mall, a lavish facility of more than 430,000 sq. ft (40,000 sq. m) framed by three huge towers, has become a major attraction.
Nevertheless, Taveira remained enamored of his native culture, and was not averse to including references to it in his work; a major development project for BNU bank (i989) produced a cluster of imposing office buildings with a bicolor facade and arch-topped silhouettes suggesting a supersize Portuguese guitar.
Other buildings, in a variety of forms, use sherbet-bright color and lively, patterned tiles that reflect his Mediterranean heritage as well as a postmodern aesthetic.
Reflecting their shared sensibility, ALESSANDRO MENDINI (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") wrote the introduction to a book on Taveira's work.37 Taveira's collection of decorative pieces, which he called Transfigured Objects, is a totally unexpected change of pace, evoking traditional Portuguese crafts but with a personal touch and considerable wit.
It includes striking cabinets, wooden chairs with saw-tooth backs and psychedelic patterns, and a series of traditional ceramic pieces that he transformed into exotic, polychromatic works of art.
With his provocative personality and maverick style, Taveira has seen many of his most original proposals remain unbuilt, but his idiosyncratic approach has attracted like-minded clients in many countries, and he has worked in locales as wideranging as the Middle East, East Asia, South America, and Africa, as wel as his native country, on projects ranging from office buildings and urban complexes to airports, stadiums, hotels, and hospitals.
He was also able to indulge a lifelong interest in film direction by working on television and concert set designs, and in Lisbon he designed three important stadiums for the 2004 Summer Olympics.
ABOVE The Torres das Amoreiras complex incorporates a shopping center, offices, and housing.
ABOVE Rendering of the BNU bank, c.
1985.
RIGHT Inside the BNU.
OPPOSITE The BNU bank, Lisbon, Portugal, 1989, combines concrete and glass with pink marble and green Viana. # STANLEY TIGERMAN b.1930 OUTSPOKEN CHICAGO ARCHITECT WHOSE UNCONVENTIONAL DESIGNS USE VISUAL PUNS AND ALLEGORY.
BELOW Vernacular-style weekend home clad in corrugated steel, Lakeside, Michigan, 1983.
Known for a propensity to speak his mind, Stanley Tigerman is equally known for conventionchallenging architecture, despite his continued admiration for the pure aesthetic of Mies van der Rohe.38 The contrary streak surfaced early in his career, when he was one of the Chicago Seven\* architects, an anti-establishment group that lobbied for greater diversity in Chicago architecture.
Though almost all his work has been in and around that city, where he was born, his reputation is international.
After a year at Massachusetts Institute of Technology, Tigerman worked for several Chicago firms and served in the United States Navy before returning to school to complete his architectural studies at Yale, receiving both a BArch and MArch.
He opened his office after graduating in 1961.
Resisting any attempts at self-promotion—"I see architecture as an ethical pursuit"39—he developed the practice gradually.
In i986 it became Tigerman McCurry Architects, in partnership with his wife, Margaret McCurry (the two work independently on most projects).
Unlike most architectural offices, the firm operates as a teaching environment as well as a practice, encouraging both individuality and collaboration among its employees.
In a city steeped in architectural heritage both classical and contemporary, Tigerman's work makes its own statement; his thought-provoking buildings almost always meld classic modernist themes with elements of high-tech and the postmodern vocabulary.
Tigerman's buildings are generally based on geometric solids, with little ornament to dilute the strength of the forms, and he is particularly fond of the grid, which reappears as both ornament and structural element in his buildings and interiors; he used it to dramatic effect in 1986 in a showroom for FORMICA (see under "Edited Design"), highlighting the firm's ColorCore laminate in two interlocking, three-dimensional grids that create a labyrinth effect.4º He is fond of allegory and humor, giving projects titles like Kosher Kitchen for a Jewish-American Princess (1978) or Dante's Bathroom Addition (198o) in a 2o12 exhibition of his work.
Some of his puns are visual; his coffee service for Alessi has hands gripping the tray in place of conventional handles, and a chair for Herman Miller has a footrest that suggests a stuck-out tongue.
He also designed several tabletop pieces for SWID POWELL (see essay under "Edited Design").
Although much of Tigerman's product design has this subtext of playfulness, his penchant for satire emerges unfiltered in his "architoons," imaginative sketches that poke fun at architectural icons and that have been widely published.
Disliking the constriction of most architectural study programs, in 1994 Tigerman co-founded Archeworks, a multidisciplinary, graduate-level design school that functions more as laboratory than classroom.
He has also written seven books, whose playful titles belie their thoughtful content, including The Architecture of Exile (i988), Designing Bridges to Burn (2oı), and Schlepping through Ambivalence (2o1).
ABOVE LEFT View from the balcony level.
LEFT Paired sleeping lofts overlook the central space.
ABOVE Clerestory windows maximize light in the doubleheight space.
His considerable reputation has come from his provocative architecture rather than aggressive promotion, which he stubbornly resists: "I still see architecture as the high road." Though he does not object to being included in a book about Postmodernism, he rejects the idea of a signature style: "It was more interesting to work within the context of, 'Everything is a tabula rasa.' A fresh slate every time."4' ABOVE Rendering of the design for a row house in the Tegel suburb of Berlin, Germany, C.
1980.

BELOW Silver coffee service for Alessi, 1983. # OSCAR TUSQUETS BLANCA b.1941 SPANISH ARTIST, DESIGNER, AND ARCHITECT WHOSE HIGHLY ORIGINAL WORK IS INSPIRED BY BOTH HISTORICISM AND LANDSCAPE.
A rare Spanish practitioner of a style largely dominated by Italians, architect Oscar Tusquets Blanca also stands out for the exceptional variety of his pursuits.
In a profession of multitaskers, he has performed more tasks than many of his contemporaries, all with considerable success.
Avoiding the modern inclination to specialize, he has been quoted as considering himself an architect by training, a designer by adaptation, a painter by vocation, and a writer through the desire to make friends Tusquets Blanca studied art in Barcelona, graduating from the Escuela Tècnica Superior d’Arquitectura in 1965, and with four colleagues (Pep Bonet, Cristian Cirici, Lluís Clotet, and Mireia Riera) formed the design and architectural practice Studio Per.
Studio Per's architectural projects included elegant one-family villas as well as larger public buildings.
Seven years later, the group organized the company BD Ediciones de Diseño\*, which produces and markets furniture and objects by the founders and other,contemporary designers, as well as reissuing works by Antoni Gaudí and Charles Rennie Mackintosh.
In i972 Tusquets Blanca, in collaboration with Clotet, designed one of the earliest postmodern houses in Spain, Casa Regàs, a freeform structure built around a central pool with an irregular plan—what the architects called "harmonious disorder." It was considered postmodern in its bold departure from rectilinear form and machine-for-living functionalism.
That same year, Tusquets Blanca published a Spanish edition of Learning from Las Vegas, which certainly would have influenced his own design.
Instead of following any specific dicta, however, his primary concern irì architectural planning is appropriateness to each particular site; he appears more inspired by landscape than by design concepts or even by the historicism reflected in much of his work.
Studio Per became known for its unconventional approach, in one example producing an urban dwelling that managed to incorporate apartments of several sizes and shapes by devising an unbalanced exterior with vari-sized, misaligned windows and irregular projections.
Many of Tusquets Blanca's own architectural projects are far more elegant, often using brick or terracotta and drawing on classical precedent in a refined use of columns, pediments, and arches.
TOP Casa Regàs, Barcelona, 1972.
OPPOSITE Villa Andrea, Barcelona, Spain, the architect's own home and office, 1991.
ABOVE Belvedere Georgina, adjoining the Casa Regàs.
Over the years, Tusquets Blanca has designed furniture and objects that have won awards and museum accolades.
When commissioned to design a wing for the Dalí Theatre and Museum in Figueres, Catalonia (i974), he became friends with the artist, whose Surrealist approach appealed to his fanciful instincts.
His Dalilips sofa was designed in collaboration with the artist for the Mae West room at the museum, though the design did not go into production until thirty years later, when a new polyethylene molding process enabled the soft texture the designers wanted for the piece.
In a more serious vein, Tusquets Blanca drew on another predecessor countryman for inspiration for the Gaulino chair (i987), an homage to Antonio Gaudí that also evokes the forms of Italian modernist Carlo Mollino.
TOP Pavillon Tusquets, Parc de la Villette, Paris, 1990.
ABOVE Entryway to the Mas Abelló housing complex, Reus, Tarragona, Spain, 1988.
Tusquets Blanca was one of the architects asked to design a building for the Parc de la Villette in Paris, and contributed a gray and white marble pavilion with three closed volumes connected by two greenhouses.
Its monumental entrances opened into inviting interiors with wood-beamed ceilings and it is an elegant counterpoint to other structures in the park; unfortunately, it has been poorly maintained.
Although not a member of or contributor to MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design"), he was not unnoticed in Italy, and was one of the eleven designers ALESSI (see under “Edited Design") commissioned for the Tea & Coffee Piazza project in i983.
In contrast to the bold, geometric lines in many of the other designs, his service is shaped in sensual curves, relating to the forms used in his furniture.
Later in his career, Tusquets Blanca's excursions into authorship have produced several books of essays with intriguing titles, such as Más que discutible (More than Disputable, 1994) and Todo es comparable (Everything is Comparable, 2006).

## 开界A
JAMES WINES b.1932 AMERICAN SCULPTOR, ARCHITECT AND THEORIST WHOSE WORK MELDS ARCHITECTURE AND ENVIRONMENT.
Like Frank Lloyd Wright, Le Corbusier, and Mies van der Rohe, James Wines is not a registered architect, though he has been designing or working on buildings for most of his life.4² His highly original and provocative designs have made him internationally known as a leading practitioner and chief interpreter of the movement called Deconstructivism, as well as a creator of intriguing urban spaces that defy categorization.
Before practicing architecture, Wines was an artist and sculptor, a predisposition that is reflected in his architecture and environmental projects.
He was born in Chicago, and attended Syracuse University School of Art, where he majored in art history and sculpture, graduating in 1956.
Winning a Prix de Rome the following year, he traveled to Italy and lived there until 1962, making semi-abstract bronze sculptures These won him an exhibition at the Baltimore Museum of Art in 1958, and his work was recognized again with a Guggenheim Fellowship in i962.
As his sculpture became larger and more geometric, Wines sought to create art that could be part of the urban environment instead of simply displayed in galleries.
To do this, he founded SITE (Sculpture in the Environment) in i97o, a collaboration of architects, artists, and technicians that describes itself as “an architecture and environmental arts studio."43 The firm has evolved into an international practice whose commissions have included public spaces, environmental artworks, landscape designs, as well as interiors and products.
Wines's approach to designing each building has been to view it in a specific cntext—not as an object in the environment, but an object that becomes the environment.
He believes that, rather than being separate disciplines, architecture and art are parts of an integrated whole.
Of all SITE's work in many countries, the firm's buildings for Best Products are most widely known, and have become legendary archetypes of Deconstructivism.
When SITE was commissioned to create new facades for nine undistinguished structures that housed Best's home goods stores, Wines's ingenious solution was to transform the storefronts into witty and provocative threedimensional works of art that mocked their banal, big-box forms.
Some appear to bc unfinished, broken, or distorted, such as the crumbling-walled Indeterminate Facade (1975), the broken-corner Notch Building (1979), and the bizarre Peeling Building (i972).
Other attention-grabbing devices included a ruined facade faced with a forest of trees, and a broken wall that revealed an ersatz interior display.
The designs drew praise from some, derision from others, and media attention everywhere,44 establishing Wines's reputation as an ingenious and provocative thinker.
In i98ı SITE was featured—along with FRANK GEHRY and OPPOSITE Highway 86, made for Expo 86, Vancouver, Canada, 1986.
STANLEY TIGERMAN—as the subject of a book on unconventional, innovative American architects.45 Wines and SITE became known for developing new concepts for cities, like the Highrise of Homes, a i98ı theoretical project that stacked up houses of diverse architectural styles as a vertical community in a steel and concrete structure.
For Expo 86, a transportation-themed world's fair in Vancouver in i986, he conceived: a 7ı2ft- (2ı7m-)long undulating concrete highway into which were set more than two hundred assorted, gray-painted vehicles.
It was one of the most-noted attractions at the fair.
SITE projects over the years have included a number of other expositions, as well as residences and public buildings, many incorporating plants or trees, in keeping with Wines's commitment to green design, In i987 Wines generated conversation and controversy with his lively book De-Architecture (the title a play on Vitruvius's De architectura), which placed SITE's work in historical context and explained his view of architecture as “the only art form that is by nature and neceśsity public."46 He sought a rethinking of the practice of architecture and its failure to communicate—an idea that resonates with postmodernist thinking.
Throughout his career, Wines has taught art, architecture, and environmental design at the School of Visual Arts in New York, at New York University, and Penn State University, and his seven books include Green Architecture (2ooo).
Wines has won dozens of art and architecture awards, and his projects for SITE have been featured in retrospective exhibitions in the United States, Europe, and Japan.
He sees his approach as an alternate view of Postmodernism and believes that SITE has a place in the movement because of its “fusion of art, architecture and context as a basic philosophical premise." He believes that "the 'freeing up' aspect of Postmodernism has been its most productive legacy."47 ABOVE Isuzu Space Station-Children's Plaza, Sakuragicho train station, Yokohama, Japan, 1989.
LEFT Peeling Building Best store.

OPPOSITE Wendy Maruyama, Mickey Mackintosh chair, Zolatone on wood, 1981.
By the early i98os the architectural world was engaged in heated discussions about the pros and cons of the new style, as exemplifed by the early postmodern build ings, with their historicist forms and quirky, classically informed ornament.
But for most people, and even many design professionals, Postmodernism could be summed up in a single word: MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design").
Although Postmodernism's boldest architectural incarnations had drawn attention, the high-profile debut of the Italian collaborative at the 198ı Salone del Mobile in Milan brought a storm of unprecedented media coverage.
With its use of inexpensive material, strident colors, and zingy patterns, Memphis designs were nothing like that already on the market, or in anyone's homes.
In making expensive furniture and objects from inexpensive materials, Memphis challenged preconceptions about luxury products.
And unlike the conceptual designs of the Italian Radicals, its products were for sale.
For the first time, furniture and product design were as newsworthy as couture fashion, and sometimes as difficult to put to actual use—the extreme designs were not readily compatible with other styles.
But high prices and limited production made Memphis inaccessible to most of the general public, even had they wanted to own it.' Although Memphis was the most high-profile and prolific source of postmodern design, it was by no means the only one.
Designers and producers in a few other countries introduced limited-edition experimental designs, though none made much impact in the marketplace.
In America, several major architects had designed built-in or custom furniture to furnish the postmodern spaces they had designed, but the new style was inevitably acknowledged by the furniture industry, and adapted for a broader consumer market.
Cautiously responding to what they saw as a coming demand for postmodern furniture, some manufacturers took the most obvious course: hiring architects to design it for them.
In the vanguard was Knoll\*, which commissioned ROBERT VENTURI (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design' and “Living with Postmodern Design") in i979—even before the Memphis explosion—to design a collec tion of chairs.
Introduced in i984, the series of nine pieces was a tongue-in-cheek riff on classical-period styles, with chair backs cut into Queen Anne, Sheraton, Hepplewhite, Chippendale, Empire, Biedermeier, Gothic Revival, Art Nouveau, and Art Deco silhouettes, screened with appropriate colors and occasional ornament.
MICHAEL GRAVES (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") designed Biedermeier-inspired furniture for Canadian producer Sunar\*, and later a popularly priced collection for Drexel; and Gunlocke commissioned furniture craftsman Wendell Castle\* to create limited-edition designs, while other manufacturers such as Niedermeyer and Thayer Coggin introduced pieces that modified the postmodern look for more conservative American tastes.
In short order, much of the industry jumped on the bandwagon.
Established Italian producers including Cassina, Driade\*, Giorgetti, and Sawaya & Moroni\* added pieces by high-profile postmodernist architects to their collections.
Dubbed neomodern by the media,² they were clearly Memphis-influenced, using primary colors, pattern, and a provocative mix of materials.
Design galleries like Néotù and Xo in Paris, Aram in London, and Art et Industrie\* and Max Protetch Gallery\* in New York staged exhibitions of limited-edition or one-off works by artists, designers, and architects, which were linked to Postmodernism primarily in their rejection of conventional forms and materials: few adopted the historicist imagery used by the leading postmodern architects.
The new design aesthetic was interpreted somewhat differently across countries: Italian Postmodernism remained cheeky and subversive, American and British translations used classical themes and ornament drawn from historical styles, while the Japanese were more disposed toward futuristic concepts.
And some designers, like MARIO BOTTA (see under “Architecture"), SHIRO KURAMATA, and PHILIPPE STARCK, had feet in both camps.
In rejecting the strictures of established Modernism, however, both Memphis and the more historicist designs evolved from the same roots.
Meanwhile, young designers in London were experimenting with cast-off objects and industrial materials for furniture they assembled by hand, with results such as Ron Arad's Rover chair, made from a discarded automobile seat, and Danny Lane's Etruscan Chair of broken-edged glass, suggesting an archeological ruin.
Others engaging in transgressive explorations included Tom Dixon, André Dubreuil, and Marc Newson, whose Lockheed Lounge is postmodern in its references to both the form of a neoclassical chaise longue and the exterior of a i96os aircraft.
These designers did not necessarily pursue a postmodern BELOW Michael Graves, dining table for Drexel Furniture, wood and glass, 1989. aesthetic, but certainly shared the attitude of rebellion against establishment standards.
In Germany, the Neue Deutsche Welle (New German Wave) of the late 197os and early ı98os awakened a parallel interest in the social possibilities of design, as a new generation challenged establishment ideas and the dominance of Modernism.
A group of designers in Berlin and Cologne, though influenced by their Italian counterparts, forged their own creative path to Postmodernism, with minimalist, rectilinear forms that were visually arresting but refreshingly unpretentious, using contrasting tone and texture and unexpected embellishments for decorative interest.
These experimenters in furniture and industrial design worked with detritus and demolished materials like cardboard, glass, and perforated metal.
In i984 at the Cologne and Milan furniture fairs, Hanover-based Anthologie Quartett\* introduced a collection that included designs by BÖREK ŠíPEK (also see under “Living with Postmodern Design"), ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design"” and "Living with Postmodern Design"), and MATTEO THUN.
In i986, Akaba in Spain produced avant-garde designs by the likes of JAVIER MARISCAL, Santiago Miranda, and Santiago Calatrava, while in Japan the avant-garde architects Kisho Kurokawa and ARATA ISOZAKI (see under "Architecture") designed furniture as well as buildings.
In Prague, meanwhile, a Memphis-like cohort explored the use of traditional materials in non-industrial production.3 As some of the best (and costliest) postmodern objects became status symbols for the design elite, many American producers, anxious to keep pace with fashion, introduced designs that were more wannabes than true examples of the style.
Translated for mass-production, most of these pieces were pastiches that merely grafted classical columns and other elements onto conventional forms, decorated with postmodern motifs and colors.
The results were more surface than substance, muting the look of the original designs # For the first time, furniture and product design were as newsworthy as couture fashion, and sometimes as difficult to put to actual use. and compromising the postmodernists' disregard for functionality; making a point was one thing, but making furniture that could sell was something else altogether.
After the original Memphis group disbanded, some of its supporters tried to revive the movement with Meta Memphis\*, presenting in i99ı a collection of conceptual pieces by artists including Franz West\*, Alighiero Boetti, Sol LeWitt, Mimmo Paladino, and Sandro Chia.
These artist-as-designer efforts made little impact on the collective consciousness. however.
And as part of a craft revival that had begun in the i97os many artisans made visual references to Postmodernism without necessarily having any allegiance to its underlying concepts.
Working on one-off or limited-edition pieces, craftspeople could explore new ideas without worrying about how many they could sell.
And they could always do something different the next time around. # New German Design: Brief History, Big Impact Hans Maier-Aichen German design has long been linked with a highly functional aesthetic and a focus on workmanship.
The phrase "Made in Germany" brings to mind concepts such as robust technology, high-quality engineering. ingenuity, and reliability.
Institutions such as the Deutscher Werkbund, the Bauhaus, and, for a brief period, the Hochschule für Gestaltung Ulm came to symbolize functionality, ergonomics, and usability, as well as a socially and economically informed perspective, and had a lasting impact on German design.
For decades, German industrial design was thought to possess the recipe for surefire market success.
Nevertheless, in the early i98os the influence of eclectic postmodern and neomodern movements, such as those that gave rise to the MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") group in Italy, was increasingly being felt in Germany as well.
By 1983, design theorists Rolf-Peter Baacke, Uta Brandes, and Michael Erlhoff were announcing its arrival, seen in a veritable boom in design as well as an overthrowing of the doctrine of functionalism.
For a brief period, "New German Design' attracted attention on the international stage.
A generation of young designers—some of whom were still students—began to rebel against the prevailing ideology of academic design and in its place posited a supposedly new kind of anti-functionalism.
This uprising against the functionalist ideology of German industry and conventional product design was heavily influenced by leftist ideas.
In this regard we should not forget the effects of the widespread student unrest across Europe in i968, which also exerted a political influence on the younger generation of designers.
These new designers believed that function should no longer be the sole driving force of furniture design: it should also perplex or provoke feelings in the user, or convey a message.
As well as individual designers, several design groups emerged in this period, as did design galleries and artists' cooperatives that organized the manufacture of their own products, exhibited them in their own spaces, and sold them directly to the public.
These designers were using communal action as a strategy, and creativity and experimentation, with all their potential risks and shortcomings, played a key role.
An important element of this was what is known as “naive testing," according to which play, chance, and tinkering were accepted methods.
In response to the success of the design establishment and its mass production mentality, these groups developed a range of formal, sensory, Brutalist, and socially and politically motivated alternatives.
While some of the New German Designers succumbed to the fascination of Italy's Memphis group, or were at least strongly influenced by its bold patterns and brightly colored surfaces, a larger proportion of these avant-garde designers, or "author-designers," took a much stricter approach, in the form of a radical detachment from all forms of classicism and an irreverent attitude toward established ideologies.
At least for a time, a true break from the design history of the twentieth century seemed to be taking place in Germany.
ABOVE Ginbande (Uwe Fischer and Klaus-Achim Heine), Tabula Rasa, wood and steel, 1987.
OPPOSITE Heinz Landes, Solid chair, steel wire and wood, 1986.
The Bellefast group aspired to occupy an autonomous position within New German Design.
In this self-proclaimed "workshop for experimen tal design," the designers worked in tandem with craftspeople to define and control the entire development process of an object.
Bellefast created one-offs and limited editions that, though based on relatively simple themes, were elevated through the use of experimental design methodologies.
The overriding goal during this period was to seek out the freedom to explore new ideas, unencumbered by those that already existed.
In autumn i98o, Gerhard Fischer and Harald Hullmann, former members of the Dreistädter group, formed the Düsseldorf-based Kunstflug along with industrial designers Heiko Bartels and Charly Hüskes.
A ı983 exhibition at the Treppengalerie in Krefeld entitled “Kunstflug— Neues Deutsches Design" gave the New German Design movement its name.
Most Kunstflug designs were intended to represent a complete departure from conventional industrial design.
The group developed a distinctive ecologically oriented aesthetic, which contrasted with the work of Bellefast and its use of exposed Brutalist materials.
Another innovation of Kunstflug was to challenge the sociocultural patterns of behavior relating to the object; their Kaffeebaum (Coffee Tree), for example, is best suited to informal meetings over coffee in a standing position, and so became a potential site for alternative forms of interaction and communication.
In 1985 the Frankfurt-based designers Uwe Fischer and Klaus-Achim Heine founded Ginbande.
The result was a new kind of furniture that was both simple and technically challenging; though both designers declared their commitment to a form of classical Modernism, critics viewed their multifunctional works as belonging within the context of New German Design.
Indeed, their Tabula Rasa pull-out table and benches, which despite its technical complexity remains a simple and discreet piece of furniture, anticipated the New Simplicity movement that followed.
ABOVE Volker Albus, Wiener Blut chandelier, sheet steel, 1987.
At around the same time, a group of architects came together to work as designers, first under the name LUX Neonlicht and later as Möbel Perdu.
They provoked the public with bizarre furniture designs that not only resisted the ideas of good form and taste but also contrasted strongly with the smooth aesthetics of the Italian avant-garde.
Their objects were based around a kind of aesthetics of ugliness and adulteration through which they hoped to overcome the taboos of classical design and break free of its traditions, blurring the borders between architecture, art, and design.
Some individuals are notable for the fact that they did not join these ideological group projects, or quickly left them to pursue their own interests: Volker Albus\*, a designer and author of some of the most important texts on the New German Design movement; Herbert Jakob Weinand\*, a curator and gallery owner; the architect Heinz Landes, with his cantilevered Solid chair; and Wolfgang Laubersheimer\*, whose braced steel shelves are one of the few designs to still survive from this shortlived wave of protest.
Weinand, with a gallery in Berlin, primarily served as an organizational and inspirational force, raising the profile of solo shows and serving as an intermediary between designers and clients who were interested in acquiring avant-garde furniture.
In this respect, the concept of the gallery clearly stood out as a place that permitted the emergence of gut response and marginal action.
Weinand even lined the facade of his gallery with reinforced glass peppered with holes, as though from a bank after a robbery, like a testimony to the violence within.
In i984 the English designer Jasper Morrison\* came to Berlin on a DAAD scholarship.
His pieces, such as Flower-Pot Table (i984) or Directional Light Source with Small Table (i984), count among the most striking examples in the field.
With Andreas Brandolini and Joachim Stanitzek he became a member of Bellefast and co-designed the student project “Kaufhaus des Ostens" (Department Store of the East), which went on to become one of the most important and influential exhibitions of New German Design.
The project's premise—to work without a brief, using readymade materials-aimed to document the mood of rebellion among the students and staff at the Berliner Hochschule der Künste against Bauhaus-inspired good taste, form, and function.
By ı984, however, criticism was already coming from within the movement's own camp.
Jörg Ratzlaff produced a piece called Auf den Hund gekommen (Gone to the Dogs), an industrial lamp mounted on a wood-veneered plywood board on wheels.
Ratzlaff said of the piece: “What was once undoibtedly a fresh approach, namely 'New German Design,' has now become jaded.
Today art objects are shunted from exhibition to exhibition, without having any real influence on industrial production."4 Other critics too suggested that the honeymoon was over; Jochen Gros commented, "A true stylistic alternative cannot be based solely on the principle that everything is permitted, except good form."5 He accused the designers of navel-gazing, of being devoted purely to self-promotion and ignoring the social responsibilities of design.
New German Design RIGHT Möbel Perdu (Michael Feith), Tyranny lamp, wire, papier mâché, 1984.
BELOW Volker Albus, Römerberg seating, steel, concrete, wood, and elastomer, 1988. was seen as little more than a commercial wave of fine art, reworked in a media-friendly manner.
By the end of the i98os, both the press and the public had lost interest in the movement.
The ret rospective exhibition “Gefühlscollagen—Wohnen von Sinnen" (Feelings Collages—Living through the Senses) in Düsseldorf in i986 ended up being both the apex and swansong of New German Design.
Even Kunstflug, which had several works on show, called the exhibition “the highpoint and endpoint of this orgy of insolence and free-for-all groinkicking." criticizing the anti-functionalist rebellion for having “taken place predominantly on a formal level."6 It was not by chance that the abrupt end of the movement coincided with the reunification of Germany in November 1989.
New German Design, with its brash, strident attitude, burned out, and the New Simplicity movement prevailed.
Using one-off pieces and limited editions to fight against a powerful industry with clearly defined market sectors and an audience receptive to conservative design was always going to be a risky undertaking.
Every revolution has its time.
Although this anti-establishment uprising lasted barely a decade, its efforts have continued to pay off.
The spirit of this raw and challenging movement is now experiencing a long-awaited revival, and may yet inspire a new generation of designers to counteract the tired and routine offerings of the marketplace and instead concentrate on producing “less, but better." # MARTINE BEDIN b.1957 FRENCH DESIGNER WHO FOUND THE EXTROVERTED MEMPHIS DESIGN PALETTE SUITED HER MEDITERRANEAN SENSIBILITY.
TOP Cucumber vase, ceramic, for Memphis, 1985.
RIGHT Super Lamp, from the first Memphis collection, 1981.
Bedin's name is not as well known as many of her colleagues, but the Super Lamp she designed for the first MEMPHIS collection (see under “Edited Design") became one of the collective's bestknown and widely published works.
A foreigner among the Italians, and one of only two women in the group (the other her childhood friend NATHALIE DU PASQUIER), Bedin was born in Bordeaux, France, and grew up in Corsica.
She began her architectural training in Paris, but in 1978 received a scholarship and went to Florence to continue her studies In Italy, Bedin met leaders of the Radical Architecture movement and became interested in their innovative ideas.
She worked on a Düsseldorf project with Superstudio\*, and in 1979 Superstudio founder Adolfo Natalini\* invited her to Milan, where she met ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") and MICHELE DE LUCCHI.
When Memphis was founded, Bedin was invited to join.
The new design vocabulary that the group was exploring, which replaced the spare, minimalist color palette with extroverted hues, appealed to Bedin's Mediterranean sensibility.
After returning to her studies in Paris, she traveled back and forth to Italy to maintain her connection with the avant-garde designers, and began to design pieces influenced by their ideas, concentrating on small-scale objects that could travel with her.
When Sottsass visited her in Paris, he admired a lamp design in her sketchbook and invited her to work in his studio.
Bedin moved to Milan, and developed the sketch into the Super Lamp, which would be introduced by Memphis ın i98ı.
With a bright blue fiberglass chassis and rainbow-colored bulb holders, it moves on industrial wheels and resembles a child's pull toy.
The lively colors and playful air may be the secrets of its success, since it falls far short as a functional light source.
After the Super Lamp, Bedin became interested in exploring ideas for mobile furniture, and designed several other pieces that could move.
She also contributed more lighting to the Memphis collections; the Western, Holiday, and Charleston designs are attractive floor lamps, but none garnered the same attention as her debut piece.
Bedin remained in Sottsass's studio for three years, working on her designs and supporting herself by writing for a French publication.
As she later told an interviewer, "We were funding our economy to keep spending time all together with Ettore.
Every one of us had a second job," adding that “it was much more interesting to be with him than to work on his projects."7 and commentary.
On her ongoing exploration of new paths in design, she wrote, “The same obsession always: can we imagine a new world by drawing another chair, another table, another light, another vase?"8 None of Bedin's designs is just “another." BELOW Eastern lamp, metal with a halogen bulb, for Memphis, 1982.
BOTTOM Lampe de Poche, lacquered wood, edition of 6, 1980.

1985.

RIGHT Archi Fusi Forcelle table lamp, produced by Cassina, 1988.
OPPOSITE TOP Series of table lamps, produced by Zanotta, 1992.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM Neolia illuminated headboard, produced by Zanotta, 1989.
Andrea Branzi does not consider himself a postmodernist, although for more than two decades he participated in every important Italian avant-garde group, and so helped to chart the course of progressive Modernism in twentiethcentury design.
Among the most versatile of his contemporaries, he believes he is more researcher than designer, a student of technology and particularly of nature, the latter of which has informed his work over a career lasting almost six decades.
“Elements from nature are always unique," he says, "Nature doesn't repeat itself; industrial materials will never achieve the same thing."9 Branzi was trained as an architect, but has focused his attention on design experiments and objects rather than buildings.
He was born in Florence and graduated from the faculty of architecture at the University of Florence in i965.
The following year he joined with Gilberto Corretti, Paolo Deganello\*, and Massimo Morozzi to found Archizoom Associati\*, organizing the conventionflouting “Superarchitettura" exhibition and developing Pop art designs including the seating units called Superonda (iı967) and Safari (i968).
Branzi opened his design studio in Milan in ı973 and, while pursuing his own projects, was also associated with Global Tools\* and Alchimia\*, two other radical groups.
For Alchimia he contributed to the Bau.Haus I and Il collections of experimental furniture (1979–8o) and the following year designed experimental pieces for Alchimia as he becarne a participant in the newly formed MEMPHIS (see under "Edited Design") group.
Branzi's affliation with conventionchallenging Memphis did not sidetrack his interest in nature, and his Animali Domestici collection (i985) combined natural materials—tree trunks and branches—with man-made ones, an aesthetic concept that he has continued to explore.
He also designed decorative objects for CLETO MUNARI (see under “Edited Design") and jewelry for ACME (see under “Edited Design"), as well as furniture for several other producers.
He is a three-time winner of the coveted Compasso d'Oro, Italy's most prestigious design award.
Aside from his design work, Branzi has been an influential force through other activities: he was a co-organizer of “Il design italiano degli anni '50," an important 1977 exhibition in Milan that showcased midcentury Italian design, chronicling its movement away from conventional Modernism to avant-garde design.
He became involved with the most influential design publications, writing for Casabella from 1972 to 1976 and directing Modo from 1982 to 1984, and has been a contributor to Domus since 198o.
He coordinated the International Design section of the i5th Milan Triennial, and participated with personal shows in the following two triennials as well as in several Venice Biennales.
Branzi's role as an educator includes years of teaching at the Politecnico di Milano, where he headed the School of Interior Design, and he was co-founder in i983 (with ALESSANDRO MENDINI; see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") of the postgraduate design school Domus Academy.
His book The Hot House (i984) recounts the history of progressive design movements in Italy, primarily in the decades following the Second World War, highlighting the dramatic changes that were radically reshaping the domestic landscape.
He noted the need to “[put] together a different domestic culture...recovering a system of ties and functions that cannot be explained in purely ergonomic or functional terms."o As his work indicates, Branzi seeks a close connection between man and his environment.
Though Branzi has reprised his use of raw tree trunks in recent designs, his design vocabulary has consistently evolved, as he continues to explore new themes and introduce more emotional elements: "You can't spend your life doing the same kind of project: design should not solve problems, but should open new ones." ALDO CIBIC b.1955 THOUGH UNTRAINED IN DESIGN, THIS ITALIAN WAS A KEY MEMBER OF THE MEMPHIS COLLECTIVE.
Alhough best known for his connection to MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") and ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), Aldo Cibic differed from his colleagues in that iconoclastic venture.
His designs are more subdued, as though reluctant to demand attention, and when he left the collaborative he moved in an altogether different direction.
A native of Vicenza, Cibic moved to Milan in i979 and began working with Sottsass, who became his mentor in the study of design.
The only Memphis collaborator without an academic background, Cibic was in effect an apprentice who trained on the job to become a designer.
Like the others who converged in the nucleus of the postmodern movement, he felt constrained by the rigidity of functionalist design, responding with enthusiasm to the siren song of the avantgarde.
Cibic worked with MATTEO THUN and MARCO ZANINI when all three were employed by Sottsass Associati and with them became a founding member of Memphis in 198o.
He designed pieces like the Belvedere console (ı982) and the Cabbage Pepper Radish ceramic tea service (ı985), both using black-and-white graphics rather than the livelier colors most associated with Memphis designs.
Despite his contributions to the Memphis collections, Cibic functioned primarily as an organizer and administrator rather than a designer.
After Memphis disbanded, he established Cibic & Partners in 1989 and implemented his own ideas with a new furniture collection and an entirely different approach.
He felt that Memphis had become an “indigestion of forms."1² Instead of individual objects with distinct appearances, Cibic developed what he called "families" of basic everyday pieces, which were produced and marketed under the trade name "Standard." The furniture was clean-lined and well-proportioned, with a look that was contemporary but of no specific identifiable style.
The logo for the venture was a jovial JAVIER MARISCAL-designed figure, suggesting that the pieces, though simple, had personality and flair.
The Cibic firm expanded its scope, assembling a team of architects, interior designers, graphic designers, industrial designers, and a network of collaborating consultants, and has since designed furniture and accessories for a variety of companies as well as handling architecture and interior design projects.
Cibic's professional interests also include teaching: at Domus Academy, and in the industrial design programs at the Politecnico di Milano and at the Università luav di Venezia.
In contrast to the activities of the partnership, another group, the Cibic Workshop, in Vicenza, was established primarily to focus on research into the relationship between design and society.
Concerning his work ethic, Cibic has said, "I'm always looking for less professional plans and more poetic ones."3 Spoken like a true visionary.
