NIGEL COATES b.1949 MASTER OF NARRATIVE-DRIVEN DESIGN WHOSE PROVOCATIVE INTERIORS MERGE HISTORY AND FANTASY.
BELOW LEFT Caffè Bongo, restaurant and club in Tokyo, Japan, 1986.
BELOW RIGHT Preliminary drawing for Caffè Bongo.
OPPOSITE Caffè Bongo interior.
Nigel Coates is an architect, theorist, and educator whose idiosyncratic style was enthusiastically received by lapanese clients before it was appreciated in his native England.
His storytelling approach, more closely akin to that of fashion and theatrical designers than of his architecture colleagues, has produced extravagant and immersive spaces that inject historic replication into fantasy environments.
The almost indescribable variety of his designs places him somewhere between Postmodernism and its offshoot, Deconstructivism.
Coates originally planned to study engineering, but in 1972, after graduating from the University of Nottingham, he entered London's Architectural Association, studying under Bernard Tschumi\*, and later won a scholarship to the University of Rome.
Inspired by the work of the Italian Radicals, he sought to move beyond architecture to design buildings and interiors that were closer to experiences than objects.
He was intrigued by London nightclubs, which, like avant-garde theater, erased the separation between audience and performing space.
His controversial approach, developed with several of his students at the Architectural Association, where Coates had been teaching since 1977, led to the formation of the NATO group (Narrative Architecture Today) in i983.
Brash and ambitious, the group saw itself at the frontiers of architecture, staging exhibitions and publishing an eponymous magazine that showcased narrativedriven designs inspired by art, fashion, and the aforementioned nightclubs.
NATO believed that buildings should relate to their users, viewing architecture as a language drawn from everyday experience.
In 1984 a Chinese developer saw Coates's Kensington flat, a whimsical space that evoked a Venetian palazzo (and was at the time his only built project) in a magazine and invited him to Tokvo to design.
At the time, Cóates recalls, "London was, for them [the [apanese] the most fashionable place on the planet,"'4 and a British architect was a desirable import.
Moreover, they were as fascinated by history as they were by avant-garde ideas—making them ideal clients for an unconventional designer.
Coates partnered with Doug Branson as Branson Coates Architecture to produce the Metropole restaurant (i986), an elegantly theatrical environment embellished with archaeological references.
Caffè Bongo, built the same year, was a sculpted space evoking both ancient Rome and current fashion in a commentary on the passage of time, reportedly inspired by Federico Fellini's flm La dolce vita.
Its undulating walls enclosed diversc and unrelated elements like classical columns, a jet engine, and historical figurines.
Both projects, which would have been far too extreme for London, were enthusiastically received in Japan.
Coates's frst total building project, L'Arca di Noè (ı988), was a Sapporo restaurant and bar suggesting a landlocked Noah's Ark.
Another celebrated project, The Wall, was a multi-use structure suggesting a remnant of ancient Rome that had been excavated in modern Tokyo.
Coates's provocative, even bizarre, designs were widely published, and made his reputation back home.
His subsequent projects in England, however—including shops for Katharine Hamnett and Jasper Conran—were considerably more restrained, although their RIGHT Noah armchair, sculpted wood and steel, designed for a restaurant in Sapporo, Japan, 1988.
BELOW Bohemia Jazz Club. elegant, tradition-evoking interiors were still radical departures from the prevailing high-tech of the time.
In i986 the Victoria and Albert Museum dubbed Coates's style "New Baroque," grouping him with André Dubreuil, Tom Dixon, and Ron Arad in an exhibition featuring the London avant-garde.
Though happy to be considered nonconformist, Coates did not necessarily think of himself as postmodern—even commenting, "postmodernists were the enemies"'5—until he was designated as such by the V&A.
Like others so labeled, Coates uses classical language in rebellious ways, but departs from them in drawing from other areas of culture and society.
In this way, Coates is as much artist as architect. # MICHELE DE LUCCHI b.1951 FERRARA-BORN DESIGNER OF BEST-SELLING PRODUCTS FOR BOTH AVANT-GARDE AND CORPORATE CLIENTS.
Like many of his contemporaries, Michele De Lucchi has worked in several areas of design throughout his career but he has also worked in different markets, in an admirable balance of theory and practice.
He has been an important contributor to two of the most influential Italian avant-garde design groups, but has also designed award-winning objects for corporate clients as concerned with basic function as cutting-edge aesthetics.
De Lucchi studied architecture in Padua and at the University of Florence, completing his studies in i975, as the Radical Design movements were at their peak.
In i973 he joined other architects and designers in Padua to form Cavart, a Radical Design group that, like Global Tools\* and others, staged events and organized seminars.
He taught in Florence for a few years before moving to Milan in i978 to work for the manufacturer Kartell\*.
But most of his career has followed more adventurous paths.
As he recently explained about the appeal of avant-garde groups, "The style was always the problem...it's very rare for an established style to lead to innovation."16 bulbs, He later joined MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design"), designing the colorful Lido sofa (ı982) and, in i983, the First Chair, a space-agelooking piece that marked Memphis's first collection using industrial fabricátion to make furniture in quantity (though the quantities were not large).
First became a bestseller and gained its designer star status.'7 De Lucchi designed more furniture for Memphis than anyone other than Sottsass, all imbued with characteristic lively color and wit.
These included the perky Kristal table and the distinctive Horizon bed.
Pursuing a parallel path in the commercial world, De Lucchi was a design consultant to Olivetti froin 1979 and was appointed its director of design in i988.t8 After Memphis disbanded and Postmodernism began winding down, he began to work for other corporations on less radical products.
His Tolomeo lamp, designed with Giancarlo Fassina for Artemide\* in 1987, was an instant success and has been in continuous production since.
Its sleek, polished aluminum arms and swivel head became status symbols for design-conscious desks in the high-tech era.
Though he has also designed for Compaq, Philips, and Siemens, he has said, "For me, the objects which function really perfectly are not the most interesting ones,"'9 implying that financial considerations rather than creative opportunity motivated his work for industry.
His creative side was nurtured in jewelry designs for ACME (see under “Edited Design") and CLETO MUNARI (see under “Edited Design"), where he could play with shape and color.
A man of many facets, modernist De Lucchi has remained convinced of the value of artisanry, and in i99o he founded Produzione Privata to produce and market designs made with traditional handicraft techniques, thus feeding his personal interest in diametrically opposed areas: technology and crafts.
The two are not incompatible, in his view: "When you design, you try to give a sense of things, a sense about life.
A lot of the work we do is about creating differences that make things personal."2o Though trained as an architect, De Lucchi has designed relatively few buildings, yet like many postmodernists he considers design and architecture to be opposite sides of the same coin.
In recent years he has applied his architectural talents in designing or restoring buildings for several banks, power plants, and major corporations, while continuing to explore ideas for innovative design by working as a curator for art- and design-related museum exhibitions.
De Lucchi is one of the postmodernists who left the fold, disavowing the association with Memphis and labeling the collaborative a “disaster" and a “catastrophe."²' But his contributions remain among its most admired, and his name is inseparable from its history.

OPPOSITE Sinvola ceiling light, metal, fabric, and painted wood, 1979. # NATHALIE DU PASQUIER b.1957 BORDEAUX-BORN DESIGNER WHOSE DECORATIVE PIECES DISTILL AFRICAN PATTERNS THROUGH A GALLIC SENSIBILITY.
It is not surprising to learn that Nathalie Du Pasquier began her career as a painter rather than a designer.
Most of her patterns could easily be framed and mounted on walls; in fact, she refers to her work as "decorated surfaces." Du Pasquier is known for exuberant designs for textiles and floor-coverings whose irresistible appeal mixes vivid Mediterranean colors with patterns inspired by non-Western cultures.
She and her friend and compatriot MARTINE BEDIN were the youngest members of the MEMPHIS (see under "Edited Design") collaborative, drawn to Italy by the appeal of a new, more colorful approach to design than that of Bauhaus-inspired Modernism.
Born in Bordeaux, France, Du Pasquier was fascinated by the intensity of the textiles she saw as a teenager on visits to Africa.
She began to translate them into her own designs, naming many after the countries she had visited.
Her patterns are not African, however, but an ingenious melding of ethnic inspiration and her own sensibility.
They were a radical departure fromn the formal imagery that was popular at the time, and indeed from any of the historicist patterns embraced by others who rejected the modern aesthetic.
Distinctive for both their originality and the impossibility of being labeled with a particular style, her textiles and carpets are unmistakable in their extravagant pattern and saturated colors.
In 1978 Du Pasquier moved to Italy, where she met GEORGE SOWDEN. who became her husband and frequent collaborator.
In i98ı both became part of the initial group drawn into ETTORE SOTTSASS's (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") circle to establish Memphis.
The Mamounia chair, in Memphis's first collection, was a joint effort: Sowden's design and Du Pasquier's fabrıc.
She also designed textiles for the collaborative, as well as bold area rugs that complemented the furniture in deliberately unmatched effects—a kind of compatible clashing.
"It was probably the beginning of a new era," she said in 2o14, "Form did not have to follow function any more, and design was about communication."²2 Du Pasquier and Sowden continued to collaborate on special projects as well as designing independently; they showed a series of one-off ceramics in Lucerne in i986, and "Objects for the Electronic Age" (i983), an exhibition of twentyfour colorful small pieces such as clocks and lamps, brought them considerable attention.
The collection was designed to show that electronics. as opposed to mechanical objects, could take virtually any form; to enhance their decorative merit, Du Pasquier and Sowden wrapped the designs in eye-popping laminate patterns, much like those that had enlivened Memphis designs.
In fact, ABET LAMINATI (see under “Edited Design") produced patterns designed by Du Pasquier specially for the collection.
In the peak years of Postmodernism, retailers Esprit\* and Fiorucci found that Du Pasquier's designs were ideal companions for their trend-setting clothing, and she also designed small objects for ARC74 and timepieces for Lorenz.
Except for the timepieces, she classified everything she did as "decorated surfaces"—two-dimensional designs that were almost endlessly varied, and distinctively her own style.
When Memphis disbanded in 1987, Du Pasquier left the design world and returned to painting and sculpture in her Milan studio, expanding her aesthetic to explore a dialogue between European and Chinese painting traditions.
She began to show her work in galleries and abroad.
In more recent years she has returned to design after being rediscovered by the fashion world, where her 198os patterns are being translated into decorative accessories.

## JAMES
EVANSON b.1946 NEW-YORK BASED ARCHITECT WHO COMBINES CRAFT AND DESIGN TECHNIQUES IN ABSTRACT FORMS.
RIGHT Masque table and chairs, mahogany, plywood, ColorCore, and glass, 1986.
OPPOSITE TOP Chaise longue, mahogany and lacquered wood, 1983.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM Primazoid armchair, plastic laminate, 1980.
Making art furniture can be more complicated than making art, as the furniture needs to be functional as well as aesthetically appealing.
Iames Evanson (also see under “Living with Postmodern Design") has successfully negotiated the delicate balance between art, design, and craft in several decades' worth of furniture and lighting designs, while pursuing a parallel path as an architect and builder.
Montana-born Evanson applied his several skills as a participant in the avant-garde art movement that developed in New York as Postmodernism was emerging in Italy.
He was one of the group of young designers, artists, and craftspeople converging around Art et Industrie\*, the influential gallery opened by Rick Kaufmann in i977 in SoHo.
In shows that defied the conventions of furniture, rejecting stylistic labels and prefiguring the twenty-first-century category of design-art, Evanson and his cohort presented furniture in tune with the hip-hop and graffiti culture of the time.
In addition to introducing innovative American designers and artisans, Art et Industrie was also a pioneer in showing avant-garde work from Italy.
Evanson's furniture, assembled using traditional craft techniques, combines abstract forms in unconventional combinations of materials with historical iconography.
It straddles the dividing line between fine and decorative art; he is concerned with art that functions, rather than art as an end in itself.
This can be credited to Evanson's early training with his father, in carpentry and cabinetmaking, which led him to enroll in the industrial design program at ArtCenter College of Design in Los Angeles.
After interrupting his studies to serve as an army helicopter pilot in the Vietnam War, Evanson returned to school, switching first to illustration and then to fine art.
He left to open his own storefront studio, and gradually began to apply his skills to making hybrid objects combining art and cabinetry.
In i975 he was invited to participate in a show by the influential dealer Ivan Karp, and moved to New York.
Setting up a workshop in the developing NoHo neighborhood, Evanson began to make artwork as well as design/build projects to support the artmaking.
Deciding to complete his education, Evanson enrolled in the architecture program at Pratt Institute.
After graduating in i977, he continued to refine his approach to functional art, using fragmented forms and mixed materials in a direction that paralleled the work of the Italian avant-garde.
His best-known designs are probably the Lighthouse lamps (i984), a series of eight pieces suggesting tall buildings with rays of light and color streaming from the window openings.
They were, Evanson explained, designed as pleasuregiving art objects: “I wanted them to reflect the spirit of the i98os, not the international modernistic style of the 1950s and 196os."23 They brought him media attention and an invitation to exhibit with MEMPHIS (see under "Edited Design"), after ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") attended an Art et Industrie opening and admired the work.
Evanson showed two pieces modeled after the Lighthouses in 1986 in a Memphis exhibition of new designers, and two others in a i988 Memphis lighting exhibition.
Evanson added plastic laminate to his vocabulary of materials, as in the harlequinlook Masque table and chairs (i986).
His furniture designs, like the three-legged Orbit (i985), are sometimes futuristic in form and postmodern in color, but historicist in their use of luxurious materials like silver leaf and lacquer.
Evanson's career has taken him along twin paths: while making the furniture, lighting, and art objects for which he is best-known, he has continued to practice architecture.
Unlike most architects, however, he builds as well as designs the houses, in a designer/developer partnership with his wife Lisa, whom he met at Pratt.
LEFT House at Whaley Lake, New York, living room, mantel with built-in lights, 1986.
BELOW Living-dining room with floating staircase, Whaley Lake house, 1986.
BOTTOM Renovated loft on Bond Street, New York City, 1982.
ABOVE Lighthouse lamps, wood, paint, lacquer, acrylic, steel, 1984.

## GODLEY-SCHWAN
Lyn Godley, b.
1956 Lloyd Schwan, 1956-2001 AMERICAN ARTISTS WHO MARKETED THEIR COLOR-SATURATED PRODUCTS AT DESIGN EXHIBITIONS.
In the years when Italians were being lauded for design leadership, Godley-Schwan were among the relatively few American practitioners of innovative, unconventional furniture design.
Their spirited shapes and comic-book colors were strikingly different from anything then being produced in a country that came late to Modernism.
Their colorful and appealing pieces drew considerable media attention, one reporter calling them "children's furniture for adults."24 Lyn Godley originally planned to be a fine artist, receiving a BFA from Ohio State University and an MFA from the University of Wisconsin (ı98o).
Lloyd Schwan's training was also in art, but when he began to work with Godley, the pair decided to take a more practical direction, designing objects that satisfied their desire for creative expression but would be saleable as well.
This led them to design and make pieces that could have some actual function, even if that function was second to their ornamental value.
Godley and Schwan started off small, establishing their business in Chicago in 1984, but decided the following year to move to New York and set up a studio in Brooklyn to produce their handmade artworks.
Their first success was a collection of jewelry that mixed geometric-shaped beads with unusual materials like rubber rings and colored wire.
The pieces were fanciful combinations of Pop art and atomic age imagery, and an immediate success with fashion-forward consumerslooking for something new and different.
These were accessible artworks, proclaiming that fashion need not be stodgy and that jewelry need not be made of precious materials.
From jewelry, Godley and Schwan moved into designing furniture, like the Otto cabinet, a bicolor hourglass shape set sideways and mounted on slim legs, and the sculptural Dancer cabinets, which could be siblings to some Studio Alchimia\* designs.
They also made lighting and accessory pieces, selling the works through galleries or directly to clients.
But that was to change as they looked for new ways to showcase their work.
Taking an ambitious and unprecedented leap in 1988, Godley-Schwan became the first American firm to exhibit at the Salone di Mobile in Milan.
The timing was perfect; although MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") had just disbanded, Postmodernism was still in the ascendant, and the move brought them considerable attention and retail distribution.
Unlike most frms making limited-production objects, Godley-Schwan began to show their designs at trade exhibitions in America, broadening their own distribution and helping erase the elitist image of designer products, almost all of which were then being sold exclusively through specialist galleries.
The partnership ended in ı998, and Lyn Godley has continued to design under the Godley-Schwan name.
Her primary focus has been on lighting, working with different light sources and most recently with fiber optics, in fine art and public art projects.
To her, design and art are inseparable.
LEFT Angular bookcase, lacquered wood, c.
1990.
BELOW Checkers cabinet, veneered and lacquered wood on steel legs, 1991.

## ABOVE
Dancer cabinets, lacquered plywood, 1990.
RIGHT Inkle bookcase, lacquered plywood, 1993. # MASSIMO IOSA GHINI b.1959 BOLOGNA-BORN ARCHITECT AND DESIGNER KNOWN FOR HIS DYNAMIC, STREAMLINED FORMS.
Postmodernism is not all about jagged-edged design and disconcertingly clashing colors.
It also includes designs that are unquestionably avantgarde but sinuously curvilinear and subtly hued: witness the work of Massimo Iosa Ghini, whose contributions to the postmodern vocabulary are very different from those of his contemporaries, and impossible not to recognize.
His distinctive aesthetic of sweeping, asymmetrical curves and dynamic furniture forms evoke both Futurism and Streamline style Bologna, known as the source of some ot Italy's finest cuisine, seerns an appropriate birthplace for an original like losa Ghini.
His education followed fairly conventional lines; he studied architecture in Florence, graduated from the Politecnico di Milano in i98t, and opened his own studio that year.
Departing from the orthodox career track, however, he began working as an artist and illustrator for several periodicals, and his first recognition was as a comic book illustrator By 1985, Iosa Ghini had become interested in the Italian avant-garde, founding the Bolidismo group with Pierangelo Caramia\*, Stefano Giovannoni, and other Florentine designers.
Bolidismo focused on speed as a dominant element óf design and believed that through an emphasis on sensory perceptions, objects could have an emotional relation to their users.
These concepts clearly influenced losa Ghini's furniture, as they would his later work in scenic and exhibition design, and retail spaces losa Ghini was invited by ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") to participate in MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") presentations, and designed several pieces for the collaborative, ıncluding the Juliette chair (r987) in metal with plastic and straw webbing, and the much-published Bertrand wood and metal sideboard (i987).
Both pieces, strikingly different from the irregular geometric forms of most other Memphis designs, have losa Ghini's characteristic streamlined silhouettes, and neither employ the materials, color, or ironic tone associated with the collaborative.
Their fluid, contemporary forms strike a note of traditional elegance, something that most postmodern designs studiously avoid.
LEFT Otello armchair, chromed metal, lacquered wood, and silk, edition of 3, for Memphis, 1986.
Despite their eccentricity, or perhaps because of it, Iosa Ghini's provocative industrial design objects found favor with an international roster of producers.
He has designed for companies in Japan, Austria, and Germany as well as for Italian firms like Moroso, Poltrona Frau\*, and Cassina.
In architecture, he has been involved in museum planning, public transport, and retail design, and has won numerous awards in Italy, Germany, Japan, China, and the US.
In 2o13, La Triennale di Milano sponsored a presentation of works designed over his thirty-year career.

BOTTOM Genio coffee table, glass and aluminum, produced by Fiam, 1990.
RIGHT Numero Uno armchair, produced by Moroso, 1986.
BELOW Juliette chair, metal, plastic, and straw, for Memphis, 1987.

## SHIRO
KURAMATA 1934-1991 TOKYO-BORN DESIGNER WHOSE ICONIC FURNITURE COMBINED EASTERN MINIMALISM AND WESTERN ORNAMENT.
Some of the most expressive—and most refined— objects of the postmodern era are the work of this celebrated Japanese designer, who combined craft and technology in furniture that won accolades from museum curators and connoisseurs for its fusion of Japanese minimalism with Western ornamentation.
The intriguing names he gave them enhance the visual appeal of his designs, which include two chairs that are among the most admired objects of twentieth-century furniture Kuramata studied woodcraft and design at Tokyo Polytechnic, graduating in ı953, and worked for furniture producer Teikokukizai before returning to school to study interior design at Kuwasawa Design School, where he became familiar with Western aesthetics.
After completing his studies he worked for the department stores San-Ai and Matsuya before opening his own practice in 1965 He was part of the new generation of architects and designers who brought international recognition to Japanese design in the decades after the Second World War, not only helping Japan move out from under the shadow of their defeat but establishing a tradition-bound country as a fertile source of modern design.
During his prolific career, Kuramata designed the interiors of more than three hundred bars and restaurants, but became far more celebrated for his innovative furniture designs, which show a particular interest in transparency.
He used simple materials like wire mesh, aluminum, acrylic, and glass, working them in unexpected ways into forms that transcended function.
The leaning towards minimalism reflects his Japanese heritage, but his fondness for ornament was drawn from Western precedents.
Kuramata's earliest and simplest exploration of transparency, the Glass Chair (i976), was an armchair of solid glass slabs secured with a glue that made it almost invisible.
More experimental, and instantly iconic, were How High the Moon (i986), which rendered a classic club chair in steel mesh to gave the massive form a look of weightlessness, and the ethereal Miss Blanche (i988), an entirely original piece with paper roses floating in an armchair of thick Lucite.
The latter is perhaps his most famous design, and has set sales records at auction.
Kuramata contributed to MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") collections, but his designs are notably more restrained than those of his colleagues; the Kyoto and Nara tables embed bits of broken colored glass in concrete for a terrazzo effect, and the Sally table wraps broken glass in a metal frame.
During the Memphis years, Kuramata was also designing boutiques for celebrated Japanese avant-garde fashion designer Issey Miyake, who shared his experimental sensibility.
In i988 Kuramata opened a studio in Paris, where he designed interiors and continued to create furniture.
OPPOSITE LEFT Pyramid cabinet, acrylic resin, 1968.
OPPOSITE RIGHT Glass Chair, laminated glass, edition of 40, produced by Mibaya Glass, 1976.
LEFT Cabinet de Curiosité, acrylic, edition of 40, produced by Ishimaru, 1989.
BELOW Placebo table, acrylic. glass, and steel, 1989.
Kuramata was not averse to the occasional witty comment; for example, a work called Homage to Josef Hoffmann, Begin the Beguine, for which he wrapped a Hoffmann bentwood chair in steel and burnt out the wood to leave a ghost image, poked fun at the same time as it paid homage to its inspiration.
Many of Kuramata's furniture designs are ubiquitous in design history books and museum galleries, and he is second only to ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") in being coveted by collectors of postmodern and contemporary design, possibly because his objects, which prioritize appearance over function or process, are more design-art than pieces of furniture.

ABOVE Vases, acrylic and glass, C.1989.
RIGHT Kyoto occasional table, colored glass fragments in concrete, for Memphis, 1983. # JAVIER MARISCAL b.1950 SPANISH MASTER OF MANY DISCIPLINES.
KNOWN FOR HIS WITTY AND COLORFUL DESIGNS.
It is virtually impossible to label a designer whose career transcends disciplines.
Bouncing between categories and styles with a jovial abandon, polymath Javier Mariscal has illustrated comics, posters, and books; designed textiles and a variety of other products from jewelry to furniture and ceramics; created paintings and sculptures; produced animation and multimedia works; and designed interiors.
None of his designs reflects a style other than his own; all are agreeably extroverted, invariably colorful, and postmodern in their freedom of expression and their irrepressible humor.
His best-known, and most copied, design was the friendly mascot Cobi designed for the 1992 Summer Olympics in Barcelona, which became so popular that it inspired a Spanish television series Mariscal was born in Valencia, Spain, and studied philosophy at the University of Valencia, followed by graphic design at the ELISAVA design school in Barcelona, graduating in i97ı, in the final years of the Franco dictatorship.
His early work included a radical Pop art-style underground comic (i973) that was banned by the government, and a variety of graphic design projects.
When Spain emerged into a less restrictive era, Mariscal designed a lively new graphic identity for Barcelona, a pictogram of the words BAR CEL ONA (bar, sky, wave).
In i978 he designed a textile collection in dynamic, primary-hued patterns, and received commissions to decorate houses in Barcelona and Valencia.
He opened a bar, the Duplex, for which he designed an asymmetrical bar stool (i98o) that brought him to the attention of ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), who invited him to contribute to the MEMPHIS (see under "Edited Design") collection.
Hilton, a backward-slanting tea trolley, and the oblique Colon side table were introduced in i981.
Mariscal also designed furniture for BD Ediciones de Diseño\*.
Moroso, and Akaba When the Centre Georges Pompidou staged an exhibition in 1987 that parodied Spanish culture, Mariscal contributed two chairs, including the Tio Pepe, a side chair with cartoony arms.
In 1989 he opened Estudio Mariscal, enlisting a team of illustrators, editors, animators, architects, and consultants, and broadened his activities to work on a variety of schemes, including visual identity and branding programs as well as logo design, editorial work, and corporate projects.
He has collaborated with many other designers and architects, without losing the stamp of individuality that marks all his work.
In addition to participating in a number of design exhibitions, Mariscal was given his first retrospective, "1oo Años con Mariscal," in i988 at La Lonja, Valencia.
More recently, London's Design Museum organized “Drawing Life" (2oo9), a Mariscal exhibition designed by the artist himself.

BELOW Hilton tea trolley, metal, glass, from the frst Memphis collection, 1981. # GAETANO PESCE b.1939 ITALIAN DESIGNER OF PROVOCATIVE FURNITURE SUFFUSED WITH SOCIAL COMMENTARY.
Eccentric objects and environments that incorporate lively color, personality, and often social commentary are the stock-in-trade of this designer.
His UP5 chair (i969) may have been the first design object with an overt political agenda: its bulbous form suggests a female torso, and the ottoman a ball and chain—imagery suggesting the persecution of women in modern society.
Moloch (ı97ı) is an ordinary desk lamp writ large, the grand scale making it into an iconic artwork.
Even in his less explicit designs, Pesce always has something to say, since his intentions go beyond the mere creation of aesthetically pleasing objects Though much of his furniture is unorthodox in form, he considers function an essential part of design, saying, "If it is not practical, it is not a service to those who use it ""r, Born in La Spezia, Italy, Pesce received his training at the luav architecture school in Venice, and at that city's experimental Istituto di Disegno Industriale.
He opened a studio in Padua in 1959 and was a founder of Gruppo N, an avant-garde collective that experimented with programmed art, and was involved in the Antidesign activities of Gruppo Strum and UFO\*.
He has continued to explore different forms of visual communication throughout his colorful and multifaceted career; while active as an interior designer, he has experimented with kinetic and serial art, worked with light, movement, and sound'in audio-visual and film projects, and taken on public and private architectural projects in many countries.
In 1972 Pesce was one of the twelve designers invited to create a domestic environment for the New York Museum of Modern Art's seminal exhibition "Italy: The New Domestic Landscape." His contribution was a module he envisioned as ruins from a subterranean city from the year 2ooo, excavated a thousand years later—a disorienting enclosure divided into irregular room-like spaces.26 Pesce's most widely recognized work, however, has been in furniture design, which he has been doing since i968, most often exploring the use of plastics and new technology.
He has designed for established frms like B&B Italia and Cassina.
They include the provocative Tramonto a New York sofa (i98o), which suggests a setting sun hidden behind skyscrapers, and Feltri (i986), an armchair of resin-infused felt whose back and arms adjust to the occupant's taste.
He has also designed limited-edition or one-off works that are closer to experimental art; among the most intriguing of these are the Pratt chairs (ı983), a series of nine chairs formed of urethane resin poured into the same mold.
The chairs vary in intensity of color depending on how the dye settles in the mold, and in firmness of structure depending on the amount of hardener in the mix.
Passing on his unorthodox views to the next generation, Pesce has taught at the University of Venice and Pratt Institute, New York, as well as in universities in Strasbourg and Brazil.
Pesce was based in Paris for some time, but since 1983 has lived and worked in New York, where he has continued to experiment with silicone and other fluid materials in a SoHo studio and a Brooklyn workshop, creating objects whose distortions and irregularities point to the alienation of people by consumer culture.
These sometimes bizarre but always intriguing objects have ranged from furniture and accessories to jewelry, watches, and shoes.
Refusing to repeat himself, Pesce believes that design must respond to the changing nature of the world it serves.

RIGHT Tramonto a New York sofa, produced by Cassina, 198o. # PETER SHIRE b.1947 CALIFORNIAN CERAMICIST WHO BECAME THE ONLY NON-ARCHITECT CONTRIBUTOR TO MEMPHIS.
It is a big leap from teapots to discos, but Peter Shire (also see under “Living with Postmodern Design") has made more than one unlikely crossover move in a career that has included jewelry design, furniture, exhibitions, and fine art—even though his primary medium has been ceramics.
The designer's laid-back, California approach is reflected in his use of lively color and irregular, playful forms.
The only non-architect invited to design for MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design"), and the only one with a background in crafts, Shire was postmodern before the term was applied to design.
Shire was born in Los Angeles and trained at that city's Chouinard Art Institute.
He began to work in ceramics, inspired by the examples of forerunners like Peter Voulkos and Ken Price, who helped to erase the barriers between craft and art.
But instead of shaping amorphous forms, Shire chose a pedestrian object, the teapot, as a vehicle for his creative expression.
Forming his pots by hand with the slab method, rather than working on a wheel, he reinvented the idea of what a teapot should be.
Shire became known for idiosyncratic pieces whose clashing colors and angular shapes turned utilitarian objects into collectible art.
He continued to produce a variety of teapots in an aesthetic that, despite making occasional references to classical styles, is impossible to label and very much his own.
Invited to design jewelry for ACME (see under “Edited Design"), Shire jumped at the opportunity to diversify, and created a group of enameled pins and earrings to form the firm's debut collection.
The colorful, collage-like pieces were like nothing else on the market, and their success led to other designs for the company.
When ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") saw Shire's work and identified in it a shared sensibility, Shire became the first non-member invited to contribute to the Memphis collection.
His first piece was the Brazil table (i98t), a composition of triangles half-cylinders, and circles.
It was joined by Bel Air (1982), a high-impact colored, asymmetrical armchair that starred in a number of magazine features about postmodern design.
The Big Sur sofa (i986) was another success.
As Shire expanded his range to design glassware, his witty approach transitioned into rounded forms, but with the same combinations of intense. sometimes clashing colors.
The angular silhouettes of intersecting planes and swaths of saturated color put Shire's furniture designs into the same classification of object-cum-artwork.
Although he credits the influence of such predecessors as Bauhaus, Futurism, Art Nouveau, and Art Deco, Shire's tables, seating pieces, lamps, and cabinets reflect no apparent style so much as a refusal to commit to any single aesthetic—barring that of playful, free expression.
Nevertheless, it is almost impossible not to recognize one of his designs.
Expressing himself in other directions, Shire has designed store interiors and worked on the aforementioned disco, as well as displays for the 1984 Olympic Games in Los Angeles.
His artwork includes works on paper and ceramic-glazed paintings on slabs of clay that either become individual pieces or are assembled into murals; the latter have been commissioned for private and public locations in and around Los Angeles.
Teapots, however, have remained in Shire's design vocabulary, as documented in the book Tempest in α Teapot (i991).
His work has been featured in many exhibitions internationally, as well as in catalogs and books, and as curators have taken notice, his designs have been collected by major museums.
Whatever the mediunı, Shire's works are clearly the products of a designer who is having fun with everything he does.
OPPOSITE TOP RIGHT Cahuenga floor lamp, metal, for Memphis, 1985.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM Big Sur sofa, lacquered wood and fabric, for Memphis, 1986.
BOŘEK ŠÍPEK 1949-2016 CZECH ARCHITECT AND DESIGNER WHOSE NEO-BAROQUE STYLE MADE HIM A CELEBRITY IN AMSTERDAM.
RIGHT Four Gudrun chairs cluster to form a multi-seat unit, wood, paint, 1984.
OPPOSITE Prosim Sni chaise longue, cherry wood, leather, and fabric, produced by Driade, 1987.
Neo-Baroque is a term that has been applied to designs that recall the most ornate and pretentious periods of the past, not always in a flattering context.
But Bořek Šípek's (also see under “Living with Postmodern Design") work adapts that style's usual ornateness with a fine sense of proportion and a refreshing note of whimsy.
His subtle wit, seen in the titles of his objects as well as their appearance, recalls that of the leading postmodernists, though the sumptuous aesthetic is very much his own. Šípek was born in Prague, Czechoslovakia, and his training helps to explain the diversity of interests shown in his work.
He studied furniture design at the Prague School for the Applied Arts, graduating in i968 and moving to Germany to study architecture in Hamburg at the Hochschule für bildende Künste.
Balancing practical skills with more esoteric interests, he took up the study of philosophy and aesthetics at Stuttgart University.
After working for two years as a scientific assistant at the University of Hannover's Institute of Industrial Design, he then finished his architectural schooling in i979 at the Technische Universiteit Delft, completing what may be the broadest educational background of any designer of his generation.
Nevertheless, Šípek considered himself specifically an architect rather than a designer, explaining that “Design consists of packing things into form," which he does not do.27 Continuing his peripatetic movements, Šípek moved to Amsterdam in i973, where he lived for many years, establishing an architecture and design practice and later forming Alterego in partnership with industrial designer David Palterer.
Over the next decade, as design became a prestigious and high-profile profession, Šípek became a celebrity in his adopted country, appearing in the media so often that a local publication headlined an article, "Enough Borek Šípek: Why Him?"28 His popularity was unsurprising: his designs were sumptuous, curvilinear forms, often in surprising mixes of materials and spiced with dashes of traditional artisanry.
Bambi (i983), a slender chair of tubular steel, was adorned with brass mounts and a silk back, and the attention-grabbing Prosim Sni chaise longue (i987) paired the classic form with an abstract-shaped back that seemed almost an afterthought. Šípek's glassware, which he considered a diversion from his other design pursuits, mixed frivolous and functional in silhouettes that were often punctuated with blobs or projections of contrasting color.
Enormously popular, it has been credited with helping to revive a moribund Czech glass industry. Šípek's prolific output also included furniture for Driade\*, accessories for CLETO MUNARI (see under “Edited Design") and ALESSI (see under “Edited Design"), and designs for Sawaya & Moroni\*. Šípek taught design theory at the University of Essen and architecture at the Prague Academy of Applied Arts, and in ı993 began to accept commissions for architectural projects in the Netherlands and the Czech Republic that included private residences, public spaces, and retail facilities.
His buildings, like the award-winning Glass House in Hamburg (i983) and his own residence, are as appealingly whimsical as the objects he designed.
His interiors projects included the redesign of state rooms in Prague Castle for then Czech president Václav Havel.
Summing up almost four decades of work, Šípek's book Life in Motion (2oo8) chronicles his work and his inspirations, and includes fanciful illustrations that reveal his considerable drafting skills.

RIGHT Palace chair, lacquered wood, for Memphis, 1983.
FAR RIGHT Metropole table clock, plastic laminate on wood, 1982.
One of the founding members of MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design"), George Sowden was not yet thirty years old when he joined forces with a group of young Italians, one Japanese, and two Frenchwomen (one of whom he married) to invigorate the design world with a new vocabulary of convention-flouting color and form.
Of the group, Sowden was perhaps the most conservative, as his pre- and post-Memphis work suggests, but his designs for the collaborative's collections reveal a youthful exuberance and subtle sense of humor that inform even his most practical work.
Over several decades, he has practiced in two parallel but altogether different areas, designing both electronics and art objects with equal skill and success.
Sowden was born in Leeds and studied architecture at Gloucestershire College of Art, graduating in i968.
In ı97ı he was hired by Olivetti and moved to Milan, and has not left Italy.
His firm, Sowden Design, was founded in ı98ı, and in his preferred field of industrial design he has designed hundreds of objects, from computers and electronics to houseware, appliances, cookware, lighting, glassware, and even telephones for a variety of prominent firms.
Having worked with Sottsass Associati on Olivetti projects, Sowden was recruited to be part of Memphis.
For him, Postmodernism was an invigorating change of pace from more practical pursuits, and the objects he devised for Memphis are almost polar opposites of his industrial design work.
His contributions include the leggy D'Antibes cabinet (i98ι) and the perky Saragoza chair (ı984), both of which are sleek forms with historical overtones, sparked with lively color.
His engineering skills are evident in the imaginative clocks of the Objects for the Electronic Age collection that he and his wife and frequent collaborator NATHALIE DU PASQUIER exhibited in i983.
Sowden also designed textiles for Memphis whose simple, geometric forms contrast with the exuberant patterns of Du Pasquier, and he later played with pattern in wallpapers for Rasch.
Like many of his Italian colleagues, Sowden contributed several pieces to ALESSI's (see under “Edited Design") collections.
In his post-Memphis career, Sowden's work has become more focused on industrial design, with work for firms such as Bodum and Swatch.
These have primarily been practical household goods that utilize his engineering skills—objects with refined silhouettes and only occasional touches of color.
With the revival of interest in Postmodernism, however, Sowden was persuaded to reprise his earlier frivolities in a collection of Swiss-made home textiles, designed with Du Pasquier in 2015.

1990.
BELOW LEFT Oberon chair, with fabric by Nathalie Du Pasquier, for Memphis, 1981.
BELOW RIGHT Star table lamp, painted wood and acrylic, 1972. # PHILIPPE STARCK b.1949 FRENCHMAN WHOSE PROVOCATIVE DESIGNS HAVE BROUGHT HIM INTERNATIONAL CELEBRITY.
Joie de vivre is the perfect phrase to describe the aura surrounding the designs of Paris-born Philippe Starck.
His products are ubiquitous, transcending categories to include everything from furniture to lighting, tableware, and all manner of household accessories, bringing celebrity status to something as quotidian as a lemon juicer.
Starck's designs are imbued with personality, humor, and a bit of swagger, as is his public persona; in 2o04, an awards jury designated him the "most unusual, quirkiest, and exciting designer of the past twenty years.' He is certainly one of the best-known, even to a non-design-savvy public.
Starck was trained in design at the prestigious École Nissim de Camondo in Paris, graduating in ı968, and in 1969 was named art director at Pierre Cardin, where he designed furniture under the Cardin name.
He formed his namesake firm in i98o and began designing chairs that gained notice for both their offbeat design and provocative names, like Miss Dorn (1982), Dr.
Sonderbar (1983), Von Vogelsang (i984), and Dr Glob (i988).
He designed several nightclubs, but it was his work for the Café Costes (i984) in Paris, and the much-copied three-legged Costes chair, that made him internationally famous.
Starck went on to design two New York boutique hotels: the Royalton (i988) and Paramount (i99o), both of which, widely published, served to secure his reputation as designer of the moment and trend-setter for the young and hip.
These interiors were not so much postmodern as eclectic mixtures of untraditional, freewheeling forms and mismatched furniture.
He reprised his success with the Delano Hotel (i995) in Miami Beach, where glossy white walls and floors set off an assortment of furniture in retro-modern styles.
Since then, several other hotels have enjoyed the benefits of the striking interiors and copious publicity gained from the designer's deft touch and marketable name.
Starck soon became the public face of avant-garde design in France, a country more often known for its ties to traditionalism, and he was awarded commissions for high-profile projects including, in i984, redecorating the interiors of the Élysée Palace quarters of then president François Mitterand, and in ı988 designing a major exhibition showcasing French design at the Centre Georges Pompidou.

## He
has also designed objects for ALESSI (see under "Edited Design"), the most notable being the Juicy Salif (i99o), a skinny, three-legged lemon juicer that is more a piece of sculpture than a functional kitchen utensil.
Starck has also applied his design skills to glassware, lighting, cutlery, and numerous other objects, all of which have benefited from bearing the designer's name.
Following MICHAEL GRAVES's (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") successful example, Starck brought his distinctive style to the mass market in 2oo2 with acollection of colorful and curvy houseware for Target that included what might be the ultimate designer object: a seductively curved toothbrush (i992).
He has designed for Driade\* and for his firm XO, which he established with Gérard Mialet in 1985.
For Kartell\*, his designs include the bestselling Louis Ghost chair, a traditional form rendered in transparent polycarbonate.
The book Philippe Starck (2oo4), chronicling his career and his designs, sports a fittingly tongue-in-cheek cover depicting the artist, barechested, with his head on backwards.

BELOW RIGHT Juicy Salif lemon squeezer, cast aluminum, for Alessi, 1990. # MATTEO THUN b.1952 THIS ITALIAN'S FUNCTIONAL OBJECTS ARE DISPLAY-WORTHY DECORATIVE ACCESSORIES.
BELOW Stillight light sculptures, plastic, produced by Bieffeplast, 1985.
Perhaps his initial training in sculpture at Oskar Kokoschka's Schule des Sehens (School of Seeing) in Salzburg, Austria, accounts for the individualistic forms of Matteo Thun's objects.
Or do they stem from his earlier schooling from his mother, who worked with clay?
Perhaps it is simply his desire to make design a sensory as well as visual language; his ceramic objects demand that they be touched as well as looked at.
And though he became most celebrated for his inventive ceramics, he has ventured into other areas with equally happy results.
Thun was born in Bolzano, Italy, and studied architecture in Florence, graduating in i975 from the University of Florence.
He then moved to Milan at the recommendation of ceramicist MARCO ZANINI in order to work for ETTORI SOTTSASS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design” and “Living with Postmodern Design") at Sottsass Associati.
This placed Thun in an ideal position to become one of the founding members of MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") in i98o, along with his co-workers ALDO CIBIC and Zanini.
The collaborative's congenial environment was conducive to developing Thun's particular aesthetic, which in his designs for Memphis led to quirky pots and vases that cheekily flouted the conventions of good, modern design.
Some had zoomorphic silhouettes with jagged edges or angular forms, clearly prioritizing looks over function and seeming better suited to a display shelf than the dining table.
For the first Memphis collection Thun contributed the Nefertiti tea set, an assemblage of classical pyramid forms, and his ceramic pieces for subsequent offerings included the striking Danubio and Volga vases of i983.
Anxious to pursue his own path, Thun left Sottsass Associati and Memphis in i984 and established his own Milan practice, turning his talent to designing for a variety of clients.
The most highly visible of these was Swatch, for whom he was creative director from 199o to 1993, introducing a series of eye-catching watches that helped transform an inexpensive accessory into a collectible fashion icon.
For CLETO MUNARI (see under “Edited Design") he designed an appealing leaning pitcher, a shape he reprised in “walking" coffee pots for WMF (i985); his other product designs included tableware, carpets, flatware, and jewelry for ALESSI (see under “Edited Design"), Anthologie Quartett\*, Arteluce\*, Kartell\*, and Vorwerk.
Indulging his humorous side in a i989 project for Thonet, Thun playfully dressed the company's classic bentwood chair in skirts and slipcover tops to suit each season of the year.
Despite the sometimes unorthodox nature of his designs, they were favorably received by critics; Thun is a three-time winner of the prestigious Compasso d'Oro award for good design.
He shared his ideas with students at the University of Applied Arts Vienna, joining the faculty in ı983 and remaining there for almost two decades, teaching ceramics and product design.
In i988 he oversaw a special project for Villeroy & Boch\* called In the Spirit of the USA, for which students produced witty and highly original ideas like a teapot shaped like the Statue of Liberty and another in the form of a muscular football player.
For a broader audience, Thun produced the tongue-in-cheek “Baroque Bauhaus" manifesto in 1985, exhorting contemporary designers to learn from the work of the past.
In recent years, Thun has broadened his scope to include interiors as well as products, and he has designed several luxury hotels as well as store interiors.
But the lessons of Postmodernism were well learned: he has stated that his work for Memphis differs only superficially from his more recent designs; the principles remain unchanged Once asked about his favorite project, he replied, "The next one."29 TOP Pitcher, sterling silver and wood, for Cleto Munari C.1982.
ABOVE Nefertiti cup, eggcup, sugar bowl, and teapot, glazed ceramic, from the first Memphis collection, 1981.
OPPOSITE TOP Vase, ceramic, 1982.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM Lesbia Oceanica teapot, glazed ceramic, 1982.
RIGHT Stagione chairs, dressed for the four seasons, for Thonet, 1989.
BELOW Columbina Descendens teapot, glazed ceramic, from the Rara Avis collection, 1982. # MASANORI UMEDA b.1941 JAPANESE DESIGNER WHOSE INVENTIVE FURNITURE HAS PERSONALITY AND BROAD APPEAL.
Like many of his colleagues in Postmodernism, this Japanese-born designer transitioned easily from commercially produced objects to esoteric, avant-garde designs.
He was responsible for what is probably the most photographed—though certainly the least marketable—object in the panoply of MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") collections, the Tawaraya, an overscaled seating unit in the form of a boxing ring.
Famously, in the year of the group's founding the entire Memphis crew was photographed in one; that image of a group of revolutionaries reclining on a completely impractical piece of furniture has been reproduced countless times in publications all over the world.
Apart from the Tawaraya, Umeda's designs were only moderately functional, though consistently imaginative and enlivened with straight-faced humor.
Ginza (i982) is a bookshelf shaped like a robot, an appealingly decorative form perhaps best used for its ornamental value. since it holds relatively few books.
He also contributed ceramic objects to the Memphis collections, including the Orinoco vase and Parana bowl in ı983.
Trained at the Kuwasawa Design School in Tokyo, where he graduated in i962 with a diploma in design, Umeda then went to Italy, which was by then becoming the nucleus of design innovation.
He worked from 1967 to 1969 in the office of Achille and Pier Giacomo Castiglioni, brothers who were among the most respected of the postwar generation of Italian modern designers, and from 197o to 1979 as a design consultant for Olivetti, where he met ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), who invited him to participate in Memphis's first collection.3º Tawaraya was the happy result.
Umeda left Italy and Memphis in ı986, returning to Japan and opening his firm U-Metadesign.
He designed several flower-shaped seating pieces for Edra\* in i99o, which reveal the same ability to infuse objects with personality as do his Memphis designs—but these later works, for a producer requiring saleable goods, are comfortable as well as eye-catching.
He has also designed a bank interior, participated in international design competitions, and has won awards in Japan and Germany.
RIGHT Orinoco vase, ceramic, for Memphis, 1983.
BELOW Parana fruit bowl, ceramic, for Memphis, 1983.

## # MARCO
ZANINI b.1954 ITALIAN ARCHITECT AND DESIGNER KNOWN FOR CERAMICS AND GLASS PIECES, CONTRASTING COLORS.
WITH PLAYFUL SILHOUETTES AND Although he was trained as an architect and has designed a number of furniture pieces, Italian Marco Zanini is best known for the shapely and colorful ceramic and glass objects he designed for MEMPHIS (see under "Edited Design") in the i98os.
His appealing pieces had a whimsical charm and their almost anthropomorphic forms were a counterpoint to the angularity of most postmodern design.
Zanini studied architecture at the University of Florence, graduating in i978, but while still a student worked freelance for companies in Los Angeles and San Francisco.
After meeting ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") at a workshop organized by the Radical Design group Global Tools\*, he was invited to join Sottsass Associati.
From there he was recruited, along with fellow employees ALDO CIBIC, GEORGE SOWDEN, and MATTEO THUN, to become one of the original members of the Memphis collaborative.
He was among the most prolific of the group, showing work at all of the annual exhibitions, from the Dublin sofa in i98ı to Roma, a plastic armchair suggesting a classical throne, in 1986.
Zanini's well-received glassware designs were unorthodox combinations of conflated shapes and contrasting colors.
They include the Cassiopea vase (i98o) and Alpha Centauri (982), which layered segments óf clear and blue glass.
Moving to ceramics, he continued to play with form, combining two silhouettes in a single piece and sometimes topping them with a cockscomb motif, as in the Baykal vase (i982).
In i983 he created three intriguingly shaped teapots.
Colorado, Sepik, and Mississippi, all more likely to be used as decorative accessories than as functional objects.
In contrast to the raucous colors of most Memphis furniture, Zanini's ceramics and glass were usually pastel-toned, perhaps to temper the disconcerting effect of their quixotic silhouettes.
While at Sottsass Associati, Zanini designed a variety of objects, including jewelry, graphics, industrial machinery, and accessories.
He also worked with Sottsass on store designs for the American firm Esprit\*, a popular chain of stylish, mid-priced clothing stores that in the i98os made use of postmodern interiors and graphics to establish an avant-garde brand identity.
Throughout his career, Zanini has taught design and architecture at a number of universities and schools in several European countries.

## GRAPHIC
DESIGN BELOW David Carson's cover of the first issue of Blue magazine, 1991.
On the face of it, Postmodernism in graphic design would seem to have no particular relation to the style in archi tecture.
But the two are almost inextricably connected.
Both were rebelling against a restrictive establishment and a style born at the Bauhaus, both turned to historical precedent for inspiration, and, perhaps most important, both sought to assert the importance of their practice as means of communication.
Postmodernism, in every area, erased the boundaries between high and low culture, melding the vernacular and high art to suit a diverse and multicultural society.
This was particularly apparent in graphic design, where old rules were broken, experiments ran rampant, and weird became acceptable.' Unlike furniture, a printed piece did not have to stand up, hold things, or support weight, but just had to be noticed, and type's only function was to be readable, though sometimes legibility seemed to take a back seat to special effects.² Graphic design as a profession had not existed before the mid-twentieth century; practitioners in what would become that field were called commercial artists, and worked for design studios, as freelancers, or for advertising agencies, designing relatively conventional works to suit conventional clients.
Those who sought more creative outlets turned to fine art and galleries, or sold their work privately.
The prestigious Switzerland schools that taught typography (as derived from the Bauhaus, or what became designated as Swiss Style) and their American counterparts at Yale and a few other colleges were thought of as trade schools; the skills they taught were considered applied, not fine, arts.
The postmodern revolution in graphic design began—and continued--on a parallel path with the Modernism practiced by midcentury (and later) designers like Paul Rand, Herbert Bayer, and Massimo Vignelli.
Emerging in small cohorts of like-minded rebels, some European-trained and some converting by chance or circumstance to the feld, they applied their talents to whatever came their way.
The most interesting opportunities were in designing pop-culture artifacts like album covers, posters, and magazines that, in the days before the Internet and social media, became the key forms of communication for a generation of street-wise young rebels.
Combinations of vintage and new typefaces, bright colors, and asymmetric designs that mixed images and type had transgres sive appeal and seemed an ideal expression of the zeitgeist.
For the designers, these vehicles were personal showcases and promotion pieces, as well as a means to support a career in an uncertain new profession that until the i96os lacked even a name.
Graphic design is more closely allied with commercial interests than other disciplines.
In being inseparably linked to selling, it is not considered as culturally significant as architecture or industrial design.
It is also generally short-lived—paper tends to be read and discarded—but that is precisely what makes it an immediate reflection of its time, and even an influence on public taste.
Postmodernism as it developed in graphic design saw a revival of historical imagery, typefaces that had been abandoned by Modernism's allegiance to sans serif, collage and mixed-up styles, clashing colors, broken or # Postmodernism as it developed in graphic design saw a revival of historical imagery...
TOP LEFT Barney Bubbles, lan Dury and the Blockheads record sleeve, 1979.
TOP RIGHT David Carson, Beach Culture cover, 1990.
CENTER RIGHT Neville Brody, The Face cover, 1982. jagged lines, and copy splashed at random across a page— often with a heavy dose of irony thrown in.
Rarely was it ideologically motivated, except in its commitment to individuality.
The new aesthetic spread through a small network of publications, some—like Emigre (i984-2o05), Typographische Monatsblätter\* (TM, 1933-), and Push Pin Graphic (i957–8o)—specifically dedicated to the field, and others—like The Face (i98o-2oo4) and Ray Gun (i992-200o)—reflecting lifestyle and culture.
Although some designers began exploring new directions as early as the i96os, postmodern graphic design began to take hold only after the introduction of the Macintosh computer in i984.
The Macintosh introduced a new visual language and enabled unique graphic effects that were previously possible only in film.
It also saved time: freed from tedious cutting, pasting, and typesetting, designers could spend their time being creative.
Though many designers resisted its use, fearing that they might be replaced by technology, the most forward-thinking among them saw the potential for expanded personal expression.
The software not only gave designers new options, but facilitated collaboration over distances, even internationally.
As it developed, postmodern graphic design had no unifying theme or stylistic standards but represented a new attitude toward the practice, and a new visibility for its practitioners.
As its influence grew to incorporate public signage and corporate branding, graphic designers came to see themselves as communicators rather than merely agents for expressing a client's message, and the works they produce have increasingly been influential elements of the contemporary environment.
To paraphrase Marshall McLuhan, the medium became a critical part of the message,3 if not the message itself. # The PoMo Brouhaha Steven Heller The clash between Modernism and Postmodernism (also known as PoMo) triggered a seismic shift in culture, and particularly art and design, during the final third of the twentieth century.
It also caused plenty of confusion as to what Postmodernism actually represented: ideology or style, neither, or both.
In graphic design, PoMo was less quake than natural transformation-as took place in pop music from, say, the era of big bands and young Frank Sinatra to rock 'n' roll and The Beatles; in other words, a generational adjustment in popular taste influenced by everything from aesthetics to technology to marketing.
The modern versus postmodern brouhaha was not nearly as cataclysmic as the conflict of the early twentieth century, before the real modern revolution had lost steam, when kindred anti-establishment movements known throughout Europe by their evocative names— Vorticist, Futurist, Dadaist, and so on—were lumped together under a single modernist banner.
Modernism was the comet that wiped out the dinosaurs; yet it gave way to rote modernistic styling, notably in advertising and graphic design.
This made Postmodernism inevitable.
Postmodernism began in architecture as critiques by disillusioned modernists that quickly spread to other disciplines during the late 197os and developed into a process.
Postmodernism rejected Bauhausian rigidity and geometric precision in favor of more fluid, alluring forms, and encouraged the reintroduction of ornament and ostentatious space, sometimes spiced with selfmocking wit.
Modernism's potential to enliven advertising and editorial genres was incalculable.
The Second World War, however, put a stop to experimental Modernism, and when the war ended, Modernism's priorities became commerce-oriented.
Postmodernism's impact on graphic design, and vice versa, was at first inconsequential because Modernism was (and still is) widely practiced in its midcentury form.
Even today the two isms share some common traits.
In the late 194os through the early 196os, during the periods of Abstract Expressionism, Pop, and Op art, the International Style of graphic design, known for its elemental economy and typographic rigidity, inherited the Bauhaus mantle.
Taught in the 195os at the Kunstgewerbeschule in Zurich and the Schule für Gestaltung Basel, the pedagogy stretched far beyond its borders, to Yale in New Haven, Philadelphia University of the Arts, Illinois Institute of Technology (New Bauhaus), and other American art colleges.
The primary visual characteristics included sans serif type (primarily Akzidenz-Grotesk, Helvetica, and Univers), generous amounts of white space, geometric iconography, and high-contrast photography.
Yet even Swiss-inspired Modernism did not come from a mold.
Distinct attributes defined certain countries and their respective designers.
American modernists, among them Paul Rand, Lestęr Beall, Saul Bass, and Bradbury Thompson, allowed playful instincts to emerge.
To see where the two approaches merged to create hybrids, one need look no further than the incipient neomodern insurgences that prevailed during the i96os in Eastern Europe.
Through the introduction of conceptual thinking, surreal illustration, and expressive lettering, # The EndofimintCORSON DA IS O O FEBRU-SARESEN N F R A NO15SO CALIFORN7OOPEG THE AMERICANI.S TI TU TE O F chronicle books RA P HIC A R T + THE ·SA" F RAN CENTEREARETHE 700 howard street at third ABOVE Poster by Neville Brody, 1981.
Iron Curtain nations like Poland, Czechoslovakia, and Hungary were hotbeds of innovative cultural, film, theater, and art posters that both echoed and diverged from Western European Modernism.
Work by Polish designers like Henryk Tomaszewski and Roman Cieślewicz and the Czech Zdeněk Ziegler were respected by admiring Westerners through feature articles in the Zurich-based magazine Graphis, edited by Walter Herdeg.
Postmodernism did not entirely supplant Modernism but coincided with and sometimes smashed into it—and, like atoms in a collider, produced another, sometimes explosive style.
Postmodernism arose in the mid-i98os when Tibor Kalman, founder of New York-based M&Co., began designing with so-called vernacular or untutored forms (the tasteless stuff that drove modernists out of their skins), like Chinese menu design, as a means of protesting what he called an elitist modernistic professionalism.
He did not call himself postmodern, but railed against corporate Modernism: "[Mly overall worry about the design business is whether as a group we are becoming overly influenced by money and professional success," he argued in 2oı1, "and whether that's impinging on our ability to criticize our clients and make an impact on the world and as a group influence culture."4 The corporate system that Modernism served was being critiqued by a new generation.
This was one reason why, when Apple's 1984 television commercial announced that the Macintosh was the next major design tool, fearless designers went computer-crazy.
The power of the computer implied power for designers and signaled new opportunities, postmodernizing design through primitive techniques like pixelating, overlapping, underprinting, and serendipitous digital flaws that soon became de rigueur.
The new digital tools were boons that allowed postmodernists to play with illegibility and engage in other such typographical sins.
Postmodernism, which even the curators of the 2oıl exhibition “Postmodernism: Style and Subversion, 1970-1990" at the Victoria and Albert Museum refused to categorically define, was incorporated into graphic design long before the term was coined.
Setting aside for a moment the PoMo theory and pedagogy of the 198os and 199os, even in the late 1940s and 1950s modernists battled against renegades who rejected or ignored the changes in design form that modern designers had accomplished.
But the battle lines were sometimes blurry.
Paul Rand was a devoted form-follows-function modernist who made photocollage and cut-paper illustrations that might seem postmodern today by comparison with the more rigid work of his Swiss colleagues.
Although his design was not as risqué as those of, for example, NEVILLE BRODY, APRIL GREIMAN, or David Carson\*, Rand did not always adhere strictló to minimalism.
Dan Friedman\*, who studied at the Hochschule für Gestaltung Ulm—a hotbed of orthodox Modernism—and later at the Schule für Gestaltung Basel, with both the rebellious WOLFGANG WEINGART and modernist Armin Hofmann, called his later work "radical modernism"⁵ because it contained eccentric RIGHT April Greiman, business cards for Vertigo, 1979. visual traits and color play that later suggested a strain of Postmodernism.
Even earlier, the late i96os rock-poster master Victor Moscoso, who studied with modernist guru Josef Albers at Yale, ignored all the modern rules of form and function in work that could arguably be called postmodern, but was referred to as psychedelic.
And of course, Push Pin Studios, the first of the design and illustration studios of the 195os and 196os, proudly rejected the sterility of Swiss Modernism's limiting typo-pictorial guidelines.
Milton Glaser and SEYMOUR CHWAST, Push Pin's co-founders, had proto-postmodernist leanings, and Glaser has admitted that they rejected "the strong authoritarianism of modernism."6 Then there was Herb Lubalin, who became known for smashed and overlapping typography that was resolutely contemporary but avoided modernist rigidity.
His work was not postmodern, but today it might be considered so.
Asked to define Modernism, Paul Rand said, “It means integrity; it means honesty; it means the absence of sentimentality and the absence of nostalgia; it means simplicity; it means clarity."7 Postmodernism, presumably, was the opposite of all that.
By the late i98os and i99os the new generation of PoMo designers finally had a soapbox.
Emigre magazine, the voice of new type and digital typography edited by Rudy VanderLans, was dedicated to showcasing a coterie of avid contra-modernists.
In 1995 in a story for Emigre, Jeffery Keedy decried “Zombie Modernism" as a walking dead style.
But whatever the next thing was, Postmodernism—or the New Wave, Newer Wave, and so on—was an enigma.
In his review of the V&A exhibition in Eye magazine, design critic Rick Poynor wrote: “any attempt to reduce such an intricate set of cultural factors across so many kinds of art, design and media to a brief, all-encompassing definition would be a misrepresentation.' ″8 Perhaps PoMo is a bracket within which is anything that does not fit or is contrary to the definition of Modernism.
Arguably, there is a lot of unintentionally un-modern design from the period spanning 1970-20oo.
Punk, for instance, was a visceral approach that defied the rules of all proper design but was a slap in Modernism's face in particular.
It is rarely put in the PoMo category; whereas what is sometimes called grunge, which is a lot like punk in its defacement of classical design, is considered postmodern by some.
The work of ART CHANTRY, the celebrated punk and post-punk era poster designer, sometimes falls into PoMo because he employs vernacular type and imagery that routinely bust the grid, and uses vintage stock art.
Stock cuts characterize the styles of Charles S.
Anderson\*, who seized the monopoly on vintage commercial art clichés, which he refined and retrofitted.
His work fits squarely into a retro style that signals a nostalgia anathema to orthodox Modernism and leans toward Postmodernism.
Retro came into focus when Philip B.
Meggs, the pioneering graphic design historian, wrote the article "The Women Who Saved New York."9 It featured four women, leaders of the retro "movement" PAULA SCHER, Louise Fili, CARIN GOLDBERG, and Lorraine Louie, who were each dipping into design history for inspiration.
The story focused on the rise of women and the fact that men had dominated the modernist field; and it also supported the notion that retro fell chronologically and aesthetically under the postmodern umbrella.
Consequently, other postmodern women designers began to get publicity in the trade press.
Many, in fact, were modernist-trained designers who were making turns toward postmodern aesthetics; among the most visible was DEBORAH SUSSMAN, who studied at the New Bauhaus and worked at the Charles and Ray Eames Studio, and in i984 created a postmod ern graphic identity for the Los Angeles Olympics, in a style some critics dubbed California Modern.
And, after working for Unimark (co-founded by modernist extraor dinaire Massimo Vignelli), KATHERINE MCCOY took over the graphic design department at Cranbrook Academy of Art, which was a wellspring of designers who helped defne postmodern-Deconstructivist style.
Influenced by the architects ROBERT VENTURI and DENISE SCOTT- BROWN's postmodernist theories (see under “Masters UCLA Extension : Summer Quarter begins June 26, 1993 ABOVE Deborah Sussman, catalog cover for UCLA Extension courses, 1993. of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), the study of semiotics, and, later, the French philosophical and literary theoretical movements poststructuralism and deconstruction, McCoy's work and teaching argued that “visual phenomena are analyzed as language encoded for meaning.
Meanings are deconstructed, exposing the dynamics of power and the manipulation of meaning."º Cranbrook under McCoy was the theoretical sweet spot of generation PoMo.
It is important to realize that new technol ogy increased the power of women in design, and the number of postmodern women as well.
April Greiman was one of postmodern graphic design's pioneers.
A student of Wolfgang Weingart, she had been entrenched in Modernism until 1984, when the Macintosh was introduced.
Greiman took the leap of creating digital collages that commingled video, still photography, and type.
Her mixed media work was known as New Wave in the design press but it also fell under the PoMo banner: "She had been rocking the Modernist boat for a few years when she undertook a major assault upon the design community's sensibilities and preconceptions of what constitutes design in i986, in an issue of Design Quarterly." Greiman was the subject and designer of the Walker Art Center's Design Quarterly issue 133: an opportunity for Greiman not only to present her digital work “but to ask a larger question of the work and the medium: Does it make sense?
Reading Wittgenstein on the topic, she identified with his conclusion: 'It makes sense if you give it sense.'"1² Her influence in multimedia design continues to have far-reaching implications.
Digital typeface design was a postmodern outlier, and no one captured the essence of the 198os and 199os better than Zuzana Licko, the designer of such early digital fonts as the pixelated Lo-Res, Citizen, and the dot matrix-styled Matrix.
“I'm sure that being free of preconceived notions regarding typeface design helped me in exploring this new medium to the fullest," Licko told Eye in 2oo2, "It's interesting how the gradual sophistication of my type design abilities has been matched by advances in the Apple Macintosh's capabilities, so it has continued to be the ideal tool for me."13 Dozens of her typefaces, both precise and grungy classical and novel, helped in some instances to pinpoint the moment of conception of postmodern graphic typography.
Today the banners still wave, but modern tenets and postmodern theatrics coexist separately as well as together; Sinatra and The Beatles exist on the same playlist, so to speak, along with disco, punk, and hip hop—as well as cross-genre combinations and more.
The modern versus postmodern discourse may occasionally prompt a critical or theoretical debate—like the difference between readability and legibility—but the polarization that spiced up the earlier discourse is over.
Yet for the sake of history, we hope that it is not forgotten.
