Here are gathered, quite obsessional, French currents of # MICHAEL BIERUT b.1957 AMERICAN GRAPHIC DESIGNER WHOSE STRIKING DESIGNS COMBINE LEGIBILITY WITH HUMOR.
If he hadn't happened on a book about graphic design in a small school library in suburban Ohio, Michael Bierut might never have found his way to what was then a relatively new profession.
To him, Aim for a Job in Graphic Design/Art suggested that there might be a career in art-making that actually had a function in the outside world, rather than leaving one languishing in the studio or cloistered in a museum.
He immediately decided that was what he wanted to do.
He enrolled in the University of Cincinnati, graduating in ı98o from the graphic design program at its College of Design, Architecture.
Art, and Planning.
After an internship with a Boston public television station, he moved to New York for his first real job, with Vignellı Associates.
Massimo Vignelli, with his wife and business partner Lella Vignelli, had emigrated from Italy to become a leading name in the design profession and was known for graphics with a spare, modernist aesthetic, a strictly limited color palette (mostly black, white, and red), and an allegiance to the Helvetica typeface.
Bierut remained at Vignellı for ten years, becoming vice president of design, but developed a broader design vocabulary than that of his mentor, expressing himself in work for friends and non-profit groups.
When it came to corporate projects, his goal was not only to make the printed pieces legible, but to make them something people actually wanted to read.
He became as much a communicator as a designer, with the result being that his designs are invariably eye-catching and often sparked with wry humor.
Bierut is a compulsive collector of information, always armed with a schoolboy's notebook in which he jots down ideas, inspirations, and interesting things he hears through the course of a day.
That accumulated knowledge, which has filled more than one hundred notebooks, informs the work he produces.
"Being interested in things," he has said, "makes you a better designer."14 Bierut's work became more varied as he explored the possibilities offered by the computer, which came into use just as a postmodernist worldview was making unconventional design acceptable.
His flexibility of style brought him assignments from both luxury and mass-market clients.
Though not generally considered transgressive, he is postmodern in his versatility and his openness to new ways of doing things.
In 1990, Bierut became the youngest partner in Pentagram, one of the world's leading graphic design firms, where he has continued to design striking logos and graphics for larġe corporations including United Airlines and DISNEY (see under "Edited Design"), educational institutions such as Yale and Princeton universities, and progressive groups such as the Architectural League of New York, along with promotional pieces for sports, and book jackets.
He has combined his design work with teaching and writing; as a founder and frequent contributor to the respected online journal Design Observer.
Bierut sees graphic design as “art that can do things;" his art does.

BELOW RIGHT Brochure for Yale School of Architecture, 1999. # NEVILLE BRODY b.1957 BRITISH DESIGNER WHOSE EXPERIMENTAL IDEAS LED TO INNOVATIVE MAGAZINE DESIGN.
Neville Brody has said that he was "drawing before I could even walk."'5 His first graphic design project, made in grade school, was a brand identity for an imaginary postal service.
Choosing graphic design over other art careers, he saw its potential as a means of communication that could influence the way people think, and what they think about.
Brody, however, declined to limit himself by specializing in a single area.
He has worked in print and digital, branding and environmental design, and typography.
In all these he has been more insurgent than compliant, discarding traditional printing techniques to experiment with type and visual effects.
The result has been a career that made him one of the best-known and most innovative designers of his generation.
Brody attended Hornsey College of Art and the London College of Printing, opening his own studio shortly after leaving.
His first assignments were album covers for trendsetting bands like Depeche Mode and Cabaret Voltaire, and these highly visible projects led to assignments in other fields.
Though influenced by predecessor art movements like Dada and Constructivism, Brody shaped an aesthetic of his own.
Brody is most often linked with The Face, an iconoclastic publication launched in i98o that documented London's burgeoning youth culture, reporting on the commingled art, fashion, celebrity, and music scenes and becoming a trendsetting instrument in itself.
The Face spoke to a generation that disdained the standards of establishment tastemakers, adopting an aesthetic that was more mass-market than upper class.
As the magazine's art director from i98 to i986, Brody upended the conventions of stuffy periodical design, with hand-drawn or mixed-up typefaces and oddly cropped images.
The in-your-face, punk-influenced style established an unforgettable visual identity that became the prototype of what a modern magazine could (and should) look like.
Following this, Brody became art director of another hip publication, Arenα (i987–9o), and has since worked on several others.
Brody founded The Studio, his Londonbased firm, in ı987 and began working with computers to continue his ongoing exploration of typography and graphic design.
The Graphic Language of Neville Brody, published in 1988 in connection with a retrospective exhibition at London's Victoria and Albert Museum, became a bestseller.
In 199ı he launched the FUSE project, publishing experimental typefaces and posters and organizing conferences dealing with new concepts in typography and communications.
In 201o Brody was named Dean of the Royal College of Art's School of Communication.
His commitment to experimental ideas endures; "Design shouldn't have to make money," he said in 2016, "it can just be out there doing great stuff."16 ABOVE LEFT Cover for Cabaret Voltaire, James Brown, 12", Some Bizarre/Virgin Records, 1984.

## ART
CHANTRY b.1954 SEATTLE DESIGNER WHO AVOIDS COMPUTERS IN HIS INFLUENTIAL COLLAGE-TYPE POSTER ART.
Art Chantry has been called the "poster boy of poster art,"'7 since perhaps his most provocative and attention-getting work has been in the form of posters, which he has been making since he was a teenager.
Most closely associated with the underground music and theater scene in Seattle, his work reflects a variety of influences ranging from outsider art to comic books, psychedelic culture and avant-garde European design.
Born in Seattle and raised in Tacoma, Washington.
Chantry received his bachelor's degree from Western Washington University in Bellingham, graduating in i978, and returned to Seattle, which was then the center of a burgeoning subculture of punk rock music and avant-garde theater.
Though his degree was in painting. he rejected fine art in favor of graphics, building a reputation in the i98os with his album covers and posters for Washington-based record companies Sub Pop and Estrus as well as desięns for books and publications like the Rocket, a newspaper covering the local music scene.
For the latter, where he was art director in i985, his pages had a chaotic look that belied their meticulous planning, and for one issue he moved the masthead from its usual position, turning it upside down at the foot of the page.
Over a career of more than forty years, he has studiously avoided the mainstream, in his techniques as well as his design.
While many of his contemporaries use computers in tandem with low-tech tools, and others have adopted the computer exclusively, Chantry does neither.
He rejects computers altogether in favor of an entirely hands-on approach with old-fashioned studio tools and techniques.
Often, his work does not seem "designed," but simply assembled from an assortment of unrelated elements brought together in chance encounters.
That is far from the case, and the precisely planned results are always original, often provocative, and sometimes disturbing in their in-your-face assertiveness.
He puts his designs together by assemblage and collage, combining type, photos, drawings, and colors to make a statement that suits the project, and the client.
Though his work is not definably postmodern in style, he has embraced the designation, believing that all designers are essentially postmodernists in that they appropriate ideas from the past: "We put those ideas into new contexts and juxtapositions to create new ideas...none of the ideas we toss around are new."18 Chantry refuses to equate his profession with fine art; indeed, he believes that design is more influential, more powerful, and ultimately more dangerous in its ability to manipulate consumers.
A provocative spokesman for his profession, he has written and lectured extensively about his ideas.
His career was documented in a monograph, Some People Can't Surf: The Graphic Design of Art Chantry (2ooi),'9 and his often controversial opinions are captured in Art Chantry Speaks: A Heretic's History of 20th Century Graphic Design (2015).

RIGHT "Ready for War" poster for Alonzo Critinı Salon Politique, 1982. # romeo ALONZO CRETIN'S SALON POLITIQUE9:25 T0 109602 19TH AVE EASTOPENING 7PM 9-25 # SEYMOUR CHWAST b.1931 NEW YORK-BORN DESIGNER KNOWN FOR HIS VIVID ILLUSTRATIONS AND CARTOON IMAGERY.
As co-founder of Push Pin Studios, one of the best-known graphic design firms of the midcentury vears.
Seymour Chwast was a key player in the development of graphic design as a valid means of artistic expression and as a sophisticated communications tool.
He pioneered the transition from strict realistic imagery and Swiss formalism to an altogether different type of art, using cartoons and visual puns, linking illustration and typography, and paving the way for what would blossom into postmodernist graphic design, In a career encompassing more than six decades, he has produced thousands of influential designs in magazines, posters, book jackets, album covers, advertisements, packaging, and children's books.
Much of Chwast's early work anticipated Postmodernism's rejection of established norms, avoiding the conventional look of advertising and package design that had prevailed since the 192os He replaced it with a vocabulary of simple forms, bold color, and almost always humor, to make a point beyond what was written in the headline.
In the i96os the Push Pin look, emanating from Chwast and his partners Milton Glaser and Edward Sorel, was ubiquitous.
New Yorker Chwast attended art classes while still in grade school, and some of his most yalued training came from a public school with an exceptional graphic design course.
He went on to study illustration and design at Cooper Union, where he met his future partners.
Chwast's early jobs included stints at the New York Times, Esquire, and Condé Nast, but the three classmates decided that opening their own studio held greater promise, and established Push Pin in 1954.
The partners collaborated on design projects but worked individually on editorial illustration.
Their publication, the Push Pin Graphic, was begun in i957 initially as a promotion piece, but it became an influential periodical that presented radical new ideas to the design community.
It continued for twenty-three years and a total of eighty-six issues, becoming a bimonthly in 1976 and ending publication in ı98o.
Even with the diversity of design emanating from Push Pin, Chwast's own hand is almost unmistakable.
His work reflects influences from Victorian, Art Nouveau, and Art Deco styles, filtered through a contemporary sensibility but undeniably rejecting conventional modernist aesthetics.
His eye-catching posters helped to elevate the genre to a collectible art form, and throughout his career he has produced countless sketches, drawings, and limited-edition prints.
His work, and that of Push Pin, though more populist than elitist, was nevertheless the subject of a 1970 exhibition at the Musée des Arts Décoratifs in Paris, where it garnered accolades from critics and public alike LEFT Drawing of imaginary car for Ettore Sottsass, from a series for Frankfurter Allgemeine Magazin, 1988.
Sorel left Push Pin in i956 to work independently; Glaser opened his own office in 1970; and in i98ı Chwast established the expanded partnership that became the Pushpin Group, which moved into broader areas of packaging and corporate design.
Even in his corporate work, Chwast's originality and irrepressible sense of humor prevail.

2, 1967. # ED FELLA b.1938 DETROIT NATIVE WHOSE IMPROVISATIONALGRAPHICS MIX TYPE, PHOTOGRAPHY,DRAWING, AND COLLAGE.
Fella spent three decades working as a commercial artist in his native Detroit before changing direction to a creative pursuit that had not existed when he entered the world of work.
When he finally found his métier, he embarked on a teaching career that influenced a generation or more of graphic designers.
Over almost half a century, Fella has produced a provocative body of work that helped propel the transition of graphic design from rigid Modernism to convention-flouting Postmodernism.
"I'm a twentieth century postmodern designer, from the middle of the last century," he claims.2º Fella's original art training took place in high school, where he was taught the Bauhaus foundation method, an avant-garde aesthetic in an era when Beaux Arts was still dominant.
Working as a commercial artist (the term graphic designer did not come into use until the i96os), he produced competent but conventional advertising and promotion pieces for a variety of clients, while experimenting on his own to develop a more personal, eclectic style that he could use to solicit freelance assignments.
A voracious reader, he incorporated influences from historic documents and printing techniques into works he called "flyers." These were I1 × 17in.
(28 × 43.2cm), two-sided mini-posters that folded into thirds like self-mailers.
He used these as promotion pieces distributed to attendees at art events or lectures, and later to market his own work.
A single flyer might incorporate photography, collage, and idiosyncratic mixtures of type fonts in an approach that took its cue from Deconstructivism but had his own quixotic tweak.
Fella modified typefaces and combined elements from printed matter with drawing and hand-lettering, using photostats, photocopiers, and X-Acto knives to create effects that prefigured what would become common practice in the age of computers.
Closer to art than graphic design, Fella's work is highly improvisational and visually arresting, but also more than meets the eye—hidden allusions and interjected sentence fragments mean that it requires close reading to be fully appreciated.
By the late i97os, Fella's reputation was growing through exposure in design journals and print anthologies.
He became part of the community of avant-garde designers who convened around Cranbrook Academy of Art in Bloomfield Hills, Michigan, visiting the school to show his work to students in KATHERINE MCCOY's graphic design classes.
Finally leaving his commercial career at the age of forty-seven, he entered the school's MFA program to formalize his credentials and, he has said, to avoid empty-nest syndrome, as his children were leaving for college.
After graduating in i987 Fella began teachirg at the prestigious California Institute of the Arts, and ultimately became more celebrated in his second career than he had been in his first.
He continued to produce idiosyncratic posters, scrapbooks, and photography, winning recognition from colleagues, professional organizations, and major museums.
He has retired, but remains Professor Emeritus at CalArts and has acquired a new following of young designers and design aficionados through his regular postings and chatty commentary on his website edfella-yestoday.com.

## CARIN
GOLDBERG b.1953 NEW YORK DESIGNER WHOSE BOOK JACKETS BROUGHT INNOVATIVE GRAPHICS TO A WIDE AUDIENCE.
Carin Goldberg is known for her ingenious use of type to create images and her ability to work at both high art and mass-market level; both have served her well in a career that includes the design of, according to the artist, "a zillion book covers."2ı A native New Yorker, she grew up on Long Island and New Jersey and studied at Cooper Union, where she majored in painting, since graphic design was not yet a strong component of the school's program.
She graduated in i975 and, like several other prominent graphic designers of her generation, began her career in the communications industry.
She was sent for an interview at CBS, where, despite lacking a design portfolio, she was hired as a junior designer to do the advertising for its TV Guide.
Moving to CBS Records, she designed for all levels of the music market, from rock 'n' roll to jazz to classical album covers, and gained further experience at Atlantic Records and Mademoiselle magazine.
After she established her own firm in i982, one of Goldberg's early commissions was from Warner Bros.
Records for the cover of Madonna's debut album, and she continued to design for labels including Motown, Nonesuch, and EMI.
Looking to diversify, and interested in work that would be visible in many retail outlets as opposed to those intended for a narrow target audience,²² she began designing book jackets—perhaps a natural transition—and rapidly built a reputation in that important category, designing more than 125 covers a year for virtually every major American publisher, including Simon & Schuster, Random House, Alfred A.
Knopf, HarperCollins, and Doubleday, as well as small university presses.
In the era when most reading matter was on paper, the book jacket was the most effective stimulus for sales.
Goldberg has designed books by authors as popular as Raymond Carver and Kurt Vonnegut, and as esoteric as Reiner Maria Rilke and Michel Foucault.
Her designs have no trademark style or preferred technique, but all make strong visual statements, with arresting and evocative imagery that draws the eye to the title rather than the design itself.
Her book jacket for the 1986 reissue of James Joyce's Ulysses is one of her best-known designs; though it was somewhat controversial at the time for its use of retro type, it is indicative of her ability to meld historic influence with contemporary styles—a characteristic that puts her in the vanguard of postmodern graphic design. # thensanity of normality realism as sickness: toward understanding humandestructiveness RIGHT Sly & the Family Stone record cover, Epic Records, 1981.

## APRIL
GREIMAN b.1948 EARLY COMPUTER ADOPTER WHO HELPEDINTRODUCE SWISS NEW WAVE GRAPHICSTO AMERICA.
Calling herself a transmedia artist, a provocative term that doesn't begin to describe the intriguing range of her work, April Greiman has moved easily between hand and computer, making adventurous choices that have raised her to the top ranks of American graphic designers.
Among her important contributions to the profession has been her role as a cutting-edge progenitor of what was called California New Wave style A native New Yorker, Greiman wanted to attend Rhode Island School of Design but was directed instead to the graphic design program at Kansas City Art Institute.
At the time, she did not know what graphic design meant, but that did not stop her; she applied and was accepted.
Though trained in the prevailing International Style, she was inspired by her instructors at Kansas City to go beyond it, and traveled after her i97o graduation to Basel, Switzerland, for graduate work at the Allgemeine Künstgewerbeschule Basel (now Schule für Gestaltung Basel).
There, the innovative designer WOLFGANG WEINGART introduced her to a radical new approach that departed from the grid, introducing varied typefaces and type weights and broadening the communicative potential of typography.
This aesthetic would later evolve into New Wave, and Greiman would become one of his most influential students, helping to introduce the style in America.
In i976 she moved to Los Angeles— a courageous choice, since it meant leaving an established design community for one with a limited arts culture and little design identity.
It was an opportunity to chart new territory, and Greiman named her new studio Made in Space, reflecting her interest in transforming printed matter into something that occupied more than simply two dimensions.
For several years, she collaborated with Jayme Odgers in work for clients like the China Club and California Institute of the Arts.
In i982, CalArts asked her to head their graphic design program, and she lobbied to rename the department Visual Communications, to more clearly describe the potential scope of the field.
She left in 1984 to return to full-time practice.
Always intrigued by technology, Greiman was an early adopter of the computer as a tool of graphic design, helping to usher in a new era in the field.
While many of her contemporaries resisted computers, considering them a threat to handwork, she embraced them, making use of pixelation and other irregularities as integral elements of her graphic art.
She began to work with video as well, exploring combinations of video imagery with New Wave photography and elements of traditional design.
One of Greiman's most radical projects was for Design Quarterly, the influential journal published by Minneapolis's Walker Art Center.
Issue 133 (ı986), which she designed, presented her work in a radical format: instead of a thirtytwo-page magazine, she created a 3 × 6ft (90 × 18ocm) fold-out poster, composed using a Macintosh, that layered text and images, including video images and one of her own naked body.
Titled "Does It Make Sense?" the project encouraged designers to accept computers as integral to contemporary graphic design, or to an entirely new, more multidisciplinary and as yet unnamed practice of communication.

Los Angeles County Museum of Art ART IN LOS ANGELES Seventeen Artists in the Sixties The Museum as Site: S i x t e e n Projects # KATHERINE MCCOY b.1945 AMERICAN DESIGNER WHO HELPED REINVENT GRAPHIC DESIGN EDUCATION IN A TIME OF SOCIOCULTURAL CHANGE.
Katherine McCoy has made her mark on the graphic design profession as much by contributing to advancing education in the field as by creating her own envelope-pushing designs.
Born in Decatur, Illinois, McCoy first discovered industrial design during a visit to the Museum of Modern Art in New York with her family.
Deciding to pursue that profession, she studied at Michigan State University, graduating in i967 and moving to Chicago to take a job at Unimark, a leading design firm whose partners included committed modernist Massimo Vignelli.
There, she was exposed to the strict aesthetic of Swiss typographic design, the prevailing style of the time and a parallel to architecture's International Style.
After Unimark, she worked on corporate identity at Chrysler Corporation, then with the Boston firm Omnigraphics, and finally Designers & Partners, a Detroit advertising studio, alongside a variety of other graphic arts practitioners who were seeking to broaden the potential for innovative design in the field.
She then, along with her husband Michael, also an industrial designer, established her own practice, McCoy & McCoy.
In 97ı the McCoys were invited to co-chair the design program at Cranbrook Academy in Bloomfield Hills.
Michigan, the school that had become celebrated under the leadership of Finnish architect Eliel Saarinen and had produced graduates including Charles and Ray Eames, Eero Saarinen, Florence Knoll\*, and many others.
Katherine was responsible for the graphic design program, and Michael took over industrial design.
Upending conventional procedures, they reinvented the curriculum, eliminating exams and encouraging students to work independently and to experiment.
There were no classes, grades, or requirements other than a final thesis exhibition.
This entirely new, and ultimately very influential, approach to design education was very much in line with the social and cultural climate that was beginning to transform attitudes toward design in every area.
Influenced by ROBERT VENTURI and DENISE SCOTT BROWN (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") and Steven lzenour's Learning from Las Vegas and the avant-garde graphics of Dan Friedman\*, ED FELLA, and WOLFGANG WEINGART, McCoy also introduced a new approach to typography, one that included the study of semiotics.²3 She encouraged the use of historical and vernacular type forms that had been rejected by modernists.
Her objective was to educate students to make work that was not only theoretical but marketable, encouraging them to break new ground in the profession.
The graphics that McCoy designed for Cranbrook used collage and variations in type drawn from avant-garde precedents instead of doctrinaire Modernism; she also produced work for other clients, including a poster for Colorado Native American Heritage (i978) and a Fluxus book (i978).
She became active in trade groups, was the first woman to head the Industrial Designers Society of America (1983-85), and was on the advisory board of the American Institute of Graphic Arts (AIGA).
LEFT Cover of Visible Language journal, by Cranbrook students under direction of McCoy, 1978.

## PAULA
SCHER b.1948 DESIGNER, EDUCATOR, AND PENTAGRAM PARTNER WHO USED TYPE FOR DECORATIVE IMPACT.
As the first female member of the internationally known firm Pentagram, Paula Scher has broken new ground in more ways than one, creating memorable brand identities for major corporations like Coca-Cola and institutions including the Museum of Modern Art and the New York Public Theater, as well as teaching graphic design and, in her spare time, painting intricate map-and-text artworks that have been exhibited internationally.
Scher was born in Virginia and grew up knowing she wanted to be an artist, at a time when graphic design was barely identified as a profession beyond basic commercial art.
She took a traditional BFA at the Tyler School of Art in Philadelphia, graduating in i97o and moving to New York to begin her career.
She worked in the children's book division of Random House but, like several others who found their métier in graphic design, was given creative opportunities in the flourishing music industry.
After working for CBS Records, she was hired by Atlantic Records in 1974 as an art director and began designing album covers.
At a time when pop music was flourishing and MTV didn't exist, covers made the difference between a hit and a flop, and Scher's assertive designs were an immediate success.
Hired back by CBS, she designed ı5o albums a year over an eight-year period—an impressive output by any standard, and a well-received one as well; several of her designs won Grammy nominations.
Scher's graphics introduced a variety of historical typefaces rather than the standard modern forms then popular.
Her bold vocabulary, inspired in part by Russian Constructivism and Art Deco precedents, used contrasting typefaces, often set along the diagonal.
She was a pioneer in a new generation of graphic designers who used type for its decorative value as well as for communication.
After leaving CBS to establish her own practice, Scher went into partnership in 1984 with Terry Koppel, taking on a variety of assignments that included advertisements, packaging, and book jackets.
That year, she designed a now celebrated poster for Swatch, inspired by the work of Herbert Matter, the Swiss designer who became art director of Knoll\*.
She also contributed frequently to Print magazine.
In 199ı Koppel & Scher dissolved when Koppel became creative director at Esquire, ABOVE Advertisement for Swatch, 1984.
ABOVE RIGHT Letterhead for Manhattan Records, 1984.
RIGHT Record label for Manhattan Records, 1984. and Scher joined Pentagram, bringing her creative approach to a new roster of high-profile corporate clients.
At the same time, she began a two-decade teaching career at the School of Visual Arts, New York, and has also taught at Yale Cooper Union, and Tyler School of Art, passing on her knowledge to the next generation.
Extremely articulate in speech as well as print, she has lectured and written articles about her design philosophy, often noting that she considers her practice to be more like play than work.
Asked what she would do if şhe were not a designer Scher replied, "I don't know how to do anything else.
I'm completely unemployable."24

EAMES-TRAINED DESIGNER OF GRAPHICS WITH LIVELY IMAGES AND CALIFORNIA COLORS.
Deborah Sussman enjoyed a more diverse and more extensive education than most of her contemporaries: she took classes at the Art Students League of New York and Black Mountain College, then studied acting and painting at Bard College, before going to the Institute of Design in Chicago for a degree in graphic design;²5 she also has a Doctorate in Humane Letters from Bard.
She later interrupted her working life to study for a year at the Hochschule für Gestaltung Ulm, the prestigious art and design school in Germany.
Sussman's professional career began as art director in the California offices of Charles and Ray Eames.
"Of all designers in the world, I was with the best,"26 she said, and she worked with them for more than a decade, designing exhibitions, films, and furniture showrooms and absorbing Charles's enthusiasm and willingness to break new ground, and Ray's love of ornament and color.
She opened her own office in i968, and four years later married Paul Prejza, an architect and urban planner with whom she opened Sussman/ Prejza in i98o.
They took on assignments that moved beyond graphic design to incorporate environmental installations, turning a twodimensional practice into one more suited to a three-dimensional real world.
In i984 Sussman used cołor and temporary materials to create brilliant graphics for the Summer Olympics in Los Angeles, transforming the various venues around the city into telegenic attractions—this was the first Olympics to receive live television coverage.
Instead of the usual red, white, and blue color scheme, she chose a vibrant palette of hues inspired by the diversity of ethnic influences in Los Angeles, splashing colors like magenta, vermilion, turquoise, hot pink, and chrome yellow on cardboard arches, fabric banners and balloons, and using shipping-tube columns and scaffolding in lieu of permanent structures.
As early as ı97o, Sussman designed a shoe-store interior in colors that prefgured the luminous Olympics scheme.
She referred to her approach as "supergraphics," saying that it was "not just big, but bigger than the architecture."27 The idea, now widely used in retail, entertainment, and even corporate projects, was radical at the time.
Sussman was the first woman to exhibit in the School of Visual Arts' Masters Series awards show, and she received several major awards in graphics arts, in i988 becoming an honorary member of the American Institute of Architects.
She founded the Los Angeles chapter of the American Institute of Graphic Arts, and was a board member of the organization.
Through a career that included many types of design, for many types of clients, Sussman never lost the characteristics that pervaded every one of her projects: an irrepressible love of color and an abiding sense of fun.
LEFT Catalog cover for UCLA extension courses, Los Angeles, 1988.
LEFT Program cover and four brochures for the Hollywood Bowl, Los Angeles, 1970.
ABOVE Promotional materials for the Summer Olympics: award-winning program, showing visual alphabet and motifs adopted for sports venues, Los Angeles, 1984.
LEFT Signage for California City, a planned development in the Mohave Desert, 1967-68. # MICHAEL VANDERBYL b.1947 SAN FRANCISCO DESIGNER WHOSE WORK CROSSES DISCIPLINES.
FROM PURE MODERNISM TO TRANSGRESSIVE AVANT-GARDE.
Though some of his work seems to follow the tenets of classic Modernism, Michael Vanderbyl's career has seen him move effortlessly from its rigorous principles to less restrictive avant-garde design.
He refers to himself as an interdisciplinary designer, explaining, "I like to do it all."28 A native Californian, Vanderbyl graduated from the California College of Arts & Crafts (now California College of the Arts) in i968, and opened his own office in San Francisco in 1973 San Francisco had given birth to a youth culture that spawned underground graphics in comic books and psychedelic art, and a new generation of graphic designers who would bring it into the mainstream.
Vanderbyl was one of those who made the Bay Area a center of innovative graphi design in the postmodern years In moving from graphics and print into broader territory, he enhanced the public profile of his profession, helping to put it on a level with other disciplines more customarily classified as art.
He also helped to expand graphic design beyond its New York-centered base, as California New Wave concepts migrated east, introducing color and humor to serious, Swiss-based practices Although he was not the only designer to move in this direction, Vanderbyl was distinctive in his use of three-dimensional effects, something that may have evolved from his early interest in architecture.²9 He used the technique in work for Esprit\* and later in brochures and posters for Simpson Paper that featured objects by SWID POWELL (see essay under “Edited Design") and MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") and postmodern iconography, with a depth of imagery rarely seen in two-dimensional work.
Vanderbyl went on to design furniture, showrooms, decorative accessories, and fabrics, all with a consistent sense of style that draws on the principles of graphic design.
He often set bold images against fields of strong color, using type for titles and letting the illustrations tell the story.
His work is more classic than transgressive; always legible, crisp, and with a sense of humor that is subtly clever without being raucous.
This ability to bring an air of establishment substance to avant-garde style brought Vanderbyl commissions from major corporations to develop brand identity programs.
For Hickory Business Furniture (i983), a client unable to afford an architect, he conceived a showroom design that combined a postmodern vocabulary with high-concept Modernism and luxury materials, with arresting and awardwinning results.
Vanderbyl was Dean of Design at his alma mater for more than thirty years, encouraging students to follow his example by thinking of graphic design as extending far beyond the printed page.
He has also taught at Cranbrook Academy of Art, ArtCenter College of Design, the School of the Art Institute of Chicago, and other prestigious colleges, and lectures frequently on design and branding.
In addition to winning awards, his work has been acquired by several important museums.
Probably his most out-of-the-box project was created in 2ooı, when he designed the graphics for an America's Cup yacht—a most rewarding project for a longtime sailing enthusiast.
LEFT Poster for a lecture series at San Francisco Museum of Modern Art, co-sponsored by the San Francisco American Institute of Architects, 1986.
BELOW Poster for Simpson Paper, 1980.

BELOW Showroom for Bernhardt Furniture, New York City, 1986. # WOLFGANG WEINGART b.1941 GERMAN DESIGNER AND INFLUENTIAL TEACHER WHO PIONEERED NEW WAVE IN GRAPHIC DESIGN.
In the middle years of the twentieth century, Swiss style—a grid-based form founded on linearity, sans serif type, and elements drawn from Bauhaus-aesthetic typography—dominated the practice of graphic design.
A serious and academic approach, it focused on content, rather than distracting decoration.
Wolfgang Weingart is credited with changing that narrow view, opening up new possibilities for type to be regarded a design tool, graphic design an art form, and trade schools to be considered design institutions.
Discarding the constrictions of right-angle layouts, he rearranged formats, resurrected traditional typefaces that had been abandoned by the modern movement, and moved away from manual typesetting and the letterpress to using halftone film, collage, and offset printing as well as the new Xerox photocopier and, later, even the Macintosh.
His work introduced what came to be called Swiss Punk, and foreshadowed the New Wave in graphic design.
His advanced class in graphics at the Allgemeine Künstgewerbeschule Basel (now Schule für Gestaltung Basel) was an intensive, interdisciplinary program that encouraged creative expression and took an intellectual approach to what had been treated as a somewhat mechanical exercise.
Type weights varied, typefaces were mixed and set at irregular angles or in curved configurations, and the type shop was treated as a laboratory for experimentation.
Weingart was born in southern Germany and attended the Merz Akademie in Stuttgart, learning linocut and woodcut techniques.
Apprenticing as a typesetter, he was trained in the Swiss Style, but rebelled against the limitations of the strict regimen and began to seek a more creative direction.
He became interested in design, and traveled to Basel to study at its prestigious school of applied arts.3º In ı968, still virtually unknown, Weingart was put in charge of the typography class in a new postgraduate program in graphic design for professionals, established by Emil Ruder and Armin Hofmann.
He continued to teach the course for thirty-seven years.
Weingart's revolutionary approach drew students from thirty-five countries to a program that was sometimes called “anti-Swiss." and trained next-generation students including NEVILLE BRODY, David Carson\*, and APRIL GREIMAN, all of whom went on to pursue their own, innovative paths.
He also ran a summer program in graphic design for Yale and lectured internationally.
Independently, Weingart designed posters, covers, and graphics for Typographische Monatsblätter (TM)\*, a publication for typographers that he helped to transition into a design magazine when he was on the editorial board (i970–88).3 By questioning the formalism of existing type practices with questions like, "What is typography?
What is communication?
What is text?
What is an alphabet?"3² Weingart challenged his students, flouted tradition, and brought new life to a stodgy profession.
In My Way to Typography, published in 2ooo, he summed up his theories, and his career.
LEFT Poster for an educational fair in Basel, Switzerland, lithograph, 1981.
RIGHT AND BOTTOM RIGHT Cover designs for Typographic Process, 1974 and 1972.
BELOW "The Swiss Poster," poster, lithograph, 1984. # EDITED DESIGN Postmodern product design took two directions.
The first was in expensive, limited-edition pieces by major architects and designers, and more improvisational one-off objects by artisans, who could explore new possibilities without committing to a particular style.
The second was in machine-made production pieces that used the classical ornament associated with postmodern architecture as well as the ice-cream colors that elicited both enthusiasm and consternation regarding the style.
Though the architecture and design communities had followed Postmodernism from its earliest incarnations, it was only with its translation into functional objects that the style was assimilated into popular culture.
At first, the most successful of these objects were designed by architects, who already understood the principles behind the style.
The idea was not entirely new—architects such as C.
F.
A.
Voysey and Josef Hoffmann had designed products—but now architects were not designing to express their aesthetic impulses but working at the behest of producers, who were using the architects' celebrity as a marketing tool.
Most of these collaborations were successful; some extraordinarily so, making Postmodernism part of current fashion.
The earliest and costliest production pieces were, as could be expected, the most admired (and most admirable).
As it moved into more commercial categories, the quality of both production and design suffered, with commensurate damage to Postmodernism's image.
Objects like toasters, vacuum cleaners, mugs, and desk accessories, gussied up with postmodern colors and classical details, commercialized the style and made little design sense.
The only asset of this miscellany was its disposability.
Producers, however, were not necessarily interested in promoting the style; they were simply taking advantage of a marketing opportunity.
Market-savvy manufacturers realized that attaching "starchitect" names to their products would add fashion-forward cachet to otherwise conventional goods.
For the architects, designing on a much smaller scale was a challenging change of pace, though admittedly with less at stake.
Some produced what were in effect miniature buildings, or merely decorated existing forms, but others explored options not possible in their usual projects, with often felicitous results.
The Italian firm ALESSI was a pioneer in this area.
In i983 its Tea & Coffee Piazza project, with silver services designed by eleven prominent international architects, followed by other elegant serving accessories, established Alessi as a prestigious and forward-thinking firm, despite occasional complaints about elitism.
Of MICHAEL GRAVES's (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design” and “Living with Postmodern Design") iconic tea kettle, one critic mocked its elevation from utensil to “a symbol, a lightning rod for the economic and class aspirations of its owner.
That's yuppie pornography—the objectification of objects." The same year, FORMICA's competition to promote their ColorCore material produced FRANK GEHRY's (see under “Architecture") equally iconic fish sculptures, transforming Formica into a credible source for designers and architects.
OPPOSITE Robert Venturi for Design Tex, Yukata textile, C.!982.
RIGHT Dorothy Hafner for Rosenthal, Mars Landing vases, porcelain, 1984. # Postmodern styling eventually reached almost every area of the home and leisure market and...inspired countless new products.
In 1984 the American firm SWID POWELL brought a new focus on gracious dining in a fast-food era by commissioning dinnerware and flatware from a marquee-name roster of postmodernists (and late modernists).
Their urbane products were more restrained and less whimsically postmodern, however, avoiding any taint of trendiness or kitsch.
CLETO MUNARI, another Italian producer, introduced a collection of sterling silver objects by twenty high-profile architects in 1987, and later added a collection of fine modern jewelry by architectural stars.
The jewelry used strong color and assertive forms in historicist-influenced pieces that were unlike anything else on the market.
Textile designers welcomed the new freedom of expression, either adapting the eyeopening graphics of MEMPHIS designs or drawing motifs from pop sources and ethnic cultures.
New approaches to pattern and texture were made possible with computerized looms.
Japanese designers Junichi Arai, Issey Miyake, and Rei Kawakubo, and OPPOSITE TOP LEFT Alessandro Mendini for Cleto Munari, candlesticks, silver, C.
1985.
OPPOSITE TOP RIGHT Hans Hollein for Cleto Munari, architectural brooch, gold, lapis, 1985.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM LEFT Michele De Lucchi for Cleto Munari, coffee pot, silver, 1980.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM RIGHT Robert Venturi for Swid Powell, prototype Village tea and coffee service, porcelain, C.
1985.
Swiss producer Mira-X, experimented with plastic or metallic fibers, with results that transgressed any designation of style.
Postmodern styling eventually reached almost every area of the home and leisure market and, Memphis, in particular, inspired countless new products.
Lamps, many by the same architects who had created postmodern furniture, appeared in unconventional forms and lively colors, though were not always efficient for illumination.
Glassware sported quirky silhouettes and bright hues, dinnerware introduced vivid, if sometimes distracting, pattern, and clocks or containers often took the form of miniature buildings.
Postmodernism surfaced in a Hannukah menorah display at Jerusalem's Israel Museum in i986 in which ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), Peter Eisenman\*, and Richard Meier\* participated.
Michael Graves even designed a set of tools for DIYers.
In many of these cases, the objective was to 'package" products with fashion and eye appeal, but without consideration for function or production process.
Certainly the most successful venture into mass-market goods were the designer housewares commissioned by Target, which made Graves's Alessi tea kettle and PHILIPPE STARCK's (see under “Design") curvy toothbrush ambassa dors for avant-garde style at an affordable price.
In general, however, even the best-conceived products focused on a nařrow market of sophisticated consumers, or served as showy loss-leaders.
Most manufacturers regarded the style as superficial and certainly not concerned with functionality and, with few exceptions, postmodern products failed to make inroads beyond design-conscious homes and trendy commercial spaces.
“There are no postmodern cars," said Mario Bellini\* in a ı984 interview, “Fiat makes cars that are banal, but you can use them."² # ...with few exceptions, postmodern products failed to make inroads beyond design-conscious homes and trendy commercial spaces. # Swid Powell and the Postmodern Tabletop John Stuart Gordon “The age of architect-designed objects is upon us," declared the Chicago Tribune in i985, and few companies better embodied this trend in houseware than the American firm Swid Powell.
Its products transformed the kitchen counter and the dining table into miniaturized cityscapes populated by coffee pots, bowls, and other items that emulated the look and exemplified the theories of postmodern architecture.
Its highbrow yet accessible houseware encapsulated the era's desire for eye-catching prestige objects, its infatuation with architects, and its recalibration of what constituted good taste.
Swid Powell was established in New York in i982 by Nan Swid and Addie Powell.
They met while working at Knoll International\*, where they saw first-hand how interested architects were in designing domestic objects, such as ROBERT VENTURI and DENISE SCOTT BROWN's (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") line of chairs.
Realizing there might be a market for corresponding houseware, they presented their concept to a close-knit group of architects over lunch at the Four Seasons restaurant, with its famed modernist interior designed by Philip Johnson\*.
Swid Powell then expanded its list of potential architect-designers, asking them to create any type of houseware they wanted and inviting them to assess the submissions as a group.
“We were afraid they would be very clinical, harsh on one another," Swid recalled, "But exactly the opposite happened."3 Their shared enthusiasm for the project created camaraderie rather than competition.
The first Swid Powell collection of porcelain, glassware, and silver plate debuted in June 1984 with designs by Charles Gwathmey and Robert Siegel, ARATA ISOZAKI (see under “Architecture"), Richard Meier\*, Laurinda Spear, ROBERT A.
M.
STERN (see under "Architecture"), STANLEY TIGERMAN (see under “Architecture"), and Robert Venturi.
The roster eventually expanded to include FRANK GEHRY (see under "Architecture"), MICHAEL GRAVES (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), Zaha Hadid, and many others.
The company's promotional materials included photographs and biographies of the architect-designers and each object was marked with their signature or monogram.
Swid Powell presented its houseware as an opportunity for design aficionados of average means to “experience the aesthetic" of famous architects, but it was simultaneously a way for the architects to broaden their commercial reach.4 Swid Powell's timing was propitious.
Branding products with the names of freelance designers to attract sales was a long-established practice, but in ı98ı the New York Times observed that "designer names are coming to dinner," noting a resurgence in houseware styled by fashion houses and artists.
Swid Powell and the Italian manufacturer ALESSI garnered particular editorial and commercial attention by virtue of their collaborations with the generation of younger contemporary architects.
Their rebelliousness became a marketable commodity both for corporate patrons and those for whom the avant-garde was a marker of status.5 Unlike Alessi, an established company with extensive manufacturing capabilities, Swid Powell was a newly founded "design editor" that brought together a stable of contract designers, an international array of manufacturers, ABOVE Steven Holl for Swid Powell, Planar pattern buffet plate, 1986.
RIGHT Ettore Sottsass for Swid Powell, Silveshade candlestick, silver-plate, C.
1985.
TOP Michael Graves for Swid Powell, vase, glazed porcelain, 1989.
BOTTOM Ettore Sottsass for Swid Powell, Medici pattern place setting, porcelain, 1985. and retailers.6 Its products did not have a unified aesthetic but mirrored the fractious nature of postmodern architecture.
The company also hired Marc Hacker to work as an intermediary between the manufacturers and the architects in order to realize the often exacting designs.
Working with innovative advertising firms like Arnell/ Bickford Associates and graphic designers Nancy Skolos and Tom Wedell, Swid Powell created a visual identity that was as eyecatching as its huseware and that allowed the company to thrive within a particularly American commercial culture.
Swid Powell's goal was to use manufacturing to make progressive design accessible to the public.
"I don't think you can say these are collectors' items," Powell advised in i984, “We want people to eat off these dishes, use these things for entertaining."7 Ultimately, however, Swid Powell's and Alessi's products were priced just beyond the reach of the average consumer and coded with visual references geared toward the intellectual elite.
They embodied the era's penchant for conspicuous consumption, which some critics felt was as much a by-product of political conservatism as of financial prosperity.8 Notably, when Spy magazine parodied this materialism, it used architect-designed houseware to make its point: Now imagine a teapot.
It's been designed by a postmodern architect with a household name (well, in the right households)..
This teapot is no longer about boiling water.
It's about being able to pay a lot of money for a teapot.
It's a teapot that, once in your possession, reflects your obvious good taste.9 Swid Powell's symbolic, aspirational objects made the company an immediate commercial success.
Given this demand, it faced pressure to produce new, marketable lines each season, much like a fashion company, and the architects were eager to contribute, as many found designing houseware unexpectedly satisfying.
Swid Powell's most prolific collaborator was Richard Meier.
As with the articulation of his buildings, Meier's silverand dishware relied upon repeated and intersecting geometric shapes evocative of German and Austrian Modernism.
A band of pierced squares encircled the King Richard silver plate bowl, simultaneously evoking rows of windows and the square perforations of Josef Hoffmann's metalwork for the Wiener Werkstätte.
Plates formed the core of Swid Powell's line.
To facilitate production and regulate cost, they used standard-sized porcelain blanks, which also made them tabulae rasae upon which the architects could project their distinct ideas.
Robert Venturi, who saw his work for Swid Powell as the logical extension of his furni ture designs for Knoll, covered one set of dishes in a black-and-white pattern derived from the cover of a quotidian composition book,'º undermining the type of highbrow motif traditionally used on fine porce lain.
For Stanley Tigerman, “a plate is not a building and therefore can exhibit a good deal of whimsy and humor."" This allowed the cartoon angles and clouds that existed on the periphery of Tigerman's architectural drawings to become the subject matter of his Heaven and Sunshine patterns.
Steven Holl used theory as subject, with imagery that occasionally broke free of the rim and extended into the plate's center, raising questions about the relationship between structure and decoration.
In these, as with most of Swid Powell's plates, the figure-ground relationship was perpetually in flux, rendering them simultaneously utilitarian and conceptual.
Among Swid Powell's most experimental and influential designs were porcelain tea- and coffee pots.
Graves's The Big Dripper reduced a drip coffee maker to a series of crisp geometric forms accented with symbolic decoration: waves of blue on the filter and pot signaling areas in contact with liquid, and dark red at the base evoking a heat source.
Tigerman's Teaside service recreated one of the firm's building projects in miniature while the name punned on Teesside, the region in the North of England, and Seaside\*, the postmodern planned community in Florida.
Venturi's Village tea and coffee set used a different historical building type for each vessel, creating an accumulation of styles that evoked the heterogeneity of many town centers.
The economic recession in the late i98os and the growing number of AIDS-related deaths eroded Swid Powell's consumer and retail base.'² Competition from pirated designs sold at lower prices also affected them.'3 To generate publicity, in i99o the company embarked on an ambitious project titled The Architects' Collection, consisting of limited-edition objects made from precious máterials including sterling silver, lapis lazuli, marble, and enamel.
Twelve prominent architects designed sumptuous houseware that ranged from glass goblets to towering candelabra.'4 Stern recreated the Choragic Monument of Lysicrates as a silver ice bucket, and a modular centerpiece by ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") quoted his work with MEMPHIS.
Robert and Trix Haussmann\* revisited an earlier design for a trompe l’oeil-decorated bureau for a cloisonné enameled jewelry chest.
The collection, with prices ranging from \$5,ooo to \$30,00o, was unveiled in a gallery-like setting; each piece stood on its own pedestal, suggesting that these housewares were in fact art objects.
The Architects' Collection echoed the influential Tea & Coffee Piazzas that Alessi commissioned in i98o, which were also costly limited editions designed by a group of international architects.
These were atypical products both for Swid Powell and Alessi, but as opulent yet rigorously theoretical statements they proved to be important promotional tools.
Together, they bookend the decade when architect-designed housewares captivated the marketplace.
Swid Powell continued to expand its product line by developing houseware for companies like DISNEY and Calvin Klein and partnering with the established silver manufacturer Reed & Barton on flatware and serving pieces.
But by the mid-ı99os Postmodernism and the celebrity architect had lost their novelty amid a new set of cultural references.
Over the course of the i98os Swid Powell was instrumental in creating objects that reflected an economically prosperous, aspirational society and that expanded the discourse surrounding contemporary architecture.
Through its extensive roster of architect-designers, its heterogeneous aesthetic, and its promotion of attainable luxury Swid Powell domesticated Postmodernism and brought the skyline to the tabletop.

## ABET
LAMINATI MILANESE FIRM WHOSE WORK WITH MEMPHIS MADE PLASTIC LAMINATE INTEGRAL TO POSTMODERN DESIGN.
There are three major ways in which manufacturers can ally themselves with new design and designers.
They can commission the designers to make new products.
They can use the products in their advertising or displays.
Or they can sponsor exhibitions of the designer's work.
Abet Laminati took a fortuitous fourth option, in a unique collaboration that made the company and its products integral to the development of postmodern furnishings.
When Postmodernism was in its infancy, Abet Laminati was an established producer of plastic laminates, founded in Milan in the late ι95os as Italian modern design was making its first inroads into the international marketplace.
Looking for innovative ideas and embracing new technology, many designers turned to plastics, erasing the stigma associated with cheap, man-made materials.
Abet laminates provided the “neutral surface" in the black-and-white grid pattern of the Ollo collection by ALESSANDRO MENDINI (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") for Alchimia\*.
Alerted to the possibilities for more varied applications of its products, Abet worked to develop new textures, colors, and patterns to show the virtually limitless potential of its material as a new design medium, and even a participant in design.
Silk-screened rather than digitally printed, the laminates could achieve a greater intensity of color and clarity of pattern than would otherwise be possible.
When the fledgling MEMPHIS collaborative was formed, Abet became a key supporter, helping to underwrite the venture and supplying material for its products.
Using an inexpensive material to make luxury products was part of the Memphis rebellion against consumer culture, and its introductory exhibition in i98ı was as much a showcase for Abet as for the collective.
Barbara Radice, in her book about Memphis, called the materials “privileged vehicles of sensory communication...that cohabit the design without mixing,"'5 and the fluorescent-colored, squigglypatterned laminates drew as much notice as the skewed geometry of the furniture forms.
Memphis and Abet was an ideal marriage of product and purpose: the riotously patterned laminates conveyed the ornament-loving approach of the postmodernists, and the products served as unpaid advertising vehicles for Abet, propelling its name to international recognition.
Both ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design” and “Living with Postmodern Design") and NATHALIE DU PASQUIER (see under "Design") designed special patterns, which Abet was willing to produce in the required small quantities.
The returns on its investment, in terms of publicity and prestige, were more than worth the effort.
ABOVE Showroom display of historic Abet Laminati designs from the 198os.
OPPOSITE TOP Ettore Sottsass, Letraset design for laminate and textile, c.
198o.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM Ettore Sottsass, Schizzo design for laminate and textile, c.
1980 # THIS FIRM BROUGHT POSTMODERN STYLE TO AFFORDABLE JEWELRY AND ACCESSORY OBJECTS.
RIGHT Adrian Olabuenaga, Boing! radio, plastic, styrene, steel, enamel paint, c.
1986.
In the first few years of the i98os, Postmodernism was becoming the defining style of the decade in architecture and furniture, but it would soon move into categories that had not been anticipated by either historians or the progenitors of the style.
That move would be made by forward-thinking merchandisers who saw the potential for translating the new aesthetic into other mediums.
One of the early, and smallest, firms to carry postmodern design beyond its first manifestations, Acme marketed affordably priced jewelry and accessories with lively colors and provocative patterns, enlisting the miost prominent names in the field to shrink their creative visions to a scale of inches.
Acme Studios was started in i985 by Lesley Bailey and Adrian Olabuenaga, husband-and-wife jewelry entrepreneurs.
New Zealand-born Bailey had a jewelry business in Sydney, Australia, and Buenos Aires native Olabuenaga had one in Los Angeles when they met, married, and opened a gallery there, selling Olabuenaga's furniture, jewelry, and colorful radio designs.
The decision to form Acme Studios came about almost by chance.
As the founder explains "My father was an architect, so I grew up around design.
We saw that all the pieces were there... we just put them together."'6 Olabuenaga had long admired the ceramic teapots of PETER SHIRE (see under “Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), and when he learned that Shire. conveniently based in Los Angeles, was also designing for MEMPHIS, it seemed an opportunity too good to miss.
Since Shire had already shown an interest in working in other materials, Olabuenaga proposed the idea of a jewelry collection, and the resulting twelve enameled metal brooches, each named for a different Los Angeles street, became the first products made by Acme Studios.
Shire introduced the Acmé principals to ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") when they flew to New Orleans to hear him lecture.
After admiring the new collection, Sottsass sketched out an idea for jewelry, which would develop into his first designs for an American firm.
Sottsass then suggested that he invite his Memphis colleagues to also design some jewelry.
The response was enthusiastic on both sides, and the result was the collection Memphis Designers for Acme, which debuted at the Cooper-Hewitt, National Design Museum in 1984.
It included more than one hundred pieces by ANDREA BRANZI, ALDO CIBIC, MICHELE DE LUCCHI, NATHALIE DU PASQUIER.
MARCO ZANINI (see all under "Design"), Ettore Sottsass and others, as well as—at Sottsass's suggestion—several by Olabuenaga himself.
The collection became part of a Memphis exhibition that traveled around the US and helped spread the postmodernist message to an increasingly responsive American market.
Curators, too, took note, and many of the Memphis Designers for Acme collection were acquired for several museums' permanent collections.
The business started small, operating out of one bedroom of a rented house, with a closet serving as warehouse.
The association with Memphis was a boost to Acme's growth, leading to international distribution, and the collection was followed by others, equally noteworthy: Architects for Acme, Alchimia\* for Acme, and other collections that included watches, pens, and sterling silver accessories, most with postmodern elements.
The company oversees production in several Southeast Asian workshops and has relocated its headquarters to the Hawaiian island of Maui, where Sottsass designed his first American projects: a home for the couple (in 1995), and a warehouse for Acme.
What began as a bright idea in a Los Angeles bedroom is now a creative facility encompassing 1½ acres (6,070 sq. m) in the middle of the Pacific Ocean.

ALESSI ITALIAN FIRM WHOSE HIGH-PROFILE PROJECTS BROUGHT MARQUEE-NAME DESIGNERS INTO PRODUCT DESIGN.
In the years when architects were becoming superstars with international reputations, nothing helped that reputation spread beyond the design community as much as their ventures into the previously uncharted territory of product design.
The pioneer in taking them in that direction was Alberto Alessi, who in i97o took over the company his grandfather had founded in ı92ı to produce nickel, chrome, and silver-plated brass household objects.
He had just graduated from law school.
"Being the first of my generation in the family," he says, “I knew from the beginning my destiny was to take care of the company."17 At the time, Alessi was a successful firm with a fine reputation, though not a particularly glamorous one.
Alberto sought to change that, believing that it was just as important to appeal to consumers' aesthetic senses as to meet their practical needs—that even ordinary products could be beautiful.
In a 1983 project for which he enlisted ALESSANDRO MENDINI (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") as “choreographer," twelve celebrated architects were commissioned to participate in what was called the Tea & Coffee Piazza, designing services that would be produced in sterling silver.
The group included American postmodernists MICHAEL GRAVES (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design” and "Living with Postmodern Design"), ROBERT VENTURI (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), STANLEY TIGERMAN (see under “Architecture"), and CHARLES JENCKS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), Italians Mendini, ALDO ROSSI (see under “Architecture"), and PAOLO PORTOGHESI (see under “Architecture") and to round out the internationál roster, Richard Meier\*, OSCAR TUSQUETS BLANCA (see under "Architecture"), Kazumasa Yamashita, HANS HOLLEIN (see under “Architecture")—and ARATA ISOZAKI (see under "Architecture"), who failed to complete his design on time.
Mendini called the eleven results “micro-architecture" or "Domestic Landscapes," recalling the Museum of Modern Art exhibition of i972.
Some were indeed landscapes, of geometric forms on land-like trays that created an architectural ensemble.
Some were clearly postmodern, with overtly classical elements.
Others were riffs on different styles, or basic architectural shapes, reflecting the eclecticism of an era when Postmodernism coexisted with late Modernism The invited designers were generally venturing into unfamiliar territory, and not all the results yielded coffee pots that poured well or were comfortable to hold. but all were highly original, and several carried messages with symbolic meaning.
Robert Venturi followed his “more is more" approach by designing each object in a different historical style.
Michael Graves designed jugs that looked like watchtowers, Charles Jencks interpreted the formal vocabulary of ancient columns, and Aldo Rossi's lids resembled gabled roofs.
OPPOSITE Robert Venturi, for Tea & Coffee Piazza project, silver, 1983.
The collection made its debut at Milan's Pinacoteca di Brera and the Max Protetch Gallery\* in New York, and toured several American museums.
Limited to editions of ninety-nine, they were priced between \$10,000 and \$30,000.'8 The project was a huge success for Alessi, positioning the firm as a producer of prestige objects.
For the architects, it garnered publicity and accolades for their versatility in designing on a scale so different from that of their usual projects.
Several went on to design additional Alessi pieces, and most were commissioned by other firms to exercise their skills in product design.
Alessi added to its collection of architectdesigned wares, most notably with a series of teapots, including one by Richard Sapper\* with a two-note whistle inspired by the sound of a locomotive horn and the celebrated Whistling Bird tea kettle by Michael Graves, both of which were unprecedented successes for both the company and the designers.
The New York Times reported that as of August i988, over 10o,000 of the Graves teapots had been sold, at șı15 each.'9 The company also introduced a variety of accessory items, including salt and pepper shakers and an elegant postmodern clock by Graves, a bar cart by Rossi, and a witty corkscrew by Mendini in the shape of a miniskirted woman.
And in 2003 Alessi reprised its original success with the Tea & Coffee Towers, designed by a second roster of architectural superstars.
By proving that designers trained in one field could work skillfully in another, Alessi's successful use of prominent designers set the stage for similar ventures and anticipated today's broad acceptance of multidisciplinary design ABOVE Hans Hollein for Alessi, service for Tea & Coffee Piazza project, silver, 1983.
LEFT Richard Sapper, tea kettle, stainless steel, 1992.
OPPOSITE Oscar Tusquets Blanca, service for Tea & Coffee Piazza project, silver, 1983.

## CLETO
MUNARI ITALIAN COLLECTOR WHO COMMISSIONED DESIGNERS TO CREATE MUSEUM-WORTHY LUXURY OBJECTS.
Cleto Munari is the archetype of the design editor-entrepreneur.
His commissioning and marketing of elite products played a key role in the dissemination and promotion of the postmodern style.
Left to themselves, the architects whose decorative objects and jewelry he produced would never have ventured into areas so far removed from their customary practice.
Munari, who lives and works in Vicenza, Italy, operated a metalworking firm in the i96os.
No stranger to the idea of using outside talent, he had become close friends and collaborators with great Italian designers including Gio Ponti and Carlo Scarpa, Marco Zanuso\*, ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), and ALESSANDRO MENDINI (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), and over the years met and worked with other prominent names around the world.
He established a workshop for luxury goods and, beginning in the late i97os, began inviting prominent designers to create objects that could be produced in silver, for his private collection.
This would lead to the collection of Cleto Munari silver, which was introduced between i982 and 1987, bringing design by renowned architects and designers to an exceptionally broad range ABOVE Ettore Sottsass, ring, gold, diamonds, c.
1985.
RIGHT Richard Meier, ring, gold, enamel, 1985. of products.
The designers came from several countries, though most were Italian, and included those working in a number of different styles, not all of them postmodern.
The first Cleto Munari colléction to be introduced in the marketplace consisted of almost one hundred sterling silver objects—bowls, trays, pitchers, vases, centerpieces, and other accessories—by a roster of names that included postmodernists MICHAEL GRAVES (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), MICHELE DE LUCCHI (see under "Design"), HANS HOLLEIN (see under "Architecture"), ARATA ISOZAKI (see under "Architecture"), Mendini, and Sottsass.
Munari commented, "It is most interesting to me to have a poet design a table, a painter design a credenza, and an architect design a spoon."20 The silver collection was followed by one of jewelry, an even more ambitious undertaking that took some five years to produce.
The first group of pins, brooches, and earrings, in gold combined with precious and semi-precious stones, was introduced in i985 and designed by an impressive group of world-famous architects, designers, and artists, including Sottsass, Isozaki, STANLEY TIGERMAN (see under "Architecture"), Hollein, Graves, and PETER SHIRE (see under "Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), as well as prominent modernists Peter Eisenman\* and Richard Meier\*.
By 1987, the collection consisted of two hundred pieces and had cost some one million dollars to produce.² The unabashedly elegant objects made no attempt to be anti-establishment or to challenge the boundaries of good taste, as had the more aggressive designs for MEMPHIS by some of the same architects.
These, instead, were explorations of a luxury medium, though many of them paid homage to classical forms.
Each designer submitted drawings, elevations, and painted RIGHT Peter Shire, teapot, silver, 1981.
BELOW Marco Zanuso, coffee service, silver, 1981.
ABOVE Ettore Sottsass, brooch, gold, 1985.
RIGHT Ettore Sottsass, coffee and tea pots, silver, 1977 BELOW Robert A.
M.
Stern, fruit bowl, silver, 1988. renderings, and the pieces were made by Vicenza craftsmen who were usually responsible for suggesting the stones appropriate to each design.
The pieces of jewelry were treated as objets d'art, which indeed they were, in the most luxurious materials: diamonds, rubies, sapphires, coral, lapis lazuli, and black onyx set in gold.
Some suggested miniature works of architecture, though many drew on Egyptian and Grecian motifs for inspiration, recalling relics of the ancient world.
Most of the pieces could have been shown in display cases along with Tutankhamun's treasures or other royal artifacts.
Sottsass commented, tongue firmly in cheek, “My jewelry isn't for every day, but may be used for ritual moments...maybe big parties."22 The collection debuted in America in a i987 exhibition at Montreal's Musée des Arts Décoratifs and traveled to museums in Dallas, Memphis, San Francisco, and Chicago.
Part of the collection was documented in the book Jewelry by Architects (i988) by Barbara Radice.
Initially not for sale, the collection was later marketed in limited editions through jewelers and design galleries.
Following the favorable response to the jewelry, in the fall of i987 Cleto Munari introduced four watches, by Graves, Sottsass, Hollein, and Isozaki—priced from \$5,ooo to ș8,ooo—in designs that were relatively conservative but equally image-enhancing.
Cleto Munari has continued to commission designs by prominent architects and designers, including jewelry, glassware, and, most recently, furniture.
His various collections have been the subject of several museum exhibitions.
The Cleto Munari collections helped to position Postmodernism at its most prestigious and desirable.
It would only be with the proliferation of mass-market, poorly executed interpretations that the style would lose momentum, descending into kitsch and suffering a premature demise.
More enduring, however, was the name-brand syndrome that began with Munari and the other design editor-entrepreneurs, which, in a status-conscious culture, gave architect-designed objects a prestige comparable to that of couture fashion labels.

## DISNEY
THE INTERNATIONAL ENTERTAINMENT STUDIO USED MAJOR POSTMODERN ARCHITECTS TO PROJECT A DISTINCTIVE CORPORATE IMAGE.
The Walt Disney Corporation would not generally be considered a promoter of any designs other than its own, but it was a surprisingly successful participant in the spread of postmodern architecture.
In a happy coincidence for both sides.
Postmodernism was the style of the moment in the years when Disney was expanding, and planning major building projects.
Michael Eisner, a longtime architecture aficionado who became Disney's CEO ín i984, decided that famous architects could help market its brand identity and pursued the idea of developing "entertainment architecture," first for hotels, then corporate buildings, and finally an entire planned community.
The roster of prominent names commissioned for Disney projects included postmodernists MICHAEL GRAVES (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), ARATA ISOZAKI (see under “Architecture"), ROBERT A.
M.
STERN (see under "Architecture"), FRANK GEHRY (see under “Architecture"), and ROBERT VENTURI and DENISE SCOTT BROWN (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design”).
Since Postmodernism's double coding and its use of color and ornament were perfect companions to Disney's people-pleasing identity, the postmodernists designed buildings that were ideal projections of the Disney message, generating consumer approval and considerable publicity.
There were specific guidelines: architects could recreate historic buildings (as had been done within the Disney theme parks), they could adapt storybook images, or they could devise images of their own that would capture the imagination or engage the emotions.
All of this was very much in line with the ideas expressed in the Venturis' Learning from Las Vegas.
The most crowd-pleasing result of the collaborations was Graves's Swan and Dolphin hotels in Orlando, completed in i99o.
In an exuberant departure from staid luxury hotels, they eschewed gilt and mirrors in a fantasy environment that was warm, welcoming, and relatable to families with children.
The Swan's attractions—a pair of 28-ton (25-tonne) turquoise rooftop swans, seashell fountains, pilasters in the form of potted palms, and parrot light fixtures—created irresistible inducements to guests to come in and have fun.
Across a lake, the Dolphin had a similar blend of pastel color and whimsical decor.
Graves outfitted the hotel interiors with Disney-inspired products, such as Mickey Mouse dinnerware and accessories.
OPPOSITE Michael Graves, Mickey Mouse tea kettle, 1991.
TOP Robert A.
M.
Stern, Walt Disney Feature Animation Buildıng, Burbank, California, 1994.
ABOVE Michael Graves, prints for the Walt Disney World Swan and Dolphin hotels, 1990.
LEFT Robert A.
M.
Stern, Witch's hat cabinet and fantasy ceiling for an office interior in the Feature Animation Building, Burbank, C.
1990.
Graves also designed Disney's best-known work of architecture, the Team Disney building consolidating the company's corporate offices in 350,000 square feet (32,500 sq. m, 1990).
Its facade reinterprets the Parthenon, using Disney's Seven Dwarfs as columns supporting the pediment Of the fanciful motifs, Eisner said, "We're the only company who can get away with it."23 Other highlights of the architectural banquet are Robert A.
M.
Stern's Casting Center at Walt Disney World (ı989) and Isozaki's Disney headquarters (i99o), whose whimsical facade contrasts with its austere, businesslike interior.
Despite its artificiality, part of Disneyland's appeal, as architect and gadfly CHARLES MOORE (see under "Architecture" and "Living with Postmodern Design") noted, was that the theme park environments recaptured elements of communal public life that had been lost in modern towns and cities.
Those elements were part of the plan for Celebration, the company's planned community in Florida near Walt Disney World, the first phase of which opened in 1996.
Celebration became one of the earliest examples of the New Urbanism movement, seeking to replicate the ambience of a nineteenth-century town before the automobile.
The requirement of diverse and pedestrian-friendly space called for buildings in styles that were evocations rather than precise copies of traditional architecture, making Celebration an ideal site for postmoderninfluenced design.
The Town Center included buildings by Graves, Philip Johnson\*, and Moore, along with others less committed to Postmodernism but following the guidelines of diversity and historicism.
Disney subsequently sold Celebration, but the community has continued to develop along the original principles By sponsoring postmodern architecture in facilities marketed to a broad and diverse market, Disney helped not only to spread the new style but to legitimize it in the minds of consumers who were neither architecture fans nor interested in avant-garde ideas.
BELOW Downtown Celebration, Florida, the planned community developed by Disney.
