## FORMICA
LAMINATE PRODUCER WHOSE DESIGN COMPETITION WITH STAR ARCHITECTS LAUNCHED A NEW PRODUCT.
When Postmodernism was making news in the media, there were many advantages to appropriating the style for marketing activities.
Formica was one of the earliest and most creative in making the most of the controversial—and attention-getting—new aesthetic.
At the time, the company was a wellestablished and successful producer of popular-priced plastic laminates, for unglamorous applications like kitchens and coffee-shop countertops.
It had developed a new product, ColorCore, a solid surfacing material with homogeneous color, allowing the material to be shaped without exposing the base material and eliminating the black edge that was a drawback of laminates.
Seeking to establish Formica as a source for the design industry, the company's new marketing director Susan Grant Lewin decided to launch the product to a prestige market with a i973 competition called Surface and Ornament.
The competition was announced with great fanfare, including a call-for-entries poster designed by former Museum of Modern Art curator of architecture and design Emilio Ambasz soliciting designers to submit objects using ColorCore.
To ensure media-worthy results, high-profile participants FRANK GEHRY (see under “Architecture"), CHARLES MOORE (see under "Architecture"), Helmut Jahn\*, STANLEY TIGERMAN (see under “Architecture"), and JAMES WINES (see under “Architecture") were invited to submit designs as well.
The submissions, as diverse as they were imaginative, showed ColorCore's ability to be routed, beveled, layered, sandblasted, or broken, and included an Art Deco-inspired elevator cab from Jahn and a zoomorphic fish lamp from Gehry, assembled from broken shards of the material.
Tigerman's two-tone tête-à-tête lounge was probably the most published work, and Gehry's fish lamp the most enduring, though the competition was won by Jim Lewis and Clark Ellefson of Lewis+Clark\*.
As might be expected, the results included a number of objects (including the winning chair) reflecting the postmodern style, as designers took the opportunity to translate historical imagery through a new, modern material, without the requirement of making a saleable product.
The Formica project elicited more than seven hundred entries, and the debut exhibition of the winning designs, at the Art Institute of Chicago in June i983, was covered by the New York Times and dozens of other publications.
The winners were also showcased at Milan's Salone del Mobile, and the following year, Surface and Ornament II followed up on its success, awarding prizes to a kitchen design, a patterned table, a series of signs, and a bead necklace.
Pursuing the postmodern aesthetic, Formica commissioned THOMAS BEEBY (see under “Architecture") to design its Merchandise Mart trade showroom in Chicago (i984).
His sophisticated interpretation of postmodern classical elements was a high-fashion environment in bright pastel colors that underscored the company's move out of the kitchen and into in the designer market.
Making the most of every opportunity, Lewin documented the imagebuilding activities in a i99ı book, Formica and Design: From the Counter Top to High Art.
In his 1996 showroom for Formica, Tigerman used an intersecting "Grid Axonometric" of ColorCore to create the illusion of depth in a modest space.
Formica toured the winning pieces in a five-year traveling exhibition called “Surface and Ornament" to cities in the United States and abroad—everywhere that the company's products were distributed—adding projects by local designers in each city and garnering considerable media coverage.
Pieces from the exhibitions were thereafter donated to several major museums.
Another i984 exhibition, at the Victoria and Albert Museum in London, called "Post Modern," featured one-off products by prominent-name designers, reasserting ColorCore's self-positioning as a material for architects and designers.
The same year, a furniture exhibition opened at the Workbench Gallery in New York in which nineteen artisans who customarily worked in wood, including Garry Knox Bennett\*, Mitch Ryerson\*, and Wendell Castle\*, showed designs in ColorCore.
When the exhibition closed, the Smithsonian's Renwick Gallery in Washington, DC, organized another touring exhibition.
Unlike the other producers profiled in this book, the program for Formica used architects and designers to create products never intended for actual use, but only to enhance the company's image.
In so doing, it contributed to the rise of the architect-as-brand-name, and gained attention for postmodern design in many countries where it had not yet been seen.
OPPOSITE "Surface and Ornament”, Art Institute of Chicago, Chicago, 1983.
TOP LEFT James Wines/ SITE, door with broken section, made for the Formica ColorCore competition, 1973.
FAR LEFT Lewis+Clark, Throne chair, winner of the Formica ColorCore competition, 1973.
LEFT Stanley Tigerman, têteà-tête chaise, for the Formica ColorCore competition, 1973.
BELOW Garry Knox Bennett, desk, for the Formica ColorCore competition, 1973. # MEMPHIS INTERNATIONAL DESIGN COLLABORATIVE WHOSE ICONOCLASTIC DESIGNS MADE POSTMODERNISM A MEDIA SENSATION.
Architecture might have been postmodern design's first expression, but for many people the term Postmodernism is synonymous with Memphis, whose shamelessly attention-grabbing objects flew in the face of convention.
Making costly pieces from inexpensive materials, they redefined the concept of luxury products, with assertive forms and colors that defied expectations of what furniture should look like.
Memphis upset the equilibrium of the design world—and the aftershocks continue to reverberate three and a half decades later.
The collaborative was born in December 198o in the Milan apartment of ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design").
Bemoaning the sad state of current design, sixty-four-yearold Sottsass and a group of younger architects decided to produce and market a collection of objects unhampered by restrictions of formn, functionality, and mandated “good taste." In an oft-told story documented by journalist Barbara Radice, they chose the Memphis name from a Bob Dylan song playing on the phonograph.
The initial group of twenty-somethings included ALDO CIBIC, MATTEO THUN, and MARCO ZANINI (see all under “Design"), then working for Sottsass Associati, as well as MICHELE DE LUCCHi, GEORGE SOWDEN, MASANORI UMEDA,MARTINE BEDIN, and NATHALIE DU PASQUIER (see all under "Design").
ANDREA BRANZI (see under “Design") joined soon after, and ALESSANDRO MENDINI (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), who had been involved in Studio Alchimia\* with Branzi and Sottsass, contributed to the collection.
The roster of international participants ultimately included PETER SHIRE (see under "Design” and "Living with Postmodern Design"), JAVIER MARISCAL (see under "Design"), and SHIRO KURAMATA (see under “Design"), as well as architects ARATA ISOZAKI (see under “Architecture"), MICHAEL GRAVES (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), HANS HOLLEIN (see under "Architecture"), and ARQUITECTONICA (see under "Architecture").
The first collection of fifty-five pieces by twenty designers included Sottsass's Carlton bookcase, which would become an icon of postmodern design, as well as Bedin's appealing four-wheeled Super Lamp.
In truth, it was not so much a collection as a series of unrelated objects— Memphis pieces were intended to clash with their surroundings, and with one'another.
Instead of materials generally associated with quality furniture, the designers used bright plastic laminates in kitschy patterns, printed glass, celluloid, neon tubing, and plated sheet metal.
Although each item had a function, practicality took second place to visual impact.
Furniture pieces were initially named for luxury hotels; lamps and fabrics for exotic locales.
Despite its fun-loving facade, however, Memphis design was rooted in deeply serious ideas.
Its members rejected the commercialization of design and the constraints imposed by self-appointed arbiters of taste who dictated the form and function of objects.
The Memphis project was an outgrowth of Alchimia's experiments in transgressive design but though Alchimia's designs were prototypes, Memphis objects were actually produced.
A primary supporter of the project was ABET LAMINATI, whose products were used in much of the collection; Memphis objects were virtual billboards for the firm's candy-colored, riotously patterned laminates.
Lighting manufacturer Artemide\* was the other major backer, agreeing to produce and market the collection.
Most production was contracted to a variety of small, specialist firms that were willing to devise new techniques to make the designs, most of which were not compatible with conventional production methods.
The Memphis collection made its debut in the Arc '74 showroom at Milan's Salone del Mobile in September i981.
The launch itself was a major event, drawing some two thousand people who waited in long lines, creating traffic jams that delayed the arrival of the principal designers.
Memphis was an instant phenomenon, its impact eclipsing anything else presented at the fair.
The New York Times called it "an outrageous collection of bizarre colors and shapes," but acknowledged that it was “an effervescent, seductive and undeniably sympathetic group."24 The founders referred to their project as the “New International Style." Despite beng hailed OPPOSITE TOP The founding Memphis designers in the Tawaraya boxing ring, 1981.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM The same group gathers twenty years later, 2001. as flag-bearers of the genre, Memphis's principals did not consider their designs to be postmodern, since they did not follow the style's prescribed embrace of historicism.
“There are no quotations from the past in these works,"25 asserted Sottsass.
Whatever their designation, however, Memphis's extravagant, extroverted creations were impossible to ignore.
The media-savvy group took pains to be noticed by the press, and their designs drew unprecedented coverage, both favorable and unfavorable, in the international press; De Lucchi, in a 2oo9 interview, recalled seeing the work on fifty-two magazine covers in its first six months.26 The design community was equally polarized.
In a much-quoted comment, British designer Jasper Morrison\*, a student at the time of the Memphis launch, confessed to being “repulsed by the objects but also freed by the rule-breaking.' And Museum of Modern Art chief curator Paola Antonelli, then studying in Milan, thought it atrocious."²7 But others were captivated; fashion designer Karl Lagerfeld furnished an entire apartment with Memphis, as did photographer Helmut Newton.
The collection made its American debut in October i982 at Memphis at Midnight, an event at Furniture of the Twentieth Century that according to the New York Times drew more than three thousand people to a Chelsea loft, where it met with a similarly polarized response.
Architecture critic Paul Goldberger wrote that it was "brash, exuberant, loud and bears roughly the relationship to classic modernism that punk rock bears to Stravinsky"²8 but acknowledged it as a sign of vitality in modern furniture design, noting the appeal of objects that, notwithstanding their subversive elements, could not fail to elicit smiles.
Memphis designs were sold through galleries like Art et Industrie\* and Max Protetch\*, both in New York.
Distributed nationally through Urban Architecture, primarily to architects and designers, they developed a dedicated following of avant-garde collectors.
Following fashion industry practice, Memphis made special events of its annual product introductions, generating attention from the media and a public becoming more familiar with, though not universally enamored of, the styles.
Later collections modified the initial exhibitionism in more accessible objects, particularly in ceramic and glass.
An exhibit of Memphis design, including objects and original architects' drawings, opened at the Brooks Museum of Art in Memphis, Tennessee, in 1984,29 traveling to ten American museums before arriving in i986 at the Cooper-Hewitt Museum in New York, its first major exposure in the city.
Exhibitions were also staged in London and Tokyo.
Presenting the material was one thing, but acquiring it was another: the Metropolitan Museum of Art in New York was one of relatively few museums to recognize the historic significance of Memphis, purchasing examples for its permanent collection.3º The Memphis collaborative had an advantage over conventional producers in that it had no particular concern for saleability.
The designs were intended to make a point, rather than a profit.
This was fortunate, since limited production, by hand, meant that the pieces were costly,3' and therefore affordable only for a small market of adventurous consumers.
With few exceptions, even the well-received items sold no more than fifty pieces.
Memphis published a new catalog every year, introducing a total of some 65o products, including furniture, lamps, jewelry, tableware, fabrics, even neckties and underwear.
By the third collection, the designs were less adventurous, and many were factory-produced in larger quantities, diluting the original concept.
Weakened by Sottsass's departure in i985, the group disbanded in 1988.
The collective's name and design copyrights were subsequently sold, and though many of the pieces are still being produced, the original Memphis became a historic artifact, representing a moment in design whose impact reverberated well beyond its brief life.
In terms of commercial success, Memphis was a failure; its importance lies in its influence.
As Mendini later put it, "Memphis marked the transition from modern [to] postmodern...[Dlesign afterwards was no longer the same as it had been before."32 ABOVE Ettore Sottsass, Nathalie Du Pasquier, and George Sowden, textile designs, 1993.
ABOVE Ettore Sottsass, Sol fruit bowl, blown glass, 1982.
RIGHT Ettore Sottsass, Murmansk fruit bowl, silverplate, 1982.

## LIVING
WITH POSTMODERN DESIGH # INTERIORS: TRANSLATION, MODIFICATION, COMPROMISE Postmodernism did not translate painlessly into interiors.
Its design vocabulary was formulated for architecture, and its execution in furniture resulted in pieces so extravagant as to preclude the use of several of them in a single room, unless that room had an explicitly postmodern aesthetic.
There was in any case a time lag between the creation of architectural environments and the availability of furniture to fill them.
Most of the earliest postmodern interiors were illustrated in design publications without decoration or furniture, acknowledging that conventional furnishings were altogether inappropriate.
It took a while for manufacturers to respond.
Architects were the first to show interest in designing postmodern interiors; first because they were already thinking about, or working in, the new aesthetic, and second because interiors could offer them opportunities for commissions in a new area, an idea that was especially welcome in a decade of economic recession.
Some architectural elements (such as columns) translated comfortably into interiors; others (pediments or elaborate arches, for example) somewhat awkwardly.
In many cases, however, a focus on contextualism led to the use of narrative, in the form of murals and photomurals that told their own stories.
The most striking postmodern interiors were in public rather than private spaces.
In its banner years, showrooms, restaurants, retail stores, even theaters sprouted the columns, arcades, and pediments of the style's most familiar architectural elements, executed in a bright pastel palette that promised relief from mostly white International Style walls.
Some of the most notable examples were designed by marquee-name architects like MICHAEL GRAVES (also see under "Masters of Postmodern Design"), ARATA ISOZAKI (see under "Architecture"), HANS HOLLEIN (see under “Architecture"), who created environments that made even conventionally modern furniture look up-to-the-minute.
Commercial spaces like showrooms and stores took little risk in being fashion-forward, since they were likely to be redecorated within as little as a year.
Other locations required design-savvy clients, or corporations that welcomed the opportunity to commission projects that would serve as branding statements.
For a firm like DISNEY (see under "Edited Design"), the exuberant new style was the ideal garb in which to dress its Graves-designed Swan and Dolphin hotels, as well as most of their corporate facilities.
Despite wanting to project an up-to-date image, however, few other major companies were willing to go so far out on the postmodern limb.
Graves's designs for Sunar-Hauserman\*, a producer of office furnishings, began in i982 with a showroom filled with postmodern architectural elements and flowing displays of draped fabrics.
Its muted palette of apricot, gray, French blue, taupe, and ochre was widely copied: Voorsanger & Mills\* designed the Le Cygne restaurant and the Janovic/ Plaza paint store, both in New York, in a similar vein to Graves's showrooms, and the color scheme became ubiquitous in advertising and graphics as węll.
Isozaki (for Sunar), THOMAS BEEBY (see under “Architecture") and STANLEY TIGERMAN (see under “Architecture"), FORMICA (see under “Edited Design"), and MICHAEL VANDERBYL (for Bernhardt; see under "Graphic Design") were among other postmodernists recruited to give furni ture showrooms an on-trend look, although the furniture on display was far less extreme than the environments # ...postmodern homes were limited to an infusion of colors or an occasional piece of furniture, rather than an entire series of rooms.
ABOVE Robert Stern, architects' offices, New York City, 1961.
OPPOSITE TOP Michael Graves, showroom for Sunar-Hauserman, Chicago, 1982.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM LEFT Michael Graves, Reinhold apartment renovation, New York, 1981.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM RIGHT Charles Moore, bedroom in the architect's home, New Haven, Connecticut, 1969.
ETTORE SOTTSASS (also see under "Masters of Postmodern Design") designed showrooms for the clothing retailer Esprit\* in a user-friendly interpretation of his lively, MEMPHIS-inflected style (see under “Edited Design").
And PHILIPPE STARCK (see under "Design") designed the Royalton and Paramount hotels in New York, in iterations of the postmodern style he had begun with Paris's Café Costes.
TERRY FARRELL's (see under “Architecture") TV-am headquarters made a design statement in keeping with the news-making introduction of morning television to the United Kingdom, with a bold interior that complemented the equally attention-getting building.
And the jubilantly colored interiors of JOHN OUTRAM's (see under “Architecture") Judge Business School at Cambridge University benefted from the fact that the building was privately funded, allowing a complete departure from the staid university norm.
Despite such striking and widely published interiors, permanent spaces of this kind were relatively rare, and many of looked dated just a few years after they węre built.
Postmodernism made relatively few inroads into residential interiors; it was often considered something to look at and talk about, rather than to actually live with.
Consumers, even those taken with the style, found it too “trendy" to commit to on a large scale, and most so-called postmodern homes were limited to an infusion of colors or the occasional piece of furniture, rather than an entire series of rooms.
Arcane touches of classical architecture only intermittently migrated into interiors, as ambiance took precedence over authentic ity.
Even within the congenial environments of assertively postmodern houses, the vocabulary was somewhat subdued, or even watered down, for everyday consumption.
Architects, however, often carried out the theme when designing for themselves: the homes of CHARLES JENCKS (also see under “Masters of Postmodern Design") and CHARLES MOORE (also see under “Architecture") in particular were outfitted in postmodern style at its most provocative; and high-ceilinged lofts, like those designed by Alan Buchsbaum, took well to the postmodern vocabulary.
The mostly Memphis, overdone interiors of a few Hollywood films brought greater visibility but probably few new admirers for the style, since they were usually designed tó highlight ostentatiousness or bad taste—neither of which helped it win fans.
At its most fully developed, Postmodernism was perhaps too extreme a break with its predecessors, too "high style" to endure.
Nonetheless, with repeated exposure in the media it became more familiar and its influence began to take hold among even conservative designers and consumers, particularly in America, where keeping up with fashion is considered a sign of sophistication.
Although relatively few postmodern interiors survived the impulse to redecorate, they often sported accessories and accent pieces in the new style.
The introduction of a varied color palette and a permissive attitude toward mixing styles and periods—a new freedom for individuality and self-expression—are postmodernism's most important, and much-appreciated, legacies. # Postmodern Design Moves Indoors Arlene Hirst As night follows day, so postmodern interiors were an inevitable offshoot of postmodern architecture.
The buildings of CHARLES MOORE (also see under "Architecture"), MICHAEL GRAVES (also see under "Masters of Postmodern Design"), ROBERT A.
M.
STERN (also see under "Architecture"), ROBERT VENTURI (also see under “Masters of Postmodern Design"), and their peers demanded an interior aesthetic to match.
In the United States, the wider design world soon followed.
Crumbling arches, free-standing columns, faux neoclassical pediments, classical references, trompe l’oeil, and a pastel palette were all postmodern elements that began to appear in profusion in the late 197os and early 198os.
Pedestals were perhaps the defining signature of the style; no postmodern interior was complete without one.
Designers used them in every possible material, in an almost unending array of sizes and shapes.
It was the easiest way to add architectural detail to a room.
Postmodernism made the past popular again, and even modernist designers were lured by its siren song.
Classical statues were favorite props and marble became chic, especially when imitated by a skilled painter of faux finishes.
Plastic laminate, once relegated to the kitchen and bathroom, moved into the front room, frequently with metallic and textured finishes.
While any of these details unmistakably marked a project as postmodern, the interiors could be as stylistically different as a building by Stern and one by Graves.
In a story by Suzanne Slesin in the New York Times in 198o, Stern described his approach to one project as a "classical theme with unclassic organization.' But, he added, "Postmodernism is not just a way of having fun with columns."' Stern's interiors to this day are filled with architectural details; these embellishments are his signature, be they pediments, porticos, pilasters, lintels, or all of the above.
In a room that he designed for Metropolitan Home's ShowHouse to benefit DIFFA (Design Industries Foundation Fighting AIDS) in i991, he created dramatically overscaled swags of fiberglass-reinforced plaster that provided an eye-popping accent to an otherwise classic formal room.
Stern's furniture, frequently of his own design, is generally more classically traditional than his architecture.
Graves took postmodernism in a different direction—a style he incorporated into his own life.
He filled his home in Princeton, New Jersey, with Biedermeier furniture, Roman antiquities, and reproductions of Romantic paintings.
His favored color palette of pale teal and terracotta became a postmodern signature.
Graves's ample use of Biedermeier furniture helped initiate a renaissance of the genre.
Before then, it was an almost forgotten style, having originated in Germany and Austria in the early nineteenth century, and was virtually unknown in the United States.
This warm, Teutonic version of Directoire and Empire style appealed to Graves, who was drawn to its use of honey-colored woqds, espe cially bird's-eye maple.
Carol Vogel, writing in New York Times Magazine in 1985, authenticated Biedermeier's popularity: “It's what everyone is gravitating to," she wrote, "It used to be scarce.
No one knew what it was."2 Graves, the postmodern master of the theatrical gesture, gave the public a close look at the style with his creations for the Swan and Dolphin hotels in Orlando's ABOVE Robert A.
M.
Stern, living room for Metropolitan Home ShowHouse, New York City, 1991.
Walt Disney World resort.
Here, sunny beach murals lined the halls, with cloud-shaped sconces lighting the way, and parrot lamps and a floral-stenciled desk chair gave the pastel-hued guest rooms a touch of humor—another Graves signature that soon found its way into the design mainstream.
When MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") came along in i98ı, what was once classic and refined became boisterous and rowdy—a cartoon version of the style.
It created a storm of publicity and made postmodernism itself front-page news.
The impact of Memphis was perhaps most visible in the production design of movies of the i98os.
“It became the production designer's go-to style to communicate Reaganite excess and i98os anti-urbanism," wrote Will Wiles for Dezeen, "The triumph of postmodernist design was to perfectly capture its age.
Its tragedy was that the age was rotten."3 For many people, the first image that comes to mind at the mention of postmodern interiors is the i986 film Ruthless People.
Lilly Kilvert, its designer, was drawn to the Memphis look, which suited her purposes perfectly.
Kilvert has been quoted as saying that a house full of Memphis furniture is more than anyone should have to endure; she wanted Bette Midler and Danny DeVito's home to have a threatening, obnoxious quality, and filled the set with a mother-lode of avant-garde LA art, borrowing furniture from the Whiteley Gallery as well as from the local Memphis member PETER SHIRE (also see under "Design").
Memphis never had a chance in the court of public opinion.
In movies, postmodernism was repeatedly cast as the villain and always used for satirical effect.
The following year, Oliver Stone's Wall Street used postmodernism to represent the era's rottenness, and the film has come to be seen as the archetypal portrayal of i98os excess.
Production designer Stephen Hendrickson turned a white-walled luxury duplex on Manhattan's Upper East Side into a postmodern temple; the transformation is chronicled on-screen as an interior designer, played by Daryl Hannah, decorates it and fills it with allusions of corruption and decadence.
Furnishings are savage, with jagged edges and crumbling surfaces.
Concrete and rusty iron abound, along with gold leaf, verdigris and faux marbre finishes.
While it was clearly over the top. for many of the furniture designers who supplied props for the project, it was also déjà yu.
They had real-life Wall Street clientele clamoring for just such pieces.
Beetlejuice, Tim Burton's 1988 comedy about ghosts who return to their old house only to find it garishly redecorated, also used postmodernism to dire effect.
Burton called it “post-mortemism".
Production designer Bo Welch denies that he took the style too far, claiming it embodied the ideas of Richard Meier\*, FRANK GEHRY (see under “Architecture") and PHILIPPE STARCK (see under “Design") rolled into one.
And while he admits that his design teetered on the brink of parody, he defends it, saying, "You go so far and think no one would ever do this.
Then you got to Long Island and see one that's been taken even further." Wiles has pointed out that in all three of these movies, released around the same time, the rich clients "clearly consider themselves discerning, but their tastes are being held up for ridicule...They are either trashy new money or pseudish cosmopolitans.
They are nearly always contrasted against wholesome small-town blue collar types." He postulates that postmodern furniture and interiors never took hold because of the damning treatment it received in the movies.4 But postmodern interiors did take hold for a time.
They were seen not only on movie screens, but in restaurant and hotel designs, and were thus introduced in a more positive way to the general public.
Graves's DISNEY (see under “Edited Design") hotels helped; as did the work of Starck, who burs onto the international scene in i984 with his design for Café Costes, a restaurant in Paris which, he said with his ABOVE Karl Lagerfeld's Memphis-filled Monte Carlo apartment, 1981.
TOP Memphis furniture in a Dallas home, c.
1985. now famous wit, evoked a third-class waiting room, in a Budapest railway station, circa i956.
With its pale green and pink walls and huge clock dominating the top of a central staircase, it was unlike anything seen before.
The project soon won him many commissions in the United States.
Like most of Starck's work, the Royalton Hotel in New York City felt like a glamorous stage set, with a lobby filled with his spiky, metal-legged furniture and horn-shaped light fixtures.
It set a new paradigm for hotel design.
Two years later, at the Paramount, Starck acted as a curator, filling the monumental two-story lobby with a dramatic sweeping staircase and a collection of designer furniture not his own, including Marc Newson's famous Lockheed Lounge, which now fetches millions of dollars at design auctions.
American designers also got involved, and postmodern restaurant designs appeared all over the country, including Spiaggia in Chicago, a place described by some as a PoMo temple to pizza.
Frank Gehry worked on Rebecca's, a Los Angeles restaurant that opened in i986, adorning it with two giant crocodiles suspended from the ceiling, a glass octopus chandelier, and two of his now iconic fish lamps.
He emploved a host of local artists to finish the job.
A new breed of restaurant designers—Adam Tihany, Sam Lopata, and the firm Haverson/Rockwell among them—turned postmodernism into something exuberant and joyful, extending its reach into the farthest corners of the United States.
When the economy went sour, the excesses of the i98os—and postmodernism with them—faded from the scene.
Today, however, the movement is showing new signs of life, with a revived passion for Memphis furniture and Memphis-style decorations leading the way.
Postmodernism's greatest achievement was its liberation of interiors from the straitjacket of period styles; until it came along, most American homes were done to a turn in everything from French provincial or Louis XV to colonial or Art Deco style.
Ironically, the only period still alive and well today is modernism itself.
Otherwise, eclecticism reigns, relegating period rooms primarily to museums.
It is an approach that postmodernism surely brought about. # DEAN POND HOUSE PUTNAM COUNTY, NEW YORK, UNITED STATES, 1988 JAMES EVANSON A CITY-POLISHED-MEETS-COUNTRY-CASUAL DESIGN, WITH A CONSISTENT USE OF CONTRASTING TONES AND TEXTURES.
Pursuing parallel careers as both an architect/ builder and designer/craftsman, JAMES EVANSON (see under “Design") has often used one to explore ideas for the other.
Such was the case with the Dean Pond House, built on speculation in the bucolic Kent township.
Though Evanson refers to it as a "natural house," made with natural materials and neutral colors that suit the quiet landscape, the effect is more city-polished than country-casual.
The exterior of colored natural cedar interlocks with a massive stucco base to anchor the house neatly onto the site.
Both the angular form and the tripartite facade suggest a postmodern take on Palladian architecture.
In the stripped-down interior, oversized rectangular skylights flood every room with light, bringing out the tones and texture of the natural wood on the floor, upper walls, and ceiling.
A stone freplace, subtly echoing the columnar form of the main entrance, divides the main space into living and dining areas.
The almost stark background is a perfect foil for Evanson's quirky furniture designs, which combine provocative forms with fine materials and craftsmanship.
Instead of color, the contrasting textures create decorative interest: a terracotta tiled floor and lacquered cabinets in the kitchen, polished and unfinished wood against stucco walls, and nubby or glazed cotton fabrics.
Although Evanson's furniture is very much at home here, so would be almost any other collection of contemporary pieces—which, of course, was precisely his intention.
BELOW Cedar siding over a massive stucco base anchors the house to its site.
OPPOSITE TOP LEFT Terracotta floor and white lacquered cabinets in the clean-lined kitchen.
OPPOSITE TOP RIGHT A stone freplace divides the high-ceilinged central space into living and dining areas.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM LEFT Evanson's quirky furniture works well in the simple surroundings.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM RIGHT Cedar and gypsum define the ceiling and walls, echoing the two-tone theme of the exterior. # THE WAREHOUSE PRINCETON, NEW JERSEY, UNITED STATES, 1984 MICHAEL GRAVES A RUNDOWN WAREHOUSE TRANSFORMED INTO A MODERN VERSION OF THE CLASSIC ITALIAN VILLA.
When an architect designs for himself, it is generally less problematic than designing for a client.
Certainly, nobody knows more about the specific needs and tastes of the potential occupant.
But working for oneself can bring unexpected challenges.
When MICHAEL GRAVES (also see under "Masters of Postmodern Design") found a dilapidated 1927 structure that had been built by Italian craftsmen working on the Princeton University campus, he was taken by its resemblance to a Tuscan barn and bought it for the bargain price of \$3o,ooo, expecting to do some fairly simple fixing up to make it livable.
In the event, it took terı years and hundreds of thousands of dollars—but the result was an elegant home that combined Graves's postmodern aesthetic with the warmth of an Italian country villa.
The inviting entrance courtyard was created by removing part of an exterior wall.
In the rest of the two-story building, which he called The Warehouse, referring to its one-time role as a storage facility, Graves removed walls and added glass doors and steel casement windows, transforming a rabbit warren of small and mostly windowless rooms into airy, gracious spaces defined by barrel-vaulted ceilings and milk bottle-shaped columns.
Working within the existing footprint, he created a floor plan that orients the house to receive maximum morning light.
To create the effect of greater height, the ceilings were lowered around the edges, and the resulting center recesses were painted light blue.
Soffits were installed around each room to house heating and cooling ducts and other mechanicals, avoiding the need to tear up the existing concrete floors.
A two-story library, adjoining the living room has a greenhouse-style glass roof and floor-toceiling bookcases of PVC pipe faux-painted to imitate wood.
The house is furnished with classic Biedermeier pieces, whose architectural qualities inspired Graves's own furniture designs.
OPPOSITE Biedermeier furniture stars in the inviting living room.
ABOVE The exterior has the ambiance of an Italian country villa.
RIGHT Pebble-coated driveway flanked by greenery.
OPPOSITE TOP LEFT Traditional art and furnishings carry through the classical look.
OPPOSITE TOP RIGHT The two-story library, with tall bookcases of faux-woodpainted PVC pipe.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM The architecturally detailed dining room mixes classic and contemporary.
RIGHT Barrel-vaulted atrium, with ceramic objects displayed beneath the oculus. # PLOCEK HOUSE WARREN TOWNSHIP, NEW JERSEY, UNITED STATES, 1977 MICHAEL GRAVES A POSTMODERN PALAZZO, WITH CLASSICAL ELEMENTS INCORPORATED INTO EXPANSIVE MODERN INTERIORS.
ABOVE RIGHT The palazzolike residence, set on a sloping hillside, is entered from the second level.
RIGHT The driveway leads to a side entrance and auto court.
OPPOSITE The domed atrium is framed by modern torchères.
Located on a heavily wooded, steeply sloping site in southern New Jersey, the much-published Plocek House is a classic example of the interpretation by the firm of MICHAEL GRAVES (also see under “Masters of Postmodern Design") of classicism in a postmodern vernacular.
With the imposing air of an Italian palazzo, the residence (which occupies an area of 4,ooo sq. ft/37o sq. m), completed in i977, was meticulously restored in 2oil to return it to its original elegance.
The rusticated base, piano nobile, and attic follow the traditional tripartite form of classical architecture, adapted to an unusual site.
The main entrance, facing the street, is actually on the lower level, while the second entrance is off the first floor, accessing the parking area.
Inside, a series of layered spaces are organized around a central stair column, which rises to roof height and is crowned with a skylight-topped capital that brings light into the center of the house.
On the second floor, metal railings edge the bridges that wrap around the stair column.
Clerestory window openings overlook the two-story living room, with its marble-faced fireplace surmounted by a mural that is an abstracted reflection of the landscape outside.
Wall colors are in Graves's characteristic postmodern pastel palette—tones of lilac, aqua, and peach—and the furnishings, including rugs and lighting, were all designed by Graves.
OPPOSITE An enfilade in shades of lilac frames a vista of the living room.
RIGHT A roof-level, skylighttopped capital brings light into the interior.
BELOW RIGHT Clerestory windows add refined detail.
ABOVE The dining room, with Graves-designed furniture and wood cabinetry.
ABOVE In the double-height living room, a mural evokes the surrounding landscape.

## GARAGIA
ROTUNDA WELLFLEET, CAPE COD, MASSACHUSETTS, UNITED STATES, 1977 CHARLES JENCKS BUILT ON A TINY BUDGET, THIS VERNACULAR COTTAGE IS A LABORATORY FOR EXPLORING COLOR AND DIMENSIONAL EFFECTS.
RIGHT The entrance is a layered composition of geometric elements.
OPPOSITE A prefabricated garage was transformed into a unique country cottage using off-the-shelf components.
This quirkily appealing house, in an isolated site, surrounded by sand dunes, ponds, and occasional trees, was both a logistic and an intellectual challenge for CHARLES JENCKS (also see under “Masters of Postmodern Design"), who was at the time crystallizing his concept of Postmodernism.
In planning the house, which he called his “first steps to symbolism," Jencks made unrestricted use of ornament, mixing classical and vernacular elements and introducing touches of anthropomorphism.
Beginning with the form of a prefabricated garage, Jencks applied his principles of adhocism, building the house entirely of readymade components, purchased as inexpensively as possible; the total cost was a mere \$5,50o.
The resulting building reprises a vernacular Cape Cod cottage, embellished with aspects of Queen Anne design: a widow's walk on the roof, a pediment over the front door, classical touches in the ornament.
In the absence of luxury materials, the house gets its designworthy credentials from color: no fewer than ten shades of blue were used on the exterior and interior to create eye appeal as well as intriguing dimensional effects.
Reflecting the variations in tones of blue in the Cape Cod sky and water, the colors take on different intensities as the natural light changes throughout the day.
The interior is a sequence of spaces framed by posts and beams, leading to the central living area, which is defined by a cross-axis on the floor.
Wall studs are painted in graduating shades of blue, making them decorative as well as functional.
The furniture, all by Jencks, is also painted in various shades of blue.
The anthropomorphic element is introduced with images of the face or body— a Medusa head above the entrance, the suggestion of a face in the form of a rear bay window.
The house's tongue-in-cheek name unites opposing building types—the lowly covered shed and the heavenly, domed space—although the "dome' in Jencks's building is an almost imperceptible curve of seven-eighths of an inch.
OPPOSITE The main living area, defined by posts and beams and a cross-axis on the floor that splits the space in two.
ABOVE Ten different shades of blue paint create varying dimensional effects.
RIGHT As the natural light changes, so do the colors of the interior. # CASA DELLA FELICITÀ LAKE ORTA, ITALY, 1988 ALESSANDRO MENDINI STARS OF POSTMODERN DESIGN COLLABORATE ON A LAKESIDE FAMILY RESIDENCE.
Described by the owner, Alberto ALESSI (see under "Edited Design"), as “a kind of postmodern manifesto," this extraordinary residence reflects the communal bent of its primary designer, ALESSANDRO MENDINI (also see under "Masters of Postmodern Design"), who often likes to take the role of design director, inviting others to participate in a project that he oversees and coordinates.
This one, in a charming lakeside area near an old Roman road, called for a romantic, fairy-tale house—one that would not only fit comfortably into the surroundings but would satisfy the needs of the client.
The list of collaborating designers was impressive, including Francesco Mendini and Giorgio Gregori, Mendini's associates in Studio Alchimia\*, and several architects connected with Alessi; ANDREA BRANZI (see under "Design") designed an outside fountairı, Riccardo Dalisi\* the gables, FRANK GEHRY (see under "Architecture") the conservatory, ALDO ROSSI (see under "Architecture") the tower, ETTORE SOTTSASS (also see under “Masters of Postmodern Design) a fireplace, and ROBERT VENTURI (also see under “Masters of Postmodern Design") the conservatory.
The house was furnished, of course, with pieces designed by the participants and congenial collaborators, representing both Alessi products and the postmodernist aesthetic then dominating Italian design.
Notwithstanding its high-fashion credentials, the results of the collaboration were more understated than exaggerated, more intellectually satisfying than visually aggressive, and altogether compatible with its designation as a "house of happiness." Two decades later, for another residence to be built, in the middle of a vineyard, Alessi declared himself "allergic" to other architects, and again chose Mendini to interpret his family's needs for a very personal, life-enhancing home.
OPPOSITE The angular ceiling and curved balustrades of the central space.
ABOVE Rounded walls and arched floor-to-ceiling windows in the grand, sparely furnished salon.
ABOVE The romantic lakeside house hugs the water's edge.
ABOVE Looking out to the garden and pool structures.
LEFT In the library, pilasters flank the booksheives. # SELBY AVENUE HOUSE LOS ANGELES, CALIFORNIA, UNITED STATES, 1977 CHARLES MOORE UNCONVENTIONAL TOWNHOUSE ENHANCED WITH QUIRKY SPACES AND VISUAL PUNS.
One of a cluster of three townhouses designed for himself and two fellow professors while he was teaching at UCLA, this highly original residence by CHARLES MOORE (also see under "Architecture") was among his several efforts to explore different architectural aesthetics while expressing his irrepressible fondness for unexpected juxtapositions and visual puns.
The three townhouse exteriors are companionably aligned, in convenient walking distance from the college campus.
Bridging a large, partly covered courtyard shared with its neighbors, the house fits five levels into a four-story volume, with angled stairs, lofts, and overlooks creating an intriguing spatial puzzle.
A series of ingeniously located seating areas provide perches from which to view the interior from different angles, each revealing another aspect of the unconventional space.
Informally arranged furnishings of no single style and an accumulation of collected objects reflect the architect's eclectic taste, standing in whimsical contrast to the severe architectural background and crisp white walls.
Light streams into the interior from three sides, and is shaped into patterns by the multifaceted roof.
Moore's characteristic humor is apparent in the transgressive manipulation of perspective, and his designation of the fan-shaped fenestration as Falling Windows, a playful riff on Frank Lloyd Wright's iconic Fallingwater.
ABOVE Narrow stairs set at angles create seating nooks alongside bookshelves.
OPPOSITE Quirky window openings set the house off from its neighbors.
LEFT Flanking the stairs, quirky collections reveal the architect's diverse interests and quixotic taste.
ABOVE A classical arch laid upon the bookcase wall for trompe l’oeil effect. # VISTA GORDOI SHIRE ART HOUSE LOS ANGELES, CALIFORNIA, UNITED STATES, 1984 PETER SHIRE FIRE-DAMAGED COTTAGE RESTORED TO MAKE THE MOST OF LIMITED SPACE.
BELOW The compact living space is sparely furnished, with light-scale pieces.
The restoration and renovation of a fire-damaged house in Echo Park, one of Los Angeles's early suburbs and the site of many cherished midcentury homes, became a family project for PETER SHIRE (also see under “Design") and his father Henry, who contributed most of the woodwork.
The quirky exterior, suggesting both Postmodernism and its Deconstructionist offshoot, is of wood, metal, and corrugated fiberglass.
Inside, white walls are sparked with polychrome paint and a wellchosen mix of light-scaled furniture that makes the most of limited space.
The compact house is only 1,40o sq. ft (i3o sq. m), excluding the garage—ample for a city apartment, but tight for the customary expanse of most California homes.
Taking that into account, the compact kitchen was outfitted with upper cabinets 18in.
(45.7cm) deep on one side and just 6in.
(i5.2 cm) deep on the other, and the bathroom commode was curved to give the feeling of more space.
Shire's extroverted style ran free in the bathroom, where colorful handmade and hand-painted glazed tiles provide a lively change of pace after the restraint of the other rooms.
A sure attention-grabber for passers-by, the house was designated a Historic-Cultural Monument by the City of Los Angeles.
It remains in the Shire family; Peter's brother Billy now lives there, while Peter and his wife Donna are just a block away.
OPPOSITE TOP Mixed materials and colors give the exterior a Deconstructivist look.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM LEFT Contrasts of color and shape reflect the distinctive Shire style.
OPPOSITE BOTTOM RIGHT In the kitchen, colorful details set off a narrow space. # ŠÍPEK HOUSE AMSTERDAM, NETHERLANDS, 1988 BOŘEK ŠÍPEK INNOVATIVE TOWNHOUSE THAT KEEPS CLOSE COMPANY WITH CONVENTIONAL DUTCH BUILDINGS.
A true original needs a house that's something special, and this one that architect and designer BOŘEK ŠÍPEK (also see under “Architecture") created for himself and his family, in the city where he spent much of his time, fits the bill perfectly.
The bright exterior makes the first statement, its geometric form suggesting an exaggerated, modern take on a traditional peaked-roof cottage.
Cheek-to-cheek with its neighbors, the house has only front and rear exposure, but its tall glass-paned door and windows and large skylights ensure that maximum light floods the interior.
Throughout the house, whimsical touches reveal the humor that pervades this designer's work: abstract floral cut-outs on the narrow doorways, a sky-and-clouds painted vault in the dining room, and a rear hall floor enlivened with a broken tile pattern.
The kitchen, however, is resolutely serious, a showplace of stainless steel and white tile that is too beautiful to mask from view; it opens directly into the dining room.
Wisely eschewing the curves and curlicues that mark most of Šípek's furniture and accessories, the relatively understated interiors are an ideal complement to showcase the objects they contain.
ABOVE Floral cut-outs accent a narrow door.
OPPOSITE The entrance, seen from a narrow street between adjacent houses.
TOP RIGHT The floral cut-outs are repeated in several areas.
ABOVE RIGHT Aterrazzo pattern adds interest to a floor on the lower level.
ABOVE The sleek, modern kitchen focuses on function.
LEFT In the dining room, a deep-coffered ceiling with bright-blue painted “sky." # CASA MAUI MAUI, HAWAI, UNITED STATES, 1997 ETTORE SOTTSASS BOLD GEOMETRIC FORMS SHAPE A HILLTOP RESIDENCE SUFFUSED WITH COLOR.
INSIDE AND OUT.
RIGHT Color-block forms cluster on a plinth-like base.
OPPOSITE Approaching the house, the assertive forms and colors demand notice.
Lesley Bailey and Adrian Olabuenaga, partners in business and life, had successfully collaborated with ETTORE SOTTSASS (also see under “Masters of Postmodern Design") on a collection of jewelry by MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") designers for their firm ACME (see under “Edited Design") when they asked the architect if he would design a home for them on a hilltop site in Maui, where they had recently relocated from Los Angeles.
It didn't concern them that Sottsass had never designed a house, or that he lived in Milan and would not be able to personally supervise every aspect of construction.
They wanted his extroverted style and brilliant use of color.
And they were willing— anxious, in fact—to be thoroughly involved in the process as design collaborators and facilitators.
Communications were via fax and snail mail, which in part explains why the project took six years.
But the results were everything they had hoped for, and more: a remarkable two-story structure of vari-shaped block forms in saturated colors that resembles nothing on the island, or almost anywhere else.
The interior deploys color with equal abandon, but primarily in softer hues that provide a background for color-block furnishings —many by Sottsass, others by Olabuenaga—that reflect the owners' postmodernist leanings but manage to mix congenially with classic Hawaiian pieces.
The result: a livable residence for Bailey and Olabuenaga, despite the attention it continues to draw from passers-by and architecture fans; a design magazine called it “the most Memphis house imaginable." It is one of only three Sottsassdesigned homes in America.

## CASA
MUNARI VENICE, ITALY, 1985 ETTORE SOTTSASS BRIDGING OLD AND NEW, A MODERN INTERIOR IS BUILT INTO A CLASSIC VENICE APARTMENT.
BELOW Throughout the apartment, embellished doorways serve as wall decor.
Comfortably ensconced on the parlor floor of an old Venetian palazzo, this apartment is a part-time home for the producer and design editor CLETO MUNARI (see under “Edited Design") and his wife Valentina, who had long sought a pied-à-terre in that romantic city as an accessible and appealing location for forays from their home (and work) base in Vicenza.
When they found it, they turned to ETTORE SOTTSASS (also see under "Masters of Postmodern Design") to outfit the interiors, although it had been some time since he had dealt with an entire living space, let alone such a traditional environment.
But the Munaris are not traditionalists, and Sottsass took on the challenge.
He and his associate ALDO CIBIC (see under “Design") came up with an ingenious solution: they built an entirely new structure within the existing one, creating a fluid, contemporary space without destroying the classical surround.
In the high-ceilinged main room (the portego), which flows from the front to the back of the palazzo, a tall, totem-like storage unit stands squarely in the center of the space, modulating the too-grand scale of the room and incorporating not only bookshelves and a desk, but hi-fi equipment, a bar, coat rack, and umbrella stands, as well as halogen lights.
Doorways throughout the apartment are imaginatively framed, each differently, in bright or patterned laminate; the tall white walls are otherwise bare.
The furnishings are a discreet but discernably modern blend of old and new, with antique carpets underscoring a combination of MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design") furniture and less assertive, functional pieces.
The result is a comfortable, modern residence wrapped in history.
OPPOSITE The clean-lined modern interior is enclosed in the traditional shell of a Venetian palazzo OPPOSITE The multi-purpose desk-bookshelf-storage unit serves as room-divider in the large rectangular salon.
TOP LEFT Even the displays on the built-in shelves are deliberately spare and uncluttered.
TOP RIGHT A custom-made wood cabinet with a stepped design.
ABOVE Cleto Munari silver pitchers on a stone fireplace.
RIGHT Memphis furniture and a complementary postmodern-style doorway.
ABOVE LEFT An informal dining area off the kitchen, in soft blue and white.
ABOVE RIGHT Marble and light wood outfit the bathroom.
RIGHT In the dining room, modern furnishings under a classic frescoed ceiling.
BELOW In the living room Memphis-modern sits on an Oriental carpet.
BELOW RIGHT Cleto Munari silver pieces, on a marble shelf in the dining room. # RESIDENCELONG ISLAND,NEW YORK,UNITED STATES,1981 ROBERT A.
M.
STERN POSTMODERN MEETS SHINGLE STYLE IN A TIMELESS BEACHFRONT VACATION HOME.
For a beachfront home in New York's fashionable Hamptons resort area, the firm of ROBERT A.
M.
STERN (also see under “Architecture") breathed new life into the traditional shingle style of early twentieth-century vacation homes with this highly refined interpretation of Postmodernism.
The result: a house that is clearly historicist in its inspiration, but just as clearly contemporary in its execution.
The main feature of the boldly symmetrical form is a tall arched window facing the water, evidence of what the architect describes as the building's "Palladian pretensions." The design and siting consider both the location, at the crest of a dune, and the desire to maximize light and views.
An overscaled stoop leads up to the main floor and provides a perfect front-porch site for viewing the sunset, while the primary living rooms face a generous expanse of sea and sand.
The master bedroom, on the top level beneath the high pitched roof, also looks out on the water, through a grand-scale arched window.
The pleasantly eclectic furnishings,make no attempt to compete with the adventurous exterior, leaving the architecture to speak for itself.
ABOVE In the attic-level master bedroom, a bold, arched window faces the water.
ABOVE Perched at the top of a dune, the house suggests a Stickley-inspired beach cottage.
LEFT The light-flooded interior is oriented toward sea and sky.
BELOW The living-room walls are punctuated by curtainless windows.
OPPOSITE Circular molding in a kitchen window—another focus on the scene outside.

## BRANT
HOUSE GREENWICH, CONNECTICUT, UNITED STATES, 1972 ROBERT VENTURI AND DENISE SCOTT BROWN A BOLD EXTERIOR SETS OFF A HOUSE DESIGNED AROUND A MAJOR ART COLLECTION.
RIGHT The exterior of the house is a contrast of bold curves and rectangular forms.
BELOW The main exterior facade has neatly aligned doors and windows, and two-toned glazed brick in an Op Art pattern.
OPPOSITE The black and white floor and marble stairs leading to the living and dining rooms complement the artworks.
Beautifully sited in a clearing in the middle of a 3o-acre (ı2-ha) lightly wooded site, this house designed by Venturi, Scott Brown and Associates is both a family residence and a showcase for a fine collection of Pop art paintings and Art Deco objects.
Two shades of green brick in an Op-Deco pattern transgress the many-windowed mien of a conventional Georgian country house, in an assertive architectural statement underscored by the bold curve of the facade.
On one side, a low flight of stairs leads up from the sunken auto court into a two-story gallery that displays some of the larger artworks.
The gallery opens on to a series of other rooms, and to the garden outside.
In the living room, a vividly patterned carpet shares the stage with artworks and Art Deco pieces, while admitting the view through the wall of windows, On the opposite side of the house, the kitchen overlooks a children's playroom and the library.
Notwithstanding the interplay between interior and exterior, white walls and polished wood floors throughout help prioritize the art— the project's focal point—although its starring role is shared by the ingeniously conceived architecture.
ABOVE Looking from the living room to the dining room: the architecture frames the art.
ABOVE The living room: understated furnishings and a wall of windows with exterior fretwork, to filter the light.
RIGHT Approached from the driveway, the auto court is a striking architectural element.
LEFT Art and artful cut-outs make a narrow staircase look wider.
BELOW Lively geometry on the walls and ceiling of the auto court.
ABOVE The entrance from the auto court, with stairs up to the main level. # RESIDENCE NEW CASTLE COUNTY, DELAWARE, UNITED STATES, 1983 ROBERT VENTURI AND DENISE SCOTT BROWN VERNACULAR FORM AND POSTMODERN DETAIL COMBINE IN AN INVITING MUSIC-CENTERED HOME.
Every project has special requirements, but the specifications for this house by Venturi, Scott Brown and Associates were particularly challenging.
The wife, a professional musician, needed a music room that could accommodate an organ, two pianos, and a harpsichord, and which would also provide space for informal parties.
The husband needed a study in a quiet part of the house, and both required large windows facing the woods to facilitate their avocation of birdwatching.
Located at the edge of a valley, the house sits in rolling fields in an area where traditional fieldstone barns are a familiar sight.
The architects drew on this vernacular form to make the house fit comfortably into the rural setting and suit the clients' unpretentious lifestyle.
But there is nothing recessive about the architecture, which makes bold statements OPPOSITE TOP AND BOTTOM Set in a bucolic landscape, the vernacular form of the house is overlaid with postmodern details. inside and out.
At the front, a Palladian-inspired crest sits on the gabled roof, announcing its postmodern credentials, and a similar element on stylized two-dimensional columns is grafted onto the rear facade.
Inside, the conservatory takes center stage, with a soaring double-height space framed in pastel-trimmed, pointed arches.
In the center of the house, the dining room is a light-filled pyramid-shaped atrium, punctuated by windows and cut-outs in the walls.
The other rooms are comfortable, unpretentious, and altogether wellsuited to both the house and its occupants.
ABOVE Tall spindle-back chairs bring the trapezoidal two-story dining room down to a comfortable scale.
OPPOSITE The centerpiece of the house, the music room is framed with decorated arches.
ABOVE The blue tiling brings a warming note of color to the mostly neutral-toned interiors.
RIGHT On the open-plan main level, rooms are defined by changes in flooring rather than by doorways. # ZANONE RESIDENCE MEMPHIS, TENNESSEE, UNITED STATES, 1985 MEMPHIS POSTMODERN FURNISHINGS TAKE CENTER STAGE IN THIS COLLECTOR'S HOME.
This otherwise unexceptional midcentury house is furnished with what is surely one of the most impressive collections of MEMPHIS (see under "Edited Design") furniture extant.
Even more impressive is the fact that photographer Dennis Zanone lives with all of it, every day, without any desire to trade, sell, or make use of it for any commercial purpose.
"I just love this stuff," he says happily.
Zanone first saw the furniture in i984, in his hometown, when the inaugural United States tour of the first Memphis collection came to what was then the Brooks Museum of Art (now Memphis Brooks Museum of Art), and was immediately captivated.
Since then, Zanone has been a single-minded collector without a particular cause, except to sing the praises of what he feels are thoroughly admirable and functional objects that should not be dismissed as mere attention-seekers.
He sleeps in the Tawaraya boxing ring couch, speaks on an ETTORF SOTTSASS (also see under “Masters of Postmodern Design") telephone, sits comfortably in the Bel Air chair, and stores books and objects in colorful angular shelves and cabinets.
His solution to the problem of finding coordinating furnishings is not to try; he avoids anything that might compete or distract from the collection—meaning no curtains on the windows, no carpets or area rugs, and only an occasional postmodern graphic on the wall.
It works perfectly: the Memphis objects look very much at home in the company of their peers.
Zanone's living room has been photographed many times and featured in countless publications, eliciting both admiring and critical comments.
Unfazed, he understands and accepts that not everyone shares his taste.
But the environment is one that makes him happy, which is what anyone's home should do.
ABOVE Seating units are clustered in the center of the space.
RIGHT In the sparsely furnished bedroom, the Tawaraya boxing ring makes a striking bed.

## POSTMODERN
DESIGN CONTINUES # THE AFTERMATH, THE REVIVAL, THE FUTURE Too much of any good thing is overkill, and too much of something not universally admired to begin with is surely destined to fail, Like many styles before it, Postmodernism faded in the heat of the spotlight.
By the end of the decade that had seen its rise to prominence, even notoriety, it was being dismissed by those who had most eagerly welcomed its arrival.
Perhaps, as some suggested, it was the emphasis of style over substance;' perhaps it was its assertiveness (the movement's proponents were not subtle in their public statements, and neither were most of its designs).
Or perhaps it was, as Paul Greenhalgh suggests, simply that debates about style, naturally evolved or artificially created, are no longer relevant in our age of instant communication.² The mere fact of a design's popularity inevitably makes it subject to replication in larger quantities, which also inevitably leads to shortcuts—simplified decoration, substituted materials, copyists in lieu of designers.
The result cannot be anything other than inferior to the original.
And if the originals, as with many postmodern objects, were eccentric to begin with, the copies will be even more so.
The result will be their rejection in favor of the next, and perhaps less unconventional, new thing.³ It was no different for Postmodernism.
More than any style since Art Deco, Postmodernism was avidly copied by me-too builders and massmarket manufacturers looking to capitalize on the latest fad.
In a proliferation of bad taste, countless new office buildings sported columns, pilasters, and pediments, and MEMPHIS-like (see under "Producers") patterns ran rampant on sheets and towels, popular-price dinnerware, even houseware.
The New York Times noted the appearance of Memphis motifs on apartment building facades, china, and T-shirts.4 Furniture sported watered-down forms and copycat colors.
Postmodernism seemed an easy-to-do style refresher, but it wasn't easy to get right.
Superficial rather than serious, more kitsch than clever, the copies spread awareness of the style at its least attractive, only helping to accelerate its decline.
As the i99os ushered in a decade of eclecticism in which no single style held sway, the rebellion against doctrinaire Modernism subsided into a noncommittal but unexciting late Modernism that absorbed some of the lessons of Postmodernism; it was more human-centered, more contextual, more respectful of the lessons of history.
But it was not until some three decades after it originated that Postmodernism began to reappear—if not precisely as revival, then as reassessment.
By the start of the present century, collectors had already begun to show interest in postmodern design; some scholars deemed it an important part of the history of Modernism from its earliest manifestations, though only a few museums had paid it much attention.
The rethinking intensified after the death of ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") in 2o07.
