BELOW Kelly Behun, Neolaminati bench, laminate and wood, 2016. # Collecting Postmodern Design Alexander Payne Postmodern design has been getting more attention in the past few years as a new category in collecting It has actually been collected since the mid-i99os and has been selling consistently at auction since the beginning of this century.
In fact museums were interested in Postmodernism from the get-go.
Some curators bought the originals from the early design exhibitions, and when the secondary market developed, auction houses were selling to museums before they were selling to collectors.
The first collectors were Europeans who were in Italy when postmodern design began; they visited designers in their studios or saw the work at early exhibitions in Milan galleries.
The market now is much more international.
What does this mean for the individual who wants to start collecting postmodern design?
Is there much left, and is it affordable?
Fortunately, there is now an abudance of design coming into the market, and most of which is not unduly costly, especially when compared to styles like French Art Deco.
Where should you begin?
It is perhaps best to start by educating yourself on the subject.
Radical Design came first, and Postmodernism per se dates from the late i97os.
Find out what interests you, then do some serious research.
Look at the main protagonists in this story; learn which Italian design movements came out of the period.
Read about the principal designers to understand how their work has gone on to influence design today.
Much of the experimental design of the i98os, for example, has informed textile or silverware design today, and many connections can be made.
It is worth looking up the key works that were presented in the seminal exhibitions and events that put this design language on the map, including those associated with Archizoom\*, Superstudio\*, Alchimia\*, and other Radical Design groups.
Identify the most important pieces by designers like ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), ALESSANDRO MENDINI (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), ANDREA BRANZI, MICHELE DE LUCCHI, and SHIRO KURAMATA (see all under "Desięn"); also check out NATHALIE DU PASQUIER (see under "Design"), PETER SHIRE (see under “Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), and GEORGE SOWDEN (see under “Design").
Discover old issues of Domus, Casabella, and other publications that showcased the period (some of these are collectible as well).
Look for interesting concepts and experimental design to see which themes and types of objects appeal.
Most seasoned collectors of postmodern design are not collecting objects by category; the specialist collector is more the exception than the rule.
Most people will buy a design object, whether it is furniture, ceramics or glass, metalwork, lighting, or jewelry, because they are particularly drawn to it.
Serious collectors will make a purchase in order either to live with the work, to incorporate it into another collection, or perhaps to connect it with the architecture of their home.
They're motivated by a feeling for the object itself, which is the only way to collect.
If you're inspired by a particular piece and feel a passion to own it, you're not likely to make a mistake.
One important caution: you will need to find out how many editions were made of the object you like—the most collectible pieces are major works that were produced in limited editions, or of which few examples are known to exist.
This is more difficult than it might sound: information such as this was not always recorded, and some pieces were produced only for a salone or triennial, or were one-offs for avant-garde happenings.
When they surface, such objects are always highly prized, and are rarely affordable for the new collector.
ABOVE The exhibition "Memphis=Milano: 198os Italian Design” at Dixon Gallery and Gardens, Memphis, Tennessee, 2014.
When looking specifically at MEMPHIS designs (see under “Edited Design") , dates are important.
Many are still being produced, decades after they were originally designed.
The newer ones are not as valuable and serious collectors will only consider works that can be categorized as coming from the original postmodern period.
This is why provenance—a record of where the piece came from and/or who owned it—makes an enormous difference.
While this is important for all collectors, it is especially so for collectors of postmodern design.
If you cannot date the piece to its conception/presentation to the world at large, then you can't be sure that you are collecting a “genuine" work.
You could be buying something that was made much later: even if the design looks the same the value won't be.
How do you tell an item's provenance?
Invoices are best, if they are available, but there are other possible clues: the piece may have been illustrated in a catalogue, written about in a newspaper or magazine, or otherwise documented.
It may have been created for a specific performance, an exhibition, or a display, sometimes even for a special client.
Fortunately for the novice collector, postmodern design is not unduly costly.
Even with a significant piece, prices do not compare to categories like, for example, Art Deco design.
There are exceptions, of course.
Proust chairs, by Alessandro Mendini, have been bringing in strong prices from collectors looking for good early examples.
And Shiro Kuramata's Miss Blanche chair has set auction records.
But there is much more to look for, and it can be found at fairly reasonable prices.
The market for postmodern pieces is still in its infancy and has a long way to go as more people discover the period.
And while investment is never a sure thing, if you collect in a category that is still developing, there is a good chance your purchase will increase in vaiue.
If you're interested in collecting Postmodernism, now is a good time to start. # On FreedomJohanna Grawunder In the fall of i986, Design Gallery Milano installed an exhibition of Murano hand-blown glass objects: thirty four vases designed by ETTORE SOTTSASS (see under "Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design") for MEMPHIS (see under “Edited Design").
I had been working at Sottsass Associati in Milan since the summer of i985 and had seen a few previous Memphis exhibitions.
They were big events; Sottsass's international tribe came out in full force.
Many participated, designing furniture and objects for the Memphis collections, and those are the famous names we all know: MICHELE DE LUCCHI, ANDREA BRANZI, NATALIE DU PASQIUER, GEORGE SOWDEN, MARCO ZANINI, ALDO CIBIC, MATTEO THUN (see all under “Design"), and PETER SHIRE (see under “Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design"), as well as MICHAEL GRAVES (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and "Living with Postmodern Design"), SHIRO KURAMATA (see under "Design"), HANS HOLLEIN (see under "Architecture"), and others.
Eventually, younger designers entered the group, including James Irvine.
MASSIMO IOSA GHINI (see under “Design"), Ferruccio Laviani, Beppe Caturegli, Giovannella Formica, and Shuji Hisada.
In Milan around that time, designing a piece for Memphis meant serious street cred.
This created a polarization between one school of thought and others, stimulating discussion, criticism, and backlash minimalism, forcing the design community to take positions—something all great revolutions do.
On a tall, almost room-sized block of whitepainted MDF, Sottsass displayed his thirty-four shocking sculptures of translucent brightly colored glass.
It was a Warriors of Xi'an effect (the celebrated Terracotta Army) but in geometrical, elegant glass, blown high and thin: some with dangling earrings tied to looping glass rings with thin gold threads, some with forms blown inside forms, and some heretically glued together (a no-no in traditional glass blowing) to form death-defying constructions.
Madonna's “Like a Virgin" was pounding out of speakers.
I was alone in this room for perhaps four minutes, but the experience revealed to me the emotional, transcendent, drug-like power of design in one dramatic moment.
Here, illuminated simply with a few spotlights, was human achievement: the result of hundreds of man hours of Murano glass-blowers, each artisan a master of the ancient art, with thousands of hours of experi ence, failures, and successes, a continuing process of error-correction not only within the knowledge process of mastering the specific skill of glass-blowing, but also live, with each piece blown.
Here was performance art.
OPPOSITE Fernando and Humberto Campana, Sushi IlI chair, felt, textiles, plastic, EVA, painted tubular steel, 2002.
ABOVE Johanna Grawunder, Perf bench, perforated metal with colored fluorescent tubes, 2006.
Here was art.
Though Sottsass avoided the label "artist" most of his life, I think he began to enjoy the confusion and the opportunity for pure theoretical and formal speculation.
Though all of these improbable glass objects were “vases"—they had small or large compartments where you could, in theory, put water and flowers—this was a construct to keep these objects in the realm of design: not to minimize their power or worth, but to enrich the discourse and challenges of design by bringing it towards a more theoretical art.
Memphis created design-art.
The cognitive disfluency that resulted in the difficulty of categorizing these artifacts as design or art, or within micro-genres and stylistic labels like neo-expressionist, Pop, minimalist, or postmodern, led to more thoughtful consideration and, ultimately, deeper underštanding.
I think that Memphis, and for me this particular collection of glass objects, was a defining moment, in a long line of cultural transgressions, that allowed design a new and brighter freedom.
Design is free.
Unharnessed from the traditional limits of industry (costs, production, marketing, sales) through technology and materials science on the one hand, and the very specific market needs for innovation on the other, design-art is a recent nomenclature that covers what used to be called decorative art, but also gets into what we could call theoretical design.
The idea is that design can be an idiosyncratic art form, not solely site-specific, and/or not necessarily based on function or the industrial prerogative.
Some of us designers have a residual moralism that tells us it must be functional.
That is why when I design, say, a chandelier, I spend a lot of my time and production research concerned with the quality of light.
Most of us also have respect for production technique and artisan craft, and this translates into intense micro-education with every new piece produced, listening carefully to artisan expertise, and then, sometimes, trying to exalt or deny this craft.
But content-wise (and design is a richly saturated content-based endeavor), we all bring something unique to the discourse.
For example, I am attracted to that part of American cultural heritage defined by austerity and hard, strong gestures.
American minimalism and industrial ingenuity or machismo are phenomena that are complicated and nuanced, and I have tried RIGHT Studio Job, bench from the Perished series, ebony with laser-cut inlays, 20o6.
BELOW Marten Baas, Secondhand bookcase (from the series Hey, Chair, Be a Bookshelf!), mixed materials, PU coating, 2005. to incorporate this spirit into my work.
This theoretical aspect of design, very personal, is an intangible quality that cannot be described so much as experienced.
It can be part of a narrative used to place an object or a collection of objects into the contemporary landscape, but it is also an expression of freedom.
Designers and designer-artists of my generation— Marc Newson, Marcel Wanders, Hella Jongerius, Arik Levy, Konstantin Grcic, Robert Stadler, Karim Rashid, Campana Brothers, to name just a few—are not at all from the same cultural lineage, but many of us cut our chops at some point in Milan, during or soon after Memphis.
Later, others like Maarten Baas, Joris Laarman, Nacho Carbonell, and Studio Job unabashedly embraced a system that had been broken open to expressionistic artistic endeavor.
The issues and obsessions each of us metabolizes into our work are idiosyncratic by definition.
Our processes and objectives are different.
And it is really, really hard to pin most of us down into a category such as “artist" or “designer" (we squirm).
Contemporary culture is story-based.
Though there are very deep currents flowing within it that tie together all aspects of culture, from politics to philosophy to music, art, and, yes, design, the popular consumption of "narrative" in contemporary culture often and oddly makes it seem like a neoteric tail-chase.
Arguably, during the time of Memphis and, before that, of Radical Design and Superstudio\*, contemporary culture was more idea-based.
There seemed to be common cause in reacting, one way or another, to the failures and successes of Modernism, and to proposing “solutions” (Superstudio's Continuous Monument comes to mind).
Now design, I mean art-design, theoretical design, is fragmented.
There are threads, like the return of materiality and technology as prime initiatives (a neo-neo-Modernism?) and an awareness of sustainability, but there is really no “movement" to speak of, beyond the meta-idea of the existence of the art-design market itself.
Design-art can be found in galleries and at art fairs.
This is new.
It gets discussed, published, and collected.
Sometimes it resonates in the larger cultural landscape—and sometimes it is slightly out of sync.
Though the abstract nature of much art-design work today, including some of my own, might seem to be striving for a sort of timelessness, design is anything but timeless.
The very personal act of establishing frameworks from which to work is empowering and subjective, but temporal.
We (designers) live within our culture and time.
We are not outsiders.
We are storytellers.
Our stories, even nostalgic ones, or futuristic ones, are based and realized in our specific, technological cultural, and (sometimes) market-based moment.
Sottsass was a guerrilla.
Even his earliest work was a shot across the bow of the business-minded cultural elite yacht that had reduced Modernism from an ideology to a stylish new fashion, losing much significance in the process.
Memphis was urban guerrilla warfare on the pretensions of “good-taste" design.
It questioned the concept of objective perfection and the tyranny of codified “correct" design solutions.
And therefore, it exists comfortably in the intellectual worldview of postmodern philosophy.
It offered an alternative, sometimes transcendent, revolutionary way to see and interact with the world.
And, once you have seen Sottsass's thirty-four glass vases, you cannot unsee them.
LEFT Joris Laarman, Bone chair, aluminum, 2006.
BELOW Jeroen Verhoeven, Cinderella table, handassembled CNC-cut birch plywood, 2005. # AFTERWORD: A POSTMODERN FUTURE?
Denise Scott Brown This book illustrates works of a broad range of postmodern designers.
Their achievements have all the joy and relevance of Postmodernism.
But the movement was later abused, and ROBERT VENTURI and I (see under “Masters of Postmodern Design" and “Living with Postmodern Design") have tried to distinguish our approach from what was subsequently called PoMo.
The roots of our Postmodernism lie in thoughts on social and moral responsibility and turning a blind eye, formulated during and after the Second World War.
Some artists joined in, but architects and planners who re-envisioned cities during the war, and began rebuilding quickly thereafter, followed their own visions and their idealistic but uninformed convictions that their projects would benefit the world.
Their naive idealism, ignorance, and lack of questioning had a part in the damage done to the poorest in cities, but I like to think that Le Corbusier, had he been given the chance, would have faced urban realties with more insight than have generations of architects since, who merely ask, “what did Corbu do?" In the 196os, we formulated our ideas by researching, describing, teaching, and practicing.
In all these we argued against the late Modernists and with the early Moderns, not in their Cubist vocabulary (though we love it) or their ignoring of history, but in their support for Neue Sachlichkeit.
New Objectivity meant facing the realities of industrialization and new ways of life in Europe.
From these conditions and the ugly industrial architecture across the tracks they intended to draw a new beauty for a new era.
We did too.
With Corbu, we cried out against “eyes that will not see" and hoped to bring seeing eyes to social reality, the messy vitality of the existing city, and the beauty of some disdained environments.
In aligning ourselves with early Modernism we felt we were updating, not overturning, the movement.
But at times we shifted its focus, trying for example to broaden Modern concepts of functionalism to cover issues missed by early Modernists.
One is that the client's brief is the first but far from the last use of a building.
How should functionalists plan for changing activities over time?
Another is architecture's communication function, its many types and levels.
A third concerns the rela tionships of our projects with the city.
Architects do “site analysis," but until we vastly broaden its scope and our understanding of urban functional relations, we will continue our urban mistakes.
In i976 Philip Johnson\*, studying our exhibition “Signs of Life" (Renwick Gallery, Smithsonian Institution, Washington, DC), concluded that the relation of Modernism and the Chicago School was not being repeated between Postmodernism and 197os commercial architecture.
He set about righting the situation and for some years PoMo branded commercial architecture.
Then, it died.
Although descended from Postmodernism, PoMo was not our offspring.
It was a specious version that borrowed Postmodernism's forms, though not its principles, and applied them without talent.
But in about 2ooo, a new generation of architects began to reconsider Postmodernism as we defined it.
This interest continues, and, as Judith Gura suggests, the story of Postmodernism is not complete.
But I feel it now has a good chance of surviving as a continuation of the main trunk of Modernism because it reflects the movement's brave objectivity, its good causes, and its intention to draw art from hard reality.
And although postmodern rule-breaking is sassier, it's pushed by the same high aims.
ABOVE Denise Scott Brown and Robert Venturi in the Las Vegas landscapes that inspired their landmark book, Learning from Las Vegas (1972), 1966.

## A-2
OF DESIGNERS AND PRODUCERS

## ABET
LAMINATI See p.338.
ACME See p.
342.

## ALBUS,
VOLKER b.
1949 Born in Frankfurt and trained as an architect at the Rheinisch-Westfälische Technische Hochschule in Aachen (i968–76), Albus became an important figure and spokesperson for New German Design.
He has been influential as a teacher and interior and product designer and has written regularly for design publications in Germany.
He is a professor of product design at the Staatliche Hochschule für Gestaltung in Karlsruhe and has designed exhibitions as well as furniture and interiors.

## ALCHIMIA
(STUDIO) Avant-garde group founded in Milan by Alessandro Guerriero\* whose members included ETTORE SOTTSASS, Paola Navone, and ANDREA BRANZI.
The group was best known for its riffs on famous objects, intended as critiques of established standards of good design.
Their Bau.Haus I (i979) and Bau.Haus II (i98o) collections transformed iconic objects such as a Mies van der Rohe chair and included ALESSANDRO MENDINI's Proust armchair and Kandinsky sofa.
ALESSI See p.
346.

## ALIAS
Italian furniture producer, founded in i979, of chairs, tables, upholstered seating, and accessories.
Commissioned designs by some of the leading names in modern furniture, including MARIO BOTTA, MICHELE DE LUCCHI, PHILIPPE STARCK, Jasper Morrison\*, and other internationally known designers.

## ANDERSON,
CHARLES SPENCER b.
1958 Minnesota-born graphic designer Anderson effectively put the Midwest on the design map, using vernacular design as a vehicle for a playful style that mixes pop culture with midcentury Modernism.
He formed his eponymous firm in ı989, reviving what had been considered visual clichés into graphic design with a sensibility suggesting folk art.
He also assembled an archival collection of commercial images which has become a design resource for others.

## ANTHOLOGIE
QUARTETT Producer of avant-garde furniture that began in i983 in Hannover, Germany, as a gallery for architecture, design, art, and fashion, showing work by major designers including BOŘEK ŠÍPEK, Ettore Sottsass, MATTEO THUN, and Daniel Weil.
In i987 Anthologie Quartett became an independent company and has since developed collections of furniture, lighting, objects, and jewelry by more than fifty European designers.

## ARCHIGRAM
The first of the radical groups was Archigram, organized in i96ı at the Architectural Association in London by Peter Cook, Warren Chalk, Dennis Crompton, David Greene, Ron Herron, and Michael Webb.
In the exhibition "Living Cities” at the Institute of Contemporary Arts in 1963, they presented a reconception of urban design that would "bypass the decaying Bauhaus image." Their futuristic ideas, influenced by Antonio Sant'Elia and Buckminster Fuller, included concepts like Plug-In City, in which standard cells or self-contained modules would serve as living spaces, connected to a central infrastructure.
None of their ideas were executed, but they opened a dialogue about the role of architecture in a technology-oriented society, and Archigram's concepts for dealing with urban development were later realized, though in far more conventional form.

## ARCHIZOOM
ASSOCIATI Archizoom, founded in Florence in 1966 by Andrea Branzi, Gilberto Corretti, Paolo Deganello\*, and Massimo Morozzi (later joined by Dario and Lucia Bartolini), conflated the names of Archigram\* and the journal Zoom Their visionary project for urban design, the No-Stop City, looked to what they viewed as the “elimination of architecture itself." They co-organized the 1966 “Superarchitecttura” exhibition in Pistoia (with Superstudio\*) and designed witty pieces of furniture, like the Safari and Superonda sofas and the Mies lounge, before disbanding in 1974.

## ARQUITECTONICA
Bernardo Fort-Brescia, b.
1951; Laurinda Spear, b.
1950 See p.
72.

## ART
ET INDUSTRIE This New York gallery was part of the downtown Manhattan scene in the 1980s and introduced important postmodern

## ALCHIMIA
Scivolando chair, by Alessandro Mendinı, mirrored glass and wood, 1983.
ART ET INDUSTRIE Table by Alex Locadia, designed for Art et Industrie, multimedia, wood, glass, metal, 1984.
ART ET INDUSTRIE The Lesson, chair by Howard Meister, painted bent steel and plate, 1982 BEHHETT, GARAY KNOX A Nite on Lindquist Ridge, sinuous table lamp, 1990. work from Europe as well as American objects that blurred the barriers between art and design at a time when the idea was new and controversial.
Founded by Rick Kaufmann, the gallery was named for a French magazine and drew a loose collection of neighborhood artists from the newly developing SoHo area.
Most of the designs were one-offs, though some were licensed to European and Japanese manufacturers to enable them to be made in quantity.
Most of Art et Industrie's clients were designers and art collectors.
Designers shown by the gallery included Howard Meister\*, Terence Main, Forrest Myers, and Michele Oka Doner, and it was the first to show designs by Alchimia\* and Ettore Sottsass.

## ARTELUCE
Founded by Gino Sarfatti, an aeronautical engineer, Arteluce was among the firms that established Italy as the dominant source of innovative modern lighting design in the midcentury years, producing pieces by Marco Zanuso\*, Franco Albini, and later Matteo Thun and others.
In 1974 the company became a division of Flos.
Many of the company's designs are now in museum collections.

## ARTEMIDE
One of the most recognized brands in lighting, Milan-based Artemide was founded in i96o by Ernesto Gismondi and Sergio Mazza.
Operating on its philosophy of “the human light," the company was among the pioneering firms that made use of new lighting technology to produce innovative modern designs to accompany the furnishings that brought Italy to international renown in the decades after the Second World War.
When Postmodernism came to the fore, Artemide commissioned designs to complement the new aesthetic.
Probably its best-known products are the self-balancing Tizio (i972) by Richard Sapper\*, one of the first lamps to use halogen bulbs, and Tolomeo, an incandescent desk lamp designed in i986 by Michele De Lucchi and Giancarlo Fassina.

## AULENTI,
GAE 1927-2012 One of the most prominent of the post-Second World War generation of Italian designers, and one of the few women in the field, architect Gae Aulenti was most noted for her spare furniture and lighting designs as well as for the Musée d’Orsay in Paris, housed in a former railway station.
She also worked for the influential magazine Casabella doing graphic design as well as editorial work.
In 1982 she was selected to design one of the conceptual "environments" for the influential Museum of Modern Art exhibition "Italy: The New Domestic Landscape" in New York.
Like most designers in the i98os, she responded to the rebellion against Modernism with objetts like the Tavolo con Ruote coffee table, which is supported by industrial wheels, and the Jumbo marble coffee table, whose legs suggest classical columns.

## BD
EDICIONES DE DISEÑO Spanish furniture producer BD Ediciones was established by OSCAR TUSQUETS BLANCA and others.
They made and marketed designs by themselves as well as by MARISCAL, Mendini, and Sottsass, and revived furniture by Antoni Gaudí and Charles Rennie Mackintosh.

## BEDIN,
MARTINE b.1957 See p.174.
BEEBY, THOMAS b.
1941 See p.76.

## BELLINI,
MARIO b.
1935 Architect Bellini was the influential design director of the Milan department store La Rinascente before opening his own office designing for Olivetti, Renault, and many other companies, including Cassina, B&B Italia, Artemide\*, and Flos.
He contributed to the exhibition “Italy: The New Domestic Landscape" at MoMA in New York, which also honored him with a retrospective exhibition in 1987.
From 1985 to 1991 he edited Domus magazine, featuring in it many of the designs of the postmodernists, though his own designs did not reflect a postmodern aesthetic.

## BENNETT,
GARRY KNOX b.
1934 Celebrated Californian furniture maker Bennett is known for his intricately detailed, finely crafted wood pieces, and his work is held in museums as well as private collections worldwide.
He has been honored by the Furniture Society, the American Craft Council, and the lames Renwick Alliance.
His work was included in a i983 exhibition at the Gallery at Workbench, New York, during the height of the postmodern movement, and his designs from that period sometimes reflected the whimsy and historicism that characterized much of the era's design.

## BERGHOF,
LANOES, RANG see Draenert BIERUT, MICHAEL b.
1957 See p.
282.

## BINAZ22I,
LAPO b.
1943 Florence-born Binazzi was a founder of UFO\* (1967) and Global Tools\* (1973), two groups associated with Italy's Radical Design movement, whose experimental forays into conceptual architecture and design set the stage for what would become Postmodernism.
With them, he participated in the ı4th Milan Triennial (i968), the 197ı Paris Biennale, "Contemporanea" (Rome, 1973–74), and the 1978 Venice Biennale.
He is known for his interest in melding artistic experience and experimentation, and his whimsical objects incorporate strong social commentary, for example the Paramount table lamp, which takes the form of a mountain topped with a parasol—a play on the film studio's logo—and the Dollaro, which references 2oth Century Fox.

## BITOSSI
CERAMICHE Bitossi was established in Montelupo Fiorentino, Tuscany, in i92ı by a family that had been producing ceramic works since the 187os, and works in the ceramics tradition of the area.
In addition to the factory, still on its original site today, Bitossi operates the Museo Artistico Industriale Bitossi, which houses the firm's extensive archives.
The company's collaborations with Ettore Sottsass produced some of the most admired postmodern ceramics, including his large-scale Totems.

## BLEY,
THOMAS b.
1954 One of the early members of the MEMPHIS collaborative, for which he designed the Astor table lamp (1981).
Although he trained in Germany, he has spent much of his career in America, where he was a principal of Zebra Design in New York and frogdesign in Los Angeles before founding International Design Network and Institute, a graduate program in design.
BOFILL, RICARDO b.
1939 See p.80.

## BOHLIN,
JONAS b.
1953 Something of an iconoclast among his often conservative Swedish contemporaries, Bohlin is perhaps best known for the Concrete chair (i981, his graduate project at Stockholm's prestigious Konstfack college), a steel and concrete contribution to the postmodern vocabulary of transgressive designs.
Bohlin's often provocative pieces for the innovative manufacturer Kallemo and others have included lighting, glass, and textiles.
He has also designed exhibition installations and interiors and taught at the Beckmans Designhögskola, Stockholm.
Despite the rebellious nature of his designs, he has received several major awards and design grants, and his work has been featured in Nordic-focused design exhibitions in several countries.
BOTTA,MARIO b.1943 Seep.84 BRANZI, ANDREA b.
1938 Seep.180.
BRODSKY, ALEXANDER b.
1955 Brodsky was a maverick in the conservative Russian architectural world.
After Nikita Khrushchev abolished the Academy of Architecture, classical references were supplanted by a state-imposed architecture of faceless utilitarian forms.
Rebelling against this aesthetic of the Communist state, Brodsky and his partner llya Utkin created intricate copper-plate etchings of neoclassical structures and visionary cityscapes that parodied the decaying cities of the Soviet Union.
These thinly veiled criticisms of the regime, which they submitted in 1984 to competitions organized by Japan Architect and Architecture and Urbanism, brought them international renown when exhibited in Europe and America.
In the 1970s and 198os they were the best known of a group of rebels called the "paper architects." Brodsky and Utkin's building designs used local materials like glass and timber to both evoke Postmodernism and Deconstructivism and to recall elements from Russian history.
None of the designs were built, but they became known through publications and competitions; it was only after 2ooo that Brodsky's projects began to be realized, rather than merely artistically imagined.
BRODY, NEVILLE b.
1957 Seep.284.
BUBBLES, BARHEY Album sleeve for Elvis Costello and the Attractions, 1981.
CASTLE, WENDELL Donut endtable, wood and glass, c.
1980.
CASTLE, WENDELL Limited-edition twodoor cabinet, c.
1980.
CARAMIA,PIERANGELO Arcadia Swing table, polished aluminum and glass, 1987.

## BUBBLES,
BARHEY [COLIN FULCHER] 1942-1983 Barney Bubbles was a radical graphic artist whose designs for album covers were emblematic of the emerging New Wave.
He designed for underground magazines and was art director of several record companies in the 1970s and ı98os, working for a variety of rock groups, including Hawkwind, who were associated with the flourishing British independent scene.
His versatile style shifted from comic book-style to Art Nouveau-inspired, to Cubist-influenced designs that prefigured the angularity of postmodernist work.

## CAPPELLINI
One of Italy's leading producers of contemporary furniture, Cappellini has been known for its commitment to ahead-of-the-curve design in a wide range of categories, from seating to storage to offbeat accessories.
The collection includes the iconic Proust chair, originally designed for Alchimia by Alessandro Mendini, as well as designs by SHIRO KURAMATA, Marcel Wanders, and Marc Newson.
The company is now part of Poltrona Frau\*.
CARAMIA, PIERANGELO b.
1986 A graduate of Milan's Domus Academy, where he studied scenic design under Andrea Branzi, Caramia has practiced both architecture and product design, working for such firms as ALESSI, Cassina, Sawaya & Moroni\*, and Poltrona Frau and collaborating with Philippe Starck in designs for the Royalton Hotel in New York.
In i986 he designed a stool for Memphis.
His designs tend to be curvilinear, in contrast to the angular look of most of his postmodernist contemporaries.

## CARSON,
DAUID b.
1964 A latecomer to graphic design, iconoclast Carson credits his relative lack of formal training for his willingness to flout convention.
After designing for California surfing and skateboard magazines, he became celebrated for his work on Ray Gun, an alternative music publication, whose varied pages often combined photographs with distorted or overlapping type and oddly shaped or upside-down images.
Carson gained status in the i99os as a standardbearer for his profession as it was being reshaped by technology.

## CASTLE,
WENDELL b.
1932 One of the most prominent members of the studio craft movement in the 196os, Castle has evolved into a major figure in contemporary art furniture.
Like many other designers and craftspeople of his generation, Castle experimented with Postmodernism, departing from his signature stacked and carved natural wood designs to create objects that played with irregular forms and uncharacteristic colors.
Though they were perhaps not purely postmodern, since they adopted neither ad-hoc materials nor classical forms, they were certainly transgressive and constituted brief diversions in his ongoing exploration of new materials and techniques.
CHANTRY,ART b.1954 Seep.290.

## CHICAGO
SEVEN The group of architects known as the Chicago Seven helped change the course of Chicago architecture with “Exquisite Corpse," a 1976 counter-exhibition protesting the emphasis on the International Style in an exhibition at the city's Museum of Contemporary Art.
The show presented postmodern historicist and transgressive architecture.
The group's original members THOMAS BEEBY,STANLEY TIGERMAN, Larry Booth, Stuart Cohen, Ben Weese, James Ingo Freed, and James Nagle were later joined by Helmut Jahn\*, Gerald Horn, Kenneth Schroeder, and Cynthia Weese.
CHWAST, SEYMOUR b.
1931 See p.292.
CIBIC, ALDO b.
1955 See p.186.
CLETO MUNARI See p.
350.
COATES, NIGEL b.
1949 See p.
190.

## COOP
HIMMELB(L)AU This Austrian architectural firm was founded in i968 by Wolf D.
Prix, Helmut Swiczinsky, and Michael Holzer (Holzer left the firm in 197ı; Swiczinsky retired in 2ooi).
The firm is best known for its Deconstructivist designs, most notably the Falkestrasse rooftop remodeling project (i988) in Vienna, which has become an iconic example of the style.
In 1994 the firm designed the east pavilion of the Groninger Museum, Netherlands, whose other pavilions were designed by Alessandro Mendini and Philippe Starck.

## DALISI,
RICCARDO b.
1931 A founder of the Radical Design group Global Tools in i973, Dalisi was born in Potenza, Italy, and graduated from the University of Naples, where he taught for many years and directed its institute for industrial design.
He became internationally celebrated for the Neopolitan coffee maker he designed for Alessi in i987, updating the traditional tin coffee pot in a project that took eight years and involved some two hundred prototypes exploring variations of the form.
The project won him the coveted Compasso d'Oro in i981, and many of Dalisi's coffee pot variations have since featured in important museum exhibitions.
Dalisi has also designed chairs for Zanotta\* and a variety of products for other companies.
Since the i99os he has turned to sculpture, working primarily in brass and copper.

## DE
LUCCHI, MICHELE b.
1951 See p.194.

## DEGANELLO,PAOLO
b.
1940 From 1966 to 1973, Deganello was a founder of and contributor to Archizoom Associati\*, Florence's entry in the roster of avant-garde design groups; he later worked independently in Milan on architecture and restoration projects.
He also designed furniture for a number of Italian firms, participating in six Milan Triennale exhibitions as well as designing exhibitions in several other countries.
He received his architectural training at the University of Florence, worked as an urban planner, and has taught or lectured at the Architectural Association in London, the Domus Academy in Milan, and other European institutions.
His most recognizable furniture design is probably the curvy Torso chair (1983) for Cassina.
DISHEY See p.
354.

## DRAENERT
In the early 198os Dr.
Peter Draenert, founder of this German producer launched a unique collection of Artifacts, artistic furniture pieces.
He believed that a traditional manufactory with strong craftsmanship skills was the best pre-requisite for producing limitededition masterpieces, heirlooms, and works of art by select architects and designers.
The arrival of Postmodernism had revived interest in ornament, color, diversity of material, and form, and symbolic references from history, all of which would be introduced in the company's special collections, and would bring Draenert international recognition.

## DRIADE
Another of the small firms that have helped make Italy a leading source of design innovation, Driade was founded in i988 by Enrico and Antonio Astori with the aim of operating as “a factory of art." Their notable designs in the postmodern vernacular have included Philippe Starck's Costes chair and designs by Oscar Tusquets Blanca and BOŘEK ŠÍPEK.
DU PASQUIER, NATHALIE D.
1957 See p.
202.
DUANY PLATER-ZYBERK Andrés Duany b.
1949 Elizabeth Plater-2yberk b.
1950 Husband-and-wife team Duany and Plater-Zyberk were co-founders of Miami's ARQUITECTONICA (with Bernard Fort-Brescia, Laurinda Spear, and Hervin Romney), leaving to found their own eponymous firm in 198o and becoming innovative practitioners of the New Urbanism.
They received international recognition for their design of Seaside\*, Florida, and have since designed scores of new towns and redesigned others to revive the diversity and livability of traditional urban communities.
Seaside's town center features buildings by postmodern architects including LÉON KRIER and ROBERT A.
M.
STERN, along with others in modern and nineteenth-century revival styles.
Both Duany and Plater- Zyberk received undergraduate degrees in architecture and urban planning from Princeton, and Master's degrees from Yale School of Architecture

## EDRA
Founded in i987, when Postmodernism was at its most prominent and Italian design led the way in design innovation Edra produces sofas and seating pieces intended to be distinctive and technically advanced as well as comfortable.
Many of its designs are soft-edge pieces in unconventional forms designed for eye-appeal as well as comfort; notable examples include the flower-like seating of MASANORI UMEDA and the provocative shapes of Fernando and Humberto Campana.
DALISI, RICCAADO Curving teapot, for Cleto Munari, 1992.
DALISI, RICCARDO Tea and coffee service. for Cleto Munari, 1992.
DRAENERT Writing desk, by Berghof, Landes, Rang for Draenert, 1985.
DUANY PLATER-ZYBERK A typical street in Seaside, Florida, with the ambiance of a historic Southern town and classical-vernacular architecture.
EISENMAN, PETER Wexner Center for the Arts, Ohio State University, 1989 DUANY PLATER-ZYBERK Aerial view of the planned community of Seaside, Florida.

## EISENMAN,
PETER b.
1932 Generally characterized as a Deconstructivist, though often mentioned in postmodern dialogue, New Jersey-born Eisenman received a BA in architecture from Cornell, an MS from Columbia, and MA and PhD from the University of Cambridge.
He founded the Institute for Architecture and Urban Studies and edited its journal, Oppositions, from 1973 to 1982.
Moving from diagrams of fragmented structures to actual buildings, he designed a series of inventive numbered houses (i967-75) that rejected the functionalism of Modernism with elements like nonsupporting columns and stairways with no destination.
His work has been criticized as focusing on theory at the expense of function.
He was designated one of the New York Five—along with MICHAEL GRAVES, John Hejduk\*, Charles Gwathmey, and Richard Meier\*—also called the "Whites" for their shared adherence to Le Corbusierprincipled Modernism.
His most noted work includes the Wexner Center for the Arts (i983) in Columbus, Ohio.

## ESPRIT
Esprit is an American manufacturer of popular-priced fashions and accessories founded in San Francisco in 1979 by Doug and Susie Tompkins, who had previously founded outdoor clothing company The North Face.
Esprit hired Sottsass Associati to develop the design concept for its retail stores, which followed the Memphis design aesthetic.
Esprit's collections of casual sportswear and urban fashion became successful with a young generation of consumers looking for clothing to suit a less formal lifestyle, but the company faltered in a competitive market and closed its American stores in 2o12 (recently reopening two stores in Canada).

1946 See pp.

## FRIEDMAN,
DAN 1945-1995 An American graphic designer and teacher, Friedman was one of the influential designers responsible for the shift from Swiss rationalism to freer expression in graphic design He studied fine arts at Carnegie Mellon University and traveled to Europe on a Fulbright grant to study at the prestigious Hochschule für Gestaltung Ulm, Germany, and then at the Schule für Gestaltung in Basel Switzerland, where he was encouraged by WOLFGANG WEINGART, who was teaching the new typography program, to work with a less dogmatic approach.
Friedman began teaching on Yale's graphic design program, combining fundamentals and innovation, and also taught at Purchase College, SUNY, before leaving for professional practice in New York.
At Anspach Grossman Portugal, and then at Pentagram, he worked for corporate clients, before leaving to pursue more experimental paths in the downtown New York art community, designing fantasy furniture and postmodern objects as well as inventive graphics.

## GEHRY,
FRANK b.
1929 Seep.94.

## GILARDI,
PIERO 1942 An artist who became Influential in the Arte Povera movement in Italy—which fed into the avant-garde Radical Design groups—Gilardi was best known for his Nature Carpets, made of molded and painted polyurethane and shaped like rocks or natural objects.
First shown in the i96os, when they were considered radical, they were reintroduced and praised in the i98os postmodern era, when his environmentalist themes were thought current and culturally relevant.

## GLOBAL
TOOLS Global Tools, which existed from 1973 to 1975, was an experimental educational program organized in Florence by Radical Design thinkers including Andre Branzi, Ettore Sottsass, MARCO ZANINI, and Ugo La Pietra\*.
Calling itself a "non-school," it sought to replace existing architectural instruction with multidisciplinary methods that would lead to a new form of education, focused on “happenings" and interactions between craft and design.

## GODLEY-SCHWAN
Lyn Godley, b.
1956 Lloyd Schwan, 1956-2001 See p.
212.
GOLDBERG,CARIN b.1953 Seep.302.

## GORLIN,
ALEXANDER 1955 A graduate of the Cooper Union School of Architecture with a Master's from Yale School of Architecture, Alexander Gorlin opened his office in New York City after a residence at the American Academy in Rome.
He is best known for his elegant residences, though he has also designed schools, places of worship, and affordable housing.
Though not assertively postmodern his buildings are clearly inspired by elements of classical architecture.

## GOUDE,
JEAN-PAUL b.
1940 Polymath Goude, a French-born graphic designer, illustrator, photographer, and film director, came to public attention as the art director of Esquire magazine in the i97os and has since been celebrated for his advertising campaigns and illustrations, particularly in the fashion and music industries.
He is famous for cutting and reassembling photographic images into fantastic figures far removed from their originals.
Many of his most recognizable images are of the singer, model, and actress Grace Jones, such as the poster and cover image for the Victoria and Albert Museum's 2o1l exhibition "Postmodernism: Style and Subversion, 1975-1990." GRAVES, MICHAEL 1934-2015 See pp.
26, 378.
GREIMAN, APRIL b.1948 Seep.306.
GUERRIERO, ALESSANDRO D.
1943 Alessandro Guerriero is an architect and designer who in i976 established Alchimia, one of the most influential of the Radical Design groups that prefigured Memphis and full-blown Postmodernism.
He was also a founding member of Domus Academy, the Milan postgraduate design school, and has designed furniture and completed many architectural projects, including working on the Groninger Museum in collaboration with Alessandro Mendini Philippe Starck.
Michele De Lucchi, and Coop Himmelb(l)au\*.
He is also an accomplished artist.
In 2oo3 he was appointed president of the Nuova Accademia di Belle Arti in Milan.

## GUFRAM
Founded by the Gugliermetto brothers in 1966 as a producer of upholstered seating for public venues like theaters, hotels, and universities, Gufram soon moved into more distinctive furnishings.
The frm is best known for its quirky Pop art-like objects, whıch include the Pratone lounge (i971) and Capitello seating pieces (ı972) by Radical Design group Studio 65\*, and the whimsical Cactus coat stand by Guido Drocco and Franco Mello (1972).

## HAFHER,
DOROTHY b.
1952 A ceramist and sculptor who has worked in New York since 1974, Hafner is known for her tableware designs as well as her architectural installations, all of which are characterized by irregular geometric patterns and striking color combinations that suggest the aesthetic of postmodern design.
She was the first American artist commissioned by Tiffany & Co (in i979) to design handmade porcelain dinnerware, and was chosen to direct the ceramics program at Parsons School of Design in i98o.
In the following decade, she designed bold-patterned dinnerware for the Rosenthal Studio line, a carpet for V'Soske, and domestic pieces for Fieldcrest Cannon.
She later began to work in glass, designing bold abstract pieces that have been shown in many museum exhibitions.

## HAUS-RUCKER
Vienna-based group Haus-Rucker was founded in ı967 by architects Günter Kelp Zamp and Laurids Ortner in collaboration with painter Klaus Pinter, later joined by Carol Michaels and Manfred Ortner.
Paralleling the activities of the Radical Design groups in Italy, they staged exhibitions and proposed utopian ideas in search of a new architectural concept.
They produced fantastic drawings and pneumatic enclosures as well as films, to illustrate their ideas of transforming the relation between man and his environment to remedy the damage done to industrialized cities by pollution and overcrowding.
The group disbanded in i992 but has influenced many designers in their concern for environmental issues.
HAUSSMANN, ROBERT b.
1931 HAUSSMANH-HÖGL, TRIX b.
1933 Since their marriage in 1967, these Swiss designers have been collaborating on architecture and design projects that are often as much explorations GUERRIERO, ALESSANDRO Guerriero chair, unique object, painted metal, 1978.
HAFNER,DOROTHY Round About punchbowl and ladle, porcelain, 1984 GORLIN,ALEXANDER Guard booths, Battery Park City, New York, 1994.

## GUFRAM
GUFNNM Pratone couch, by Gruppo Strum, polyurethane foam, 1971 .
HEJDUK, JOHN Wall House 2, Groingen, Netherlands, designed 1973, built 2001.
JAHN, HELMUT James R.
Thompson Center, Chicago,Illinois, 1985. in theory as they are aesthetically innovative.
From the beginning, their design vocabulary took a postmodern view, with the couple first contributing to Studio Alchimia and later to the General Design Institute, the firm they founded in 1981.
Though trained as modernists—Robert as an interior designer at the Hochschule der Künste in Zurich, and Trix as an architect at ETH—they rejected International Style precepts to draw inspiration from historical sources, calling their approach manierismo critico (critical mannerism).
Together, they have produced architecture, interiors, and products, including double-coded pieces like a provocative “musical chair" whose back is a playable string instrument (i982), and the challenging Pillar Stump (ı978), a stack of drawers shaped like a Greek column, whose classical form is disrupted when the drawers are opened.
Their designs tend to combine irony with functionality.

## HEJDUK,
JOHN 1929-2000 American architect John Hejduk was one of the original New York Five, the group of architects including Michael Graves, Robert Stern, Richard Meier and Charles Gwathmey who in the i96os were practicing classic Modernism in the tradition of Le Corbusier.
Hejduk, who received his Master's in architecture from Harvard Graduate School of Design, became one of the most influential educators of his time as Dean of the School of Architecture at Cooper Union from 1975 to 200o.
He is better known as a theorist than as a practitioner.
He sought to invest architecture with emotional content and symbolism, exploring the idea in his unbuilt Wall House I and the posthumously built Wall House II, both understated iterations of a postmodern aesthetic—as is the recently restored Kreuzberg Tower in Berlin (i988).
HOLLEIN, HANS 1934-2014 See pp.
98.
IOSA GHINI, MASSIMO b.
1959 See p.
216.
ISOZAKI, ARATA b.1931 Seep.I04.
JAHN, HELMUT b.
1940 With buildings that are simultaneously modernist and classically ornamented, German-born, Chicago-based Jahn has trod a path just short of Postmodernism, though buildings like his State of Illinois Center in Chicago (i985) include overt references to classical structures.
In his use of rich materials and colors to enhance the architecture of important buildings, he followed the practice of Mies van der Rohe, who had designed the campus and directed the architecture program at Illinois Institute of Technology, where Jahn received his architectural training.

## JAMESON,
FREDRIC b.
1934 One of the most important postmodernist theorists, Jameson's book Postmodernism, or, the Cultural Logic of Late Capitalism (i991) analysed the postmodern condition as arising from a society that has lost its connection to history and prioritizes capitalist thinking over all other ideas, diluting culture in favor of commodification.
He also criticized the breakdown of distinctions between high and low culture, which he believed resulted in a lack of depth in modern expression.

## JÄRVISALO,
JOUKO b.
1950 Finnish interior architect and furniture designer Jouko Järvisalo established his studio in i983 and has worked for a number of Finnish manufacturers, in particular designing seating pieces.
Considered one of the important Finnish practitioners in the postmodern style, he was featured in the 2015 exhibition "Postmodernism 1980-1995" at the Designmuseo, Helsinki.
JENCKS, CHARLES b.
1939 See pp.
32, 388.

## JIŘIČNÁ,
EVA b.
1939 Czech-born architect Jiřičná trained at the Academy of Fine Arts, Prague.
She emigrated to London in 1968 and worked for the Richard Rogers Partnership, where she was responsible for the interiors of the iconic Lloyds Building, and for Future Systems, where she designed the innovative Way In store at Harrods, before opening her own practice.
After twenty-two years, she reconnected with her native country after the Velvet Revolution of 1989, and has since designed projects both there and in London, including retail spaces, public buildings, and exhibitions.
She is known for her interest in lightness and transparency, manifest in the distinctive steel-and-glass staircases seen in many of her projects.

## JOHNSON,
PHILIP CORTELYOU 1906-2005 Though basically a Miesian modernist, Johnson varied his designs to recognize changing fashions and client preferences.
The AT&T Building (now the Sony Building) in New York City, nicknamed the "Chippendale" building for its broken-pediment top, is one of his best-known and most contentious works and a postmodern icon.
Two years after graduating from Harvard University (majoring in philosophy), he became the first head of the Department of Architecture at the Museum of Modern Art, an unpaid position.
He returned to Harvard, receiving his BArch in 1943 from its Graduate School of Design, and in i949 designed his first solo building— his own Glass House in New Canaan, Connecticut.
He worked with Mies van der Rohe on the iconic International Style Seagram Building (i953) and in 1967–87 produced an impressive body of work with partner John Burgee.
His many honors included the Pritzker Prize in 1979, the first year of that award.

## KAMMERMEIER,
ANDERL b.
1957 German designer and craftsman Kammermeier studied goldsmithing, and since 198o has worked independently in various applied arts fields, including furniture, jewelry, interior and stage design.
He is best known for steel furniture, like the Kaiser Friedrich chair of 1995 in patinated steel, hide, and brass mesh, which shares an aesthetic with the young London avant-garde designers of that time.

## KARTELL
Based in Milan, Kartell was founded in 1949 by Giulio Castelli and his wife Anna as a producer of automobile accessories, moving into home furnishings in ı963.
It became a prominent manufacturer of plastics, producing the first one-piece molded plastic chair—a child's seat by Richard Sapper and Marco Zanuso—in 196ı, and later expanding to make a variety of furniture designs by major designers in brightly colored plastic.
In 2015, Kartell released a group of designs by Ettore Sottsass that had been designed in 2004 but never produced.

## KISMAN,
MAX b.
1953 Dutch graphic designer Max Kisman was a pioneer in using digital technology in magazine design, illustration, and typography.
Trained at the Gerrit Rietveld Academie in Amsterdam (1977), he established his own firm and has worked for a variety of clients, becoming known for bold design and overscale graphics.
He co-founded TYP/Typografisch Papier, an alternative typography and art publication KITA.
TOSHIYUKI b.
1942 Toshiyuki Kita is a designer known for furniture and objects that meld Western style with elements of Japanese culture Trained as an industrial designer in his native Japan, he worked in Italy for Mario Bellini\* and Silvio Coppola and opened his own office there in 1971.
His best-known design is the Wink chaise, a chair-cum-bird design that makes witty reference to Mickey Mouse with its ear-like projections.
He has designed furniture, tableware, and lighting, mostly for Japanese firms, and has taught industrial design in universities in Malaysia and Singapore.

## KLOT2,
HEINRICH 1935-1999 A prominent German architectural historian, author, and critic, Heinrich Klotz was the founding director of the Deutsches Architekturmuseum (DAM) in Frankfurt.
In History of Postmodern Architecture (i988), Klotz challenged the prevailing theory of Postmodernism, viewing it as an aspect of Modernism rather than an independent movement.
He was a pioneer collector of architectural drawings, sketches, and models, and from 1979 to 1987 recorded extensive interviews with prominent architects in Europe and the United States in what are known as the Klotz Tapes; these were published by DAM as a book in 2014.

## KHOLL
ASSOCIATES The company established in New York by German immigrant Hans Knoll in 1938 has distinguished itself as perhaps the leading American producer and distributor of modern design, though its history dates back to 1865 Germany, when Knoll's grandfather Wilhelm founded the family business.
With his employee and then wife Florence Schust Knoll, Hans Knoll marketed designs by JIŘIČHÁ, EUA fable and chair. for Formica competition, Corian,1983 KARTELL Child's chair, by Richard Sapper and Marco Zanuso polyethylene, 1964 KITA, TOSHIYUKI Wink adjustable chaise. foam and steel, 1980 KUKKAPURO, YRJÖ Experiment rocker, 1960.
LAUBERSHEIMER, WOLFGANG Amazonas table, with plant insert, 1988.
LEWIS+CLARK Kimono cabinet, c.
1991.
LEBOVICI, YONEL Fiche Male lamp, steel 1978.
Bauhaus masters Mies van der Rohe and Marcel Breuer when the International Style was sweeping the country.
When Postmodernism was emerging, Knoll commissioned by ROBERT VENTURI to design a series of nine chairs that became among the most admired, and most successful, iterations of historicist style in modern materials.
Knoll also commissioned a collection of seatinig and table designs by Richard Meier that recalls the forms of Josef Hoffmann.

## KOOLHAAS,
REM b.
1944 Better known as a Deconstructivist than a postmodernist, Dutch architect Koolhaas founded OMA (Office of Metropolitan Architecture) in 1975.
His "retroactive manifesto" Delirious New York (1978) expresses many of the concepts that inspired the postmodernists.

## KRIER,
LÉON b.
1946 See p.IIO.

## KUKKAPURO,YRJÖ
b.
1933 nunnnrunv, inJu U.
IyJJ Trained originally as an interior designer, Kukkapuro is one of Finland's most celebrated furniture designers, known for his classic Karuselli chair (1965), an early use of fiberglass shell construction.
The firm Avarte was established in i98o to produce his designs, which include the Experiment seating collection (i983), whose irregular arms and lively color accents are a nod to Postmodernism's transgressive forms.

## KURAMATA,
SHIRO 1934-1991 Seep.220.

## LA
PIETRA, UGO b.
1938 Architect, designer, and theoretician Ugo La Pietra graduated from the Politecnico di Milano in 1964.
As a member of the experimental design school Global Tools, he was one of the leaders of the Radical movement in Italy in the i97os.
He worked for various furniture producers, directed the journals In, Area, and Abitare con arte from 1971 to 1973, and was in charge of the design section of Domus from 1979 to 1986.
He has written several reference books and also designed furniture that adapted objects of industrial equipment.
LAUBERSHEIMER, WOLFGANG b.
1955 An independent designer and professor, Laubersheimer trained as a metal sculptor and in i985 founded the Cologne design group Pentagon, which was part of the avant-garde and experimental design movement of the time and had a strict minimalism that was less radical than the work of its Berlin compatriots.
Laubersheimer has since designed for companies as varied as Swatch, Mercedes-Benz, and Tiffany & Co. and is a professor at the Köln International School of Design.

## LEBOVICI,
YONEL 1937-1998 Not historicist postmodern but undeniably original, Yonel Lebovici designed lighting that played with conventional forms.
These include a floor lamp in the shape of a safety pin and his limited-edition Fiche Male (i978), a huge steel socket with fluorescent lights in its two projections.

## LEVANTI,
GIOUANNI b.
1957 Palermo-born Giovanni Levanti trained in architecture at the Università degli Studi in Palermo and in industrial design at Domus Academy in Milan.
He contributed the Nastassja side chair and the Alfonso bench (both i987) to the Memphis collections, and later worked in Andrea Branzi's studio.
He has taught industrial design in Palermo and at Domus Academy and designed furniture, lighting, and other products for a number of Italian firms.
Clark Ellefson b.
1949 James Angevine Lewis 1941-2008 Winners of FORMICA's first "Surface and Ornament" competition, Lewis+ Clark partnered in a furniture design studio from 198o to 1988, working independently afterwards.
Ellefson trained in sculpture, ceramics, and film, while Lewis was a furniture-maker.
In their eight-year partnership, the pair produced experimental projects in avant-garde furniture heavily influenced by the designs of Memphis, particularly the bold colors and discordant shapes.
Many of the works include symbolic or cultural references, as the Kimono cabinet series and the Aztec chair.

## LIBESKIND,
DANIEL b.
1946 A virtuoso musician before giving up music to become an architect, Libeskind is one of the most renowned of his generation.
Known as a Deconstructivist, he has designed many high-profile projects, all with the jagged-edge, asymmetrical forms that mark his personal aesthetic.
Among his best-known works are the Jewish Museum in Berlin, the Denver Art Museum, the Royal Ontario Museum in Toronto, and the Bundeswehr Military History Museum in Dresden.

## LINDAU
& LINDEKRANTZ Börge lindau, b.
1932 Bo lindekrantz, b.
1935 Two of Sweden's best-known designers, Lindau and Lindekrantz collaborated on furniture collections for Lammhults from the 196os to 198os, creating pieces that combined Scandinavianstyle practicality with bold silhouettes attuned to postmodernism's provocative forms.
Among their many awards is the prestigious Lunning Prize, in 1969.
They also designed furniture for Zero and ICF, and now work independently.

## LINDFORS,
STEFAN D.
1962 One of the first generation of Finnish designers to break away from the classic Scandinavian tradition, designing unconventional forms in furniture and lighting , as well as public installations including an outdoor clocktower for the 1996 Atlanta Summer Olympics.
Born in the Aland islands, he was trained in interior architecture and furniture design in Helsinki's Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu.
His 1988 Scaragoo lamp for Ingo Maurer has been widely published, and he has also designed furniture, glass, and ceramics that have been featured in international exhibitions.

## MARISCAL,
JAUIER b.
1950 Seep.226.

## MARUYAMA,
WENDY b.
1952 A designer and furniture maker prominent in the American craft field for almost four decades, Maruyama's work has been featured in exhibitions around the world, and she has taught woodworking and furniture design in California since 1985.
Maruyama is often inspired by East Asian traditions, though probably her best-known piece is Mickey Mackintosh (i98t), which put big ears on a Charles Rennie Mackintosh-silhouette chair, for a design that is at once an homage and a joke.

## MAX
PROTETCH GALLERY The Max Protech Gallery, opened by then twenty-three-year-old Max Protetch in Washington, DC, while he was still a graduate student, became an early supporter of minimal, conceptual, and Pop art in its early years.
When the gallery moved to New York in 1978, it became one of the important showcases for emerging talents in avant-garde architecture and design, showing the work of Michael Graves, Aldo Rossi, and Robert Venturi, among many others.
The gallery was sold in 2oo9 and subsequently closed.

## MECOY,
KATHERINE b.
1945 See p.312.

## MCKIE,
JUDY KENSLEY b.
1944 Trained as a painter, McKie transitioned to designing and making furniture and is best known for her anthropomorphic forms that combine woodcrafting and bronze-casting techniques.
In drawing on historical references, including Egyptian, African, and pre-Columbian, her pieces are compatible with postmodern ideas.
MEIER, RICHARD b.
1934 Celebrated for his pristine white buildings in the tradition of Le Corbusier, American designer Richard Meier is perhaps an unlikely inclusion in a compendium of postmodern design.
Despite participating in several projects along with committed postmodernists —designing dinnerware and flatware for SWID POWELL, hollowware for CLETO MUNARI and objects for Alessi—Cornell University-trained Meier remained true to his modernist roots with the New York Five, and his architecture avoids fanciful touches or overt classical imagery.
But although emphatically not postmodern, Meier's products do show clear references to historic designs: those of Josef Hoffman and Kasimir Malevich.

## MEISTER,
HOWARD b.1953 A fifth-generation furniture maker, Meister was part of the downtown New York community of avant-garde artists and designers and a core member of the Art et Industrie\* gallery, participating in its shows from the i98os until its 1997 closing.
As part of the developing art-furniture movement, he designed provocative and intriguingly named works that won him awards, places in museum collections, and commissions for interiors and set decoration.
Though not specifically postmodern, his work MCKIE, JUDY KENSLEY Snake table, wood and glass, 1988.
MEIER, RICHARD Armchair, part of a collection for Knoll, black lacquered wood, 1978.
MORRISON,JASPEA Thinking Man's Chair, limited edition for Cappellini, metal, 1986.
HATALINI, ADOLFO Volumina desk (closed and open), cypress with wenge detailing, produced for Sawaya & Moroni, 1986. plays with form and materials, and was included in the 2oı Victoria and Albert Museum exhibition on the subject.
MEMPHIS See p.
360.
MENDINI, ALESSANDRO b.
1931 See pp.
38, 392.

## META
MEMPHIS An attempt to revive the Memphis idea after the original collaborative ended, Meta-Memphis recruited artists to design furniture and household objects.
The group showed furniture designs by artists such as Michelangelo Pistoletto, Sandro Chia, Franz West\* and Mimmo Paladino in a twenty-piece collection that made its debut in 1989 in a Venetian palazzo.
It failed to gain much attention or many sales, and ended after a short period, although a few of the objects are today being marketed by the firm that purchased the rights to Memphis designs.

## MOLTENI
Italian furniture producer Molteni was established in 1934 by Angelo Molteni in Giussano.
It quickly expanded from a workshop to a factory, by 1947 employing over sixty people, and after the Second World War installing modern machinery and increasing production, moving into contract furnishings for international clients.
In i96ı Molteni promoted and participated in the first Salone del Mobile furniture fair in Milan.
In the i98os the company began a twenty-year association with ALDO ROSSI, producing designs that included the classic Teatro collection and the Parigi armhair.
Molteni has also produced furniture by Michele De Lucchi and other architects and designers of international renown.
MOORE, CHARLES 1925-1993 See pp.
114, 396.

## MORRISON,
JASPER b.
1959 London-born Morrison is one of the leading designers of his generation.
A graduate of Kingston Polytechnic (now Kingston University) and with a Master's degree from London's Royal College of Art, he opened his studio in London in i986, becoming one of the coterie of young British designers exploring new directions in furniture design.
He was an original member of Narrative Architecture Today (NATO), the group organized by NIGEL COATES that sought to find a new language for architecture.
His best-known designs include the 1985 Wingnut chair, which has the look of a randomly assembled piece, and the 1986 Thinking Man's Chair.

## NATALINI,
ADOLFO b.
1941 Trainied as an architect at the University of Florence, Natalini was one of the founders and an active member of Superstudio, the conceptual design collaborative in Florence, from i966 to 1978.
With his colleagues at Superstudio he initiated the concepts of Radical Architecture.
He has specialized in town planning and worked on projects for historical cities including Frankfurt, Jerusalem, Karlsruhe, Parma, and Florence.
He has designed furniture for Sawaya & Moroni, served as artistic director for several firms, and written a number of books on architecture.

## NÉOTÙ
GALLERY Founded in Paris in ı984 by Gérard Dalmon and Pierre Staudenmeyer, Néotù was a gallery specializing in contemporary furniture in the then new category of design-art.
Showing work in one-off or limited editions, primarily by French designers and artists, the gallery helped to encourage the acceptance of avant-garde design.
In i99o, a branch was opened in New York City, and the gallery began representing VIA, the French furniture association in America.
Néotù closed in 2001.
OUTRAM, JOHN b.1934 Seep.118 PAPADAKIS, ANDREAS 1938-2008 Andreas Papadakis was the founder of Academy Editions, which has published architectural monographs and books by upcoming and established architects, including CHARLES JENCKS's The Language of Postmodern Architecture.
Academy began as a bookshop before moving into limited editions of other publishers' books, but Papadakis then decided to make his own and acquired the Tiranti publishing company and the London Art Bookshop.
He sponsored seminars, conferences, and exhibitions promoting new directions in architecture at important institutions like the Architectural Association and the Deutsches Architekturmuseum, Frankfurt, and held forums at the Tate Gallery and the Royal Academy of Art.
He also authored several books on postmodern design, Deconstructivism, and classicism.
After selling the business in i99o, Papadakis founded his eponymous publishing company.
PESCE, GAETANO b.
1939 See p.232.

## PLATER-ZYBERK,ELIZABETH(SEE
DUAKY PLRTER-ZYBERK)

## POLTRONA
FRAU A leading Italian furniture producer, Poltrona was founded in 1912 in Turin by Renzo Frau as a specialist in leather seating.
Early designs reflected Art Deco styles, and over the years the firm moved into more contemporary design, though remaining focused on quality seating pieces.
In 2oi3 the company opened a museum designed by Michele De Lucchi, in Tolentino, the location of its headquarters since the 196os.
The firm's collections include designs by De Lucchi, MASSIMO IOSA GHINI and other major international names.

## POLTRONOUA
Many of the iconic pieces associated with Radical Design were made by one company, Poltronova, which was established in ı957 in Pistoia, Italy.
Founder Sergio Cammilli wished to reinvigorate the furniture industry with new ideas and enlisted Ettore Sottsass as his artistic director, to oversee design.
Poltronova introduced products including Archizoom's Mies and Superonda seating pieces, Superstudio's Passiflora lamp, and Sottsass's Ultrafragola mirror, as well as other avant-garde works, gaining them a reputation as "the radical factory." Several of the early designs were developed from prototypes shown at the 1966 "Superarchitettura” exhibition in Pistoia, the Radicals' premiere.
Thirteen Poltronova products were included in the New York Museum of Modern Art's 1972 exhibition "Italy: The New Domestic Landscape." Many of the pieces Sottsass designed for Poltronova until he left the firm in the mid-197os were too radical to be put into production, and others were made only as prototypes or in small series.
The firm today operates as Centro Studio Poltronova and maintains an archive of its historic designs.
PORTOGHESI, PAOLO b.
1931 Seep.
122.
ROSSI, ALDO 1931-1997 See p.
126.
RYERSON, MITCH b.1960 Initially honing his craft skills as a builder of wooden boats, Ryerson moved into more creative paths by attending the Boston University Program in Artisanry, receiving a degree in furniture design (i982) and becoming a founding member of Powderhouse Woodworkers, where he developed a reputation for highly original, colorful objects in wood, metal, and other materials that deftly straddle the line between folk craft and fine art.
He has done many commissions for playgrounds and sculptural installations, but is best known for his furniture designs.
His furriture often plays with traditional forms, sparking them with ornament, color, and sometimes lettering.
He has taught at Haystack Mountain School of Crafts, Maine, Penland School of Crafts, North Carolina, and the Massachusetts College of Art and Design in Boston.

## SALADINO,
JOHN b.
1932 American interior designer John Saladino's classically inspired rooms ignited a new interest in distressed plaster, natural materials, interior columns, and other historicist elements translated for modern interiors.
Though his work is more historicist rather than postmodern, the broad media exposure of his elegant but warm and welcoming rooms was an important influence on American interior design in the i98os.
A graduate of the University of Notre Dame and Yale School of Architecture, he founded Saladino Group in 1972 and expanded from designing interiors into furniture and accessories, with a network of showrooms.

## SAPPER,RICHARD
1932-2015 One of the most important industrial designers of his time, Sapper was born in Munich but spent most of his life in Milan.
He is not associated with any specific design style.
In collaboration with Marco Zanuso he created the first ever one-piece molded plastic seating— a child's chair for Kartell\* (1964)—as well as innovative electronic objects for Brionvega.
In 1972 he designed the now classic Tizio lamp for Artemide\* (the first to eliminate SCHOENLAUBI GUILLEMETTE L'HOIR PARIS Jewelry, Galalith, c.
1980.
STANGER, JAY Stuffed Seating, leather and aluminum, 1989. unsightly wires, by running the current through aluminum arms), and for Alessi, the i979 Coban espresso machine, an updated traditional household item that sold more than a million units in two decades.
His 1983 postmoderninfluenced tea kettle for Alessi has a whistling spout that mimicks the sound of a locomotive.
As industrial design consultant to IBM, he also contributed to the design of IBM's early ThinkPad laptop computer.

## SAVAGE,
DAVID b.
949 A woodcrafter whose pieces are aptly called "furniture with soul," David Savage was born in Yorkshire, England and in i968 won a place to attend the Ruskin School of Art at the University of Oxford, leading to graduate study at the Royal Academy of Arts in London.
After graduation, he worked on the Royal Oak and then the Cable Street murals in London, leaving the project in i982 to set up his furniture workshop.
He teaches woodworking techniques, has written regularly for woodworking publications, and continues to design his distinctively organic pieces, which transcend designations of any specific style.

## SAWAYA
& MORONI Established in 1984 by architect and designer William Sawaya and entrepreneur Paolo Moroni, this Italian firm began during a time of innovation and experiment and made its mark by collaborating with designers and architects from different countries with widely varying design approaches Michael Graves was one of the first to design for the company, and it has produced objects by postmodernists Ettore Sottsass, Charles Jencks, Adolfo Natalini\*, and Bořek Šípek as well as pieces by many others of international renown.
The company's collections include furniture, silver, glassware, and accessories as well as art projects.

## SCHER,
PAULA b.
1948 See p.316.

## SCHOENLAUB/GUILLEMETTE
L'HOIR PARIS Having seen Art Deco jewelry designs made by a French factory in a material called Galalith, advertising executive François Schoenlaub established the company Guillemette L’Hoir Paris in i976, and began to make jewelry combining Galalith with materials like

## SEASIDE
1986 Built on 8o acres (32 ha) of land in the Florida Panhandle, the town of Seaside was designed as a community of diversity, in appearance as well as population.
Under the master plan devised by Duany Plater-Zyberk\*, the public buildings were designed by prominent architects, including Aldo Rossi, Léon Krier, and Robert A.
M.
Stern, in the type of regional vernacular revived by Postmodernism.
SHIRE, PETER b.
1947 See pp.
236, 400. ŠÍPEK, BOŘEK 1949-2016 See pp.
242, 402.
SOTTSASS, ETTORE 1917-2007 See pp.
