SOWDEN,GEORGE b.1942 Seepp.248 SPEAR, LAURINDA b.
1950 See p.72.
STANGER, JAY b.1958 Jay Stanger describes his work as "functional artwork," but the phrase hardly begins to describe the exuberance of his colorful and impressionistic designs, which can be considered postmodern in their refusal to follow any given standards of what a piece of furniture should look like.
His pieces, which include chairs, tables, beds, and cabinets in a mash-up of geometric forms and paintbox hues, are undeniably transgressive and in that sense congenial to postmodern ideas.
A graduate of the Boston University Program in Artisanry, Stanger attended the Leeds Design Workshops in Easthampton, Massachusetts, and Rochester Institute of Technology, New York, subsequently opening his studio.
He has been shown by leading galleries and in major museum exhibitions.
In 1983 his designs were featured in an exhibition of objects made from Formica ColorCore at the Gallery at Workbench, New York, and in i984 in "Post Modern: Colour," a show of new furniture at the Victoria and Albert Museum, London.
STARCK,PHILIPPE b.1949 Seep.256.
STERN, ROBERT A.
M. b.
1939 See pp.
132, 418.
STILETTO (FRANK SCHREINER] b.
1959 German designer Frank Schreiner (aka Stiletto) gained attention for his quirky Consumer's Rest Lounge Chair (1983), a readymade object devised from a supermarket shopping cart.
The piece echoed the ad hoc, postmodernist sensibility of the young British designers who were then exploring the possibilities of designing with everyday and cast-off materials.
Starting in i982, he co-organized and participated in festivals of Neoism, according to which all behavior is part of a performance, and became known as a representative of New German Design.
In i985 Stiletto designed the first lamp with flexible arms, appropriated from a shower mechanism and a ceiling cap from a motorcycle hubcap.
Only one hundred of these were produced, though the flexible arm concept was later adopted by many others.

## STIRLING,
JAMES 1926-1992 See p.
138.

## STOPPINO,
GIOTTO (LUIGI) 1926-2011 One of the many Italian architects who designed a wide range of furnishings for a number of companies, Stoppino was trained in architecture in both Milan and Venice and was a partner with Vittorio Gregotti and Lodovico Meneghetti from 1953 to 1968, after which he established his own office as an independent designer.
Beginning in 196o, he was active in Italy's Associazione per il Disegno Industriale (ADl), serving as its director and then president, and taught in Milan and Palermo.
He designed many pieces in plastics, including the very successful nesting tables (i968) and magazine rack (ı97ı) for Kartell in injection-molded ABS plastic and a fiberglass chair for Driade\*.
He designed a champagne and ice bucket for Cleto Munari, as well as a diversity of objects for other firms.

## STUDIO
65 Founded in Turin in 1965 by students Franco Audrito and Piero Gatti, Studio 65 transposed pop art images and classical motifs in tongue-in-cheek pieces for Gufram\* like Bocca, a sofa shaped like oversize lips, and Capitello, a foam lounge chair shaped like an ionic column, anticipating iconography often used in later postmodern architecture by Venturi, Graves, HOLLEIN and others.

## SUNAR-HAUSERMAN
Hauserman, Inc. was founded by Earl F.
Hauserman in i913.
The company developed and manufactured movable interior steel partitions, becoming their exclusive producer by 1927.
Hauserman expanded and enlarged its product line through acquisitions to include office system design and the production of wood and steel office furniture.
The firm acquired Sunar, a Canadian office furniture manufacturer and designer, in i978 and became Sunar-Hauserman in i983.
Hiring postmodern designers Michael Graves and ARATA ISOZAKI to design its trade showrooms, Sunar-Hauserman provided one of the most prominent American showcases for the new style.
By i989, overexpansion and a sluggish economy forced the company out of business

## SUPERSTUDIO
Superstudio was a Florentine collaborative formed in 1966 by Adolfo Natalini and Cristiano Toraldo di Francia, architecture students at the Università degli Studi di Firenze; they were later joined by Alessandro and Roberto Magris, Alessandro Poli, and Piero Frassinelli.
They presented conceptual Anti-Design projects at the “Superarchitettura" exhibitions in Pistoia and then Modena.
In 1970, Superstudio introduced a collection of “neutral surface" furniture, covered with a black-on-white grid-patterned laminate.
The group disbanded in 1978.
SUSSMAN, DEBORAH 1931-2014 See p.
320.
SWID POWELL Se .
332.
SZEKELY, MARTIN b.
1956 French furniture designer Martin Szekely, based in Paris, began his career as a copper-plate engraver but moved into furniture in the late i97os, working primarily in metal.
While studying in Italy he met Ettore Sottsass and Michele De Lucchi.
His designs are simple shapes and modern materials whose function dictates their form.
The 1983 Pi chair is his most celebrated design.
TAVEIRA, TOMÁS b.
1938 See p.144.
TAYLOR, GERARD b.
1955 Scottish-born Gerard Taylor trained at Glasgow School of Art and at the STUD1O 65 Capitello seating, for Gufram, polyurethane foam, 1972 TISDALE, DAVID Tabletop accessories, anodized aluminum, C.1986.

Royal College of Art in London, where he graduated in i981.
Hired by Ettore Sottsass, Taylor moved to Milan and worked in Sottsass's office on a variety of projects, including retail stores for Esprit\*.
He also collaborated on designs for Memphis's second collection.
Returning to London after several years, Taylor established his own practice focusing on furniture and product design.

## TERRAZZO
The Memphis philosophy and aesthetic was given extended life through Terrazzo, a magazine published by Ettore Sottsass in collaboration with Barbara Radice from 1988 to 1996, after the dissolution of the Memphis group.

## THUN,
MATTEO b.
1952 Seep.260.
TIGERMAN, STANLEY b.
1930 See p.150.

## TISDALE,
DAVID b.
1956 Born in San Diego, David Tisdale studied architecture and design at the University of California, Berkeley and received a Master's from San Diego State University.
Having started his career as a jewelry designer, he began in i986 to specialize in tableware and accessories and has since expanded to interior design as well.
Tisdale has become known for his collections of architectural-looking objects in brightly colored anodized aluminum, made in his own studio, but has also designed products in a number of other materials for tableware and houseware firms.

## TSCHUMI,
BERNARD b.
1944 Although he is known more as a theorist than as a practitioner, Bernard Tschumi achieved international fame for his innovative, competition-winning design for the Parc de la Villette, a i35-acre (55.5 ha) cultural park in Paris.
Born in Switzerland, Tschumi graduated in architecture from ETH Zürich.
In addition to architectural projects in many countries, he has taught at the Architectural Association in London, Princeton University, and Cooper Union School of Architecture and since 1988 has been associated with the Graduate School of Architecture at Columbia University.
He frequently refers to other disciplines in his work, as seen in his “Manhattan Transcripts" (1977–81), designs and collages using new forms of "architectonic notation," including such ideas as “form follows fiction." TUSQUETS BLANCA, OSCAA b.
1941 See p.
156.

## TYPOGRAPHISCHE
MOHATSBLÄTTER [TM] The Swiss professional journal of graphic design Typographische Monatsblätter, begun in 1933 by the Swiss typographers' union, was an early promoter of the aesthetic of "Swiss style" typography internationally.
Under the direction of Wolfgang Weingart, who served on its editorial board from 1970 to 1988, it became a key influencer in the transformation of typography and the modern practice of graphic design.

## UFO
Formed in 1967 in Florence, UFO was the most extreme of the Radical Architecture groups.
Headed by Lapo Binazzi\*, it staged interventions in public spaces, with inflatable structures that parodied ordinary objects and comic-strip interiors that mocked consumer culture.
Its activities were prominently featured in the influential magazines Domus and Casabella, putting them before an audience that would prove receptive to the idea of a design revolution.
UFO disbanded in ı978.
UMEDA,MASANORI b.1941 Seep.264 UNGERS, OSWALD MATHIAS 1926-2007 German architect, theorist, and educator Oswald Mathias Ungers studied at the Karlsruher Institut für Technologie before opening his practice in Cologne in i95o.
Known as one of the neo-rationalists, Ungers was an important contributor to the avant-garde movement in Germany.
He sought to achieve “pure form" in his work, making prominent use of basic geometric elements, most notably squares and cubes.
He headed the architecture program at Cornell University from 1969 to 1975 and taught at Harvard University and UCLA before returning to Berlin in 1976.
His book City Metaphors (i982) is a visionary work that juxtaposes city maps with images from nature.
In i984 he worked on the interior of the Deutsches Architekturmuseum in Frankfurt--which assembled the first major collection of postmodern architecture and design— adapting the historic villa by adding classical elements.
Never entirely committing to historicism, Ungers felt that it was important to look back at the past, not in order to make historical copies but to look for ideas and memories that may form a basis on which to plan for the future.

## UTKIN,
ILYA (SEE BRODSKY, ALEXANDER] DANDERBYL, MICHAEL b.
1947 See p.
322.

## DENINI
The best-known and largest of the glass producers headquartered on the island of Murano, near Venice, Italy.
Venini began in 192ı when Paolo Venini, an attorney, and Giacomo Cappelin, an antiques dealer, decided to establish a glassworks to produce modern designs When the partners separated, Venini founded his own firm, which became internationally recognized for its designs and its unique range of colors In addition to some shapes and patterns designed by Venini himself, over the years the company has produced pieces by Napoleone Martinuzzi, Carlo Scarpa, Gio Ponti, Fulvio Bianconi, Tapio Wirkkala, and other celebrated designers.
In the postmodern years, both Alessandro Mendini and Ettore Sottsass designed for Venini.
VENTURI, ROBERT, AND DENISE SCOTT BROWN b.
1925 and b.
1931 See pp.
52, 422.

## VILLEROY
& BOCH Villeroy & Boch is a German producer of ceramic wares whose origins date back to i748.
The company manufactures ceramic tiles and sanitaryware as well as fine tableware in vitreous porcelain and bone china.
In i988 Villeroy & Boch sponsored a student project at the Akademie der bildenden Künste, Vienna, supervised by Matteo Thun.
The project turned out several designs obviously inspired by the postmodernists, and though none of them were actually produced, they were heavily promoted and exhibited.
The company also organized a Spirit of Europe project in cooperation with Parsons School of Design, New York, with students creating porcelain objects celebrating particular European countries.

## DITRA
One of an elite group of furniture producers specializing in cutting-edge design, Vitra was founded in 195o by Willi and Erika Fehlbaum and remains a family-owned company, headquartered in Switzerland.
In addition to producing furniture by world-renowned architects and designers, Vitra is known for its corporate campus at Weil am Rhein, south Germany, designed by such highprofile names as Zaha Hadid (the Vitra Fire Station was Hadid's first built work), FRANK GEHRY, Tadao Ando, and Herzog & de Meuron.
In i989 the company opened the Vitra Design Museum, designed by Gehry and housing a major collection of twentieth-century furniture VOGTHERR, BURKHARD b.
1942 Apprenticing as a cabinetmaker, Vogtherr studied industrial design in Kassel and Wuppertall and has worked as a freelance designer since 1972, designing furniture for a number of prominent producers in Germany, Italy, and America.
His designs tend to have spare, modern forms and are relatively conservative, but in the i97os he designed avant-garde pieces for Rosenthal, and his chairs for Klöber and Arflex in the i98os show an interest in Postmodernism

## VOORSANGER
& MILLS Voorsanger & Mills, founded in 1978 by Bartholomew Voorsanger and Edwin Mills, were among the New York-based architects whose work was associated with the postmodern movement.
They became known for a refined interpretation of the style in upscale residences and high-profile commercial projects.
The luxury restaurant Le Cygne, now closed, was much-praised for its modern style at a time when others of its type conformed to a more traditional eighteenthcentury formalism.
The partners have since separated and today practice as Voorsanger Architects and Edward I.
Mills & Associates.
WEINAND, HERBERT JAKOB b.
1953 Herbert Jakob Weinand is a German designer who was part of the 198os avant-garde Neues Deutsches Design movement and a member of the group Berliner Zimmer.
He was known for his Kubus tables (i986), which stood on oversize industrial casters, and the UENINI Scent bottles, by Laura de Santillana, colored glass, c.
1990 VILLEROY E BOCH Venetian Carnival teapot, by Christine Folks for Parsons student design project, porcelain, 198os VOGTHERR,BURKHARD Tonga chair (closed-arm version), 1986.
VOGTHERR, BURKHARD Tonga chair (open-arm version), metal and fabric, for GMX, 1986 WILFORD, MICHAEL British Embassy, Berlin, Germany, 2000.
WILFORD, MICHAEL Lone Oak Hall, East Sussex, England, 2000.
WEST, FRANZ Snakelike lamp for Meta Memphis, metal, c.
1989.
Karajan desk and table (i988), named after famous conductor Herbert von Karajan and shaped like grand pianos, with white silkscreened keys.
He had apprenticed as a cabinetmaker and studied interior, furniture, and product design, all of which he practiced, and he also operated his own design gallery.

## WEINGART,
WOLFGANG b.
1941 See p.
326.

## WEST,
FRANZ 1947-2012 Austrian artist Franz West's work was influenced by the action and performance art of the i96os, including the Viennese Actionism movement, in which artists engaged in destructive, violent acts in public spaces.
Rejecting the traditional passive way of viewing art, West created pieces as social experiences, as in his “Adaptives," which he began making in the 197os: small sculptures meant to be picked up and carried around, and chairs, lamps, and tables that can be moved or used.
He is also known for his distinctive collages that mix over-painted magazine clippings, adverts, and pornographic images to absurdist effect, and for bulky sculptures of foam, papier-mâché, cardboard, and objects that reference Expressionist painting.

## WILFORD,
MICHAEL b.
1938 British architect Michael Wilford trained at the Northern Polytechnic School of Architecture and the Regent Street Polytechnic Planning School, both in London.
From i96o onward he worked for JAMES STIRLING, becoming a partner in i971; the partnership James Stirling Michael Wilford continued until Stirling's death in 1992.
The firm designed celebrated postmodern works, including No I Poultry, Tate Britain's Clore Gallery in London, and Neue Staatsgalerie in Stuttgart.
Since establishing his own practice, Wilford has designed many major projects, including the British

Embassy in Berlin (2ooo), continuing to be influenced by a postmodern vocabulary.

1932 See p.162.

## YAGI,
TAMOTSU In the i98os, Tamotsu Yagi, a native of Kobe, Japan, was named art director of Esprit, a San Francisco-based firm that produced popularly priced casual clothing and became influential for its lively visual presentation.
Yagi was responsible for Esprit's bold, modern graphics, which won them a design leadership award from the American Institute of Graphic Arts (AIGA) in 1986 His graphics were complementary to Esprit's store interiors, which were designed by Sottsass Associati.

## ZANOTTA
Zanotta was founded by Aurelio Zanotta in 1954, as the Italian design community was beginning its rise to international prominence.
The firm produces many categories of furniture, by a prestigious roster of designers that has included postmodernists Ettore Sottsass, Alessandro Mendini, and Andrea Branzi.
The company has gained notice for its archive collection of works that were never massproduced, including works from the 1930s to 195os by Carlo Mollino, the Castiglioni brothers, and Max Bill.
In addition to its main collection, Zanotta produces a special series of limitededition, mostly handcrafted pieces.

## ZANINI,
MARCO b.1954 Seep.268.

## ZANUSO,
MARCO 1916-2001 One of the first wave of Italian designers to become prominent in the decades after the Second World War, Marco Zanuso opened his design office in 1945.
He taught at the Politecnico di Milano, was involved in organizing Milan Triennial exhibitions, and was editor of Domus and later Casabella.
His partnership with industrial designer Richard Sapper produced several iconic designs, including a child's chair for Kartell that was the first chair to be made solely of injection-molded plastic, and electronics for Brionvega.
Though practicing in the era of Postmodernism, Zanuso did not depart from his original modernist aesthetic. # CONTRIBUTOR BIOGRAPHIES John Stuart Gordon is Assistant Curator of American Decorative Arts at the Yale University Art Gallery, where he curated the exhibition "The Architect's Table: Swid Powell and Postmodern Design." He is the author of A Modern World: American Design from the Yale University Art Gallery.
Johanna Grawunder is a designer/artist who works on projects ranging from public lighting and color installations, architectural interventions, and interiors, to limited-edition furniture and light collections for galleries in US and Europe.
She has studied and worked with Gianni Pettena and Cristiano Toraldo di Francia of Superstudio in Florence (1984-1985) and has worked for Sottsass Associati (1985-20ot), becoming a partner in 1989 With Ettore Sottsass she co-designed many of the firm's most prestigious architecture and interiors projects.
In 2ooı she opened her own design studio in San Francisco and Milan.
Sean Griffiths is Professor of Architecture at the University of Westminster, London.
He was a founding member of FAT (Fashion Architecture Taste), the celebrated postmodernist-inspired London architecture studio formed in 1995 and disbanded in 2014.
Steven Heller is co-chair of the MFA Designer as Author program at the School of Visual Arts, New York.
He was for thirty-three years an art director at the New York Times and has written and co-edited numerous books, and curated exhibitions, on the subject of graphic design.
Arlene Hirst is a reporter and journalist specializing in interior design and furnishings.
She was deputy director of design at Metropolitan Home magazine and now writes for publications including the New York Times Magazine, Dwell, Surface, Interior Design, and Elle Decor Italia.
Charles Jencks is a renowned cultural theorist, landscape designer, and architectural historian best known for his writings on Postmodernism and postmodern theory.
His bestselling books include The Language of Post-Modern Architecture, Adhocism, What is Postmodernism?, The Architecture of the Jumping Universe.
Hans Maier-Aichen is a curator, designer, and teacher.
He is the founder of the design label Authentics and a professor at several leading institutions, including Central Saint Martins in London, Karlsruhe University of Arts and Design in Germany, and China Academy of Arts in Hangzhou.
He has curated a number of exhibitions on contemporary design.
R.
Craig Miller is a curator and design historian.
He has served as Associate Curator of 2oth-century Architecture and Design at the Metropolitan Museum of Art, New York, Curator for Architecture, Design, and Graphics at the Denver Art Museum, and Senior Curator of Design Arts and Director of Design Initiatives at the Indianapolis Museum of Art.
He is the author of a number of books, including European Design since 1985: Shaping the New Century.
Alexander Payne is Deputy chairman and International Head of Design for the auction house Phillips LLC London-based Payne joined the company in i999 and was responsible for developing the design department, which has repeatedly set records for sales of avant-garde design.
Payne conducts auctions in New York, London, and Hong Kong and has lectured and written frequently about the history of design and the market for contemporary design.
One of the earliest to recognize the historical significance of Postmodernism, he has been organizing auctions of postmodern furniture and objects for almost two decades.
Denise Scott Brown is an American architect, author, and educator.
She is a principal in Venturi, Scott Brown & Associates and a longtime collaborator on architecture, planning, design, and theoretical writings with her husband, Robert Venturi.
She has taught at the University of Pennsylvania, the University of California at Berkeley, and Yale and Harvard universities.

## ACKNOWLEDGMENTS
No book is the work of its author alone, but is dependent on a support structure of associates and contributors.
This book is no exception.
I could not have done it without Christine Hauck, who worked tirelessly on photo acquisition and publication permissions, or Chike Maduabuchi, who checked and organized the bibliography.
Nor without the help of the many designers, and architects who took the time to speak with me and send me information and illustrative materials, the photographers who searched through archives for images of decades-old projects, and Dennis Zanone who shared his images of iconic objects.
My special thanks to Romina Zani, Alessandro Guerriero, Roberta Meloni, Kevin Keim, Peter Dixon, Allison Montgomery, Pilar Pardal, Carole Hochman, Evan Snyderman, Michelle Silvetti-Schmitt, Sal Forgione, and Joe Dick, who went the extra mile in filling my requests for photos and information; to Richard Wright and Todd Simeone at Wright, David Rago at Rago Arts, Darrell Rocha and Melanie Brister at Sotheby's, Katie Carder and her colleagues at Phillips, and Romain Monteaux-Sarmiento at Tajan for their cooperation in sending me images from auction house sales; and to Lorry Dudley, Keith Johnson, Susan Slesin, Michael Steinberg, Susan Lewin, Rick and Ruth Snyderman for sharing their experiences.
I am particularly grateful to the contributors; Denise Scott Brown, John Stuart Gordon, Johanna Grawunder, Sean Griffiths, Steven Heller, Arlene Hirst, Charles Jencks, Craig Miller, Hans Maier-Aichen, and Alex Payne, for their fine essays.
And to everyone at Thames & Hudson who helped bring the book into being: Lucas Dietrich, for thinking that postmodern design was a subject worth pursuing; Blanche Craig, for shepherding the manuscript through it many complex stages; Aimee Selby, for conscientious editing; and Karolina Prymaka, for designing a beautiful book.
Finally, thanks to Martin Gura, for his ongoing support and enduring patience.

1946–2001, Vicenza, 2002.
7 Sottsass would conceive each space in terms of volume, colors, materials, and textures.
He would then meticulously weave the separate elements together to create a larger coherent and vibrant whole, as if one were following a complex progression of pathways through an interior or building.
R.
Craig Miller, interview with Johanna Grawunder, July 21, 2o16.
Grawunder worked at Sottsass Associati from 1985 to 2001, becoming a partner in 1989.
8 Liana Cavallaro, email to R.
Craig Miller, July 29, 2016.
Cavallaro was Sottsass's design assistant from 199o to 2ooo.
It is important to note that his design work—his "private projects"—was kept separate from Sottsass Associati (which had divisions for architecture, industrial design, and graphics).
He wished to maintain tight control over this work Sottsass also used his designs as a way to explore new ideas quickly, which would then later be incorporated into the architectural work in his studio.
Interview with Johanna Grawunder.
Maria Sanchez, Sottsass's design assistant from 1981 to about 1986–87, has described his design process.
Sottsass would begin with small drawings in black marker pen in his sketchbooks, along with watercolor renderings on occasion.
He would bring the sketchbooks to the office, where Sanchez would photocopy them and begin the process of turning them into technical drawings.
Models or prototypes were sometimes made to allow Sottsass to further refine a design before production.
Technical drawings were then sent to artisans or manufacturers with notes on colors or finishes.
R.
Craig Miller, interview with Maria Sanchez, August 11, 2o16.
In contrast, see n.
27 for Venturi's design process.
9 Francesca Appiani, "Introduction," in Museo Alessi Design Interviews: Ettore Sottsass, Mantova, 2007, p.
35.

11 An important question is the degree of involvement of Denise Scott Brown in Venturi's design work.
Scott Brown and Venturi met in i96o.
They were married in 1967, and she became a partner in the firm in i969.
But she has more recently asserted that "Bob and ! have collaborated in everything since i96o." Denise Scott Brown, email to R.
Craig Miller, July 14, 2or6.
There was clearly a strong collaboration in the work coming out of the office during these years.
In periodic review sessions, Scott Brown would critique designs and offer suggestions, but she did not have hands-on involvement in the "decorative" designs coming out of the office; this seems to have been the work of Venturi alone.
R.

2ı The exhibition a mere two years later at Blum Helman Gallery, New York, marked the transition into the late period of Sottsass's career.
These revolutionary designs were massive, employed luxurious materials, required superb craftsmanship, and reveled in quixotic names.
This was a remarkable body of work—evidence of a master at the top of his game— solidifying Sottsass's reputation as one of the most important designers of the modern movement over the last 25o years.
22 Hiesinger has noted that Venturi "was thinking about a historical series of chairs as early as June 1976." Hiesinger, Out of the Ordinary, p.
202.
23 Venturi designed nine chairs, chronologically ranging from Queen Anne to Chippendale, Sheraton, Hepplewhite, Empire, Biedermeier, Gothic Revival, Art Nouveau and Art Deco.
Weintraub more concisely called it “a collection reflecting the history of furniture." Miller, interview with Weintraub.
24 Hiesinger, Out of the Ordinary, p.
202.
25 Ibid., p.
219.
26 Venturi developed four distinctive profiles for the Knoll chairs: Queen Anne, Chippendale, Empire, and a fourth for the other six chairs in the series.
Miller, interview with Weintraub.
27 Venturi developed a decidedly different design process to that of Sottsass (outlined in n.
8).
Most of his drawings are not dated, so one cannot always lay out a concise evolution for each design.
However, we know that Venturi would often start out with small sketches on 8½- by 1-inch (21.5 × 27.9 cm) paper, gradually enlarging the scale as the design evolved.
Assistants would then make full-size technical drawings in pencil on vellum.
Working on top of these drawings, Venturi would make dozens of full-size, free-hand sketches in black and colored markers on yellow tracing paper, as he developed and refined his ideas.
This process was repeated numerous times.
Full-size models of the chairs were also made.
It was an arduous process, given Venturi's exacting attention to every detail.
Miller, interview with Weintraub.

360, 444,457 Amazon cabinet 270 Dublin sofa 268, 273 glassware 268 jewelry 342, 345 Regolus fruit bowl 269 Roma armchair 268, 271 Rossella ceiling light 273 teapots 268, 268 Union bookcase 270 vases 268, 269, 272, 273 Zanone, Dennis, Zanone Residence, Memphis 432,432-35 Zanotta; Aurelio Zanotta 38, 43, 48, 180, 182, 184,456,469,469 Zanuso, Marco 14, 350, 453, 469 Cleopatra table 469 coffee service 351 see also Sapper, Richard Zebra Design 454 Zevi, Bruno 122 Ziegler, Zdeněk 278 Zumthor, Peter 11 Judith Gura is a design historian and author whose books include Guide to Period Styles for Interiors, A History of Interior Design (co-author), Design After Modernism: Furniture and Interiors, 1970-2010, and Sourcebook of Scandinavian Furniture: Designs tor the 21st Century.
She is on the faculty of The New York School of Interior Design, has taught at Pratt Institute and FIT, and lectures frequently about contemporary design and furnishings.
She is a contributing editor to Art + Auction, has curated exhibitions at the New York School of Interior Design, and worked on exhibitions at the Brooklyn Museum and the Whitney Museum.
Includes contributions by Hans-Maier Aichen, John Stuart Gordon, Johanna Grawunder, Sean Griffiths, Steven Heller, Arlene Hirst, Charles Jencks, R.
Craig Miller, Alexander Payne, and Denise Scott Brown.
